## Publisher’s Preface

Anyone with an interest in culture will know the historic intellectual importance of the publication of the distinguished scholar and martyr Husayn Muruwwa’s *Materialist Tendencies in Arab-Islamic Philosophy*. They will also know the gap this book filled in the Arabic library.

Muruwwa intended it to be an encyclopedic work. It became especially important to democratic and progressive readers and libraries, but also to intellectuals more generally, particularly those on the progressive left. It has also been widely used by university students and by anyone with a serious interest in Arab intellectual and cultural life.

The problem was that this very large book was originally published by Dār al-Fārābī in only two volumes. The first ran to more than a thousand pages, while the second was around eight hundred pages long.

Edition followed edition, and the book gradually became a standard reference for intellectuals, researchers at Arab universities, and university students. Today, some readers may be interested only in the pre-Islamic period, for example. Others may want only the sections on method, or Muruwwa’s materialist analysis of Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy, without needing the rest of the work.

There is also a practical advantage: buying one section of the book costs the reader less than buying the complete work.

For these reasons, we decided to publish a new edition in four volumes, which we now place in the hands of our readers:

**Volume I:** The book’s methodological introduction, followed by studies of the pre-Islamic period, the rise of Islam, and the early Islamic period.

**Volume II:** *Kalām*, or Islamic theology, with studies of the Muʿtazila and the Ashʿarites, followed by Aristotelian formal logic, since the Muʿtazila were the first to make use of it.

**Volume III:** Sufism, examined through two examples: Ibn ʿArabī and al-Suhrawardī; the *Epistles of the Brethren of Purity*; and a brief account of the development of the sciences among the Arabs.

**Volume IV:** The mature stage of philosophy: al-Kindī, al-Fārābī, and Ibn Sīnā.

**The Publisher**

# Chapter One

## Arab Philosophy in Its First Forms

### al-Kindī

#### 1 — Two Developments

How did Arab philosophy first appear in forms that marked its relative independence?

It is not easy to give a precise answer. But the history of Arab philosophical thought shows us two developments in which we can see, with some clarity, the first signs of philosophy emerging as something independent of *kalām*, or Islamic theology.

The first was Abū Yūsuf Yaʿqūb ibn Isḥāq al-Kindī,[^V4-1] who represented the first philosophical movement in Arabic to separate itself from Muʿtazilite theology.

The second was Sufism. This was another new form taken by Arab philosophy as it first moved beyond the framework of theological debate. It was quite different in character from the form philosophy took in al-Kindī’s system of thought.

Because both developments played such an important historical role in the movement from theological *kalām* toward philosophy as an independent field, we have devoted this chapter to the first. We already devoted Chapter Two of Volume Three to the second: Sufism.

#### 2 — The Person and the Historical Phenomenon

We are not studying al-Kindī[^V4-2] here as the “founder” of Arab philosophy, as histories of this philosophy often describe him.

He was not, in fact, its founder.

As we have seen throughout this study, the foundations of Arab philosophy had already been laid before al-Kindī, over a period of more than a century, by Muʿtazilite thinkers.

Those foundations did not appear by some accident outside the normal laws of historical development. To borrow Marx’s phrase, they did not “spring from the ground like mushrooms.”

This also means rejecting another common claim among historians of Arab philosophy, especially Western historians. According to this view, al-Kindī and the other representatives of Arab philosophy all drew from the same cultural sources: a mixture of Platonism, Neoplatonism and Aristotelianism. What differed from one philosopher to another was simply how much each took from this mixture, depending on personal temperament and individual preference.[^V4-3]

We reject this view, whether it is applied to al-Kindī and the Islamic philosophers who followed him, or to philosophers and thinkers in any other society or period.

We reject it for one reason: it is not scientific and does not fit the facts of history. At best, it comes from an idealist and highly subjective way of thinking that explains broad historical developments through the temperaments and personal inclinations of individuals. It isolates philosophers and thinkers from their living relationship with society and from the particular historical conditions in which they lived.

In the end, it separates them from the actual laws of historical development.

Of course, we do not deny that Greek thought—and Greek philosophy in particular—was one of the most important cultural sources absorbed by Arab-Islamic philosophy. It was present when this philosophy first began to develop among Muʿtazilite thinkers, and again when it first became relatively independent with al-Kindī, the first Arab philosopher. Greek philosophy remained an important influence throughout the later periods of Arab-Islamic philosophy, right up to Ibn Rushd, when that philosophical tradition reached the height of its vitality.

We do not deny this. On the contrary, we emphasize it.

What we deny is that Arab philosophy was produced by these Greek sources alone, apart from its primary source: Arab-Islamic society itself.

The Arab philosophers came from this society, and so did their philosophy. Within this new society, the Greek intellectual heritage itself took on a new life. It now faced new human problems and new intellectual questions within a new set of social relations: the relations of a feudal society with its own distinct features, not those of ancient Greek slave society.

For the same reason, we also reject the idea that al-Kindī somehow appeared suddenly in Abbasid society as a philosopher simply because he had read a “mixture” of Platonic and Aristotelian philosophy in the form in which it had reached him through translations from Greek or Syriac.

As we have said, such a claim does not stand up to serious examination. It also raises an obvious question: why did the translators themselves not become philosophers like al-Kindī? They had read this same philosophical material while translating it.

And why did the Muʿtazilites not become philosophers in the same sense as al-Kindī? We know historically that they too had read Greek philosophy in the same form in which it later reached him—as a mixture of Platonic and Aristotelian ideas.

There are two parts to the answer.

First, simply reading a philosophy does not make someone a philosopher. The person must also have certain qualities of their own: broad and deep learning, along with unusual intellectual abilities that allow them to understand their society and their time at a mature level. When a philosopher does emerge under such conditions, that philosopher becomes an indirect reflection of some of the most important features of the society and age in which they live.

Second, even all these personal qualities together are not enough to produce a true philosopher unless the society and the historical period are themselves ready for philosophy to become an independent form of expression, no longer confined within a theological framework. Philosophy must become capable of expressing, directly or indirectly, the major features of that society and that age.

---

We said at the beginning that we are not studying al-Kindī as the “founder” of Arab philosophy.

We can now add that we are not studying him simply as a particular individual either, within the limits of his personal life.

What matters to us is that al-Kindī appeared in the first half of the third Islamic century as the expression of a new historical development in Abbasid society at that particular moment.

Broadly speaking, this development was the growing instability and fracture within the ideology of the caliphal state. A combination of economic and social forces had begun to weaken that ideology. This allowed Arab philosophical thought, to some degree, to loosen its ties to theology and find new ways of expressing reality independently and indirectly, at a higher level of awareness and development.

But studying al-Kindī as a historical phenomenon certainly does not mean ignoring al-Kindī the individual.

For us, the opposite is true.

Studying a philosopher’s personal qualities can help us find some of the real keys to understanding that philosopher’s thought. From the standpoint of dialectical materialism and historical materialism, the personal side can reveal some of the most distinctive features of a thinker’s intellectual system—provided that we do not treat the individual as isolated from the living relationships that connected him to his society and his time.

#### 3 — Between Chance and Necessity

We must now look at the complex relationship between the different forces that shaped al-Kindī’s personality and his system of thought. Together, these represented the first appearance of philosophy as a relatively independent field.

This will help us judge how far that independence actually went, and how far al-Kindī was able to strengthen the position of Arab philosophical thought outside the framework of Islamic theology.

The historical need for philosophy to become independent had reached the point where it could act. But at that particular moment, it acted through an element of chance: it was Abū Yūsuf Yaʿqūb al-Kindī who happened to take on this role.

In other words, historical necessity appeared through a particular accidental form.

This gave al-Kindī a difficult task, but also a task of enormous historical importance. It therefore required someone capable of carrying it.

Was al-Kindī that person?

We can answer with a good deal of confidence that he was.

He possessed a wide range of abilities that allowed him to absorb much of the knowledge available in his time, at the highest level human knowledge had then reached.

Historians describe him as deeply learned in the Greek, Persian and Indian traditions of wisdom, (...) as highly knowledgeable in astrology and the other sciences,[^V4-4] and as “the outstanding scholar of his time, without equal in his age in his knowledge of all the ancient sciences.”[^V4-5]

These judgments had a real basis, even though it is only in relatively recent times that we have been able to see some of the evidence directly.[^V4-6]

Al-Kindī’s life was filled with scientific work of remarkable variety. It produced a large body of writing and achievement across the intellectual world, while his books covered every branch of philosophy and the other secular sciences.[^V4-7]

This work seems to have done more than give him a deep command of the knowledge of his time. He also helped enrich these different fields and strengthen the methods through which they could develop.

He was therefore genuinely equipped for the great task before him: responding to a historical need for Arab philosophical thought to move from a stage in which it remained tied to theology into a stage of relative independence.

Al-Kindī had to lay a solid foundation for the independent philosophical tradition that later Arab philosophers would build on.

The works of his that we have been able to read suggest that he did carry out this task—as far as could reasonably be expected in a period of transition like his, and as far as a thinker whose intellectual formation had taken place under the strong influence of Muʿtazilite thought could free himself from its deeply rooted theological influence.

#### 4 — Overlapping Forces and Historical Stages

We can now return to the question we have been building toward:

How far were al-Kindī, and the conditions of this transitional period, able to achieve the independence that philosophy had historically come to require?

At this point, we cannot focus on only one of the forces that helped shape al-Kindī’s intellectual character.

Muʿtazilite thought clearly had a deep influence on him.

But al-Kindī was also shaped by a number of other forces that were no less important and that, taken together, may have had an even stronger effect in pushing his philosophy in a direction independent of Muʿtazilite thought.

History remembers al-Kindī not only as a philosopher and theologian, but also as a mathematician, astronomer, physician, chemist, botanist and musician.

If we look at catalogues of his works in mathematics, the natural sciences, medicine and music, we find about twice as many titles as we find in theology, dialectic and philosophy.[^V4-8]

This difference in quantity also seems to reflect a difference in substance, judging by the respect later historians and specialist researchers showed toward his work in these scientific fields.[^V4-9]

Al-Kindī’s practice of these different sciences—some highly abstract and theoretical, others practical and experimental—must have affected his general intellectual outlook. In particular, it must have helped shape his scientific way of thinking.

That is one side of the picture.

The other is philosophy itself.

Al-Kindī’s intellectual character took shape at a time when Greek, Persian and Indian philosophies were no longer simply outside influences on Arab culture. They were becoming part of its internal structure.

This was especially true of Greek philosophy.

The translation movement of the period concentrated heavily on Greek philosophy. By then it had also become a mature and well-developed movement, with established traditions, rules, schools, methods and principles.

Al-Kindī himself was not simply a passive recipient of this movement. He seems to have taken part in strengthening its traditions and methods and in developing one of its schools.[^V4-10]

As the relationship between Greek philosophy and Arab culture developed to this level, and as al-Kindī actively took part in that development, his connection with Greek philosophy naturally deepened.

It therefore had to influence the direction of his thought, both directly and indirectly.

This was another major force in the formation of his intellectual character.

But alongside these two forces—his work in mathematics and the natural sciences, and his close engagement with Greek philosophy—we also have to consider the ideological factor.

Al-Kindī was deeply attached to the basic beliefs of Islam, both as a matter of faith and as a matter of ideology.

This ideological attachment was linked, first, to his social position. He came from an aristocratic family. His father had been governor of Kufa as a representative of the Abbasid caliph, and al-Kindī himself belonged to the wealthy circles of Baghdad.

Second, it was linked to his relationship with the caliphal court.

He enjoyed a highly favored position under al-Maʾmūn and then al-Muʿtaṣim. Al-Muʿtaṣim even chose him as a teacher for his son Aḥmad ibn al-Muʿtaṣim. The caliph himself appears in the title of one of al-Kindī’s works, *Al-Kindī’s Treatise to al-Muʿtaṣim on First Philosophy*.[^V4-11]

This element of faith and ideology could hardly have left his thinking untouched.

It naturally acted as a restraint on his scientific outlook and rational tendency, preventing them from carrying him too far outside the limits set by religious belief and by his social and ideological position.

Once we take all these forces together, with all the tensions between them, it becomes clear that al-Kindī’s intellectual formation was bound to be complex and internally contradictory.

That is one side of the matter.

On the other side, we must also remember what we said earlier: al-Kindī expressed a social phenomenon, not an isolated individual condition.

For this reason, it was also natural for his philosophy, taken as a whole, to reflect the overlap between two historical stages.

The first was the stage of Muʿtazilite *kalām*, or philosophy still taking a theological form.

The second was the stage in which philosophy began to acquire an independent identity of its own.

This intellectual overlap reflected an overlap between two stages in the development of the same society.

The first was the period when feudal relations of production were flourishing. The authority of the caliphs was strong, and so was their theological ideology.

The second was the period when those relations were beginning to decline. The caliphal state was weakening, and its ideology was becoming unstable and beginning to crack. At the same time, popular struggle was growing, along with new ideologies whose class character had not yet become clearly defined.

These two stages did not simply separate from one another at that point.

They overlapped for a long time.

As a philosophical phenomenon, al-Kindī embodied this overlap on both levels: the social and the intellectual.

That is why different tendencies came together within his philosophy, together with all their contradictions.

The clearest expression of this was his method of “combining,” or what later writers called “reconciliation”: combining religion and philosophy, faith and reason, knowledge drawn from divine revelation and knowledge reached through reason and theory, Aristotle and Plato, and so on.

#### 5 — The Philosopher of the Arabs

The people of al-Kindī’s own time were the ones who gave him the title “Philosopher of the Arabs” or “Philosopher of Islam.”[^V4-12]

This was an old title, not something invented by historians who lived long after him.

In our view, the title has historical and methodological significance. It has no ethnic or racial meaning, despite the interpretation given by some researchers.[^V4-13]

The sources themselves use two forms of the title: “Philosopher of the Arabs” and “Philosopher of Islam.”

Historically, this simply means that al-Kindī appeared at a time when no one else among the Arabs and Muslims had yet emerged with such a clearly defined philosophical identity.

The title was therefore not meant to distinguish him from later Islamic philosophers who were not Arabs.

It also tells us something about the intellectual path al-Kindī chose.

His knowledge ranged across many fields. He produced original work in the natural sciences, mathematics and medicine. He also had close intellectual ties to the Muʿtazilite theologians.

Yet despite all this, philosophy became the strongest and most characteristic tendency in his thought.

He took the philosophical path, and this shaped his thinking as a whole. His other scientific and intellectual interests supported this philosophical direction, helped define his philosophical method, and contributed to the structure of his philosophical system.

This was the historical form in which Arab philosophy first appeared outside the framework of theology.

If philosophy had not been the clearest and most dominant feature of al-Kindī’s intellectual identity in the eyes of his contemporaries, they would not have chosen to call him specifically a philosopher. They could just as easily have called him an astronomer, mathematician, physician, or something else.

We can also see this philosophical direction in the way al-Kindī dealt with the intellectual problems of his time and in the positions he took toward them.

The following examples make this especially clear.

**A — The Problem of Knowledge**

The problem of knowledge had been one of the central questions facing Arab-Islamic thought since the emergence of Muʿtazilite thinking and its development into *kalām*.

The Muʿtazilites approached the question through a theological method. Their position was formed largely in opposition to the school known as the People of Hadith.

The People of Hadith held that the foundations of Islamic belief could be known through only one source: transmitted or scriptural evidence, which they called *samʿ*—above all, texts from the Qurʾān and the Sunna.

They rejected another source: reason.

For the Muʿtazilites, the debate therefore remained within the limited field of knowledge of Islamic doctrine.

When some Muʿtazilites tried to widen the field of knowledge to include the natural world, they still remained within a theological method. They studied nature only through its relationship to God.

Al-Kindī was the first Arab thinker to place the problem of knowledge within a wider field.

He opened Arab thought to philosophical knowledge in the broad sense, and to theoretical and experimental knowledge of nature, without necessarily tying this knowledge to the question of its relationship with God.

One of the clearest signs of this new method appears in his definition of philosophy as “knowledge of things in their true realities, as far as human beings are capable.”[^V4-14]

In this definition, knowledge is no longer limited to knowledge of God or to knowledge of how “things” relate to God.

It now includes knowledge of things themselves.

The philosopher, in turn, becomes someone who seeks “the realities of things, insofar as they are general truths of what exists.”

Al-Kindī’s qualification—“as far as human beings are capable”—fits into his wider theory of knowledge.

He begins by dividing knowledge according to its object.

There is sensory knowledge, when the object is the world accessible to the senses: the material world.

There is rational knowledge,[^V4-15] when the object cannot be grasped by the senses and can be reached only through reasoning and the intellect, as in mathematical questions dealing with quantities.

And there is divine knowledge, whose object is God and religious law. Al-Kindī calls this the “science of divine lordship.”[^V4-16]

This division of knowledge by its objects also became the basis for another division, this time according to its source.

Al-Kindī divides knowledge into two types: divine knowledge and human knowledge.

The first comes from God. It reaches us through prophets and messengers, who brought the message of “affirming the lordship of God alone.”[^V4-17]

In al-Kindī’s view, this form of knowledge gives us access to truths that human beings cannot reach by their own abilities alone.

The second is knowledge that human beings can reach for themselves, “as far as their abilities allow.” This includes knowledge of things accessible to the senses and knowledge reached through reason.

But al-Kindī’s acceptance of a divine source for knowledge of the “world of divine lordship,” and his reliance on prophecy[^V4-18] as one way of reaching that knowledge, does not mean that philosophy has no path of its own to knowledge of that realm. Nor does it mean that he limits knowledge of God to “divine knowledge” alone.

His distinction between divine and human knowledge serves another purpose.

It separates the religious route to knowledge of God from the philosophical route.

Religion reaches God through the prophets.

Philosophy reaches God by knowing the cause—the cause of the world’s existence. In other words, it reaches God by a purely philosophical route.

Al-Kindī calls the “knowledge of the cause” *first philosophy*, following Aristotelian terminology. For him, it is “the noblest and highest-ranking part of philosophy”[^V4-19] because it is “knowledge of the first truth, which is the cause of every truth,”[^V4-20] and because knowledge of a cause is nobler than knowledge of its effect.[^V4-21]

Philosophy, then, as “knowledge of things in their true realities,” includes the things of nature, the things beyond nature, and the things belonging to the “world of divine lordship.” It seeks the truth of all of them.

But when philosophy seeks the truth of the “world of divine lordship,” it does so on a philosophical basis, not a theological one.

For al-Kindī, this philosophical basis begins when he develops a theory of causes.

Here we find a strict and interconnected system of causes and effects, descending from higher to lower levels: from the “First Cause”[^V4-22] down through a chain of higher beings and material beings.

Each earlier level affects what comes after it, but never the reverse.

The earlier thing is the cause; the later thing is the effect.

The cause stands higher than the effect in the order of existence.

What is higher acts on what is lower, while the lower does not act back on the higher.

Every link in this chain therefore acts insofar as it is the cause of what follows it, and is acted upon insofar as it is the effect of what comes before it.

The only exception is the First Cause.

It acts but is never acted upon, much like Aristotle’s unmoved “First Mover.”

In al-Kindī’s complex treatment of knowledge, then, we can see several things.

First, he works with the philosophical concepts available in his time.[^V4-23]

Second, with al-Kindī, the ways of knowing God and the arguments used to reach that knowledge move, for the first time in the history of Arab philosophical thought, beyond specifically religious forms and into philosophical ones. They are now viewed through the level of philosophical awareness Arab thought had reached at that time.

Third, for al-Kindī there is no fixed limit placed on the human mind in its search for knowledge.

Fourth, despite all this, the starting point of his thought still remains tied in certain ways to the prevailing theology.

**B — The Question of the “Ancient” Philosophers**

In al-Kindī’s time, the question of how to view the philosophy of the ancients came second in importance only to the problem of knowledge.

The philosophical heritage of the ancient world had already had a deep influence on Arab thought.

By al-Kindī’s time, attitudes toward this heritage had become part of an increasingly intense social and ideological conflict.

Al-Kindī lived through the controversy over the “createdness of the Qurʾān,” which was closely linked to Muʿtazilite thought.

Indeed, he seems to have been more than simply a contemporary observer of the controversy. He was involved in it from within.

On the theoretical level, he took part in some Muʿtazilite theological debates.

On the practical level, he experienced both sides of the Muʿtazilites’ changing fortunes: their period of favor under al-Maʾmūn, al-Muʿtaṣim and al-Wāthiq from 813 to 847, and the period of persecution that began under al-Mutawakkil in 847.

In other words, al-Kindī lived at the center of a controversy that shook Arab society.

The attitude a person took toward the “philosophy of the ancients” and toward the ancient philosophers themselves had become a test of that person’s ideological position.

A climate of fear and pressure developed around the issue in many forms, presented as a defense of Islamic belief. Philosophy itself was blamed for the crisis and accused of being the source of the “heresy” that was feeding it.

It was here that al-Kindī’s genuinely philosophical position became especially clear.

At the same time, it showed a sound philosophical method.

We can see this in two parts of his outlook.

The first concerns the philosophical heritage itself. Al-Kindī understood this heritage as the shared product of humanity, built over hundreds of generations by thousands of minds from different peoples and nations.

The second concerns those religious figures who presented themselves, in the name of religion, as representatives of the dominant ideology.

Al-Kindī’s first position has a sound basis because, first, it rests on a realistic understanding of humanity’s intellectual heritage and, second, it is free of ethnic and religious prejudice.

It is remarkable that a medieval thinker could arrive at such an understanding of intellectual heritage.

Al-Kindī expresses it clearly in the following passage:

> “If they”—meaning the earlier philosophers—“had not existed, then despite all the searching we might have done throughout our own lifetimes, we would never have gathered together these true first principles[^V4-24] through which we have been able to reach the later truths we seek. What we possess today was built up through earlier ages, one age after another, until it reached us. It required persistent research, constant effort and a willingness to endure hardship. What many generations achieved through such intense research, careful thought and sustained effort could never be achieved in the lifetime of one person alone, however long that life might be, however serious the research, however subtle the thought and however great the effort.”[^V4-25]

Al-Kindī then reinforces this philosophical position and method by citing Aristotle:

> “Aristotle, the most distinguished of the Greek philosophers, said: We should thank the fathers of those who brought us something of the truth, because they were the reason those people existed, quite apart from the fact that, through them, they also became a reason for us to reach the truth.”[^V4-26]

Al-Kindī then adds:

> “How well he spoke on this matter.”[^V4-27]

His philosophical outlook and method appear even more clearly when he declares his complete openness to knowledge, whatever its source:

> “We should never be ashamed to recognize the truth and make it our own, wherever it comes from, even if it comes from peoples far removed from us and nations different from our own. Nothing is more important to the seeker of truth than truth itself. Truth should never be belittled, nor should the person who speaks it or brings it to us. No one is diminished by truth. Truth ennobles everyone.”[^V4-28]

His attitude toward the religious establishment gives this defense of philosophy an even sharper edge.

Al-Kindī sees religious figures attacking philosophy and accusing its supporters and practitioners of “unbelief” and “heresy.”

He responds by exposing what he believes really lies behind their position.

They oppose philosophy, he says:

> “...to defend the false positions they have set up for themselves without deserving them, simply so that they can rule over others and trade in religion. Yet they themselves are without religion. Anyone who trades in something sells it, and anyone who sells something no longer possesses it. So whoever trades in religion has no religion. Anyone who opposes the pursuit of knowledge of things in their true realities deserves to be stripped of religion.”[^V4-29]

And, he continues, it is wrong to call such knowledge unbelief:

> “because knowledge of things in their true realities includes knowledge of divine lordship, knowledge of virtue, every useful form of knowledge and the path leading to it, as well as knowledge of everything harmful and how to avoid and protect ourselves from it.”[^V4-30]

In the final part of this passage, al-Kindī tries to take the weapon of religion out of the hands of the religious figures who oppose philosophy and turn that same weapon against them.

He then takes the argument further.

In defense of philosophy, he temporarily adopts the position of a legal scholar and builds a logical argument from religious premises, designed to reach the conclusion that Islamic law itself requires believing Muslims to pursue philosophy.

It is true that in this passage al-Kindī takes the whole substance of his attack on the enemies of philosophy from accepted religious premises, even though some of those premises may themselves come into tension with philosophy.

But what matters to us here is the firmness of his position: his loyalty to philosophy, his defense of it and the passionate commitment with which he takes its side.

At that moment, philosophy was only beginning to free itself from theological dependence. The ground beneath it was still soft and unstable.

At the same time, ideological conflict was increasingly taking the form of hostility toward philosophy in the name of defending religion.

For al-Kindī to take such a position at exactly this point in history was itself philosophically important. It helped philosophy establish itself on new ground, where for the first time it could begin to develop independently and follow a method of its own.

Al-Kindī carried out this historical task, as we can see, with great energy and a deeply felt commitment.

**C — The Relationship Between Religious Law and Philosophy**

This was another major problem of the period.

It was the new form taken by the older problem of the relationship between faith and reason.

The conflict between faith and reason, now appearing as a conflict between religion and philosophy, may have been an almost direct reflection of the ideological struggle then becoming increasingly intense within society.

For this reason, taking a clear position on the relationship between religion and philosophy was closely connected to taking a clear position within that wider ideological struggle.

Al-Kindī experienced the problem from both sides.

He was not able to take a completely decisive position either toward religion or toward philosophy, just as he was not able to place himself completely and decisively on one side of the wider ideological conflict.

When we say that he experienced this problem, we mean it literally.

He wrestled seriously with it at the intellectual level, and we believe he must also have experienced it emotionally.

We have seen how strongly and passionately al-Kindī defended philosophy against its ideological enemies.

Behind that defense stood both his philosophical outlook and the philosophical method that had taken shape through his broad engagement with philosophy, the natural sciences, mathematics and medicine.

But we have also seen his emotional attachment to the basic truths of religious faith, as well as his social attachment to theological ideology.

And we have seen that the historical stage in which al-Kindī appeared was itself marked by this same duality.

The forces pushing philosophy toward independence overlapped with the forces still tying it to Muʿtazilite thought, just as the continuing strength of the caliphate’s ideology overlapped with the forces weakening and fracturing it.

Al-Kindī was the concrete historical expression of this duality and overlap.

Taken together, all these conditions left him unable to solve the problem of the relationship between religion and philosophy in a final and decisive way.

Throughout his system of thought, their relationship therefore remained one of duality and overlap.

Even so, the general framework was philosophical. Al-Kindī worked mainly through philosophical concepts and categories as they had reached him through the heritage of Greek philosophy.

**D — Two Kinds of Truth**

This overlapping relationship between religion and philosophy in al-Kindī’s thought produced the first form of a theory of two kinds of truth in Arab-Islamic thought.

At this point, Arab-Islamic thought moved away from commitment to one truth alone—the truth of religion—and began to move between two kinds of truth about existence: religious truth and philosophical truth.

We may even say that this shift marks the real historical dividing line between two stages: the stage of Muʿtazilite *kalām* and the stage of independent philosophical thought.

We might also say that this is the point where two paths within Arab thought begin to separate.

One is the path of official Sunni *kalām*.

The other is the path of philosophy, whose history from this point onward begins under the name “Arab philosophy” or “Islamic philosophy.”

At first the two paths travel beside one another, almost in parallel. Then they gradually move farther apart, and after that they never truly meet again.

In its first form in al-Kindī’s philosophy,[^V4-31] this idea of two kinds of truth assumed that both religious truth and philosophical truth were genuinely true.

The overlapping and divided position in which al-Kindī found himself—a position with which he struggled seriously at the intellectual level—therefore required him to treat these two truths as harmonious or even identical, rather than as opposed or contradictory.

This was the logical result of a divided and overlapping position that had a real historical basis.

But it later led historians and scholars of Arab philosophy into a famous mistake.

They described Arab philosophy as a philosophy of “reconciliation,” and from there they gave the whole history of Arab philosophy the character of an attempt to “reconcile religion and philosophy.”

But from the beginning, this was not a deliberate project of reconciliation.

At its core, it was the result of a completely objective historical situation.

And its direct effect was limited to al-Kindī’s philosophy. It did not define the wider philosophical path followed by Arab-Islamic philosophers after him, as we will explain later.

If we stamp Arab philosophy as a whole with this fixed and absolute label, we classify it from the beginning as a completely idealist philosophy in which there is no room for any materialist position or materialist tendency.

Yet materialist positions and tendencies run through the tradition as a whole in many different ways. They also appear at many different points within the system of each individual philosopher.

We will try to identify these positions and tendencies wherever they can be found.

#### 6 — The Internal Relations of al-Kindī’s Philosophy

We now enter a difficult part of the discussion.

We still need to make a rough “survey” of three different areas: the territory that belongs specifically to al-Kindī’s philosophy; the territory it shares with Muʿtazilite *kalām*; and the territory it shares with the “philosophy of the ancients,” especially Greek philosophy.

As we have said, this is extremely difficult.

The divided and overlapping position we have discussed so often placed al-Kindī’s philosophy on a complicated map where the boundaries are blurred and heavily intertwined.

Why, then, do we need to enter such difficult territory?

There is one reason.

It matters less for al-Kindī alone than it does for Arab philosophy as a whole.

We are trying to understand how Arab philosophy entered its new path with al-Kindī, through the inner relationships that shaped it and through the internal forces that governed its movement and development.

In other words, we are trying to understand this movement in its own historical development, while of course connecting it to the wider history of philosophical thought up to al-Kindī’s time.

We cannot do this without attempting such a survey.

We should begin by pointing out that fully reconstructing al-Kindī’s thought as one complete system is not simply difficult. At present, it is impossible.

His ideas, opinions and theories on the different philosophical questions of his time are scattered across a very large number of sources.

Some of these sources—in fact, most of them—are inaccessible because the original works have been lost and, as far as anyone knows, have still not been found.

Other works survive only in Latin translation, while the Arabic originals have disappeared.

This means that we have no original text against which to check these translations and no way to know with confidence whether they reproduce the author’s meaning as precisely as he expressed it in his own language and style.

The secondary sources available to us, especially the work of Orientalist historians and researchers, are often confused and sometimes contradictory.

This situation forces us to rely on two main sources if we want to reconstruct al-Kindī’s thought, even approximately and incompletely.

The first consists of the works by al-Kindī that have been discovered and published in scholarly editions. These amount to about forty-one treatises.[^V4-32]

The second consists of the titles of his books as preserved in historical catalogues and works that serve as major sources for the intellectual life of the medieval Arab world.[^V4-33]

It is true that we do not know the detailed contents of these lost works. We do not know exactly how al-Kindī treated the subjects and problems suggested by the titles that have come down to us.

Even so, looking at the full catalogues of his writings, and at the way their titles were phrased, allows us to draw some reasonable conclusions. These can add a few clearer lines to the picture of al-Kindī’s philosophy provided by the works that do survive.

What can we conclude, for example, when we find books or treatises with titles such as these?

- *Book on the Fact That All the Creator’s Acts Are Just*.[^V4-34]
- *Treatise Explaining His Descent*.[^V4-35]
- *Treatise on Divine Unity, with Its Explanations*.[^V4-36]
- *Treatise on the Disagreement of the Religious Communities over Divine Unity, Though They All Agree on Divine Unity and Each Differs from the Others*.[^V4-37]
- *Book on Divine Unity, Known as Understanding Gold*.[^V4-38]
- *Treatise Refuting Those Who Claim That There Is an Indivisible Part*.[^V4-39]
- *Book Proving Prophecy*.[^V4-40]
- *Treatise on Declaring Others Unbelievers and Misguided*.[^V4-41]
- *A Question on Whether What a Human Being Needs Is Permitted by Reason Until It Is Forbidden*.[^V4-42]

All these works clearly deal with specifically theological subjects.

This is especially true of justice and divine unity, the two most important of the five main principles of the Muʿtazila.

Can a researcher trying to understand al-Kindī’s thought as a whole simply ignore this strongly theological side of his work, especially when the titles themselves often state the author’s views and positions quite clearly?

The most obvious conclusion, if we approach this aspect of al-Kindī’s interests historically, is that although he did succeed in freeing philosophy from the control of *kalām*, the philosophy that emerged still carried a heavy residue of theological concepts.

This remained true despite the powerful influence that philosophy, the natural sciences, mathematics, astronomy and medicine had on the general direction of his thought.

We do not know the chronological order in which his theological works were written in relation to his works in other fields.

But even if we did know, it would not change our view.

The key question is not whether these theological works belong to one period or another in al-Kindī’s intellectual life.

The deeper point is that the theological concepts of Muʿtazilite thought continued, indirectly, to shape many of his philosophical positions throughout his intellectual career.

Whenever al-Kindī’s philosophical thinking reaches the “forbidden zone” governed by theological assumptions, he stops there. He is unable to cross beyond it and free himself from their influence.

This produces an interesting contradiction with a genuinely dialectical character.

The continuing influence of these theological concepts had a negative effect because it created several gaps in al-Kindī’s philosophical system.

We have already seen examples of this in his divided position on the problem of knowledge and in his inability to reach a decisive philosophical solution to the relationship between faith and reason, or between religion and philosophy.

But the same influence also had a positive effect.

Because al-Kindī continued to hold some theological assumptions, he was forced to take philosophical positions that in some cases differed from Aristotle, Plato and Neoplatonism.[^V4-43]

And when we add to this his departure from Muʿtazilite *kalām* as a method of thought and as a theological way of thinking, the overall result is clear.

Al-Kindī carved out a broadly independent path for himself—and, with him, for Arab philosophy.

Al-Kindī drew on Aristotle, Plato and Socrates as their teachings reached him through the Neoplatonic tradition and through Alexander of Aphrodisias’s commentaries on Aristotle.

He drew on the many philosophies, schools of thought and cultural traditions that had already become an organic part of Arab culture in his time.

And he drew on the whole growing tradition of Muʿtazilite thought, which had been his basic starting point.

That Muʿtazilite tradition was itself the fresh and living product of an enormous historical process: the active social and cultural interaction between the intellectual traditions of many different peoples and nations.

Al-Kindī benefited from all these sources.

Indeed, his philosophy was without doubt the first major distillation of all of them.

But that distillation cannot be assigned to any one of those inherited traditions on its own.

Taken as a whole, it belongs directly to al-Kindī himself.

And in its real and ideological dimensions, it belongs to the historical conditions of al-Kindī’s society and age and to the social relationships that shaped them.

In this sense, we can rightly say that the complete system of al-Kindī’s ideas and philosophical positions—including his theological ideas and positions—belongs to al-Kindī himself.

It has an independent identity, and that identity is al-Kindī’s own.

It is the identity of both the person and the historical phenomenon.

In other words, it is the face of Arab philosophy as it first appeared in its new stage during the third Islamic century, carrying the name of Abū Yūsuf Yaʿqūb al-Kindī, the “first Philosopher of the Arabs.”

#### 7 — The World: Its Origin and Its Fate

The question of the world—where it came from and what its ultimate fate is—was the first great question of philosophy in both the ancient and medieval periods, when philosophy’s main task was understood as explaining the “world.”

This is why Aristotle called the part of his philosophy devoted to this question “First Philosophy.”[^V4-44] The name continued to be used in philosophical writing and in histories of philosophy after him.

The “Philosopher of the Arabs,” al-Kindī, followed this tradition.

His treatise devoted to the problem of the world was titled *Al-Kindī’s Treatise to al-Muʿtaṣim on First Philosophy*.[^V4-45]

Other surviving works help complete our picture of how he understood the world.[^V4-45]

If we want to find the first key to al-Kindī’s philosophy, this is where we must begin.

His position on this question shaped his position toward many other philosophical problems of his time, especially those that had become urgent within Arab-Islamic society.

The starting point is the great question that occupied ancient and medieval philosophy:

Is our material world eternal, or did it come into being?

Is it created or uncreated?

And if it was created, was it created from something that already existed, or from nothing?

##### 1 — Comparison with Aristotle

Here al-Kindī’s position differs sharply from Aristotle’s.

For al-Kindī, the question stands at the center of his philosophy.

He begins from it, circles back to it and returns to it again and again, almost as though he wants to make certain that he has provided convincing philosophical arguments that the world came into being from nothing.

It is true that al-Kindī uses many Aristotelian concepts and terms.

But he uses them on a path different from Aristotle’s and reaches a conclusion opposed to Aristotle’s own.

At the same time, al-Kindī develops terms of his own, with meanings and implications that clearly mark out his independent position.

For example, when al-Kindī uses Aristotle’s expression “First Mover,” he gives it a different meaning.

In form, he follows Aristotle’s terminology.

In substance, however, what he means is the “First Creator.”

The difference between these two meanings is fundamental.

Aristotle’s First Mover does not move the world as its active cause. It moves it as its final cause.

In other words, the world longs for the perfection of God—the First Mover—who is “the happiest and most exalted source of joy.”[^V4-46]

Through this constant longing, the world is always moving toward God, seeking a happiness and joy like God’s own.

This is what it means, in Aristotle’s system, to call God the “First Mover.”

For al-Kindī, by contrast, the First Mover is the first active cause: “the first cause that creates every cause”[^V4-47] and “the true, first and perfect agent.”[^V4-47]

The basic difference between the two views is this:

Aristotle’s position assumes, first, that the world is eternal and, second, that it was not created out of absolute nothingness.

Al-Kindī’s position rests on the opposite of both claims.

For him, the world came into being, and it was created out of absolute nothingness.[^V4-48]

Al-Kindī states this position directly.

Aristotle’s position, by contrast, has to be drawn from several basic principles in his philosophy.

1. **God is not the active cause of the world.**

   In Aristotle’s system, God—the First Mover—is not the active cause of motion in the world. God is only its final cause.

   This means that God is not the direct source of the world’s movement or of the movement of existence itself.

   Aristotle bases this on the idea that if God physically moved the world as an active cause, there would have to be contact between God and the world.

   But such contact is impossible.

   The world is material and bodily, while God is immaterial and without body.[^V4-49]

   How, then, could there be physical contact between them?

2. **Prime matter is eternally present as possibility.**

   Aristotle’s prime matter—the matter capable of taking on actual existence through whatever form joins with it—is always present potentially.

   This general and undetermined matter is therefore not absolute nothingness.

   Nor is it complete actual existence.

   It has a kind of eternal existence as possibility, or potential existence.

   Aristotle bases this on the principle that absolute nothingness contains no possibility of becoming actual existence.

   Unless something already exists potentially—that is, as a possibility—it cannot become actual.

   Nothingness can never produce existence.

   Aristotle therefore says that prime matter is not simply nothingness or the absence of qualities and categories.[^V4-50]

   Matter, on this view, exists eternally and is not preceded by absolute nothingness.

3. **Motion is eternal.**

   Aristotle’s theory of matter and form leads him to the principle that motion must also be eternal.

   In his view, matter and form cannot exist without motion.

   Since matter and form did not themselves come into being through generation,[^V4-51] but exist eternally, motion must also exist eternally.[^V4-52]

   The “First Mover” in Aristotle’s philosophy is therefore not the “creator” or “originator” of motion.

   It is called the First Mover only because it is the final perfection toward which matter moves as it takes on form—or, in al-Kindī’s terminology, because it is the final cause.[^V4-53]

---

The basic difference between Aristotle and al-Kindī here begins with the different starting points from which each defines God.

Aristotle begins from a rational view and treats the question rationally.

The God we find in Aristotle’s philosophy is a theoretical intellect, not God in the ordinary religious sense.

God is pure intellect.[^V4-54]

God can be understood only through reason and is pure form, completely without matter.

But Aristotle’s main starting point is not actually the question of God itself.

He was trying to define God as part of a wider attempt to solve the problem of the relationship between the universal and the particular, between universal matter and particular form, and between what can be grasped by the intellect and what can be grasped by the senses.

When he was unable to discover the real source of this relationship, the idea of God appeared within his philosophy, and Aristotle believed he had found the solution he needed.

Even so, it is fair to say that Aristotle made an important positive step here.

He moved from the particular toward the universal—that is, from the material world toward the abstract.

Plato had moved in the opposite direction, from the abstract toward the world of sensory reality.

But this positive step did not lead Aristotle to a positive final conclusion.

That, in brief, is Aristotle’s starting point in the question of God.

When we return to al-Kindī, we find a completely different position.

Al-Kindī begins his understanding of God directly from purely religious assumptions.

These include the belief that the world arose from “nothing,” that existence arose from non-existence, and that God brought everything into being from “nothing.”

His position on this question is therefore fundamentally religious, and so is his starting point.

The philosophical element lies mainly in the forms of reasoning he uses to try to prove this religious assumption through philosophy.

We can understand the real historical basis of al-Kindī’s position.

We have already discussed part of it.

When al-Kindī faced the problem of God in this way, he and Arab philosophy in this first stage of its independence were also facing a number of urgent and sometimes contradictory historical problems.

Philosophy had to respond to the growing cracks within the ideology of the theocratic order—the ideology of the caliphal state.

At the same time, it had to respond to an increasingly urgent need to develop the ideology of the social classes and groups opposed to that state.

These opposition forces were growing in both breadth and depth, socially, politically and intellectually.

Behind both of these conflicting problems stood another objective issue, indirectly connected to them through the same historical conditions.

Arab philosophical thought, now entering a new stage, needed a new form through which it could develop under the conditions of its own living growth.

It needed a form suited to the Arab-Islamic setting that had emerged from the broad cultural revival of the third Islamic century.

Could al-Kindī, as the representative of Arab philosophy in the first stage of its independence, solve all these problems in his own time?

It would be unrealistic to expect al-Kindī himself, given all the historical conditions surrounding him, to resolve the first two opposing contradictions in a way that fully favored both sides at once, or that favored one side completely over the other.

This was impossible, first, because al-Kindī’s social and intellectual position left him wavering between the two competing ideologies, as we have seen. This is why we find him occupying two very different positions between the reign of al-Maʾmūn and that of al-Mutawakkil and his successors. During the first period, when Muʿtazilite thought was dominant, al-Kindī was a thinker who enjoyed high standing with the state. During the second, when conservative Sunni theology became dominant, he became a thinker rejected and persecuted by that same state.

Second, when al-Kindī emerged as the first expression of Arab philosophy in its newly independent form, he still carried with him—as we said earlier—the legacy of philosophical theology. His thought therefore suffered from all the problems created by the overlap between two stages: the stage when philosophy was still subordinate to theology, and the stage when it was beginning to become independent of it.

This was why al-Kindī could not solve the ideological problem of his time.

But when it came to giving Arab philosophical thought a form suited to the level it had reached in his age, it is fair to say that al-Kindī put the problem on the road toward a solution. It is enough that he stripped Arab philosophy of its theological form and gave it the distinctive language, style, and terminology of philosophy. He brought it into the world of philosophical concepts and categories as these were known and used in the human intellectual traditions that had reached the Arabs, especially the Greek and Alexandrian traditions.[^V4-55]

In this way, al-Kindī laid the foundation for philosophy to become an independent field of knowledge. He also prepared the way for later philosophers to solve many of the problems that Arab philosophy would take up as it developed from al-Kindī to Ibn Rushd.

##### B — Comparison with Plato

Al-Kindī also differs from Plato, just as he differed from Aristotle, in the way he understands the relationship between God and the world.

He recognizes no eternal true existence other than the existence of the one God, who created everything from nothing. There is therefore only one being that is eternal, unchanging, without partner or equal, and free from all the features of generation and decay—from movement, change, and becoming.

But is this view compatible with Plato’s theory of the Forms?

According to Plato’s theory, every kind or class of thing, and everything in the world we experience through the senses, has a counterpart corresponding to it in the world of Forms, or “Ideas,”[^V4-56] or *eidos*.[^V4-57] The relationship is like that between a shadow and the thing that casts it.

This world of Ideas or Forms is the world of true existence, because the Forms alone are truly real. They are therefore eternal. They do not come into being or pass away. They are free from all bodily properties such as movement, change, becoming, and destruction, and they are also free from the limits of space and time.

Nor can they be known through the senses. They can only be grasped by the mind, and knowledge of their world—the world of true existence—also requires intuition (*affect*).

Our human world, by contrast, stands between the world of Ideas or Forms and the world of decay, which is the world of matter.

Plato therefore gives us three worlds:

1. **True existence** — the world of Ideas or Forms.
2. **The human world.**
3. **The world of decay** — the world of pure matter.

Our world differs from the world of decay because the latter is completely opposed to the world of Ideas. The human world, however, contains something of true existence, because human beings have a soul or spiritual element connected with the world of Ideas.

But human beings also contain elements belonging to the world of decay: material elements that change, pass away, and exist only relatively. Through these material elements, our human world also belongs to the world of decay.

The result is that the world we live in is close to the world of Ideas in one respect and close to the world of matter in another. It is therefore caught in a continuous process. It is always becoming, always moving toward stability, without ever actually being fully stable.[^V4-58]

From this Platonic account of “true existence,” it is clear that the difference between al-Kindī and Plato is no less fundamental than the difference between al-Kindī and Aristotle.

For al-Kindī, eternal and unchanging true existence—free from movement, change, becoming, and the limits of space and time—belongs to one being alone: God.[^V4-59]

For Plato, however, true existence is multiple. There are as many Forms as there are kinds of things, and individual things within those kinds, in the world of the senses. Every kind of thing, and every thing in this sensible world, has its own Form or Idea in the world of true existence.

That is the first difference.

The second is that, in Plato’s view, nothing in the sensible world is created from nothing. Everything in this world is simply a reflection of eternal Ideas that exist objectively and eternally in the world of true realities.

The third difference concerns the cause of things in the sensible world. For al-Kindī there is one single cause: God. For Plato, the causes are as numerous as the things themselves, or as the kinds to which they belong, because each Idea is the cause of the thing that appears as its shadow or visible expression in the sensible world.

There is also a fourth difference between al-Kindī and Plato in their understanding of God and the world.

For al-Kindī, God creates the world. God therefore exists apart from the world. We can say that the world is outside God, or, equally, that God is outside the world.

For Plato, however, the Idea is the essence of the world, while the world itself is the outward appearance of that essence—to use the language of modern philosophy.

The sensible world is therefore not “outside God,” meaning the Idea, nor is God separate from the sensible world. Their relationship is that of essence and appearance.

This means that the world is material in its outward form, but ideal in its essence.

If we were to ask Plato, “What is God?”, his answer would be that God is the spirit present within matter, within the material world itself. God is not separate from that world but forms its spiritual foundation.

From all this we can finally conclude that Plato sees the universe itself as divine and rejects any complete division between God and nature, or between God and the material world. Al-Kindī, by contrast, maintains a clear separation between God and the world.

So on several sides of this question we find a direct opposition between the first philosopher of the Arabs, al-Kindī, and the first great philosopher of idealism, Plato.

##### C — Comparison with Neoplatonism

When we compare al-Kindī’s view of God and the world with that of Neoplatonism (*Néoplatonisme*),[^V4-60] we again find major differences.

The basic idea of Platonism is that essence is “Idea,” and that the sensible world is the product of the Idea.

Neoplatonism went beyond Plato by trying to solve a problem that Plato himself had left unresolved: how did the sensible world arise from the Idea? In other words, how did the Idea become the world we know?

Plato had left the world of Ideas in a state of complete and permanent stillness.

To address this problem, Neoplatonism applied dialectic to processes taking place in the objective world itself. Plato’s dialectic had largely been a method for comparing opposing opinions during dialogue and debate. In that sense, it remained a subjective dialectic.

Plotinus,[^V4-61] by contrast, tried to explain the world through its movement and change rather than through its stability, as Plato had done.

From the Neoplatonic understanding of dialectic, the sensible world comes into existence through a process, and at the foundation of this process stands the “Idea.”

This Idea is God.

The process itself is the **emanation** of existence from the Idea, like sunlight streaming from the sun. In Neoplatonism, this emanation is the process of creation.

The theory describes it as follows:

- When the world first emanated[^V4-62] from the “First” or the “One”—that is, from the Idea—it emerged in a series of levels. Beings therefore differ according to how near or far they are from the source of this emanation.

  Because the sensible world is the final level of emanation, it is the furthest from God, the source of that emanation. It is therefore the lowest and least worthy level of existence.

  In the Neoplatonic view, then, the material world is seen negatively, as something low and inferior.

  Yet despite this, Neoplatonism gives the world a moving and changing character instead of Plato’s original picture of a world fixed in complete stillness.

  It was natural for the image of the world to change in this way between Plato and Plotinus. More than seven centuries separated them, and those centuries had to transform the picture from one of stillness into one of movement, as we have seen. Historical developments during those centuries changed the forms of social relations, and with them changed the image of the world in human consciousness.

- In Neoplatonism, emanation comes from God by nature and necessity, not through will or choice.

  According to the theory, introducing will would compromise the absolute unity of God.

  “By nature” here means that God’s perfection in himself necessarily causes light—the existence of beings—to flow from him according to the hierarchy of levels already described.

- The material world comes into existence through emanation from the First Principle by way of separate celestial intellects. By the time the emanation reaches the material world, very little of the divine radiance remains.

  Neoplatonism therefore describes the material world as darkness compared with the celestial spheres, which emanate directly from God or through levels much closer to him.

  At first, this may seem to imply a kind of separation between God and the world, since darkness is the opposite of light.

  But Neoplatonism understands this darkness dialectically. The darkness associated with the material world is still part of the light flowing from the highest principle. It is simply the lowest level of that supreme light.

  So for the Neoplatonists there is no complete separation between God and the world, just as there was none for Plato.

  The main difference between the two systems is that the connection between God and the world appears almost complete in original Platonism, while in Neoplatonism it appears more partial.

  Taken together with the Neoplatonic claim that God’s essence is “Idea,” this leads to a form of divine indwelling: the view that everything has a divine foundation.

  This is why the medieval Church opposed the Platonic interpretation of creation and rejected the idea of a unity between God, the world, and nature.

  But the idea of divine indwelling that follows from Neoplatonism does not amount to a materialist position. It remains an idealist idea, because it still treats God as the source of material existence.

- In Neoplatonic theory, emanation does not take place in time. It is eternal.

- There is another important point. One of the best-known ideas of the Neoplatonic school is Plotinus’s interpretation of the relation between **potentiality** and **actuality** in a way inspired by Aristotle.

  According to this view, everything material exists first as a potentiality, while “thought” creates the actual world in which that potentiality is realized.

  This is not, in fact, a faithful expression of Aristotle’s own theory. But it does imply, as Aristotle himself held, that matter is eternal.

  If matter exists as potentiality, then it already has a kind of existence before its actual realization. Its existence as potentiality is therefore eternal.

- In its details, the theory of emanation undermines the unity of the “First,” even though Neoplatonism remains committed to that unity through its principle of the First as “absolute unity,” the source of all emanation.

  If matter—*hylē*, or prime matter—is pure potentiality, without any fixed form of its own but capable of receiving any form that brings it into actual existence, then two eternal realities must already exist: the “First,” which is the source of emanation, and prime matter.

  The fact that the First exists actually while prime matter exists only potentially does not remove this duality. A difference in the kind of existence does not mean that one of the two forms of existence is unreal.[^V4-63]

---

After this outline of the general principles of Neoplatonism, we can see how many-sided the difference is between its view and the principles on which al-Kindī built his own understanding of God and the world.

The main differences can be summarized as follows:

1. One of the basic principles of al-Kindī’s philosophy is that the world was created from absolute nothingness.

   Plotinus’s theory of emanation rejects this principle.

   In that theory, the world is a ray of light from the “First,” meaning God, because “everything that comes after the First necessarily comes from the First.”[^V4-64]

   The world therefore came from something that already existed, not from nothing.

2. In al-Kindī’s view, the world had a beginning and is not eternal.

   The theory of emanation, by contrast, requires the world to be eternal.

   There are three reasons for this.

   First, the world is an emanation from eternal light and necessarily accompanies that light. It must therefore be eternal as well.

   Second, according to the theory, emanation does not take place in time. It is a process necessarily bound to eternal light. What emanates from that light must therefore also be eternal.

   Third, in Plotinus’s theory, matter is potentiality. It therefore exists eternally in potential form.

   The material world is the actual realization of this potentiality—prime matter. The world therefore emerges from something that already exists eternally, while the “Intellect” that emanates directly from the First creates the actual world in which this potentiality is realized.[^V4-65]

3. Neoplatonism sees nature as having a divine foundation.

   It therefore rejects a complete duality between God and nature, since in its view everything has a divine basis.[^V4-66]

   This contains a form of divine indwelling, or one possible meaning of the “unity of existence,” understood in an idealist sense.

   God is therefore not outside the world and separate from it, as al-Kindī believes.

   The Islamic view on which al-Kindī’s position rests rejects every form of divine indwelling and every version of the “unity of existence.” It insists on a complete separation between God and the world.

4. In the theory of emanation, the material world is a low and inferior world because it occupies the lowest level of the whole process.

   Plotinus therefore felt ashamed of his body and of the fact that his soul had entered it.

   This attitude is foreign to al-Kindī’s outlook, which here reflects the Islamic outlook more generally.

   Islam did not look down on the body—that is, on matter—in this way. Soul and body were instead treated as two independent and equally real essences.

   Al-Kindī, as a philosopher, also had a distinctive philosophical view of nature.

   In definitions found in several of his works, he describes nature as “the cause of movement and of rest from movement.”[^V4-67]

   In other words, al-Kindī gives nature a real place among the causes at work in the world of beings. He even places its relationship to God on the same level as the relationship of other created beings to God, without giving another created being priority over it.

   This appears especially clearly when he says:

   “Nature is the thing that God made a cause and a causal agent for all things that move and that come to rest from movement.”[^V4-68]

5. If our earlier conclusion is correct—that Plotinus’s theory of emanation contradicts itself on the unity of God because it contains an eternal duality—then this gives us another difference between al-Kindī and Neoplatonism.

   For al-Kindī, the unity of God lies at the very foundation of his Islamic thought.[^V4-69]

   Two modern researchers[^V4-70] have also noticed a contradiction in the Neoplatonic theory of divine unity, though they approach it from two other directions.

6. The final major difference comes from the Neoplatonic claim that existence proceeds from God by necessity, rather than through will and choice.

   Al-Kindī rejects the idea that God could create without willing to do so, as though creation were forced upon him by absolute necessity.

   The idea that emanation—or creation—takes place necessarily is completely incompatible with the attributes of knowledge, wisdom, and will. In the Islamic understanding, which is also al-Kindī’s understanding, these are among the chief attributes of the First Creator.[^V4-71]

---

Why have we spent so much time on these three comparisons in this particular question—the question of the world, its origin, and its ultimate fate?

We have done so to support and confirm an argument made earlier.[^V4-72]

Because al-Kindī wanted to preserve certain religious assumptions and theological principles, he was forced to take positions on some philosophical questions—such as God and the world—that differed, as we have seen, from those of Plato, Aristotle, and Plotinus.

From one point of view, this had a positive result. It helped al-Kindī, and Arab philosophy itself, open an independent path at the very beginning of philosophy’s separation from theological thought.

But from another point of view, the result was negative.

At al-Kindī’s stage, it placed the Arab philosophical understanding of the world at a different point in the history of human philosophical thought from the positions reached by Plato, Aristotle, and Plotinus. This was despite the fact that al-Kindī lived in a society and an age more developed than theirs in both social relations and the level of human knowledge about the universe and nature.

When comparing al-Kindī’s idea of God with Aristotle’s idea of the “First Mover,” we tried to explain this complicated situation historically.

---

#### 8 — “God” and the “World” in al-Kindī’s Philosophical Formulation

…So the core of al-Kindī’s understanding of “God,” and of the relationship between “God” and the “world,” began from a purely religious foundation.

This is clear and leaves no room for another interpretation.

But that does not mean al-Kindī remained trapped within this religious framework when he gave the idea its philosophical form. In fact, it is precisely here that his achievement stands out most clearly.

He moved beyond what we might call the ready-made “scriptural” formulations of the idea—formulations whose authority came directly from divine revelation. In his own way, he produced the first Arab philosophical formulation of an Islamic idea of God and God’s relationship with the world.

This formulation had the language and style of philosophy. It used philosophical concepts and categories, and it organized them through logical arguments.

This first move beyond ready-made scriptural formulations may itself have been an important historical achievement for Arab philosophy in the first stage of its independence.

Al-Kindī’s philosophical formulation uses a set of propositions, definitions, and descriptions to build a complete structure of proof for his central claim: that God created the world from nothing. It also establishes the supporting premises needed for that proof.

These propositions can be divided into the following groups.

**A — Propositions used as mathematical premises**

Al-Kindī uses these to build the foundation of his philosophical argument that the material world had a beginning, that matter is not eternal, and that creation therefore took place from nothing.

The most important are:

- Homogeneous magnitudes—that is, quantities—none of which is greater than another are equal.
- If a magnitude of the same kind is added to one of two equal homogeneous magnitudes, the two become unequal.
- Two infinite homogeneous magnitudes cannot exist if one is smaller than the other.
- If each of a number of homogeneous magnitudes is finite, their total is also finite.[^V4-73]

**B — The finite and the infinite**

Al-Kindī’s proof that the world was created rests on these two concepts, and his use of them depends on the mathematical premises just listed.

Here we see him working hard to prove that every material body is finite and that God alone is infinite. From this, he hopes to prove that the material world itself is finite, meaning that it had a beginning and is not eternal, while God is eternal and therefore created the world from nothing.[^V4-74]

If matter is finite, then movement and time must also be finite.[^V4-75]

This follows from the fact that matter, movement, and time cannot exist independently of one another.

Al-Kindī argues for this connection in the following terms:

> “There is no body[^V4-76] without time, because time is the number of movement—that is, a duration measured by movement. If there is movement, there is time. If there is no movement, there is no time. Movement is the movement of a body. If there is a body, there is movement; if not, there is none.”[^V4-77]

Because matter, movement, and time are bound together in this way, movement and time, like matter, must have had a beginning and cannot be eternal.[^V4-78]

**C — Propositions concerning the order of causes**

Al-Kindī imagines the system of causes as a hierarchy descending from the “First Cause”—God—to the other celestial and natural causes below it.

Within this system, each cause is acted on by the cause before it and, in turn, acts on the cause that follows it.

Al-Kindī also uses this group of propositions to prove that the “First Cause,” alone at the top of the hierarchy, is eternal and has no cause of its own. It is “creative, active, perfecting all things, and unmoved.”[^V4-79]

---

Al-Kindī’s philosophical proof itself—the general structure he uses to establish the central claim of his philosophy, that God created the world from “nothing”—follows logically from these earlier propositions and premises.

He devoted considerable effort to formulating these premises, proving them, and repeatedly insisting on them.

The proof begins from the inseparable connection between material body and movement.

Al-Kindī considers the possibility that “the body of the whole”[^V4-80]—the body of the universe—might originally have been at rest, while still capable of moving, and then later begun to move.

He describes this possibility as “necessarily false,” meaning false by rational necessity.

His reason is that there can be no body without movement and no movement without a body.

Al-Kindī insists on this connection because he does not want to allow the world any existence before movement—that is, before the act of creation.

From here, he tries to prove the falsity of the alternative view through formal logic. He considers the different possibilities and rejects them one by one until only the possibility supporting his own conclusion remains.

He finally reaches the conclusion that the “body of the whole” did not exist before movement or before time.

According to the premises already established, there is no body without movement and time, and no movement or time without a body.

If time, movement, and body do not precede one another in existence, then the existence of the body is inseparable from its movement and its time.

And if movement has an end, and time has an end—that is, if both had a beginning and are not eternal—then the “body of the whole” must also have come into existence without having existed before.

It therefore came from nothing.

In al-Kindī’s own expression, it was a case of “bringing being out of non-being.”[^V4-81]

Al-Kindī offers another proof that material body, matter, and the material world all had a beginning:

> After establishing that movement is the movement of a body, and that movement is change, he writes: “Composition and dissolution are forms of change, because composition means bringing things together and arranging them. A body is a substance: long, broad, and deep—that is, it has three dimensions. It is therefore composed of substance, which is its genus, and of length, breadth, and depth, which form its differentia. It is also composed of matter and form.
>
> “Composition is a change from the state of being uncomposed, and composition is movement. If there were no movement, there would be no composition. A body is composed, so if there were no movement there would be no body.
>
> “Body and movement therefore do not precede one another.
>
> “And with movement there is time, because movement is change, and change is the measure of the duration of that which changes. Movement is the duration of the changing thing.[^V4-82] Time is a duration measured by movement.
>
> “Every body has a duration, as we said earlier… If time is actually finite, then the existence[^V4-83] of the body must also, necessarily and without exception, be actually finite…”[^V4-84]
