Clearly, to say that something is finite here means that it had a beginning and is not eternal. It therefore came into existence because of something outside itself.

It is, in other words, created.

Matter, then, is created.

---

Al-Kindī thus gives his understanding of God, the world, and their relationship a complete philosophical form.

In its **form**, this formulation moves fully beyond the theological way of presenting the same idea, even though its underlying **content** remains theological.

Al-Kindī takes his materials and tools entirely from philosophy itself. He uses philosophical categories, and he adopts the methods and logic of philosophy to build and organize his proof.

The content of the problem remains theological, but the form in which he develops it has become philosophical.

#### 9 — The Question of Will

By this we mean the question of God’s will and its relationship to human will and human actions.

This problem appeared in Arab-Islamic thought with the rise of the Qadarites and developed further when this movement became part of Muʿtazilite theology. It became known as the problem of compulsion and choice, or divine decree and predestination.

##### What was al-Kindī’s position on this question?

Any study of this first stage in the independence of Arab philosophy from theology remains incomplete unless we try to answer this question.

But we immediately run into a problem.

None of al-Kindī’s philosophical works that have recently been discovered and published—and that now form the main original sources available to modern researchers—contains a direct discussion of this issue.

What does that mean?

Does it mean the problem disappeared once philosophy stopped being subordinate to theology and Arab philosophy entered the period of its independence from Islamic theological thought?

The historical evidence says no.

The same intellectual and ideological debate continued in the philosophies of al-Fārābī, Ibn Sīnā, al-Ghazālī, Ibn Rushd, and others after al-Kindī.

Nor did the nature of ideological conflict in the medieval period make it possible to remove the relationship between God’s will and human action from philosophical debate during the early Arab period.

How, then, could the issue have been absent from Arab philosophy at its very beginning, in al-Kindī’s own time?

There is another reason to doubt this.

Al-Kindī himself grew up, formed his ideas, and became intellectually prominent during the height of Muʿtazilite thought. He also took an active part in the Muʿtazilite intellectual struggle, in which the problem of human freedom was one of the leading issues of the time.

If al-Kindī was one of the most important representatives of the thought of his age—and he was—it is difficult to imagine that his philosophical system simply ignored one of the central questions of that age.

We therefore have to ask: why do the philosophical works now available to us contain no separate work on this problem, or even a direct discussion of it in one of the surviving works?

There are two possibilities.

Either al-Kindī wrote a philosophical work specifically on this question that has not yet been discovered, or he dealt with it in one of his theological works listed by Ibn al-Nadīm in the *Fihrist* and by later historians.

In my view, the second possibility is more likely.

The catalogues list two theological works by al-Kindī whose titles make me almost certain that he discussed the relationship between God’s will and human actions in them from a Muʿtazilite position.

Their titles appear in three of these catalogues in the following forms:

1. **A Book Showing That All the Acts of the Creator—Glory Be to His Name—Are Just and Contain No Injustice**[^V4-85]
2. **An Epistle on Human Capacity and the Time at Which It Exists**[^V4-86]

Both the form and the content of these titles closely match the language used by the Muʿtazilites in discussions of divine justice and the main questions connected with it.

Justice, as we know, is one of the five basic principles of Muʿtazilite doctrine.

And the Muʿtazilite understanding of justice is itself based on the belief that human beings freely choose their actions.

We can therefore reasonably conclude that al-Kindī’s view of the relationship between God’s will and human action agreed with the Muʿtazilite doctrine of choice rather than compulsion, even though these two works have not yet been found.

The first possibility—that al-Kindī wrote a philosophical epistle specifically on the subject that has not yet been discovered—is less likely.

None of the four surviving historical catalogues of al-Kindī’s works mentions such an epistle.[^V4-87] The earliest of these sources, Ibn al-Nadīm’s *Fihrist*, was compiled not very long after al-Kindī’s death.[^V4-88]

Still, we cannot completely rule out the possibility.

Al-Kindī may have written a philosophical epistle on human freedom from a Muʿtazilite point of view, only for opponents of Muʿtazilite thought to suppress it during the period between al-Kindī and Ibn al-Nadīm, just as they suppressed most of the original sources of Muʿtazilite thought.

This possibility is supported by what we know of al-Kindī’s intellectual ties to the Muʿtazilites, his agreement with them on questions of divine justice and unity,[^V4-89] and the fact that the persecution suffered by the Muʿtazilites under al-Mutawakkil also fell on al-Kindī himself.

One form of that persecution was the confiscation of al-Kindī’s library by the Banū Mūsā.[^V4-90]

Yet despite this uncertainty, we do not actually need these lost works in order to know al-Kindī’s real position on the relationship between God’s will and human actions.

Nor does our conclusion depend entirely on the arguments just given.

We have other sources for his position in the philosophical works that survive today.

They can be identified as follows.

**First:** there are the works in which al-Kindī repeatedly discusses the **near causes** of things and events in relation to the **distant cause**, or First Cause, which is God.[^V4-91]

His belief in near causes—that some things in the world serve as causes of other things—rests on his commitment to the universal rule of cause and effect in the material world.

He states this principle in a short and firm sentence:

> “Nothing in nature is pointless or without a cause.”[^V4-92]

We can apply this theory of near causes to human actions.

Human actions would then arise from their nearest cause: the will of the human being who acts.

At the same time, al-Kindī’s philosophy still recognizes a distant cause: the will of God.

This removes absolute compulsion. God, as the “First Cause,” does not directly and completely control human will and human action.

The result is that al-Kindī recognizes human choice in a form consistent with the Muʿtazilite position.

This is still, of course, a theological idea. It does not remove the influence of the will of the “First Cause” altogether. It only removes its **direct** control over human action.

**Second:** we have an explicit source directly connected with human choice, where al-Kindī states the principle clearly enough that it needs little interpretation.

In the work called **Al-Kindī’s Epistle to Aḥmad ibn al-Muʿtaṣim Explaining the Prostration of the Outermost Celestial Body**, he interprets the word *prostration* as meaning obedience rather than literal physical prostration—that is, what Arabic describes as “placing the forehead on the ground in prayer, with the palms and knees touching the ground.”[^V4-93]

He then explains obedience as carrying out a command and says:

> “Carrying out a command takes place only through choice, and choice belongs to beings with complete souls, meaning rational souls.”[^V4-94]

From the rest of the epistle, we understand that “rational” here means possessing reason.

We also see that the phrase “beings with rational souls” includes human beings, because another passage states this directly:

> “The spiritual substance is made either rational or non-rational. The rational consists of the higher beings and man.”[^V4-95]

Other passages later in the same work confirm that human beings fall under the category of beings that possess choice.

**Third:** we can also turn to al-Kindī’s work on the soul.[^V4-96]

Here he gives the human soul a very high status.

He does so not only because, in his words, “its substance comes from the substance of the Creator, mighty and exalted, as sunlight comes from the sun,” but also because the soul controls the impulses of the human body.

Al-Kindī compares this to a rider controlling a horse when the horse is about to bolt.[^V4-97]

Because he sees the human soul as playing this active role, he expresses amazement at “how a person can neglect his soul and distance it from its Creator…”[^V4-97]

But the soul could not control the desires of the body, and a human being could not move his soul further from or closer to its Creator, unless both soul and human being possessed some freedom of choice in willing and acting.

It would make no sense for a thinker of al-Kindī’s stature to give the soul the role of controlling bodily impulses, and then express surprise that a person might “neglect his soul,” while denying that the person and the soul possess the power to choose.

For all these reasons, we have no doubt that al-Kindī’s position on the relationship between God’s will and human will was one of commitment to human choice and rejection of compulsion.

This conclusion also shows us something important about Arab philosophy.

From the earliest stage of its independence from Muʿtazilite thought, it showed that it would preserve this positive part of the Muʿtazilite legacy. It would also continue to develop it within a philosophical framework that would gradually become freer than the form we find in the work of al-Kindī, the “first philosopher of the Arabs.”

#### 10 — The Soul

Before al-Kindī, Muslim theologians had already discussed the soul.

Their work was an early attempt to develop a new Arab-Islamic theory of the soul. It was shaped, on one side, by the Islamic age itself, and on the other by the philosophical schools and tendencies that had entered the intellectual life of that age.

But these theologians did not all move in the same direction.

Abū al-Hudhayl al-ʿAllāf, for example, one of the leading Muʿtazilites,[^V4-98] took a strongly sense-based position.

He argued that the soul was simply a property of the body, no different in principle from its other physical properties. Human beings, he therefore said, are simply the visible persons we see before us, with two hands and two feet.

When a human being acts, the action belongs to the whole person,[^V4-99] meaning the body together with all its properties. The soul has no separate activity of its own that distinguishes it from the body’s other properties.

His student Ibrāhīm al-Naẓẓām, however—also one of the major Muʿtazilite figures[^V4-100]—took a different position, though one containing an internal contradiction.

On the one hand, he moved toward a sense-based view by saying that the spirit is itself a body.[^V4-101] He described it as “a subtle body intertwined with the body, entering into the heart through all its parts, as water permeates a rose, oil permeates sesame, and butterfat permeates milk.”[^V4-102] Yet from another side, al-Naẓẓām leans toward Platonic spiritualism. He says that a human being consists of body and soul, but that the human being, in the true sense, is the soul.[^V4-103] It is the soul that has power and capacity, life and will. It has this capacity in itself, and the capacity comes before the act.[^V4-104] The body, by contrast, is for al-Naẓẓām nothing more than “the instrument and frame of the soul.”[^V4-105]

The other Islamic theologians often waver between these two tendencies: the sensory view and Platonic spiritualism. The second tendency appears clearly in one of the Baghdad Muʿtazilites, Muʿammar ibn ʿAbbād al-Sulamī (d. 220 AH/835 CE). He says that the soul is the true human being. It is a meaning or substance distinct from the body, and its relationship to the body is one of direction and control.[^V4-106] In other words, the soul is separate from the body and directs what the body does.

Muʿammar also distinguishes between the actions of the soul and those of the body. He says: “The soul is the human being. The act of the human being is the will. Everything else—movements, states of rest, and exertions—is an act of the body.”[^V4-106]

The first tendency, the sensory one, appears in another Muʿtazilite thinker: Abū Bakr ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Kaysān, known as al-Aṣamm, who lived around the middle of the third century AH.[^V4-107] He says that the human being is simply this visible person before us. There is no separate spirit: the spirit is the body itself, and so is the soul.[^V4-108]

These examples represent the main lines of thought among the Muʿtazilite theologians as they tried to lay the first foundations for a theory of the soul in Arab philosophical thought. This was the intellectual setting in which al-Kindī’s philosophy appeared.

If we start from this unsettled picture of the soul among the Muʿtazilite theologians, we can see how far al-Kindī moved the theory forward. He made a noticeable change, taking the question of the soul much closer to a scientific treatment—within the historical limits of what “scientific” could mean in al-Kindī’s time.

The first thing we notice is that al-Kindī greatly widened the scope of the subject. He brought in new questions that earlier theologians had not connected with the study of the soul, including dreams and the intellect.

Second, he made a serious effort to define what he meant by the soul and to study its different powers and states. He also examined the different ways in which the soul appears and acts, its effects on human behavior, and the links between the soul’s activity and the many forms of human activity.

Among the treatises of his that have survived, and the works listed in old catalogues of his writings, we find around eight treatises dealing with the soul.[^V4-109] We can add to these the views he expresses on the same subjects in other works, such as his *Treatise to al-Muʿtaṣim on First Philosophy*[^V4-110] and his *Treatise on the Definitions and Descriptions of Things*.[^V4-111]

But none of this, by itself, proves that al-Kindī made a genuinely qualitative change in the theory of the soul after the Muʿtazilite theologians—a change in kind, not merely in degree.

Did he actually make such a change?

If we look at all the surviving passages in which al-Kindī discusses the soul, we can identify the first clear sign of what I am calling this qualitative change.

The sensory tendency disappears completely from the concept of the soul in the earliest stage of Arab philosophy—that is, in al-Kindī’s philosophy. In its place we find a fully philosophical approach based on abstract concepts. The soul is no longer treated as a body, or as an attribute of a body.

This was an important advance in theoretical thought, begun by the first Arab philosopher.

But in al-Kindī, this advance was mixed with an extremely idealist view based on a sharp division between soul and body. The soul, in this view, is a spiritual substance that can exist apart from the body. The idea is originally Platonic.

His *Treatise on the Soul*[^V4-112] states openly that “the soul is simple … separate from the body and distinct from it, and its substance is a divine, spiritual substance.”

In the same text, however, al-Kindī describes the soul’s relationship to God in two different ways.

In one passage he says that the substance of the soul is “from the substance of the Creator … as sunlight is from the sun.”

In another, he raises the relationship between God and the soul almost to the level of equality, saying that the substance of the soul is “like the substance of the Creator.”

Both formulations necessarily lead him, with Plato, to say that the soul is immortal. The second in particular leads him, with the Neoplatonists, into language with a strongly mystical and illuminationist tone.

He says that the soul has the power, once it is freed from the body, “to know all things as the Creator knows them, or only slightly less.” Once the soul “is freed and leaves this body, and enters the world of intellect, it enters the light of the Creator and sees the Creator … It becomes aligned with His light and enters His kingdom. Knowledge of everything is then revealed to it, and all things become visible to it just as they are visible to the Creator.”[^V4-113]

Yet in another treatise,[^V4-114] al-Kindī speaks about the soul in a very different tone and in very different terms.

Here too he tries to prove that the soul is a substance rather than a body, and that it is distinct from the body in which life exists. But this time he argues in the language of purely philosophical reasoning. The spiritual and clearly mystical language has disappeared.

The starting point is now the claim that the soul is the substance “by which we name the essence of life in the body.”[^V4-115]

He also says:

“The soul is the intelligible form of the living being. It is therefore its species. The living being is a substance, and the species of a substance is a substance. Therefore the soul is a substance. And since it is a substance, and the substance of the species, it is not a body. For the species is not a body. It is the universal that includes its individual members, which are bodies.”[^V4-116]

Here al-Kindī proves that the soul is a substance in a different way, one that moves clearly in an Aristotelian direction.

He describes the soul as the intelligible form of the living being, understood as its species. In doing so, he also reveals his view of species themselves. For him, species are abstract universal concepts drawn from the individual things that belong to them. This is what he means when he describes individuals as “persons” that are bodies.

What matters most for us here is that al-Kindī now speaks of the soul as a **form**.

From the passage as a whole, it seems clear that his use of “form” is closely connected with Aristotle’s famous pair of concepts: **form and matter**.

Another text by al-Kindī confirms that this is indeed the Aristotelian meaning he has in mind. In his *Treatise on the Definitions and Descriptions of Things*,[^V4-117] he first defines form as “that by which a thing is what it is.” This is an Aristotelian definition.[^V4-118]

He then gives three definitions of the soul:

“The completion of a natural body equipped with organs and capable of life.”

And:

“The first actuality of a natural body that has life potentially.”

And:

“An intellectual substance, moving by itself, composed according to number.”

So we have three definitions of the soul. The first is al-Kindī’s own. The second points to Aristotle’s definition of the soul.[^V4-119] The third points to the Pythagorean view, also adopted by Plato, that the soul is composed of numbers.[^V4-120]

If we compare al-Kindī’s own definition here with the second and third definitions, the resemblance between his definition and Aristotle’s is clear.

We can therefore reach a straightforward conclusion: the first Arab philosopher understands the relationship between soul and body through the Aristotelian relationship between form and matter.

This conclusion is extremely important, because it rules out the idea that the soul is a spiritual substance that exists separately from the body.

Al-Kindī therefore falls into a sharp contradiction with himself.

If the soul is “the intelligible form of the living being”—its form and its species—then the soul is what gives that living being its actual existence, as opposed to existence merely in potential. This is also what al-Kindī means in the earlier statement that the soul is the substance “by which we name the essence of life in the body.”

And we know that, in Aristotle’s theory, form does not exist apart from matter, just as matter does not exist apart from form.

The soul, then, cannot exist apart from the living being, whether we consider the living being as a species or as individual members of that species.[^V4-121]

If we follow this agreement between Aristotle and al-Kindī to its logical conclusion, and remember that in Aristotle’s theory the species is drawn from the individual, the result is an inseparable link between soul and body in their very existence.

Yet this result directly contradicts what al-Kindī himself says in his special treatise on the soul, as we saw earlier.

It also seems to contradict another work of his that is listed in the historical catalogues but has not survived. Its title is: *Treatise on What the Soul Remembers While in the World of Intellect Before Its Existence in the World of Sense* (see Father Richard McCarthy, *The Works Attributed to the Philosopher of the Arabs*, p. 31, no. 167).

If this title really reflects the contents of the work, it comes very close to saying outright that al-Kindī follows Plato here in both the theory of Forms and the theory of knowledge. According to that view, knowledge comes from the soul “remembering” what it already knew in the “world of intellect”—the world of the Forms—before it “descended” into the world of the senses.

How, then, are we to explain these contradictions in al-Kindī’s writings?

Could we say that he wrote these conflicting views at different times, changing and developing his thinking as he went?

That is possible. But we do not have documents that tell us exactly when each of these treatises was written, so we cannot use their dates to trace the direction of his intellectual development.

There are, however, some clues. Most of his philosophical treatises seem to have been written during roughly the same period of his philosophical career: the period when he was connected with the court of the Abbasid caliph al-Muʿtaṣim (218–227 AH/833–841 CE). This may have continued into the reign of al-Wāthiq (227–232 AH/841–846 CE).

One reason for thinking this is that these treatises are addressed either to al-Muʿtaṣim himself,[^V4-122] to Aḥmad ibn al-Muʿtaṣim, who was al-Kindī’s student,[^V4-123] or to other students who also seem to have studied with him during this same period.[^V4-124]

If we accept these clues, they strengthen the case that the contradiction was real and make it harder to explain away by placing the different works at widely separated stages of al-Kindī’s life.

But these clues alone are not enough for a firm historical conclusion.

So there are still two possible ways of explaining the contradictions between the texts.

The first is that al-Kindī wrote his works on the soul at different times, and that during those periods he revised, changed, or developed his theory. The differences from one treatise to another would then reflect changes in the direction of his thought.

The second is that the contradiction was itself a lasting feature of an intellectual struggle taking place within al-Kindī. There are many signs that he really did experience such a struggle, as we will soon see from several examples.

I believe this struggle was between two opposing tendencies.

The first was a scientific and philosophical tendency that brought him closer to the materialist elements in Aristotle’s thought. It grew out of al-Kindī’s work in the natural sciences, both theoretical and experimental.

The second was a spiritualist tendency that drew him toward Plato’s world of Forms, or toward the Plotinian world of emanation. This grew out of his connection with Islamic theology and the theological culture that had formed the earliest basis of his intellectual education.

As we noted earlier, the weakness, lack of definition, and hesitation in al-Kindī’s ideological position helped this conflict survive and become more intense.

Al-Kindī’s attempt to bring Plato and Aristotle into agreement on the theory of the intellect, for example,[^V4-125] is a good example of the same mixture of different philosophical positions that we find throughout his writings on the soul.

When we analyze this attempt, it seems to be a way—conscious or unconscious—of dealing with the internal conflict between these two tendencies.

It is not simply the result of a philosophical method based on reconciling different schools, as most researchers have argued.

Nor can we explain it by saying that al-Kindī confused the different Greek philosophical schools because he did not know their original sources well enough.

It is difficult to claim, for example, that al-Kindī did not know Aristotle’s discussion of the intellect in *On the Soul*.[^V4-126] A comparison between Aristotle’s discussion and al-Kindī’s treatise on the same subject shows that al-Kindī knew the Aristotelian text itself well enough and did not depend only on the commentators.[^V4-127] In other words, he knew Aristotle’s *On the Soul* as a whole.

#### 11 — The Theory of the Intellect

Contemporary studies of Islamic thought confirm a historical fact that is easy for the researcher to reach independently: al-Kindī was the founder of the Aristotelian theory of the intellect in Arab philosophy.[^V4-128]

His *Treatise on the Intellect*[^V4-129] was the first work of its kind written by an Arab philosopher in his own distinctive way. It had a deep influence on later philosophers, including Ibn Sīnā.[^V4-130] As Dr. Madkour rightly says, it was “the starting point of this problem in the Islamic world, and the beginning of a subject that was to play an important role in the history of thought throughout the Middle Ages.”[^V4-131]

It is true that at the beginning of the treatise[^V4-132] al-Kindī says that he is writing it only as an “expository account.” In other words, he claims that he is simply setting out Aristotle’s view.

But his own way of presenting it—his brief explanations, his divisions of the subject, and the terms he chooses—shows more than simply a good understanding of Aristotle’s doctrine. Even more clearly, it shows that al-Kindī has taken Aristotle’s method and given it the character of his own philosophy of universals and species.

He had already set out that philosophy at the heart of his *Book to al-Muʿtaṣim Billāh on First Philosophy*.[^V4-133] There he discusses how species become united with the soul when the soul becomes actually intelligent. That discussion forms part of the complete theory of the intellect that we find here in the *Treatise on the Intellect*.

So while the *Treatise on the Intellect* may look purely Aristotelian on the surface, the *Treatise on First Philosophy*, both in appearance and in substance, clearly bears al-Kindī’s own stamp and expresses his own ideas, views, and wider philosophy of existence.

We can draw two conclusions from this.

First, there can be no real doubt that al-Kindī’s *Treatise on the Intellect* explains **his own** theory of the intellect.

Second, that theory is directly connected with its original Aristotelian source.

We can now turn to the main features of al-Kindī’s theory.

To grasp what is most important in it, we should begin with the classical division of the intellect that al-Kindī adopts in his treatise.

This division was inherited from Aristotle by generations of idealist philosophers before and after al-Kindī. They differed in some details—in names and terminology, sometimes in meaning, and sometimes in the number of kinds of intellect they recognized.

Al-Kindī himself is an example. He gives four kinds of intellect, rather than the three found in Aristotle and the three found in Alexander of Aphrodisias.[^V4-134]

In al-Kindī’s own words, the four are:

“The first is the intellect that is always actual. The second is the potential intellect, which belongs to the soul. The third is the intellect that has passed within the soul from potentiality to actuality. The fourth is the intellect … that appears from the soul when the soul brings it forth and makes it actually present outside itself.”

In the passage we quoted from al-Kindī’s *Treatise on First Philosophy*, we find an explanation of the first three kinds in this four-part division.

From that passage, we can understand the process of knowledge as passing through three states within the soul. But the fourth kind listed in the *Treatise on the Intellect* means that the complete process requires a fourth state as well.

Putting the two texts together gives us the following picture.

At first, the soul possesses intellect—knowledge—only as a possibility or capacity. This is the state of the **potentially intelligent soul**.

Then the soul becomes actually intelligent after it acquires knowledge and receives within itself the universal forms of the things that this knowledge concerns. This is the state of the **actually intelligent soul**. The soul has now moved from being merely capable of knowledge to actually possessing knowledge.

But this second state itself includes two different conditions.

The first is having knowledge simply as an ability or acquired capacity, without directly using it. Al-Kindī gives the example of a writer who knows how to write but is not writing at that moment.

The second is actively using that ability. This is like the writer actually writing, or a thinker actually thinking after acquiring the knowledge that has given him the ability to think in abstract forms.

This last state is what al-Kindī calls the “fourth intellect.” It is what he means when he describes this intellect as appearing in the soul when it becomes active.[^V4-135]

So we have three states of the human intellect:

a potential intellect;

an actual intellect that is not being directly used;

and an actual intellect in the process of being directly used.

All three states are unquestionably real. And all three occur within the human soul as part of the whole human being—that is, the human being made up of soul and body.

But here we come to a problem.

The problem begins with Aristotle, continues through both his earlier and later commentators, and then appears again in al-Kindī’s theory of the intellect.

For all of them, the question is this:

**How does the soul move from being potentially intelligent to being actually intelligent?**

They believe that this change cannot happen by itself. Something must actively cause it.

And they believe that this active cause cannot come from within the human soul itself—that is, from the powers of the soul connected with the material body—because all of those powers are passive. The cause needed here must be active without itself being acted upon.

It must therefore be, in their view, “an immaterial substance, always existing in actuality and existing independently of the world of the human soul.”

Only such a thing, they believe, could have the power to turn what exists potentially into something actual.

This is why their classifications introduce another kind of “intellect” outside the three states just described. Al-Kindī calls it the intellect that is “always actual” and places it first in his four-part division.

Its task is to act on the soul and move it from potential intellect to actual intellect.

Aristotle builds this part of his theory of intellect on his two famous concepts of **matter and form**. He originally developed these concepts as a philosophical attempt to explain the existence of nature. Here he also uses them to solve the problem of how knowledge develops within the human soul.[^V4-136]

In his *Treatise on the Intellect*, al-Kindī follows this Aristotelian method. He applies the concepts of matter and form in nature to the changing movement of knowledge within the human soul.

The basic idea, as Aristotle sets it out and al-Kindī uses it, is this:

According to Aristotle’s theory of being, there is something in nature that serves as the matter of every form—that is, something that is potentially all forms. There is also something else that acts as the cause and active force through which each thing comes into actuality.

The same distinction, they argue, exists within the soul.

On one side is an intellect that can become all things: the potential intellect.

On the other side is an intellect through whose activity all things come to exist within the soul.

This second intellect is like light. In a certain sense, light makes colors that exist only potentially become actual colors.

For Aristotle, this intellect can exist separately from the body. It is not passive, and it is not mixed with anything else, because it is an immaterial form. It does not perish. It is always actual. In other words, it has an independent existence of its own, apart from the things it thinks.[^V4-137]

This is the philosophical basis for placing an “intellect” outside the human soul among the different kinds of intellect in their classifications.

But what is the real problem behind this philosophical construction?

If we look more deeply, we can see the problem they were trying to solve by searching outside the soul.

In fact, they were right to look for a missing link in the dialectical process by which knowledge develops. The missing link was the one that explains how thinking moves from possibility to actual realization.

Why were they unable to find it?

The basic reason was an objective historical error—an error for which they themselves were not personally to blame, because it reflected the level of knowledge available in their time.

The error was the idealist assumption that the laws of thought and the laws of the external material world are separate from each other, almost completely independent.

They treated the world of the soul as a world in itself, separate and distinct from external reality. By doing this, they drove their explanation of knowledge into a dead end and closed off the path to a correct solution.

Once they had done that, the only way they could find the missing link in the process was to turn to this idealist solution.

By breaking the connection between the laws of thought and the laws of reality—or between the forms of thought and their real content—idealist philosophers made themselves unable to explain correctly how human knowledge moves through its different dialectical stages.

They were therefore forced to imagine an “active cause” that comes in from outside the soul and outside reality at the same time—in other words, from outside both the knowing subject and the object being known.

This produced an internal contradiction in al-Kindī’s theory of the intellect, just as it had in its Aristotelian source.

The contradiction appears directly in the difference between a realistic view of some stages of thought and an idealist view of the stage in which the decisive change takes place: the movement from potential knowledge to actual knowledge.

Aristotle, and al-Kindī after him, are right to see the first stage of human knowledge as one of pure potential. In modern language, we might call this the natural capacity, readiness, or ability to receive knowledge.

They are also right to see a second stage in which knowledge is actually received and acquired. At this stage, a person develops the real ability to think: the ability to separate things from their sensory forms and turn them into universal forms or species—to move, in other words, from “things” to “kinds.”

We can even say that al-Kindī noticed a third stage in this process.

This is the stage in which thought returns to the “things” themselves and applies to them the “ideas” or “species” that it had previously drawn from them.

Al-Kindī saw this stage, although only in a somewhat unclear way.

He correctly understood that knowledge takes two forms in a human being. First, “ideas” exist as a capacity or acquired ability. Second, that capacity is actually put to use in a direct act of knowing.

This is why al-Kindī added a fourth kind to the traditional classification of intellect. As we saw, he called it “the intellect that appears in the soul when it becomes active.”

Seen in this way, these stages are basically correct because they correspond to real processes.

But the account suddenly loses touch with reality when it fails to see the connection between these stages as forms of thought and the objective material reality that gives those forms their content.

It therefore misses the fact that the “cause” which moves the soul from potential intellect to actual intellect does not belong to some invisible world outside both the subject and the object.

The cause lies in the relationship between subject and object itself.

It lies within the dialectical movement of the process of knowledge, in the living and objective connection between thought and reality—or between the forms of thought and their contents, which are simply material reality reflected in forms of thought.

The contradiction in Aristotle’s theory of the intellect, and later in al-Kindī’s, does not end there.

It has two other sides.

The first appears especially clearly in Aristotle’s account of the objects of sense.

Aristotle says that sensible things are what the senses perceive. At the same time, he says that these sensible things are what turn the senses from potentially sensing into actually sensing.[^V4-138]

Here Aristotle takes a materialist position.

He is saying that sensible things—the material objects outside us—are themselves the active cause that turns what exists potentially into something actual.

Yet when he deals with the intellect, as we have seen, he takes an idealist position. To explain how the “potential intellect” becomes “actual intellect,” he looks for the “active cause” in a realm that is foreign to both subject and object—a realm beyond both external reality and the human soul.

The second side of the contradiction is found in both Aristotle and al-Kindī. Their theory of knowledge contains a position that conflicts with the position they take in their theory of the intellect.

Let us begin with Aristotle.

In opposing[^V4-139] the Platonic approach of pure intellectual contemplation, which treated nature as something secondary, Aristotle recognized the real existence of nature.[^V4-140] He asks the knowing subject to enter into nature itself.

From this comes his objectivity, which is one of the basic principles of his theory of knowledge and which he defends in the *Posterior Analytics*. By this objectivity, I mean his claim that knowledge begins with the senses.[^V4-141]

For the same reason, Aristotle can rightly be regarded as one of the founders of the other principle that says: **there is nothing in the intellect that did not first pass through the senses.**

If we connect these two principles with all the passages in which Aristotle recognizes the objective existence of the external world, we can reach the following conclusion: Aristotle’s theory of knowledge rests on materialist philosophical foundations.

Aristotle has no doubt that sensation is produced when an external object acts on the senses.[^V4-142]

At the foundation of his theory of knowledge, he describes the process of thought as a sequence. It begins with sensation, then moves to “representation” or imagination—which acts as a bridge between sensation and the idea—and ends with the formation of the idea or judgment (see *On the Soul*, III, 431, 16).

All of this gives Aristotle a clearly materialist position in his theory of knowledge.

But that position conflicts with the idealist position he takes in his theory of the intellect, where he introduces an invisible agent from outside both the soul and material reality to complete the process of thinking.

---

That is Aristotle.

With al-Kindī, we find something similar in his treatise on “the nature of sleep and dreams” (*Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, pp. 293–311). This work allows us to reconstruct one part of his theory of knowledge—the part that matters to us here.

His study of sleep and dreams is based on the connection between the senses and what he calls the “image-forming faculty.”

He explains the difference between the senses and this image-forming faculty in the following way.

The senses give us images of the things we perceive together with their material features—what al-Kindī calls their “nature.”

The “image-forming faculty,” by contrast, gives us the images of individual things, or of sensible things, after separating those images from their material features.[^V4-143]

If we remember that al-Kindī’s “image-forming faculty” acts as a bridge, in the movement of knowledge, between what is sensed and what is abstract—and his debt to Aristotle here is clear—we can reconstruct the whole process.

Thought begins with the senses.

It then moves to the imagination, which strips the sensory images of their material form.

Finally, thought takes these images and abstracts from them universal forms, or species.

This completes the process of abstraction.

So here too, like Aristotle, al-Kindī begins from a sound starting point with a material basis.

On the question of knowledge, he begins first from the objective existence of the world, and second from the senses as the starting point through which we come to know that objective world.

It is therefore clear that neither Aristotle nor al-Kindī places an impenetrable wall here between the laws of thought and the laws of reality.

Yet that is exactly the wall they created in their theory of the intellect. And because of it, they were forced to bring in an invisible agent to turn the potential intellect into actual intellect.

This is how they fell into the many-sided contradiction we have been examining.

---

There is one basic source behind all the contradictions that run through the teachings of Aristotle and the Aristotelians—including al-Kindī—on questions of knowledge. More precisely, they could not identify the bridge that connects rational knowledge with sensory knowledge, or the universal with the individual, in a dialectical relationship.

This problem is not limited to Aristotle and the Aristotelians. It appears in many ancient and modern schools of philosophy, and in modern bourgeois philosophy in general, especially among the empiricists and positivists. Marxism alone succeeded in breaking free of this limitation. The development of science in our own age has repeatedly confirmed the value of what Marxism-Leninism achieved in this area, because it recognized the role of practice in solving the problem of knowledge at its roots.

Aristotle’s failure to discover this bridge led him into an even sharper contradiction than the ones we have already discussed. On one side of his theory of knowledge, we find a tendency toward sensory experience. On the other, he relies on “rational intuition” as the only way to grasp what he calls the “first principles.” How could he combine this idealist appeal to intuition with his view that knowledge begins with the senses?

Aristotle never managed to answer this question, despite the serious and valuable attempts he made in several of his works, including *Metaphysics*, the *Analytics*, and *On the Soul*. This is hardly surprising. He could not solve the problem because he had not discovered the true relationship between rational and sensory knowledge.

Nor could we reasonably expect him to have done so. His starting point was still essentially contemplative, and scientific knowledge in his time had not yet developed far enough to push a thinker such as Aristotle beyond contemplation and toward a view that connected knowledge with practice.

For Aristotle, and for the Aristotelians who followed him, including al-Kindī, understanding this relationship would have required a fuller understanding of the social content of human life and of practical human activity. Only then could they have grasped the two-way relationship between practice and thought, between what Lenin calls “living perception” and “abstract thought,”[^V4-144] and the part this relationship plays in the whole process of knowledge.

A scientific approach requires us to keep the historical setting in mind. As we have said, we can therefore understand why Aristotle and his contemporaries did not reach the correct solution to this problem.

But the same historical approach also gives us reason to expect someone like al-Kindī to move beyond the point where Aristotle had stopped.

There are two reasons for this.

First, a long period separated Aristotle’s age from al-Kindī’s. During that time, social relations changed, and science and human knowledge advanced to new levels.

Second, al-Kindī himself was deeply involved in a wide range of applied sciences. We know this from his biography, from the lists of his works, and from several of his surviving treatises that are now available to modern researchers.

Of course, we do not expect the experimental sciences of al-Kindī’s age to have given philosophy what later science would make possible, especially in the modern period. The enormous progress of the experimental sciences in more recent centuries helped drive the rapid development of modern philosophy, whose most advanced stage, in our view, is Marxist thought and its discovery of the living dialectic at work in nature, thought, and society.

We are not expecting anything like that from al-Kindī.

But we do have the right to expect at least some movement beyond Aristotle.

Did al-Kindī achieve even that much?

The following pages are an attempt to answer this question.

#### 12 — Positions at the “Crossroads”

Al-Kindī had the originality of a real philosopher. A researcher can see this even through the tangled structure of his arguments and proofs on the question of God and the “creation of existence from nothing.”[^V4-145]

We are under no illusion that al-Kindī completely broke free of his theological commitments. But he was also a philosopher. Philosophy had taken a firm and active place in his intellectual life.

Because of this, his philosophical instincts sometimes drew him toward positions that brought him to a “crossroads”—a point which, under the conditions of Arab-Islamic society in the third century AH, he had real historical reasons to move beyond.

Yet his theological commitment would either hold him at that crossroads or pull him away from it before he could make the decisive break.

We can almost sense the deep, hidden struggle inside al-Kindī: a conflict between his theological commitments and a philosophical tendency that was being pushed in another direction by the conditions of Arab-Islamic society in the third century AH. His own intellectual preparation seems to have made him capable of responding to those conditions. What held him back was the strength of his other commitment.

We will now look at several examples of these moments. In each case, al-Kindī’s philosophical thinking takes him to the edge of an almost decisive crossroads: between the purely religious basis from which he begins on the central question of first philosophy, and a new basis for philosophical development that could have freed itself from the control of that religious commitment.

##### First: God’s Influence on Matter

In several of al-Kindī’s philosophical treatises, we find statements such as these:

> **A —** “Nature is the primary cause of everything that moves or is at rest.”[^V4-146]

> **B —** “For nature is that through which God establishes the cause and reason for all things that move or are at rest, insofar as motion is concerned.”[^V4-147]

> **C —** “Of all the things below God among His creation, the first is acted upon directly by God, and after that ‘some are acted upon through others.’ The first is affected, and through its being affected another is affected; through the second another is affected, and so on until we reach the final thing affected. The first thing affected is called an agent only figuratively in relation to what is affected by it, because it is the proximate cause of that thing’s being affected. The same is true of the second, since it is the proximate cause of the third, and so on until the final effects are reached. But the Creator, exalted be He, is in the true sense the first cause of all effects, whether they occur through intermediaries or without intermediaries, because He acts and is never acted upon. He is the proximate cause of the first thing affected, and an indirect cause of the effects that come after it.”[^V4-148]

When al-Kindī writes things like this, a serious method of research does not allow us simply to pass over what these passages imply.

They contain bold suggestions of a dialectical way of looking at the problem. To some degree, this view differs from the dominant theological position of his time, according to which God acts directly on matter without intermediaries.

Al-Kindī repeatedly stresses the role of intermediate efficient causes.

This points toward a denial of direct divine action on matter. Instead, direct action is assigned to natural causes. In other words, these natural causes become the immediate agents responsible for the existence and activity of things in nature.

Al-Kindī tries to soften this idea by saying that these intermediate causes act only “figuratively, not truly.” But this distinction between the figurative and the true appears to be little more than a matter of terminology.

After all, al-Kindī himself places these causes within his account of how causes are ordered throughout the universe. And when he describes the chain through which one cause acts upon another, he clearly presents these effects as real events in the universe, not as figures of speech.[^V4-149]

Every earlier thing that is affected becomes, in relation to what follows it, “the proximate cause of its being affected.”

Al-Kindī says this quite clearly.

Here, then, al-Kindī has placed himself at a dangerous crossroads. One more step could have taken him beyond his original theological commitment altogether.

##### Second: The Law of Cause and Effect

Let us return to the passages we have just quoted and look more closely at the complete picture they give us of a universe ordered through a chain of causes.

If we ask what philosophical—and scientific—basis al-Kindī uses to build this picture, the answer is his firm commitment to the law of cause and effect. For him, this law serves as the general foundation of the laws of nature as a whole.

His theoretical and experimental work in the natural sciences, including medicine and mathematics, had convinced him not only that this law operates, but also that causal relations follow a necessary and ordered sequence.[^V4-150]

When we read, for example, his treatise *On Explaining the Proximate Efficient Cause of Generation and Corruption*,[^V4-151] there is little doubt that al-Kindī extends the law of causality beyond what he calls the world of generation and decay, or the world “below the Moon”—in other words, beyond the earthly world.

The text of this treatise comes very close to saying explicitly that the same natural laws which govern our earthly world also govern the relationship between this world and what al-Kindī calls the realm of the “higher bodies.” By this he means the celestial spheres, or what we would today describe as the solar system.

These laws operate there with the same universality and necessity.

This remains true despite the basic distinction al-Kindī makes between the earthly world and the world of the “higher bodies.”[^V4-152] The earthly world is subject to generation and decay. The higher world, in his view, is not. Yet neither world is eternal.

For al-Kindī, the link between these two worlds begins with the relationship between the actions of the soul and the “temperaments of bodies.”[^V4-153]

He believes that a body’s temperament determines the kind of actions its soul performs. Different bodily temperaments therefore produce different actions of the soul.

And these temperaments, he says, “differ according to differences among the higher bodies in place, time, and quality.”[^V4-154]

From here, al-Kindī goes on to explain—in considerable detail and with specific examples—the material influence that the heavenly bodies supposedly exercise over human beings. He includes their influence on people’s skin color, physical temperament, character, facial features, and even the development of embryos in the womb.[^V4-155]

He tries to explain all of this through natural relations and mathematical calculations based on the material connections between earthly bodies and the heavenly bodies. He does so, of course, only to the extent that the science of his own time and society allowed him to discover such laws. But the necessary foundation of the whole system is the law of cause and effect.

At this point in his discussion of the material influence of the heavens on human beings, al-Kindī suddenly considers taking another genuinely bold step.

He asks:

> “... If this is so, what prevents something subtler than this from coming into existence through the motion of these heavenly bodies, by the will of the Creator—glory be to Him—since what is clearest is their nearest effect, that is, the effect of the heavenly bodies, and they are its proximate cause? And are things anything other than consequences that follow this remarkable coming-to-be, by which I mean natural coming-to-be and the coming-to-be of the soul?”[^V4-156]

It is difficult to read this passage without being struck by its importance.

In our view, it contains a significant move toward an almost openly materialist position. Al-Kindī surrounds the idea with a great deal of caution. He speaks of the “nearest effect,” the “distant cause,” and “the will of the Creator, glory be to Him.” By the last phrase he is pointing toward the more distant effect and the distant cause, expressions he commonly uses when referring to the Creator’s will.

He also deliberately leaves vague what he means by the “subtler” thing and by the “other consequences.”

But none of this caution succeeds in hiding the real content of the passage or its importance.

Al-Kindī has just finished explaining how the motion of the heavenly bodies produces material causal effects on human bodies. He now tries to carry the same idea beyond the body to something “subtler.”

By this, he seems to mean what we might call “spirit,” what older philosophy called the “soul,” or what modern scientific language would call “life.”

In effect, al-Kindī is trying to say—and, in fact, does say—that if the motion of the heavenly bodies can have such powerful real and material effects on the “bodies” of people on Earth, then there is even more reason to think that something “subtler” than bodies might itself “exist”—notice his use of the word “exist”—because of this same motion of the heavenly bodies.

Why should that be more likely?

Al-Kindī answers with another new and daring idea, one that is philosophically important for understanding the materialist position toward which he is moving here.

He says that if the clearest thing we already know is that bodily conditions are effects of the movement of the heavenly bodies, and that this movement is their “proximate” cause, then it should be even easier to understand a causal relationship between that same movement and things that are among the “consequences” of “natural generation and the generation of the soul.”

Notice the word “consequences.”

What are these consequences?

If we understand what al-Kindī means by something “subtler,” then we also understand what he means by these “consequences,” because the two expressions point to the same thing.

As we have suggested, he most likely means spirit, or the soul in the language of his own age, or what we would today call the principle of life.

Can we then conclude that al-Kindī had reached some understanding of the fundamental relationship between matter and life—whether through an extraordinary spontaneous intuition or through a brilliant scientific insight?

Of course, this does not become a fully materialist position unless we connect al-Kindī’s idea with what science later discovered: that the motion of the heavenly bodies is not separate from the motion of matter in earthly bodies.

To put it another way, there is no difference between the earthly world and the celestial world when it comes to the laws of nature.

---

In a passage like this, then, al-Kindī’s materialist tendency does more than bring him to an almost decisive crossroads. It takes him beyond that crossroads to a point that, in one sense, can be called decisive.

But the moment we leave this passage and turn to another part of his philosophical writings, we meet a very different al-Kindī.

There are many texts in which he describes God, the “first true One,” as possessing “complete power, perfect strength, and overflowing existence,”[^V4-157] or as being “the first cause that has no cause, the agent for whom there is no agent, the perfecter for whom there is no perfecter, and the one who brings all things into existence from nothing...”[^V4-158]

The sharp contradiction between these two positions makes something we have already said even clearer.

Al-Kindī was caught in a severe and troubled hesitation between two opposing positions.

The first was a strict commitment to the Islamic doctrine of the “creation of the world from nothing,” together with the complete separation between God and the material world that this doctrine requires.

The second was an equally strict commitment to the universal law of cause and effect. But if causality truly applies everywhere, then the material world must somehow be connected to an eternal existence. And that in turn leads toward rejecting the claim that the world was “created from nothing”—indeed, toward rejecting the idea of creation itself.

Could al-Kindī have tried to solve this problem by “reconciling” the two positions?

Perhaps he treated causality as a general law whose authority applied only within the natural world, while refusing to apply it beyond nature. This would have protected him from a conclusion he was unwilling to accept: that recognizing causality without exception would mean recognizing the eternity of the world, and therefore denying creation.

This may explain why he insists that the first cause itself has no cause, that it alone is eternal, and that every cause below it was brought into existence from nothing. In other words, the existence of these causes did not itself arise through the causal relations that govern the natural world.

Yet this claim only makes his contradiction more obvious.

At the same time, al-Kindī accepts an ordered hierarchy of causes that creates a continuous and coherent chain stretching from the highest point—the first cause—all the way down to the lowest causes.

The contradiction becomes even clearer when we connect this picture of the cosmic order with passages where al-Kindī seems to suggest that the links between these causes are produced through **emanation**.

The expression “overflowing existence” in the passage quoted above, along with similar expressions elsewhere in his writings,[^V4-159] gives us serious reason to consider a connection between al-Kindī’s system of cosmic causes and the Neoplatonic theory of emanation.

There is no strong reason to deny this connection when al-Kindī’s own texts point us toward it.

And we know that the Neoplatonic idea of emanation means that the world proceeds from an eternal existence, not from sheer nothingness.

All of this strongly suggests that al-Kindī was genuinely caught in a struggle, or at least a hesitation, between these two opposing positions.

In our view, this conflict had several real sources.

One was the theoretical influence of the Neoplatonic idea of emanation and the picture of the universe built around it.

Another was the ideological position that restricted al-Kindī, which we discussed earlier.

A third was his work in the natural sciences, both theoretical and practical. This work allowed him to see, with real awareness, how widely the law of cause and effect operates in nature and in the heavens—at least as far as the scientific knowledge of his time allowed.

And above all, though indirectly, there was the wider ideological struggle surrounding the official ideology of the caliphal state. The political authority of the caliphate’s center was beginning to fracture, while ideas of social revolt were spreading from different regions and among different oppressed social groups.

##### Third: The World Is Infinite “in Potential”

Here we find another example of the positions that place al-Kindī at an almost decisive crossroads.

More precisely, we find another example of the same contradiction that appeared in the two cases above. Just as al-Kindī could not resolve the contradiction there, it seems difficult for him to resolve it here.

Al-Kindī says that matter, motion, and time are finite, and that they cannot actually be infinite.

But when he says this, he means that they are finite **in actuality**. He is talking about matter as it actually exists—as a definite, concrete body.

He says this explicitly. Whenever he discusses the “finitude” of matter, motion, or time, he adds the words “in actuality.”[^V4-160]

Aristotle does the same when he says that the infinite does not exist in actuality.[^V4-161]

But al-Kindī then adds—again following Aristotle[^V4-162]—that infinity does exist **in potential**, and that there is no contradiction in saying that matter, motion, and time can all be infinite in potential.[^V4-163]

So here al-Kindī agrees with Aristotle that matter is potentially infinite, as are motion and time.

The question is what al-Kindī means by the expression “in potential.”

Does he give the term a meaning of his own, different from Aristotle’s? Or does he understand “finite” and “infinite” in the same way Aristotle does?

To answer this, we need to read the following passage from al-Kindī:

> “... If the body of the whole[^V4-164] can be increased indefinitely in the imagination, so that we imagine something greater than it, and then something greater again, without end, then there is no limit to this increase in terms of possibility. It is therefore potentially without end, since potentiality is nothing other than possibility. I mean that the continuous thing exists in potential. Everything belonging to that which is without end is itself potentially without end. Motion and time are examples of this. So that which is without end[^V4-165] exists only in potential. In actuality, however, nothing can be without end, as we have already shown...”[^V4-166]

If al-Kindī really means to explain “potentiality” and “possibility” simply through imagination, as this passage seems to suggest, then the explanation is too naive and superficial to be taken seriously.

If imagination alone can turn the impossible into something possible, then why should we not also let imagination turn something into an **actual** infinity? If the whole question remains inside the realm of imagination, there is no obvious reason to stop there.

For this reason, we do not think al-Kindī’s true meaning of “in potential” or “possible” can simply be reduced to imagination, even though the wording of this passage makes it seem that way.

We therefore need to return to his main source on the concepts of finitude and infinity: Aristotle.

Al-Kindī’s entire discussion follows Aristotle’s method and uses Aristotle’s categories, concepts, and terminology. It is therefore highly likely that al-Kindī uses “potentiality” and “possibility” here in the same basic sense.

So how does Aristotle understand the issue?

At the beginning of chapter six of Book Three of the *Physics* (206a9), under the heading “The Infinite: Its Existence and Nature,” Aristotle explains that completely denying the existence of the infinite would lead to “many impossible consequences.”

One of these, he says, is that “time would have to have a beginning and an end...”[^V4-167]

Some way therefore had to be found to avoid these “impossible” consequences.

The solution was to say that the infinite “exists in one sense and does not exist in another.”

The sense in which it exists, Aristotle explains, is existence “in potential.”

But what does that mean?

Aristotle says we should not understand “potential” here in the ordinary sense in which we might say, “this thing is potentially copper,” meaning that it will later become copper.

If we applied that meaning to infinity, then something potentially infinite would eventually become actually infinite. But Aristotle had already argued in the previous chapter that actual infinity is impossible.

What, then, does it mean to say that the infinite exists “in potential”?

For Aristotle, it exists in the same way that we say a day exists, or that a contest exists: it is always coming into being one part after another.

The same applies to infinity.

Here too, Aristotle distinguishes between what exists potentially and what exists actually. He gives the example of the Olympic games: we can say that they exist because the wrestling exists first potentially and then actually.[^V4-168]

A little later, Aristotle again explains what it means for the infinite to exist potentially. It exists, he says, in the same way that a day or a contest exists, and potentially “like matter,” not like a complete finite thing existing all at once.[^V4-169]

The comparison with a day, a contest, and matter is explained more clearly by two early Arab commentators on Aristotle, Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī[^V4-170] and Abū ʿAlī Ibn al-Samḥ.[^V4-171]

They write:

> “... Existing things are of two kinds: things that exist in potential and things that exist in actuality.
>
> “Among things that exist in potential, some move fully into actuality, become complete, come to an end, and leave no remaining potential behind. Copper,[^V4-172] for example, is potentially a statue. Once it becomes a statue, that particular potential is gone.
>
> “Other things never bring everything contained in their potential into actuality, and so their potential is never exhausted.[^V4-173] Matter, for example, can potentially receive contrary qualities. But contrary qualities cannot all exist in it at the same time in actuality. One contrary is realized in it, while the others remain in potential.
>
> “Among things that exist in actuality, some exist completely in actuality, though not all at once. They come into actuality part by part and little by little, like a day. A day exists actually, but not because the whole day exists at a single moment. Rather, one part of it exists and then another.
>
> “What has no end exists potentially, not in the sense that the whole of it will ever come into actuality, but in the sense that it exists little by little. And in this same sense it also exists actually...”[^V4-174]

We needed to quote these passages from Aristotle and his early Arab commentators so that Aristotle’s idea of “potential existence” in relation to the infinite would be completely clear.

What we can draw from Aristotle is this: the infinite has a potential existence comparable to the potential existence of matter.

Matter, time, and motion therefore have a potentially endless existence of the same kind.

And we know that, in Aristotle’s philosophy, the potential existence of matter is a form of real and eternal existence. It is not imaginary, and it is not preceded by nonexistence.

So when al-Kindī allows matter, motion, and time to be infinite in potential, does he mean the same thing Aristotle means by “potential existence,” as the whole structure of al-Kindī’s discussion seems to suggest?

If he does, then he falls into another contradiction with himself.

He would be implicitly recognizing that the material world has a kind of existence before its actual existence: an infinite potential existence, which means an eternal—or, more precisely, everlasting—existence.

But this implicit admission undermines his view of God and of God’s relationship with the world. In other words, it undermines one of the basic claims of his philosophy, because it destroys the idea that the world was created from nothing.

If, on the other hand, al-Kindī means something else by “potential existence,” then we find nothing in the works available to us that explains this other meaning, apart from the discussion of “imagination” that we quoted earlier.

We have already considered that possibility and rejected the idea that imagination itself can be al-Kindī’s real meaning of “potential” or “possible.”

We are therefore led to conclude that al-Kindī and Aristotle understand “potential existence” in essentially the same way.

And this gives us another example of the positions that, as we have said, brought al-Kindī to an almost decisive crossroads without allowing him to move beyond it.

He could not overcome the contradictions imposed on him, for reasons that were both personal and historical.

This is hardly surprising. A thinker in his position was being asked by the historical stage of his society to confront precisely those questions and problems most closely connected with that society at that particular moment.

##### Fourth: The Dialectic of Unity and Multiplicity

Here we begin by pointing to the dialectical content that al-Kindī discovered in a question that had already occupied the theologians before passing into philosophy through al-Kindī himself.

Once the question entered philosophy, he gave it a new and distinctive content.

The theologians had discussed the problem of unity and multiplicity in order to prove divine unity—the oneness of God.

Al-Kindī did not, in fact, remove the problem from this theological framework.

But before returning to theology, he examined unity and multiplicity within the world of things we can perceive with the senses. And there he treated the issue in a purely philosophical way, almost as though it were an independent philosophical problem.

He explored it at length through reflection and through mathematical and logical arguments.

In doing so, he discovered the dialectical character of the relationship between unity and multiplicity. He argued that we cannot think of unity in things or numbers without multiplicity being contained within it, just as we cannot think of multiplicity without unity.[^V4-175]

From this he concludes “that unity shares in multiplicity—that is, shares in it in all sensible things and in what belongs to sensible things. Wherever there is multiplicity, there is unity, and wherever there is unity, there is multiplicity.”[^V4-176]

When al-Kindī discusses this problem within the material world, he takes a scientific position with considerable insight.

Here he uses the law of cause and effect to argue that the connection between unity and multiplicity is not the product of chance. It is the result of causal relations among things “from the beginning of their coming into being,”[^V4-177] and nothing can escape this strict and universal law of causality.

This was al-Kindī’s position as long as he remained within the world of things accessible to the senses and the things connected with them.

We have seen that this position contains a dialectical insight. It allows him to recognize that a thing and its opposite can, in some sense, exist together within a single unity.

For a thinker of al-Kindī’s time to reach such an insight is striking.

But the moment he carries the same discussion into the realm of the “first true eternal One,” he suddenly falls into a deep contradiction.[^V4-178]

The connection with the world of sensible things—the world of living dialectical movement—is suddenly broken.

Al-Kindī moves instead into a world of “unity” that is absolutely still, a unity that allows no dialectical relationship with any form or meaning of multiplicity.

This sharp split between “unity” in the world of metaphysics and “unity” in the natural world is another form of the dualism that Arab philosophy had to struggle with in many of the major questions it addressed, even during the most productive periods of its growth and flourishing.

Here we must repeat something we have already said.

One reason Arab philosophical thought faced these difficulties was that its earliest life developed under the care of Islamic theological thought.

At the same time, its birth, growth, and later development were deeply and closely tied to the historical conditions in which Arab-Islamic society itself arose, grew, and developed.

In other words, the contradictions within philosophy were also connected with the contradictions within that society’s social and ideological relations.

#### 13 — Motion, Matter, and History

But we should not forget the more general rule at work here.

We must also keep in mind the objective limits faced by human thought as a whole during that historical period.

At that stage of history, human thought had not yet developed the ability to discover the laws of matter’s own internal motion. These laws became clearer only in the modern age, after the development of the natural sciences. That development, in turn, confirmed the general laws and basic categories of the dialectics of nature discovered by Marxist science.

This broad historical inability to understand the objective laws governing the motion of matter appears in many different forms among ancient and medieval philosophers.

Its clearest form is their tendency to see the material world—the world of nature—as incapable of producing its own motion.

Because they could see that motion was inseparable from the existence of the world, they were driven to look outside that world for the source of motion.

Some located this source in the “World Soul,” as Plato did.

Others found it in a “First Mover,” as Aristotle did.

And so on.

Al-Kindī, however, seems to me to have sensed the problem behind this historical limitation. He came close to confronting it through something like a scientific hypothesis.

But the hold of theological and metaphysical thought prevented this promising approach from changing the basic historical form of the problem. Al-Kindī still could not discover self-motion within nature itself.

He did, in fact, consider whether “a thing could be the cause of its own coming into being.”[^V4-179]

But he considered the question outside its proper scientific ground in the natural world. His scientific “intuition” therefore failed to open a path toward a new solution.

Even so, the very fact that al-Kindī could ask whether “a thing could be the cause of its own coming into being” should be seen as an important step in philosophy’s long movement toward understanding the workings of nature.

His final answer was negative. He concluded that “it is not possible for a thing to be the cause of its own coming into being.”[^V4-180]

We can also say that al-Kindī’s contradictory treatment of the law of cause and effect is itself one expression of the historical inability of ancient and medieval philosophy to uncover the secret of matter’s motion.

As we saw earlier, within the natural world al-Kindī takes a scientific view of the universal reach of causality.

But the moment the discussion moves into metaphysics, he suddenly gives up that view.

He limits the force and strict universality of the law of cause and effect, setting aside its absolute reach and moving to the opposite side of the contradiction so that he can still affirm the creation of existence from nothing.

---

These are examples of philosophical positions in which al-Kindī hesitated between two tendencies: a philosophical drive to free himself from the grip of strict theological assumptions, and a desire to preserve those assumptions as far as he could.

We have presented these examples because they carry the distinctive historical character of this first stage in the development of medieval Arab-Islamic philosophy.

## Summary of Chapter One

### 1

We began this chapter with a question: how did Arab philosophy first emerge in forms independent of theological thought?

In answering it, we found two very different developments in which Arab philosophy first appeared as an independent form of thought.

The first was al-Kindī, traditionally described as “the first philosopher of the Arabs.” He represented the first philosophical movement to become relatively separate from Muʿtazilite *kalām*, or systematic theology.

The second was Sufism. It represented another form of this movement, but one that was fundamentally opposed to the rationalist method of Muʿtazilite *kalām*.

### 2

We began our study of al-Kindī by rejecting the idea that this first Arab philosopher was, in the strict sense, the “founder” of Arab philosophy.

In our view, the foundations of this philosophy had already been laid at an earlier stage by the Muʿtazilites, who founded *kalām*. Al-Kindī’s importance lies elsewhere. He represented a new current of thought that grew out of the social and historical conditions of his time. He also expressed the movement of Arab philosophical thought, at that stage, toward independence from Islamic theology.

From this we drew two conclusions.

First, al-Kindī did not appear in Abbasid Arab society by pure chance, nor was he an exceptional individual who stood outside the general movement of his age. His appearance was the product of the development of that society as a whole.

Second, the new and independent theoretical form of Arab philosophy that appeared in al-Kindī’s work was not simply Greek philosophy translated into Arabic, as some Western Orientalists have claimed. It was an Arab-Islamic form of philosophy that emerged because Arab-Islamic society itself had come to need it. It benefited from the translation of Greek philosophy into Arabic, and also from contact with the cultures of the other peoples who had become part of this society. Together with the Arab people, these peoples made up the social world of the Islamic caliphate.

To support these two conclusions, we relied first on the general laws governing the development of society, and second on the laws governing the development of human thought within that broader process.

At that stage, history had created a need for philosophy to become independent of theological thought. By chance, that historical need coincided with the presence of al-Kindī, who was able to take on the role of the first philosopher to open the way toward this relative independence.

Al-Kindī was well suited to such an important historical task. For his time, he possessed an unusually high level of knowledge and intellectual ability.

But how far did philosophy actually become independent of theology in al-Kindī’s hands?

To answer that, we need to understand the different social and intellectual influences that came together in his formation.

These influences were many and varied.

One was Muʿtazilite thought. Another was the natural and mathematical sciences, which he practiced both theoretically and experimentally. There were also the philosophical sciences he encountered through different foreign sources, especially Greek thought in its Athenian and Alexandrian forms.

To this mixture we must add his grounding in Arabic language and culture, which itself included Islamic religious culture, as well as the influence of the basic doctrines of Islam.

Taken together, these elements help us understand how all these influences interacted within al-Kindī, and how that interaction produced the conflicting tendencies we find in his thought.

There is one more element we must add: ideology.

Al-Kindī was attached to the basic doctrines of Islam in two ways. One was religious faith. The other was ideological.
