This second connection was closely tied to his class position.

First, he came from a noble family. His father had been governor of Kufa as a representative of the Abbasid caliph.

Second, al-Kindī himself was one of Baghdad’s wealthy men and owned a moderate amount of landed property.

Third, he had close ties to the Abbasid court, especially during the reigns of al-Maʾmūn and al-Muʿtaṣim.

All these factors naturally pushed him, at the ideological level, in a conservative direction.

But his scientific outlook and philosophical rationalism pushed the other way. They were bound to come into conflict with this conservative ideological position.

It was therefore natural that al-Kindī’s intellectual development should take a complicated and internally contradictory form.

We must also remember something we said earlier: al-Kindī expressed a broader social phenomenon. He was not an isolated individual case.

It was therefore natural that his philosophy should reflect the overlap between two historical stages.

The first was the stage of Muʿtazilite *kalām*, or philosophy still taking a theological form.

The second was the stage in which philosophy began to develop an independent identity of its own.

This intellectual overlap reflected another overlap at the level of society itself.

The first social stage was the period in which feudal relations of production were flourishing. During this period, the ideology of the caliphate was strong because the caliphal state itself was strong.

The second was the period in which those relations were beginning to decline, the caliphal state was growing weaker, and its ideology was weakening with it. At the same time, popular struggles were emerging and giving rise to new ideologies that already contained the beginnings of distinct class positions.

In this sense, al-Kindī as a philosophical phenomenon embodied both sides of this overlap: the social and the intellectual.

That is why his philosophy contains different tendencies mixed together in all their contradictions.

### 3

Al-Kindī mastered a wide range of knowledge and sciences. He also did original work in the natural sciences, mathematics, and medicine.

Even so, we must recognize that philosophy was the strongest force in shaping the intellectual identity for which he became famous. He deserved the title given to him by thinkers of his own time: “the philosopher of the Arabs.”

The originality of this philosophical tendency can be seen in the way he dealt with the major intellectual problems of his age.

The following examples show this clearly.

**(a) The problem of knowledge**

Al-Kindī was the first Arab thinker to place the question of knowledge within a framework wider than the theological one that had existed before him.

He opened Arab thought to philosophical knowledge in the broadest sense, and also to the study of nature in both theory and experiment, without making either one necessarily depend on the conflict between God and nature.

When he defined philosophy, he described it as “knowledge of things as they truly are, as far as human beings are able.”[^V4-181]

Here, the subject of philosophy becomes much broader than knowledge of “God” or knowledge of the relationship between “things” and God. It includes the realities of all things without restriction.

And it deals with them as things that exist in their own right, not merely as ways of reaching divine or “metaphysical” knowledge.

Al-Kindī therefore divided knowledge according to its subject matter.

Since he divided reality itself into what can be known through the senses, what can be known through reason, and what belongs to the “divine,” he divided knowledge in the same way.

There is sensory knowledge.

There is rational knowledge.

And there is “divine” knowledge, which he calls “the science of divinity,” dealing with God and religious law.

This division also became the basis for another division, this time according to the source of knowledge.

Al-Kindī believed that knowledge comes from two sources.

One is divine: knowledge that reaches us through prophets and messengers.

The other is human: the knowledge people gain by learning the true nature of things as far as they are able.

But al-Kindī did not limit “divine knowledge” to prophets and messengers.

He believed that prophetic knowledge was one path to divine knowledge, but that philosophy was another.

Following Aristotle, he therefore called the branch of philosophy that investigates the “first cause” of existence “first philosophy,” because it is “the noblest philosophy and the highest in rank.”[^V4-182]

From these divisions, we can conclude that al-Kindī did not believe there was any fixed boundary beyond which the human mind could not go in its search for knowledge.

Al-Kindī was also the first to place the “science of divinity,” meaning metaphysics, on a philosophical basis rather than the theological basis established earlier by *kalām*.

The philosophical shift begins when he develops a theory of causes.

He sets out a connected chain of causes, linked through relations of acting and being acted upon, beginning with the first cause and descending through lower causes until they appear in the events of nature and society.

**(b) His view of the history of philosophy**

In al-Kindī’s time, this question had become part of a wider social and ideological conflict because of the controversy over whether the Qurʾān was created.

This dispute brought the Muʿtazilites into direct conflict with their conservative traditionalist opponents.

But the disagreement soon spread to another issue: how Muslims should view the wider heritage of world philosophy.

The Muʿtazilites defended this heritage and wanted to make use of it in developing *kalām*.

The traditionalists, by contrast, rejected philosophy and the entire philosophical heritage. They argued that the entry of philosophy into Arab-Islamic thought had produced the idea that the Qurʾān was created—an idea they regarded as heretical—or, more broadly, that philosophy was responsible for the appearance of various heretical ideas within Islam.

Al-Kindī took an original philosophical position on this question, based on a clear method.

His approach had two sides.

The first concerned the world’s philosophical heritage, especially the Greek heritage. He argued that it should be seen as a common human achievement, built over the centuries by thousands of minds belonging to many different peoples and nations.

The second concerned representatives of religious institutions who often placed themselves in the position of defending the ideology of the dominant social group.

Al-Kindī saw these people as enemies of philosophy for no reason other than that they used religion to defend their material privileges and their social position.

For such an early period of the Middle Ages, this was an extremely advanced position.

An Arab thinker was treating humanity’s intellectual heritage in this broad and scientific way. At the same time, he was openly exposing the motives of those who opposed philosophy and freedom of thought, and taking such an explicit stand in defense of both.

**(c) The relationship between religion and philosophy**

This was one of the most important problems facing both society and philosophy in al-Kindī’s time.

It took the new form of the problem of the relationship between faith and reason.

In reality, it also reflected the ideological conflict that was becoming more intense at the time.

Al-Kindī’s position on this question was hesitant and unresolved.

That uncertainty also reflected the uncertainty in his ideological position.

As we have seen, he showed real philosophical originality and defended philosophy openly and courageously against its conservative enemies.

But he still remained emotionally attached to basic theological assumptions.

That emotional attachment then took on a philosophical form, as he tried to justify the hold those assumptions still had over him.

We should not explain this simply in terms of al-Kindī’s personal character.

We also need to look at the objective historical reality in which he lived.

His age had a double character.

On one side were the forces pushing philosophy toward independence.

On the other were the forces still tying it to Islamic theology, especially Muʿtazilite *kalām*.

In the same way, this was a period in which the ideology of the Abbasid caliphate remained powerful even as the conditions causing it to weaken and fracture were already developing.

Al-Kindī became the concrete expression of both forms of this overlap and contradiction.

For all these reasons, he was unable to solve the problem of the relationship between religion and philosophy, despite his repeated attempts throughout his philosophical writings.

**(d) The two truths**

The overlapping and contradictory character of al-Kindī’s age also produced, for the first time in Islamic thought, the problem of two kinds of truth.

From the moment Arab philosophy gained a degree of independence, it found itself moving between religious truth and philosophical truth.

It had to recognize both, while also trying to deny that they were in conflict.

This marked the boundary between the age of Muʿtazilite *kalām* and the age of an independent Arab philosophy.

For this reason, we argue that Arab philosophy’s attempt, beginning with al-Kindī, to remove the conflict between these two truths was mainly the result of an objective historical situation.

Only to a limited extent was it the result of a conscious decision by individual philosophers.

This also reveals the mistake in a view that has remained widespread among historians of Arab philosophy for generations: the claim that Arab philosophy is fundamentally “reconciliatory,” meaning that its defining purpose was to reconcile religion and philosophy.

In reality, this was not a deliberate intellectual exercise in reconciliation.

It was an objective historical process that unfolded within the ideological struggles of the time, especially as repeated revolts broke out across different parts of the Abbasid caliphate against forms of social and national oppression.

### 4

In studying al-Kindī’s philosophy, we tried to use it as a starting point for understanding the development of Arab philosophy within its own historical setting.

At the same time, we naturally placed it within the wider historical development of philosophical thought up to al-Kindī’s age.

This required us to examine the internal relationships within his philosophy.

First, we looked at its connection with Muʿtazilite thought.

Second, we looked at its connection with Greek philosophy, both Athenian and Alexandrian.

Third, we tried to determine how far al-Kindī’s philosophy became independent of both.

But it was impossible to carry out this task completely.

Most of the original sources for the different sides of al-Kindī’s philosophy were still unknown when these lines were written.

The secondary works dealing with his philosophy and ideas are also, in general, full of contradictions and uncertainty.

We therefore had to rely on two main sources in order to form at least an approximate picture of his thought.

The first was the forty-one published treatises from his philosophical works.[^V4-183]

The second was the titles of his writings that had not yet been published, as recorded in the complete catalogues of his works.[^V4-184]

These titles often give us some idea of the subjects and problems he discussed. Together with his published treatises, they also provide clues that help us build a broader picture of his thought.

The titles of some of al-Kindī’s unpublished works point to subjects that belong to *kalām*.

Some also contain ideas associated with Muʿtazilite *kalām* rather than philosophy.

These ideas help us identify al-Kindī’s position on some of the major questions of his time.

From this we can conclude that although al-Kindī did free philosophy from the control of *kalām*, it emerged carrying a heavy residue of theological ideas.

But this was not entirely negative.

Al-Kindī wanted to preserve certain theological assumptions, such as the belief that the universe was “created from nothing.”

Because of this, he could not simply follow Aristotle, Plato, or Neoplatonism without reservation.

The result was that he clearly disagreed with each of these philosophical traditions on important points.

He also disagreed, of course, with parts of Muʿtazilite doctrine.

All this means that al-Kindī opened a path of his own, both for himself and for the Arab philosophy of which he became the first major representative.

In broad terms, that path had an independent direction.

### 5

From here, we can turn to al-Kindī’s philosophy as it appears in his published treatises.

It begins, naturally, with the first great problem of ancient and medieval philosophy: the origin and fate of the world.

The starting point is al-Kindī’s answer to an old philosophical question.

Did our material world come into being, or has it existed eternally?

In other words, was it created or uncreated?

And if it was created, was it created from something that already existed, or from nothing?

#### Comparison with Aristotle

Al-Kindī’s answer is clear: the world came into being and was created from nothing.[^V4-185]

This answer marks the beginning of his fundamental disagreement with Aristotle.

Even where he disagrees with Aristotle, however, al-Kindī often uses Aristotelian concepts and terms, but gives them a different meaning.

For example, he uses the term “first mover” and the doctrine of the four “causes,” without accepting Aristotle’s own understanding of those terms.

The basic difference between al-Kindī and Aristotle here comes from the very different starting points from which each defines God.

Aristotle begins from a rationalist position and treats the question rationally.

For him, God is intellect, not the familiar God of religion. God is pure intellect.[^V4-186]

Al-Kindī, by contrast, begins directly from purely religious assumptions about God.

On this question, then, his position is more religious than philosophical.

The philosophical element lies mainly in the form of his arguments.

When al-Kindī confronted the question of God, both he and Arab philosophy more generally were facing a number of difficult and sometimes contradictory problems.

The first was the growing fracture in the ideology supporting the theocratic rule of the caliphate.

Alongside this came the growth of new ideologies among social groups opposed to that rule.

There was also a third problem: Arab philosophical thought had entered a new stage and needed a new form through which it could express the conditions of its own living development.

For both objective and personal reasons, al-Kindī was unable to resolve these problems in a decisive way that completely broke with theological thought.

#### Comparison with Plato

Just as al-Kindī differed from Aristotle in his understanding of the world and God, he also differed from Plato in the way he understood the relationship between God and the world.

Al-Kindī accepts only one eternal and truly existing being: God, who created everything from nothing.

Plato, by contrast, according to the theory of Forms, accepts the eternal and real existence of many Forms, corresponding to the many kinds, classes, and things found in the world of the senses.

The things we perceive are only shadows or images of these eternal Forms, which exist in the world of Ideas.

This means that Plato’s account of true existence differs fundamentally from al-Kindī’s in several ways.

1. Plato accepts many eternal realities, while al-Kindī accepts only one.

2. Plato does not recognize the world of the senses as possessing true existence in its own right. Al-Kindī does recognize this world as having a real and objective existence.

3. For Plato, nothing in the sensible world is created from nothing. The things and kinds found in this world are reflections of the corresponding Forms in the world of Ideas. They therefore arise from the Forms, not from nothing. For al-Kindī, everything in our world comes from nothing.

4. For Plato, there are many causes behind the existence of things in our world, because each thing has its own corresponding Form in the world of Ideas as its cause. For al-Kindī, everything in this world has one single cause: God.

5. For Plato, God as “Idea” is the underlying substance of the world. The world is therefore not outside God, and God is not separate from the world. In this way, Plato ends by giving the world a divine character and removing the duality between God and nature. For al-Kindī, by contrast, God created the world and therefore stands outside it and apart from it. There is a clear duality between God and the world.

#### Comparison with Neoplatonism

We also find important differences between al-Kindī’s view of God and the world and the Neoplatonic view.

Neoplatonism developed the theory of “emanation” as a way of explaining how the world arose from “the One,” meaning God.

According to this theory, the world came into being through a process in which existence flowed out from the One, much as sunlight radiates from the sun.

The sensible world appeared at the final stage of this process.

Because it was furthest from the original source, it was also regarded as the lowest and least worthy level of existence.

From the Neoplatonic theory of the world, we can identify several differences between it and al-Kindī’s position.

1. Al-Kindī says that the world was created from nothing. Neoplatonism says that it came from something that already existed, because it flowed out from an eternal being.

2. For al-Kindī, the world came into being and is not eternal. In Neoplatonism, the world is eternal. This is because emanation does not happen in time but eternally. It is also because emanation takes place necessarily: it belongs inseparably to its source. Since the One is eternal, the emanation connected with it is eternal as well. Finally, Neoplatonism treats matter as potentiality, meaning that it exists potentially, and potential existence is itself taken to imply eternity. The material world that exists in actuality therefore emerges from an eternal form of existence and must itself be eternal.

3. For al-Kindī, the world is separate from God, and God exists outside the world. Neoplatonism rejects this separation because it sees nature as having a divine foundation.

4. In Neoplatonism, the material world is inferior because it occupies the lowest level of emanation. Al-Kindī rejects this view because Islam treats spirit and body, or matter, as two independent essences of equal standing.

5. Although Neoplatonism insists on the absolute unity of the One, the source of emanation, it undermines that unity when it says that matter exists as potentiality. The result is that there are two eternal forms of existence: the One and potential matter, or *hylē*. This conflicts with al-Kindī’s understanding of divine unity, which is a basic principle of his thought.

6. The Neoplatonic theory of emanation treats the flow of existence from the One as a necessary process. It does not depend on will or choice. This is a fundamental difference from al-Kindī, who completely rejects the idea that the world could be created without God’s will and choice.

These comparisons reveal two opposing sides of al-Kindī’s philosophy: one positive and one negative.

The positive side is that his philosophy followed a path independent of the philosophies of the ancients.

The negative side is that it remained confined by theological assumptions and never fully broke free from them.

### 6

Although al-Kindī began with religious and theological assumptions when thinking about the relationship between God and the world, he was not completely trapped by those assumptions when he gave his ideas philosophical form.

He moved beyond ready-made theological formulas.

In his own way, he produced the first Arab philosophical formulation of an Islamic understanding of God and God’s relationship with the world.

In other words, he expressed this idea through the language and methods of philosophy.

He built it out of philosophical concepts, philosophical categories, and logical relationships.

This in itself was an important step in the early movement of Arab philosophy away from its theological origins.

Al-Kindī’s philosophical proof that the world was created from nothing followed logically from the way he used these philosophical categories.

His argument begins with something he treats as certain: body and motion cannot be separated.

He rejects the idea of a body without motion, or motion without a body.

His purpose is first to deny that body could have existed before motion and time—that is, before creation—and then to reach the following conclusion:

The existence of a body is inseparable from its motion and its time.

Since motion and time are finite, body must also be finite.

The body of “the whole,” meaning the world, therefore came into being without any earlier existence.

In other words, it came from nothing.

Al-Kindī gives another argument in which he also expresses his understanding of God and the world in philosophical terms.

Here again, he moves completely beyond the form of theological argument, even though the content remains theological.

### 7

We also examined al-Kindī’s position on one of the great questions of his age: the relationship between God’s will and human will.

In other words, how free are human beings in their own actions?

From the sources available to us among his books and other writings, we concluded that al-Kindī supported free choice rather than compulsion.

This conclusion shows that from the moment Arab philosophy began to free itself from its theological origins, it preserved the positive side of the Muʿtazilite intellectual heritage.

It would then carry that heritage forward within a philosophical framework that would gradually become freer than the one we find in al-Kindī, the first philosopher of the Arabs.

### 8

Before al-Kindī, the Muʿtazilites had tried to develop an Arab-Islamic theory of the soul.

But their attempt remained incomplete.

It never developed into a fully formed theory and remained caught between two tendencies: a sense-based approach and a spiritualist one.

Al-Kindī gave the problem a new form and moved it as far toward scientific investigation as the development of science at the time allowed.

He devoted close attention to defining the soul, studying its powers and conditions, and examining the ways it appears and acts in human behavior.

He also explored the relationship between the movements of the soul and different kinds of human activity.

In doing so, he widened the field of psychological inquiry to include subjects that earlier theologians had not clearly connected with the study of the soul, including dreams and the intellect.

The major change al-Kindī brought to the Arab-Islamic theory of the soul appears in two ways.

First, the sense-based tendency disappears completely from his understanding of the soul and is replaced by a purely philosophical approach.

Second, the dominant tendency in his theory is idealist. It separates the soul from the body.

But al-Kindī’s writings on the soul also contain a contradiction that unsettles the whole structure of the theory.

In some passages, he says that the soul is a spiritual substance separate from the body.

In others, however, he seems to treat the relationship between soul and body as the relationship between form and matter, following Aristotle’s understanding of both terms.

Taken to its conclusion, this would deny that the soul is a separate spiritual substance.

Instead, it would make the soul a material substance bound to the body.

We could find no way to explain this contradiction except by returning to the intellectual conflict that, in our view, al-Kindī himself was living through.

This was a struggle between two opposing tendencies.

One was philosophical. It brought him closer to the materialist tendencies in Aristotle’s theory of knowledge. This side of al-Kindī’s thought developed through his practical and theoretical work in the natural sciences.

The other was spiritualist. It drew him toward Plato’s world of Forms and the Neoplatonic world of emanation.

This second tendency came from his continuing connection with theological thought, which had been the original source of his intellectual formation.

### 9

Al-Kindī wrote a treatise on the intellect that was the first of its kind in Arab philosophy.

From the distinctive way he presents, explains, divides, and names the different forms of intellect, it does not seem exaggerated to say that he established the Aristotelian theory of intellect within Arab philosophy.

The central feature of his theory is his fourfold division of the intellect.

He begins by dividing the knowing side of the soul into three states.

The first is a state of complete potentiality: the intellect in potential.

The second is actual understanding.

But this second state itself has two forms.

The first is having acquired knowledge as an ability without actually using it.

The second is actively using that ability.

Al-Kindī calls this final state the fourth intellect and describes it as the “manifest intellect.”

There is also another kind of intellect that he describes as “the intellect that is always in actuality.”

This is the same intellect Aristotle describes as “the intellect by whose action all things come to be.”

But this intellect exists outside the human soul.

Aristotle, al-Kindī, and the later Peripatetic philosophers all regarded it as a separate substance.

Why did they feel the need to add this kind of intellect outside the world of the human soul?

They did so because they encountered a problem when they examined how human knowledge develops.

They found a missing link in the process that needed explanation: the movement of the soul from potential knowledge to actual knowledge.

What makes this transition happen?

In their view, there had to be an outside factor that was not itself acted upon, that always existed in actuality, and that was separate from the soul.

Aristotle tried to solve the problem through his two concepts of matter and form.

Al-Kindī used the same approach.

The basic idea is this.

In nature, there is matter for every kind of thing, existing in potential, and there is also a cause whose activity brings things into actuality.

In the same way, the soul contains one thing—the “intellect”—from which all things can become known, and another intellect whose activity brings all these things into actual knowledge within the soul.

This was the philosophical basis of their proposed solution.

But behind the problem itself lies what we believe is the more important question.

From Aristotle through al-Kindī and the philosophers who came after him, thinkers had noticed an unclear link in the dialectical movement of knowledge.

They were right to search for that missing link.

It was the link through which knowledge moves from pure possibility into actual knowledge.

The reason they could not find it, however, was that they failed to see the real connection between the laws of thought and the laws governing material reality outside the thinking subject.

Because they could not see this connection, they were unable to give a realistic account of the dialectical changes that take place as knowledge develops.

They therefore fell back on the traditional idealist explanation.

They assumed the existence of an “active cause” that intervenes in the process of knowledge from outside the soul and outside reality itself—in other words, from outside both the knowing subject and the object being known.

This is the source of the internal contradiction in al-Kindī’s theory of intellect, and in the Aristotelian theory from which it came.

The contradiction appears in the gap between two parts of the theory.

On one side, there is a realistic view of the three stages through which knowledge develops.

On the other, there is an idealist view of the decisive force that moves the human soul from potential knowledge to actual knowledge.

When we examine the different contradictions found in Aristotle, the Aristotelians, and al-Kindī, they can be traced back to one basic source.

They were unable to discover the bridge through which the dialectical relationship moves between rational knowledge and sensory knowledge, or between the general and the individual.

This problem is not limited to them.

It appears in many ancient and modern philosophical traditions, including metaphysical and idealist philosophies and some forms of materialism.

It also appears, in our view, throughout contemporary bourgeois philosophy, especially among the empiricists, positivists, and neo-positivists.

### 10

Throughout the works of al-Kindī that are available to us, we find many signs that he was moving toward at least some degree of independence from the older philosophical positions.

But we must remember the problem we have already mentioned several times: the internal struggle between his scientific and philosophical tendency on one side, and his spiritual and theological tendency on the other.

Al-Kindī nevertheless possessed the originality of a true philosopher.

That originality repeatedly drew him, despite his continuing theological commitments, toward positions that placed him at an almost decisive crossroads between the two tendencies—and sometimes between the two broader directions of idealism and materialism.

The conditions of Abbasid society in the third century AH, or the ninth century CE, were pushing al-Kindī to move beyond this crossroads.

But his theological commitments kept him there.

They prevented him from fully crossing it, despite the powerful drive toward rebellion and change within his philosophical thought.

In Chapter One, which we are summarizing here, we gave several examples that support this point.

We will briefly review them in the rest of this summary.

#### A — God’s effect on matter

Some of al-Kindī’s writings contain a new view that does not fit the dominant theology of his time.

In general terms, he argues that nature is a fourth cause for everything that moves or remains at rest, and that nature is what God makes the cause of all moving and resting things in relation to motion.

There are many passages of this kind in his philosophical treatises.

They clearly show a tendency to deny that God acts directly on matter and instead to attribute direct effects within matter to natural causes.

Al-Kindī tries to soften this position by saying that natural causes are active causes only “metaphorically.”

But the difference between metaphorical and literal language here is only a matter of terminology.

In practice, the causes al-Kindī describes really do act on their effects.

Their action is not merely metaphorical.

#### B — The law of causal relations

The philosophical basis for al-Kindī’s rejection of direct divine action on matter can be found, as his philosophical writings show, in his firm commitment to the law of causality.

He treats causal relations as a universal law and as a fixed basis underlying all the laws of nature.

He extends this principle beyond the earthly world to the celestial bodies.

His starting point is the idea that the actions of the soul are affected by the condition of the body, while the condition of the body is affected by the movements of the heavenly bodies.

These movements act on physical bodies and shape their different conditions according to how near or far they are from one heavenly body or another, whether that body is rising or falling, moving quickly or slowly, and so on.

Al-Kindī explains this with concrete examples involving the movements of the sun, the moon, and other bodies in the cosmos, which he calls the “higher bodies.”

After explaining in detail how these heavenly bodies materially affect bodies in the earthly world, he suddenly asks another question.

If they can affect earthly bodies in this way, what prevents their movements from also playing a causal role in the existence of something “finer” than bodies, so long as this finer thing belongs to both the natural world and the world of the soul?

Al-Kindī does not explain what he means by this “finer” thing.

We infer that in the ancient philosophical traditions, what is “finer” than the body is the spirit or the soul.

This was their way of distinguishing what modern scientific language calls “life.”

If this reading is correct, then in this passage al-Kindī is moving toward the view that the movements of the heavenly bodies are the cause of the existence of spirit, soul, or life.

He also appears to treat this “thing”—spirit, soul, or life—as something that belongs to the body and therefore depends on matter.

If our reading of al-Kindī’s text is correct, then we can credit him here with a position based on four ideas.

First, he explains the existence of life in matter through the movement of the heavens rather than through a hidden divine cause.

Second, he treats spirit and body—or life and matter—as forming a unity.

Third, he does not separate the movement of the heavenly bodies from the movement of matter in earthly bodies.

Fourth, he treats the law of causality as applying both to Earth and to the other bodies of the solar system, linking the laws of nature to the wider movement of the cosmos as a whole.

#### C — The world is potentially infinite

In his explicit statements, al-Kindī says that matter, motion, and time are finite.

He insists that they cannot be infinite.

But we notice that whenever he speaks of the impossibility of an infinite world, he adds the qualification “in actuality.”

From this we conclude that he does not reject infinity in an absolute sense.

He rejects only “actual infinity,” not “potential infinity.”

His writings support this conclusion.

Here he uses “in potential” in its Aristotelian sense.

The infinite can exist potentially, just as matter itself exists potentially.

Matter, motion, and time are therefore potentially infinite, just as matter has potential existence.

The comparison between the potential infinity of matter and the potential existence of matter leads to an important implication.

Al-Kindī is, in effect, recognizing that the material world had a kind of existence before its actual existence.

That would mean recognizing its eternity, at least implicitly.

#### D — The dialectic of unity and multiplicity

Before al-Kindī, the theologians had already discussed the relationship between unity and multiplicity.

But al-Kindī added something new to the discussion.

He began to grasp the dialectical character of the relationship between them.

He argued that we cannot imagine unity in things or numbers without multiplicity also being present.

From this he concluded that unity and multiplicity are both present in all sensible things and in what belongs to those things.

Wherever there is multiplicity, there is also unity, and vice versa.

Here again, al-Kindī remained committed to the law of causality in the material world.

The connection between unity and multiplicity is not the result of chance.

It comes from causal relations among things “from the beginning of their coming into being.”

We can therefore say that al-Kindī recognized the possibility that a thing and its opposite can exist together within a unity that brings them together in some way.

But when he moves beyond the world of sensible things, this dialectical view disappears.

The split between these two positions is another form of the duality that Arab-Islamic philosophy had to struggle with at this historical stage.

These difficulties arose because Arab philosophy grew up within theological thought during its embryonic stage. They also arose because the birth, growth, and development of philosophical thought were tied to the birth, growth, and development of Arab-Islamic society. In other words, the contradictions within philosophical thought, as we saw with al-Kindī, were tied to the contradictions within that society itself, in its social and ideological relations.

Human thought more generally faced similar difficulties in al-Kindī’s age. Their common source was a real historical limitation: at that time, people were not yet able to see the laws by which matter moves of itself. It was precisely this limitation that led ancient and medieval philosophers to look beyond our world for the source of the motion they had correctly recognized as inseparable from its existence.

# Chapter Two

## Questions of Existence and Essence in the Early Maturity of Arab-Islamic Philosophy

### 1 — The Question of Existence and Essence in al-Fārābī’s Philosophy

#### 1 — Al-Fārābī’s Historical Role

We begin this chapter with Abū Naṣr al-Fārābī (259–339 AH/872–950 CE)[^V4-187] because he was the first major representative of Arab philosophy as it entered the early stages of maturity, between the second half of the third and the first half of the fourth Islamic centuries—the ninth and tenth centuries CE.

Al-Fārābī gave the concepts of Arab philosophy a new form. His philosophical ideas were more independent of theology and more mature in their approach to knowledge. This led some modern researchers to exaggerate his place in history, even calling him the founder of Arab philosophy,[^V4-188] or saying that he was “the first to give Islamic philosophy its complete form and establish its foundations and principles.”[^V4-189]

Closer to the truth, perhaps, is the statement by one Orientalist that “there is nothing in the philosophy of Ibn Sīnā or Ibn Rushd whose seeds cannot already be found in al-Fārābī.”[^V4-190]

To call al-Fārābī the “founder,” however, is to treat Arab philosophy as if it had no history before him. And to say that he gave Islamic philosophy its “complete” form is just as unhistorical, besides being methodologically unsound. Even so, these descriptions do reflect something real: the major historical role al-Fārābī played in developing philosophical theory among the Arabs. He built on the rapid progress of the scientific movement, which at this time was becoming ever more deeply and widely rooted in the life of society.

Al-Fārābī was the first Arab philosopher[^V4-191] to place the Neoplatonic theory of emanation within the historical development of Arab philosophy, both as a field of knowledge[^V4-192] and as an ideology.[^V4-193] This meant that the theory itself had to take a form suited to that new historical setting.

That is where the importance of al-Fārābī’s role lies.

If we separate what he did from the particular historical setting in which he did it, then his achievement loses its significance. The theory of emanation had existed long before al-Fārābī. When it first appeared in the philosophy of Plotinus (205–270 CE), it gave philosophical and ideological expression to historical conditions very different from those surrounding al-Fārābī. Those later conditions shaped al-Fārābī’s own thought and gave his philosophy its particular form.

He would not have adopted the theory of emanation if Arab thinkers of his time had not seen it as offering solutions to theoretical and ideological problems they were facing, especially the problem of the relationship between God and the world.

This also helps explain why the *Book of Divinity* (*Theology*)[^V4-194] was attributed to Aristotle at the time. The attribution was wrong, but it gave al-Fārābī important support when he drew on the book in constructing his philosophical system. Aristotle’s name, we believe, encouraged him to do so because the “First Teacher” enjoyed such enormous authority among Arab thinkers.

We can imagine al-Fārābī discovering the Neoplatonic system of emanation in the *Theology of Aristotle* and crying out: “I’ve found it! I’ve found it!”

He then seized the theory with both hands, with Aristotle’s name giving him the confidence to follow it through to the conclusions he reached while trying to solve many of the problems that the development of science had placed before Arab philosophy.

This was not simply a matter of personal preference, detached from the historical setting we have described. A purely personal desire to transfer a philosophical theory from one age to another, and from one society to another, when the two differ in their social relations and in their level of scientific knowledge, would be an empty abstraction. Such a desire has no real place in the history of actual societies, and therefore no real place in the history of human thought either.

This is why the insistence of Arab philosophers, beginning with al-Fārābī, on discussing the system of emanation as though it came from Aristotle does not mean that they knew Aristotle only through a falsely attributed work. Their attachment was first and foremost to the idea itself. Aristotle’s name merely gave that attachment further support.

Arab thinkers had known Aristotle from an earlier stage in their contact with Greek philosophy. They knew his works on logic, nature, metaphysics, the soul, natural history, ethics, and politics. They also knew most of Plato’s works, as well as the works of other ancient Greek philosophers.[^V4-195]

Nearly a century before al-Fārābī, as we have already seen, al-Kindī had written a special treatise listing Aristotle’s books and giving a scholarly account of each one.[^V4-196]

But we do not even need this historical evidence to establish the point. Al-Fārābī himself shows us, throughout his writings, that he had read genuine works by Aristotle and Plato and relied on them when presenting their ideas.

His book *The Reconciliation of the Views of the Two Sages*—Aristotle and Plato—provides especially clear evidence that he knew Aristotle’s genuine views from reliable sources, alongside the views contained in the work wrongly attributed to Aristotle at the time, the *Theology*.

This evidence shows that al-Fārābī’s attempt to “reconcile” Aristotle and Plato did not simply result from his being misled by a falsely attributed Aristotelian text.[^V4-197] He clearly knew Aristotle’s other views. He would not have relied on this particular book unless something was pushing him to choose the imagined Aristotle over the Aristotle he knew with certainty.

Nor do we think this attempt at reconciliation can simply be explained by saying that al-Fārābī “did not understand historical criticism, for otherwise he could have dealt more truthfully with the contradiction between the views found in the *Theology* and Aristotle’s views in the books he actually wrote.”[^V4-198]

Al-Fārābī did notice the contradiction. He actually tried to deal with it.

Whether his solution was close to the truth or far from it is another matter, and a relative one.

The real question is this: **What counted as “truth” for al-Fārābī when he tried to deal with that contradiction?**

That is where we must begin if we want to explain why he held so firmly to one side of what he took to be Aristotle’s thought rather than to the opposing side.

It may well be true that al-Fārābī did not know historical criticism. But it seems to us that even if he had known it, his basic position here would have remained the same. He might simply have handled Aristotle’s apparent contradiction in a more convincing way.

#### 2 — The Unity of Truth

We must therefore look for the “truth” that al-Fārābī felt compelled to defend despite all the difficulties and contradictions he faced, and despite all the strained arguments he had to make in order to place Plato and Aristotle on the same ground.

Was the “truth” behind all this ultimately fruitless effort simply that al-Fārābī “believed in the unity of philosophy and affirmed the unity of reason”?[^V4-199]

We do not think so.

Al-Fārābī defines philosophy as knowledge of existing things insofar as they exist. He also describes Plato and Aristotle as the two thinkers who created philosophy and established its first foundations and principles. He then insists that philosophy’s definition must correspond to its truth.[^V4-200]

What we can conclude from this is that al-Fārābī believed, at the deepest level, in the unity of **philosophical truth itself**. He believed that truth is one because it is objective truth: truth about things as they actually exist.

The “unity of philosophy” and the “unity of reason” follow from this. They are either logical results of the unity of truth itself, or they follow from al-Fārābī’s confidence in human reason in general, and especially in the reason of those who first created philosophy and established its foundations.

He believed that the mind is capable of making its understanding correspond to the thing it is trying to understand—that is, to philosophical truth, “the truth of existing things insofar as they exist.”

In modern terms, we might describe this as a unity between the knowing subject and the object being known. Existing things are the object.

This brings us directly to the question of existence and essence.

From what we have seen so far, we can conclude that **existence comes first** in al-Fārābī’s theory of knowledge. Existence is an objective reality that comes before our awareness of it. It is also what our awareness is directed toward, and the object of knowledge comes before the knowledge of it.

For al-Fārābī, our understanding must correspond to its object, not the other way around.[^V4-201]

The unity of “philosophical truth,” then, is the starting point in al-Fārābī’s philosophy—not the “unity of philosophy” or the “unity of reason.” The difference is the difference between a foundation and something built on that foundation.

But the claim that the “unity of philosophy” necessarily follows from the unity of truth is not realistic. It would mean that truth and our understanding of truth must always match perfectly. Everything we came to know would then become absolute truth rather than relative truth.

That is not realistic.

So why did al-Fārābī insist that reason must correspond to truth? Why did he adopt this position?

Here the ideological side of the question comes into view.

At its core, the idea that there is one “philosophical truth” challenged the theoretical foundation of the ruling social order—the Abbasid caliphate—because that order restricted truth to a single form: religious truth.

Al-Fārābī was now bringing forward another kind of truth, one disclosed by philosophy, where previously the ideology of the state had rested on only one.

Does this mean that Arab philosophy—and here we are speaking specifically of al-Fārābī’s philosophy—placed itself between two separate truths, a “double truth,” when it also tried to reconcile philosophy with religious law?

After all, al-Fārābī devoted almost as much effort to reconciling philosophy and religious law as he did to reconciling Plato and Aristotle in order to prove the unity of philosophical truth.

Did this place him in a contradiction?

Apparently not—or at least the aim of reconciling philosophy and religious law was not to abandon the unity of philosophical truth and accept two separate truths.

Quite the opposite.

This effort was another way in which Arab philosophy, with al-Fārābī leading the way, tried to establish the unity of philosophical truth and nothing more. The deeper, unspoken aim in showing that religious truth and philosophical truth did not conflict was to **bring religious truth under philosophical truth**.

We can see this clearly at several points in al-Fārābī’s own philosophy, and later in the philosophical systems that came after him.

In al-Fārābī, it appears in the following areas.

**First: his rejection of the religious doctrine that the world was created from nothing.**

This necessarily implies that the world is eternal. It therefore also rejects the other religious doctrine that the world came into existence after previously not existing.

The evidence for both conclusions can be found in al-Fārābī’s explanation of how the world proceeds from the First—God—through emanation. He writes:

> “The First is that from which all else exists. Once the First has the existence proper to it, all other beings whose existence does not depend on human will or choice must necessarily proceed from it. What proceeds from it does so through an overflow of its existence into the existence of something else (...) Its own existence is followed, by the nature of its essence, by the existence of something other than itself. Therefore the existence by which existence flows from it to another belongs to its very essence. The existence by which it exists in itself is the very same existence by which another thing receives existence from it...”[^V4-202]

These words clearly mean that the existence of the world follows necessarily from God’s own existence and is inseparable from it.

The world, then, is not created, because there was never a time when it was preceded by nothingness. Nor did it begin to exist at some point in time. It is eternal because the source from which it emanates is eternal.

All these conclusions are in complete conflict with the position of religious law.[^V4-203]

**Second: al-Fārābī rejects revelation in its theological sense.**

That is, he rejects the idea that revelation is something wholly supernatural, cut off from the normal laws by which human consciousness acquires knowledge.

Al-Fārābī gives a natural explanation for the process through which some people can see unseen worlds, predict “divine things,” and receive “present and future particulars from the Active Intellect.”

He connects this process to natural powers that exist in every human being.

For al-Fārābī, revelation begins in the human imaginative faculty.

When that faculty is exceptionally strong and fully developed, the sensory impressions coming from outside do not completely dominate or occupy it. Nor do they use up the entire rational faculty. The imagination therefore still has a large reserve of power with which it can carry out its own activity.

In this condition, the imagination can use the images it has received through the senses, together with what it receives from the Active Intellect, to form new images resembling visible things. These images are then impressed on the sensory faculty.

When these imagined forms reach what al-Fārābī calls the common sensory faculty, they affect the power of sight. The images are then projected from the eye into the illuminated air extending outward from the eye. From there, they are impressed once again on the visual faculty of the eye, and then return to the common sensory faculty and the imagination.

The result is a new kind of vision in which information from visible things is mixed with what comes from the Active Intellect.

If this vision reaches the highest degree of beauty and perfection, al-Fārābī says, then the person who sees it “will say that God possesses magnificent and wondrous greatness, and will see astonishing things unlike anything that can exist among ordinary beings.”

For al-Fārābī, this whole “process,” in which the imagination plays the central role with the help of things perceived by the senses, is “the highest level that the imaginative faculty can reach, and the highest level a human being can attain through the power of imagination.”

Nor is this level reserved for one individual or one group. Every human being can in principle reach it, although different people reach different degrees of it. Changes in a person’s physical constitution can alter where that person stands on this scale. Someone at a lower level can rise to a higher one, and the reverse can also happen.[^V4-204]

On this account, then, the prophet receives prophetic visions through the imagination.

The imagination connects the prophet with the Active Intellect, which in turn links the human intellect to the higher world.

But this process never breaks completely away from sensory experience. As we have seen, things received through the senses play a central part in the connection.

We have also seen that any human being can undergo this process under the right conditions, and that these conditions are not completely closed to anyone.

This is therefore a clear position that places the prophet among other human beings rather than giving him a unique kind of knowledge unavailable to everyone else.

The prophet’s distinction lies in the kind of knowledge he receives through the imagination, where information from the Active Intellect combines with knowledge received through the senses.

Prophetic knowledge therefore stands above ordinary sensory knowledge.

But al-Fārābī also recognizes a kind of knowledge higher than both sensory knowledge and the knowledge of the imagination: the knowledge of the **rational faculty**.

This is the philosopher’s knowledge.

In other words, al-Fārābī places the philosopher above the prophet in knowledge and, as we shall see, even in human perfection.

What, then, is this knowledge of the rational faculty?

Al-Fārābī divides our knowledge of things into three kinds:

1. knowledge gained through the rational faculty;
2. knowledge gained through the imagination;
3. knowledge gained through the senses.

He writes:

> “When a person seeks knowledge of something that is naturally grasped by the rational faculty, the act through which it is attained (...) belongs to another power within the rational faculty: the power of thought. Through this come thinking, reflection, contemplation, and inference.”[^V4-205]

Knowledge gained through the rational faculty, which is the source of this kind of knowledge, is “a form in relation to the imaginative faculty and is not matter for any other faculties. It is the form of every form that comes before it.”[^V4-206]

This means that the rational faculty stands above the imaginative faculty. Knowledge gained through reason therefore stands above knowledge gained through imagination.

The logical conclusion is that the philosopher’s knowledge stands above the prophet’s.

There is another point as well.

Al-Fārābī places our knowledge of the “first intelligibles”—what Aristotle calls the first principles of demonstration—within the rational faculty.[^V4-207]

Al-Fārābī also says that receiving these first intelligibles is the first stage of human perfection, and that they are given to us so that we can use them to reach our own highest and most proper perfection.[^V4-208]

And if reaching our highest perfection is happiness,[^V4-209] then happiness itself ultimately rests on rational knowledge.

The conclusion is clear.

For al-Fārābī, true human perfection and happiness depend on the knowledge provided by the rational faculty: the philosopher’s knowledge, philosophy.

They do not depend on the other kind of knowledge provided through the imagination: the prophet’s knowledge, religious law.

So what can “reconciling” religious law and philosophy mean after this?

In the end, it means bringing religious law under philosophy.

Yet elsewhere in the same book, *The Opinions of the People of the Virtuous City*,[^V4-210] al-Fārābī seems to place the prophet and the philosopher on the same level.

Here he describes the levels of the intellect in a way ultimately based on Aristotle.

His scheme is as follows.

Every human being begins with an intellect in potential: the rational faculty.

Here al-Fārābī calls it “the natural disposition that is ready and able to become an intellect in actuality.” By this he means the inborn ability to receive knowledge.

Everyone shares this “natural disposition.”

When a person comes to grasp all the intelligibles,[^V4-211] the receptive intellect is completed. It becomes intellect in actuality, and it also becomes intelligible in actuality—in the sense that it can know itself.

This is what al-Fārābī calls the **acquired intellect**.

Because the acquired intellect is more abstracted from matter, it is “more complete and more separate from matter.” It therefore stands above the receptive intellect and comes close to the Active Intellect.[^V4-212]

We therefore have four levels:

1. the rational faculty, or intellect in potential;
2. the receptive intellect, or intellect in actuality;
3. the acquired intellect;
4. connection with the Active Intellect.

Using the categories of matter and form, al-Fārābī describes the relationship between these levels in the following way:

> “If we treat the natural disposition—the rational faculty—as the matter of the receptive intellect once it has become intellect in actuality, and the receptive intellect as the matter of the acquired intellect, and the acquired intellect as the matter of the Active Intellect, and then take the whole series as one, this is the human being in whom the Active Intellect has come to dwell.”

A person reaches the Active Intellect in this way through both parts of the rational faculty: the theoretical and the practical.[^V4-213]

According to what we have just seen, then, the person in whom the Active Intellect “dwells” is someone who reaches this level through the successive stages of the intellect.

That description applies to the philosopher—the sage.

But there is another path to the same level: the imaginative faculty.

What flows from God passes to the Active Intellect. The Active Intellect then passes it to the receptive intellect through the acquired intellect, and from there to the imaginative faculty.

So the “human being in whom the Active Intellect dwells” can become “a wise philosopher, possessing complete intellectual understanding” through what flows from the Active Intellect into the receptive intellect by way of the acquired intellect.

The same person can also become “a prophet, warning of what will happen and reporting present particular events” through what the same Active Intellect passes “into his imaginative faculty.”

The person who becomes a wise philosopher through the acquired intellect, or a prophet through the imagination, has reached the highest level of humanity and the highest degree of happiness. His soul has become fully perfected and united with the Active Intellect in the way just described.

This, al-Fārābī says, is the first qualification required of the ruler of the virtuous city.[^V4-214]

Here, then, philosopher and prophet appear equal.

Both reach the same level of perfection. Both receive higher knowledge and revelation from the Active Intellect. And both can satisfy the first requirement for leadership of the virtuous city.

But the principle that places the philosopher above the prophet is still present.

The philosopher still reaches the Active Intellect primarily through the rational faculty, while the prophet reaches it through the imaginative faculty.

And the difference between the rational and imaginative faculties has not disappeared. Nothing in this later passage removes it. The rational faculty still stands above the imagination.

The logical result is that the prophet still stands below the philosopher-sage.

For al-Fārābī, rational knowledge remains higher than knowledge received through the imagination.

His position on religious law and revelation therefore remains at odds with the theological position, despite his outward attempt to hide the deeper philosophical conclusion.

**Third: al-Fārābī’s position on God’s knowledge.**

Here too, he rejects another basic teaching of religious law: that God’s knowledge includes everything in the world, both particulars and universals.[^V4-215]

When al-Fārābī discusses the unity of God’s essence and attributes, agreeing here with the Muʿtazilites,[^V4-216] he says that God is intellect in actuality by virtue of his own essence, and that he is also intelligible in actuality. In other words, God knows himself. His own essence is what he knows, and he is therefore also the knower in actuality.

Al-Fārābī writes:

> “He does not need another being outside himself in order to be intellect in actuality and knower in actuality. He becomes intellect and knower by knowing himself (...) He is intellect, intelligible, and knower. All three are one essence and one indivisible substance (...) The same is true of his being knowing. He does not need another being outside himself whose knowledge would give him some perfection (...) His own essence is sufficient for him to know and to be known. His knowledge of himself is nothing other than his substance. He is the one who knows, the thing known, and knowledge itself. He is one essence and one substance.”[^V4-217]

This is a clear and direct statement that God’s knowledge is confined to God himself and does not extend to anything outside the divine essence.

Otherwise, God would depend on the thing he knows in order to complete his own perfection.

But God depends on nothing. He is complete in himself.

To need something outside himself as an object of knowledge would imply some lack in his perfection, while God is by definition free of every lack.

Al-Fārābī’s understanding of divine wisdom points in the same direction. Because God is also wise, divine wisdom likewise rules out the idea that God’s knowledge extends to everything outside the divine essence.

Al-Fārābī writes:

> “Wisdom is to understand the best of things with the best kind of knowledge.”[^V4-218]

What, then, is the best kind of knowledge?

For al-Fārābī, it is God’s knowledge of himself:

> “When he understands and knows himself, he knows the best of things. And the best knowledge is permanent knowledge that can never disappear. That is his knowledge of himself.”[^V4-218]

All of this means that al-Fārābī sees the claim that God knows everything in the world as a violation of the unity of the divine essence and substance, and as a breach of the principle that God must remain absolutely free of limitation or deficiency.

**Fourth: al-Fārābī’s position on divine providence.**

Providence is another basic theological doctrine.

For Muslim theologians, divine providence means that “all existence—its origin, transformation, continuation, and development—goes back to the eternal and everlasting divine will.”[^V4-219]

Divine will creates the universe and brings it into existence after it was nothing, and divine will can destroy it so that it becomes nothing once again.[^V4-220]

Muslim theologians also used this “principle of providence” to prove that God must send prophets to humanity.

What does al-Fārābī say?

Here his position resembles his position on creation from nothing and the eternity of the world.

There is again a lack of consistency between what follows clearly, though sometimes indirectly, from the structure of his philosophical system of emanation and what he says more directly when discussing the views of others.

The system of emanation on which his philosophy is built requires that each of the ten intellects proceeding from the First Being governs its own part of the cosmic order.

Each intellect, insofar as it understands the First Being, necessarily gives rise to the intellect that follows it. And insofar as it exists in its own particular essence, it gives rise to the heavenly sphere that belongs to its own level in the system of emanation.

Following this pattern, two things proceed from the ninth intellect: the Active Intellect, which is the tenth intellect, and the sphere of the Moon.

The Active Intellect then governs the world below the Moon—the world of earthly bodies, or the natural world.[^V4-221] It also performs a role in human knowledge.[^V4-222]

That is what the theory of emanation itself requires.

We can add to this a number of passages where al-Fārābī clearly moves away from the Islamic doctrine of divine providence.

For example, when he discusses the imperfection found in natural beings, he says that these beings begin in a state of imperfection and then gradually rise until each kind reaches the greatest perfection possible in its essence and in its other qualities.

He then says:

> “This condition belongs to the very nature of this kind of being and is not introduced into it by anything else foreign to it.”[^V4-223]

When discussing the causes from which the first form of prime matter arises in natural beings, al-Fārābī says that the shared nature of the heavenly bodies[^V4-224] necessarily gives rise to the prime matter shared by everything beneath them.[^V4-225]

He continues:

> “From the differences between their substances follows the existence of many bodies whose substances differ. From the opposition between their relations and connections follows the existence of opposing forms. And from the changing and succession of these opposing relations follows the changing and succession of opposing forms in prime matter, and so on...”

This passage and the one before it clearly say that the “form and matter” of the first natural beings come from the “shared nature” of the heavenly bodies, not from the direct planning and creation of the First Being.

They also say that natural beings move from their initial imperfection toward greater perfection—from lower to higher, stage by stage—through a natural law within themselves, “without this being introduced into them by anything else foreign to them.”

Divine providence therefore has no direct role in this process.

Other passages in al-Fārābī are equally clear that matter continues to exist, renews itself, changes, extends, and develops not because of “divine will,” but because of the dialectical law governing the inner relationship between “matter and form,” which for him are “the basis of natural things.”[^V4-226]

#### 3 — The World of Nature

After tracing the existence of natural things back to properties inherent in the nature of the heavenly bodies, al-Fārābī begins to develop his theory of the natural world.

Here we find some remarkably advanced examples of the way developments in the natural sciences were reflected in his philosophy.

At times these philosophical conclusions seem almost to run ahead of the scientific achievements that gave rise to them, even though those achievements remained their main source.

We can see this in the way al-Fārābī describes the relationship between “matter and form.”

For him, this relationship operates internally, without depending on any outside force.[^V4-227]

It appears, first, as the inner forces that preserve the existence of matter.

Second, these same forces produce transformations from one particular form of matter into its opposite while preserving the common basis shared by both.

Third, they ensure the continued survival of the species and prevent it from disappearing, even though its individual members pass away through transformation, reproduction, and becoming.

Perhaps we can translate this into the language of modern dialectics by saying that al-Fārābī is describing the dialectical inner movement of natural things.

He writes that natural things, once they exist, “by their nature seek to remain and endure.”

Every natural being is made up of matter and form. Forms have opposites, and matter is capable of receiving both a particular form and its opposite.

Every natural being therefore has, as al-Fārābī puts it, two possible claims.

The first comes from its form: the claim to remain in the particular existence it already has.

The second comes from its matter: the claim to take on another existence opposed to the first.

But these two claims cannot both be fulfilled at the same time. A thing cannot both remain exactly what it is and become its opposite at once.

So, al-Fārābī says, the two claims are fulfilled in succession:

> “It exists and remains for a time, with its existence preserved. Then it is replaced and its opposite comes into existence. That opposite then remains for a time, after which it too is replaced and its opposite comes into existence... and so on forever.”[^V4-228]

If we set aside the idea of “matter and form” here—an idea Aristotelian in origin and idealist in character, because it separates the substance of matter from its form—and focus instead on the core of al-Fārābī’s theoretical view, we find a materialist position that was remarkably advanced for its time.

His materialism here also has an unusual precision and depth. He not only sees, to some extent, movement taking place within matter itself. He also begins to grasp how opposites struggle within a single unity,[^V4-229] and how matter persists at the level of the species even while its individual forms are constantly coming into being and changing.[^V4-229] The old dies and the new takes its place, “and so on forever” (p. 63).

In this sense, al-Fārābī anticipated the idea that matter cannot be destroyed. His view was still wrapped in idealism, of course, and he had no scientific knowledge of the basis on which such a theory could rest. But we can still see a kind of tentative glimpse of that basis.

In the passages we are discussing, he suggests that every existing thing contains a tendency toward another form of existence represented by its opposite. He then suggests that this tendency becomes real through the actual existence of that opposite, and that this process continues indefinitely. The species remains, while individual things arise and disappear as the process continues.

Of course, we cannot claim that al-Fārābī was describing the law of the negation of the negation in either its Hegelian or its Marxist sense. In those later theories, everything in the world contains its own opposite—that is, something that negates it. Nature as a whole, as a species, is infinite or eternal, while its individual and particular parts are finite.

But why should we not say that many truths which later came to be treated as virtually absolute—thanks to the accumulated knowledge and scientific discoveries of successive ages—had once been only relative truths when measured against the scientific knowledge available in earlier periods? Their degree of truth gradually rose as knowledge advanced, until they were finally revealed as absolute truths. And why should we not see al-Fārābī’s conception as one historical stage in the development of such a truth?

Overall, then, al-Fārābī’s version of the theory of emanation, together with the other passages we have discussed, gives us a philosophical explanation of nature that contains a number of suggestive insights. In these, the question of “essence” begins to move away from its theological meaning and toward a view strongly marked by materialist tendencies.

This conclusion is also supported by something we have already seen: al-Fārābī grounds these materialist tendencies in the objective reality of the material world and in its independent existence.

#### 4 — Social Philosophy and Ideology

That is what we found in the parts of al-Fārābī’s philosophy dealing with nature and metaphysics. When we turn to his social philosophy, we find the ideological basis of the positions we identified there.

The first thing that stands out is the place he gives to human will and choice within society.

It is true that he begins his account of an integrated social structure by comparing cooperation among the members of a city—or society—to the natural relationship among the parts of “a complete and healthy body.” The organs of the body cooperate to maintain and preserve the life of the animal. They also differ in their “natural constitution and powers.” One organ stands above the others as the chief organ, “the heart.” Other organs rank close to it. Some carry out actions according to the purposes of organs directly below the chief organ, while still others act according to the purposes of those at a lower rank, and so on until we reach organs that “serve but never rule.”

Al-Fārābī does begin with this comparison in considerable detail, and as an explanation of society it is mechanical and ultimately inadequate.

But he immediately corrects the weakness in the analogy. He points to basic differences between human relationships within society and the natural relationships among the organs of a body. In other words, he distinguishes social organization from the biological organization of a living creature.

The first difference he identifies is **will and choice**.

“The organs of the body,” he says, “are natural, and the dispositions they possess are natural powers. The parts of the city”—by which he means the people who make up society—“are also natural, but the dispositions and acquired capacities through which they perform their actions for the city are not natural. They are voluntary. [...] The powers that exist naturally in the organs of the body”—meaning that these powers act by nature, without conscious choice—“have their counterparts in the parts of the city in the form of voluntary capacities and dispositions.”[^V4-230]

He then makes a second distinction. The standards by which people differ in social rank are not the same as the standards by which the organs of a living body differ from one another.

Here al-Fārābī offers an important insight.

He accepts that the members of society differ in their natural abilities, so that “one person is naturally suited to one thing rather than another.” But he does not think these natural qualities alone determine a person’s social position or function. What matters, in his view, are “the voluntary capacities they acquire”[^V4-231]—that is, capacities acquired by people who begin with different natural abilities.

What he says suggests that these voluntary capacities depend on the knowledge, experience and practical skills people acquire through their activity in society. This interpretation is supported by the fact that al-Fārābī eventually equates such capacities with “crafts and similar things.”[^V4-232]

In other words, a person’s place in society is shaped by how far that person is able, under the conditions of a particular social system, to develop their natural abilities and turn them into acquired capacities of one kind or another.

If this reading of al-Fārābī’s social philosophy in *The Virtuous City* is correct, it leads to an important conclusion.

No social position is imposed on a person or group forever by supernatural forces, as if those forces had assigned each individual a fixed “share” of intellectual ability and social rank that could never change. Rather, as we understand al-Fārābī’s texts, human beings themselves play a decisive part through the capacities they acquire by choice and practice.

Of course, we should not expect al-Fārābī to tell us exactly who this “human being” is that makes such decisions. Does he mean the individual person acting through personal will? Or is it really the social class that decides—the class that controls the means of production and political power in a class society?[^V4-233]

We cannot expect al-Fārābī to make this distinction. Social consciousness had not yet reached that point, even in its most developed philosophical form.

Even so, when al-Fārābī discusses acquired voluntary capacities, links them to “crafts and similar things,” and gives them greater importance than purely inborn qualities, he is approaching the question from a social position. That position is indirect and probably unconscious, but it still sheds light on his own place within society.

We need to remember that al-Fārābī developed his philosophy in a society where feudal relations of production were dominant. We must also remember that the ruling political and ideological representatives of that feudal order gave the idea of “divine providence,” with its element of predestination, a class meaning of their own.

According to this interpretation, people’s natural abilities were ranked according to their position in the feudal class system. “Divine providence” had supposedly given the highest abilities to the upper class and the lowest abilities to the lower classes. Whatever divine providence had decided was final and unchangeable.

In reality, of course, it was the upper class that decided these things. It kept the means of knowledge away from the lower classes and limited them to the kinds of acquired skills considered “appropriate” to their position. At the same time, it gave itself—and those who helped preserve its position at the top of the feudal hierarchy—the opportunities needed to develop its own acquired capacities. It then blamed the supposed deficiencies of ordinary people on “divine providence.”

We need to keep this historical and social reality in mind for two reasons.

First, the idea of divine providence, interpreted in this way, became a religious expression of the ideology of the ruling system.

Second, al-Fārābī’s position stands in opposition to this interpretation. His position is therefore ideological as well. He is challenging another foundation of the ideology of the theocratic ruling order. In doing so, he rejects its absolute and metaphysical—or supernatural—way of assigning people fixed capacities on the basis of their rank in the feudal class system.

We can understand the influence of that feudal class system on al-Fārābī’s thinking more clearly if we also remember that he regarded the village, or countryside, as serving the city.[^V4-234]

Taken together, the evidence allows us to conclude that his outlook expresses the ideology of the social groups that were growing within the cities: craftsmen, middle-ranking merchants, and workers in the newer kinds of social services that appeared as urban life flourished during the Abbasid period.

Compared with the generally conservative and reactionary direction of feudal relations during the period when the Abbasid Empire was breaking apart and the central power of the caliphate was weakening, these urban groups represented new forces with a revolutionary tendency.

As they rose, they pushed toward the surface of society and tried to influence the events and conflicts around them. They had their own *futuwwa* associations, which had a guild-like character, and they took part in militant and sometimes violent struggles.[^V4-235] Meanwhile, the ruling class encouraged contempt for these groups and for the kind of productive work they performed.

But when al-Fārābī lays out the “social map of the Virtuous City,” our first impression is that it does not differ very much from the hierarchical structure of Arab-Islamic society in his own time.

He carries into the society of his ideal city the same pattern he had used to describe the universe through the theory of emanation.

Just as the First Existent—God—is the first cause in the order of the universe, so the ruler of the Virtuous City “must come first. He is then the cause through which the city and its parts come into being, and the cause through which the voluntary capacities of its parts are formed and arranged in their proper ranks. If any part falls into disorder, it is the ruler who guides it back to what removes that disorder.”[^V4-236]

And just as the beings closest to the First Existent are the noblest, while those farther away are progressively lower, the same hierarchy appears among the people who make up the Virtuous City.

Those “parts which are closest in authority to the ruler of the city perform the noblest voluntary actions. Those below them perform actions lower in rank, until we reach the parts”—meaning the social groups within the city—“whose actions are the lowest of all.”[^V4-237]

At first sight, this picture of social structure fits the outlook of the ruling class in a feudal society. It appears to present the hierarchy as fixed and final.

But this first impression begins to change when we see how al-Fārābī understands the difference between noble and lowly actions.
