He does not treat an action as noble or lowly in some absolute sense, as if the action were simply noble or low in itself. For him, the matter is relative. An action may be highly useful in itself and still be described as low because of the object to which it is directed. It may also be considered low because it produces little benefit, or because it is easy to perform.[^V4-238]

So al-Fārābī does not give us a final and absolute judgment here.

We also find that he requires the different parts of the “city” to remain connected to one another,[^V4-239] meaning that there should be no barriers separating the different groups within society.

And he gives two basic conditions that anyone seeking to rule the city must meet. First come “natural qualifications.” Second come “voluntary dispositions and acquired capacities.”[^V4-240] He also treats natural qualifications as qualities shared by everyone,[^V4-241] rather than as a special privilege granted to one person or one social group.

Finally, he says that the ruler’s own “craft” must be both a capacity and a goal toward which all the crafts of the people of the city are directed.[^V4-242]

What, then, is this “craft”?

Al-Fārābī says that the ruler is the person who knows every action through which happiness can be achieved. This, he says, is the first condition of rulership.[^V4-243]

#### 5 — The Theory of Happiness and Sufism

But what does our philosopher mean by happiness?

This brings us to a difficult and complicated subject.

In *The Virtuous City*, al-Fārābī’s discussion of happiness is wrapped in mystical language so thick that it almost hides the real earthly world from which his thought begins and whose relationships shape it.

Modern idealist scholars, both Eastern and Western, have used this feature of al-Fārābī’s thought to cut him off from that real world and remove him from his historical setting. As a result, we are left with an al-Fārābī who supposedly “moves farther and farther away from actual life” and “gives worldly matters no place in his ethical teaching.”[^V4-244]

His philosophy is then reduced to its theoretical and mystical side. Even the influence of the “environment in which he lived” is reduced to the many mystical ideas circulating in the Islamic world at the time, supposedly coming from Indian, Persian, African or Christian sources.[^V4-245]

Happiness itself is also given only one meaning. It becomes a purely spiritual goal enclosed within itself and cut off from the outside world. Its only purpose is supposedly to free our souls from everything material and bodily so that they may join the “intellectual beings.”[^V4-246]

It is true that al-Fārābī’s statements about happiness are expressed in mystical language and guided by a contemplative, rational outlook.

But did al-Fārābī live outside history—outside the history of his own age and society?

That is impossible. And if it is impossible, then none of his ideas or conceptions can be separated from that history. They arise from it and, in one form or another, express its problems.

His thought is not connected to some “absolute” world or some “absolute” human being, because neither exists or can exist.

Even when an idea seems to be about an “absolute” world or an “absolute” human being, it is in reality a complicated intellectual reflection of the objective world outside us. It is also a reflection of the real human being who lives, works and thinks within a particular period in the history of a particular society, within particular social relationships, and in a particular position within those relationships.

Why, then, should al-Fārābī be separated from all of this?

Why should his ideas be confined either to the realm of the “absolute” or to some other history—Indian, Persian, African or Christian—rather than to his own history, the history of Arab-Islamic society up to his time?

And why should his only connection to the “environment in which he lived” be his connection to mystical ideas, when those mystical ideas themselves were also products of that same history?

With this digression, we have found ourselves back at the ABCs of the problem once again. And it seems we may have to return to them more than once.

So what lies behind this mystical and contemplative wrapping around al-Fārābī’s idea of happiness?

Scholars of Arab philosophy know that the Andalusian philosopher Ibn Ṭufayl (506–581 AH / 1112–1185 CE) criticized al-Fārābī for saying in his *Ethics* that happiness exists in this life and in this world, and that anything said beyond this is mere nonsense and superstition.[^V4-247]

In fact, al-Fārābī made the same point in works other than his *Ethics*.

In *The Attainment of Happiness*, he places the question directly within human social activity. He argues that human communities have opportunities to achieve happiness in this “first life,” and that through this worldly happiness they can reach “the highest happiness” in “the next life.”

These opportunities become available through four kinds of virtue: theoretical, intellectual, moral and practical.

The theoretical virtues include the sciences through which the truths of existing things are established in the mind by rational proof.

Here al-Fārābī points to two kinds of truth. The first consists of what ancient philosophy called first principles: truths immediately accepted by reason. The second consists of truths reached through theoretical thought, experience, inference, teaching and learning.[^V4-248]

By **intellectual virtues**, al-Fārābī means the general conclusions people draw from their experience during social activity, especially while producing the things they use in public and domestic life.

From what he says, these intellectual virtues seem much like general rules people draw from experience and then use to understand what will benefit them in social life.[^V4-249]

By **moral virtues**, he means the qualities that guide people toward doing good for one another. These virtues cannot be achieved without relying first and foremost on the intellectual virtues.[^V4-250]

Finally, by **practical virtues**, he means what happens when moral guidance is actually turned into action.

This may happen by persuading a community and leading its members to make these changes willingly. Or it may happen by compelling those who resist, rebel, or are unable to grasp theoretical knowledge.[^V4-251]

In *Directing Attention to the Way of Happiness*, al-Fārābī also argues that it is not enough for the virtues that lead to happiness simply to exist in a person’s natural character. Will is also necessary, because happiness can be achieved only through voluntary action based on choice.[^V4-252]

Here al-Fārābī makes a very practical point: virtues can be developed voluntarily through habit and repeated practice.[^V4-253]

If we now return, after this discussion of those two works, to *The Virtuous City*, we find this practical outlook dressed in mystical and contemplative language.

Here happiness appears to consist in the acquired intellect joining with the Active Intellect, until the human soul reaches such perfection in its existence that it no longer needs matter in order to exist. It then becomes one of the things free from bodies, one of the substances separate from matter, and remains in that state forever.[^V4-254]

But immediately after saying this, al-Fārābī suddenly returns to practical reality.

When the soul reaches the state he has just described, he says, “its rank remains below the rank of the Active Intellect. It reaches this state only through voluntary actions: some are intellectual actions, and some are bodily actions.”

He continues:

“The voluntary actions that help us reach happiness are beautiful actions. The dispositions and acquired capacities from which these actions come are the virtues. These virtues are good, not for their own sake, but because they lead to happiness. Actions that obstruct happiness are evils, and these are ugly actions. The dispositions and acquired capacities from which such actions come are deficiencies, vices and base qualities.”[^V4-255]

So even when the soul reaches perfection, it still remains below the Active Intellect. At that level, it also remains connected to voluntary actions that are not separate from the body, since some of those actions are bodily actions.

Happiness also remains tied to virtues that are good, in contrast to evils. And there are still voluntary dispositions and acquired capacities that give rise to good actions and to bad ones.

All of this is language tied to real life—to the life people live among substances that are **not** separate from matter, meaning material things.

Happiness, then, is human happiness on this earth, in this material world.

Al-Fārābī therefore remains consistent with himself across all three works.

#### 6 — Realism and Utopianism

Yet here this realism remains blurred and wrapped in a form of mysticism.

Can this be explained only by the influence of Sufi doctrines?

Was there not also something else behind it: the troubled social and political reality of the time?

That reality may have driven free thinkers like al-Fārābī to search for happiness for oppressed, exploited, hungry and exhausted people in some world other than the one in which they suffered so greatly under caliphs, kings and feudal princes who fought among themselves and ruled unjustly.

Could al-Fārābī and thinkers like him have found a practical path to happiness in that age?

Scientific knowledge had not yet reached a level that could reveal a practical path toward human happiness. What option remained except to turn to reason and to let reason imagine a virtuous society—the Virtuous City—free from the defects of existing society?

Its members would cooperate in building it in an orderly way, without injustice. Its ruler would be unlike the rulers who dominated the feudal society that was then beginning to shake and fracture.

For a philosopher watching the movement of his age with pain, perhaps the best imaginable ruler was one who relied on reason at its highest level—or on the “Perfect Man,” someone who could somehow connect with sources of justice outside this world, even if only through imagination.

There is no doubt that al-Fārābī’s Virtuous City, created by his imagination with the help of the earlier model of Plato’s *Republic*, is a utopia.

But the reality from which it arose was entirely real.

Every one of the many qualities al-Fārābī gives to the ruler of his Virtuous City can be read as a protest against the opposite qualities he saw in the rulers of his own society.

Al-Fārābī was also aware that all these qualities could not realistically exist in a single person.

Perhaps, then, by gathering them all into the person of the ruler of his city, he was saying something to the rulers of the society in which he lived.

Perhaps he was telling them that not one of them deserved to hold power in a society seeking happiness, because not one possessed all the qualities required of a complete ruler worthy of leadership.

And if those qualities could not all exist in one person, then they would have to be distributed among several rulers.

But the conditions would remain the same. The qualities themselves would still have to be present, now distributed across a complete ruling body, and they would still have to form the basis of government and of the administration of the Virtuous City.

Since the many rulers of al-Fārābī’s own time did **not** possess these qualities among them, it is as though he were telling them that they were unfit to lead or govern, either individually or together.

Their numbers did nothing for society. They brought it neither justice nor happiness.

If we follow al-Fārābī into his description of the “cities opposed to the Virtuous City,” this purpose becomes even clearer.

He presents several corrupt kinds of society. They are corrupt because they are governed by corrupt political and social systems, false doctrines and beliefs, and mistaken ideas about the world.

In this way, al-Fārābī condemns the systems of his own time in the Arab-Islamic world and criticizes the doctrines, beliefs and ideas widespread in his society.

For example, he condemns the idea of “force”[^V4-256] as it appears in the “Ignorant City.”

He also condemns social doctrines which claim that the bond holding people together is based on human nature alone, tribal belonging, submission to a victorious ruler, shared religious beliefs, similarity in morals, character and customs, a common language, living in the same place or neighborhood, or facing a common danger.[^V4-257]

He then criticizes different ideas of justice: those based on force and coercion, on utility—we may remember pragmatism here—on fear, or on delusion.[^V4-258]

In the field of philosophical and intellectual doctrines, he also attacks the view that natural things have another existence besides the one we observe, and that the existence we see is different from their natural existence.

He also criticizes another doctrine which accepts that natural things really do exist as they appear, but claims that other things have become mixed with them, preventing them from acting according to their nature and causing many of them to take forms other than their own. Here he is describing the doctrine of the skeptics.

He also counts as false the Stoic view that human beings must submit to the rule of nature in order to live according to nature.[^V4-259]

We can therefore see how strongly al-Fārābī’s ideas were shaped by the living reality around him—a reality full of conflict and contradiction.

That reality shaped his views of the world, happiness, justice, and the best social order for the Virtuous City.

It also led him to reject the politics of kings, each of whom “governs the city under his control only in order to satisfy his own desire and inclination.”[^V4-260]

And it pushed him to imagine another model of society, the Virtuous City, in which the members would have “some things in common in which they are equal, and other things in knowledge and action that are specific to each rank. Each person reaches his share of happiness through both of these”[^V4-261]—through what is shared and what is particular.

We can also see how closely al-Fārābī remains connected to historical reality when he distinguishes different degrees of happiness in the Virtuous City according to three things: **kind, quantity and quality**.

By kind, he means differences between different crafts—for example, forms of weaving involving cotton and linen, perfume-making and writing, or dancing and jurisprudence.

By quantity, he means differences within the same craft according to how much knowledge and practice a person has acquired.

By quality, he means differences in how well or badly the craft is performed.[^V4-262]

We have deliberately spent a long time on this discussion because it leads us to an important conclusion.

Al-Fārābī’s social philosophy is consistent with his view of the order of the universe when it comes to the idea of “divine providence.”

In fact, his rejection of the theological meaning of divine providence in his theory of the universe is an objective reflection of his ideological position in social theory.

So far we have seen that his social theory is not built on the theological idea of providence which the feudal ruling order of his time used as religious support for its ideology.

That ideology classified people’s intellectual abilities according to a predetermined hierarchy based on their place in feudal society.

In al-Fārābī’s Virtuous City, by contrast, **choice** becomes a basic element alongside natural ability.

The relationship between the ruler—or rulers—and the rest of the city is not based on special class privileges either.

What matters is how far each individual, through both natural ability and acquired voluntary capacity, can reach the level of the acquired intellect, establish some kind of connection with the Active Intellect, and become capable of leading society toward the goal sought for its own sake: happiness.

And this happiness must first be achieved in this “worldly” life—in the real world.

#### 7 — Contradiction—and What Lies Behind It

But the contradiction we found earlier in al-Fārābī’s treatment of the eternity of the world appears again here in the question of divine providence.

Everything we have drawn from his cosmological and social theories seems to collapse in the face of one explicit passage from his *Book of the Harmonization of the Opinions of the Two Sages: Plato the Divine and Aristotle*—the same work in which he also attacks the idea that the world is eternal.

There he says:

“The Creator, exalted in His glory, governs the entire world. Not even the weight of a mustard seed escapes Him, and no part of the world falls outside His providence...”[^V4-263]

In his *The Sources of the Questions*, he says:

“Creation means preserving and continuing the existence of a thing that has no existence in itself, in a continuation that depends on no cause other than the essence of the creator. This means that He grants it eternal existence and completely protects it from nonexistence. It does not mean that He simply gives it existence after it had previously been nonexistent.”[^V4-264]

In both passages, the idea of divine providence is exactly the one defended by Islamic theologians concerned with the foundations of religious doctrine.[^V4-265]

Here al-Fārābī accepts the full theological meaning of providence.

His contradiction on this question is therefore double: philosophical and ideological.

But the second passage contains another contradiction as well.

In it, al-Fārābī denies that the world came into existence from nothing and at the same time affirms its eternity.

Does this not conflict with the statement that creation “grants it eternal existence and completely protects it from nonexistence,” while **not** meaning that it gives a thing existence after it had previously been nonexistent?

If divine creation gives a thing continuing and eternal existence, then that thing exists forever.

And if its existence is eternal, then, according to this line of reasoning, it must also have no beginning. It is both beginningless and endless—in other words, eternal.

Here al-Fārābī gives us new evidence not only of his contradiction, but also that the idea of an eternal world remained deeply rooted in his thought, whatever he may have said explicitly to the contrary.

The contradiction over divine providence can be resolved only in the same way we previously proposed for the contradiction over the eternity of the world and the claim that it did not arise from nothing.[^V4-266]

It would be a mistake to imagine that contradictions like these simply come from some supposedly “reconciliatory” character of Arab philosophy.

The opposite is true.

What bourgeois scholars have taken to be a “philosophy of reconciliation” is actually one outward sign of these contradictions.

The real issue is not that Arab philosophers were trying to reconcile philosophy with religion.

Rather, they were trying to express their real ideas and theories in philosophical language whose deeper aims and implications would be clear only to specialists—to those known as the “elite.”

At the same time, they circulated statements for the general public that contradicted those deeper positions.

They did this to protect themselves from the attacks of conservative religious jurists. Ruling authorities often used such jurists as the ideological face of the regime and as a weapon for intimidating thought that opposed official policy and ideology.

This practice seems to have been common among these philosophers.

A philosopher might present certain views in one book, then present opposing views in another, either for the reason just described or for another reason of the same general kind.

The Andalusian philosopher Ibn Ṭufayl[^V4-267] gives us an example in the introduction to his *Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān*.

He says that Ibn Sīnā followed the philosophy of Aristotle in *The Healing*, but that Ibn Sīnā himself stated in the introduction that his true position was different. According to Ibn Ṭufayl, Ibn Sīnā said that he had written *The Healing* according to the doctrine of the Peripatetics, and that anyone who wanted the truth stated without reserve should turn instead to his book on “Eastern Philosophy.”[^V4-268]

It later became clear that Ibn Sīnā made a similar statement in the introduction to *The Logic of the Easterners*.[^V4-269]

At the beginning of the second section, on nature, in *Pointers and Reminders*,[^V4-270] Ibn Sīnā also instructs his reader to keep the contents of the book away from anyone who does not meet the condition he states at the end of the work’s metaphysical section.

The reader, he says, must not be one of “the vulgar or ignorant, or those who have not been granted a sharp intelligence.” The person must instead be someone whose inner character can be trusted to be pure, whose conduct is upright, and who looks toward truth with sincerity and goodwill.

We find the same method even in al-Ghazālī, who was the leading voice in the campaign of intimidation directed against the philosophers.

Even al-Ghazālī wrote some books for the general public and others which he described as works “kept from those who are not fit to receive them” (al-Ghazālī, *The Book of Forty*, al-Kurdī edition, p. 24).

In other words, he told the public one thing and told “the elite” something else.

Was al-Ghazālī also afraid of public anger?

Or did he simply distrust the public’s ability to understand?

In al-Ghazālī’s case, the second explanation is the more likely one.

But there is another possibility that may fit his position even better.

Perhaps he publicly attacked philosophy and philosophers in order to encourage the authorities to act against them, while privately holding philosophical ideas and theories similar to the very ideas he publicly condemned.[^V4-271]

This is very different from the position of philosophers who placed one meaning inside their philosophical theories while publicly declaring something else in order to avoid persecution by the authorities and denunciation by reactionary religious jurists.

We say this even though, in our view, the extreme contradictions produced by this method are still difficult to “justify.”

We believe that the historical conditions which forced philosophers who had broken free from official ideology to use methods like these were the same conditions that encouraged the spread of “esoteric” ideas and movements, especially during the Abbasid period.

They were also behind the rise of methods of *taʾwīl*—interpretation that searches for meanings beneath the surface—which themselves often took esoteric forms.

This way of reading texts became so widespread that it did not remain confined to theology or to the writings of religious sects and schools. It spread into philosophy and philosophical thought, and then into the interpretation of literature, poetry, language and literary production.

It even extended into fields such as chemistry, astronomy and the study of the mind.

As a result, forms of magical practice became common in chemistry, astrology spread within astronomy, and strange “interpretations” of psychological phenomena helped encourage the spread of the symbols and language used by “professional” Sufis.

#### 8 — The Source of Rational First Principles (Self-Evident Truths)

There is one more question connected with existence and essence that we need to examine in al-Fārābī. His position on it may shed further light on his wider view of the subject.

The question concerns what al-Fārābī calls the “first intelligibles,” and what Aristotle calls the “first principles of demonstration” or simply the “first principles.” The logical basis of the problem is clear. In discussing scientific demonstration,[^V4-272] Aristotle argues that some knowledge does not itself need to be proved. If everything required proof, we could never prove anything at all, because the chain of proofs would never come to an end. The chain must therefore stop at something that needs no further proof. Aristotle calls this primary knowledge.[^V4-273]

Some of this non-demonstrative knowledge is necessary or self-evident. One example is the law of non-contradiction, which for Aristotle means that the same thing cannot both exist and not exist at the same time. Principles of this kind form a common basis for all the sciences.[^V4-274] Aristotle also speaks of principles that belong to individual sciences. Each science has its own starting principles, which it relies on when dealing with its particular subject.

But how do we acquire this kind of knowledge if it is not reached through proof? Do we acquire it, as Plato says, because the soul remembers knowledge it already possessed in the world of Forms before entering the body? Is it simply present in us from birth? Or do we gain it through learning?

Aristotle rejects all three explanations. He believes that human beings, like other animals, possess a natural power of perception. Through it, after repeatedly perceiving a number of similar individual things, we come to grasp these self-evident truths. But this does not mean that we reach them through a deliberate process of inference, working systematically from particular cases to a universal conclusion. The process begins directly from sense perception.

Aristotle calls the natural power responsible for this knowledge **intellect** (*Nous*). In his view, this kind of intellect is more exact and more certain than scientific knowledge itself, and nothing else has the same standing in relation to science. Aristotle’s intuition here is therefore intellectual, not mystical or based on spiritual illumination.[^V4-275]

That, in brief, is Aristotle’s account of how we know the first principles of demonstration. What, then, does al-Fārābī say about his own “first intelligibles”?

He discusses the question in *The Opinions of the People of the Virtuous City* (p. 84), while explaining how the potential intellect becomes an actual intellect and eventually reaches the stage of contact with the Active Intellect. He writes:

> “When the rational faculty receives from the Active Intellect something whose relation to it is like the relation of light to sight, the sensible things preserved in the imaginative faculty become intelligibles in the rational faculty. These are the first intelligibles shared by all people, such as the fact that the whole is greater than the part, and that quantities equal to the same thing are equal to one another.”

He then divides these shared first intelligibles into three kinds:

> “One kind consists of the first principles of scientific geometry. Another consists of the first principles by which we judge what is good and bad in human action. A third consists of the first principles used to understand things that human beings do not produce: their conditions, principles and ranks, such as the heavens, the First Cause, the other principles, and the things that arise from those principles.”

From this passage, we can see that al-Fārābī thinks human beings acquire the “first intelligibles” in two ways at once. One involves the Active Intellect, which gives the rational faculty something that al-Fārābī compares to the effect of sunlight on sight. The other involves the sensible things stored in the imagination from earlier sensory experience.

Al-Fārābī therefore seems to be restating Aristotle’s position in a new form, one influenced by Neoplatonism through the introduction of the Active Intellect. Yet the meaning that the Active Intellect takes on here is actually closer to Aristotle’s *Nous*—his intuitive intellectual power—than to Plotinus’s Active Intellect.

If we look at al-Fārābī’s various explanations of thinking, which he describes as the movement of the human intellect from potentiality to actuality,[^V4-276] we can see why. The thing that the Active Intellect gives to the rational faculty, and that al-Fārābī compares to the effect of sunlight on sight, is not something spiritual. Nor is it a form of mystical illumination.

In al-Fārābī’s own explanations, it functions instead as an outside agency that he believes is necessary to move the intellect already present in human nature from potentiality into actuality. He calls this natural human intellect the “material intellect.” The same outside agency also turns potential intelligibles into actual intelligibles.

Here we need to understand the intellectual background that led ancient philosophers to feel this “need” for an outside cause whenever they tried to explain movement in nature, society or thought.

Historically, they were unable, first, to discover the objective laws through which things move and develop from within themselves. Second, they did not yet understand the higher and more complex forms of movement beyond the simplest form known to them: movement from one place to another.

This limitation was historical. It reflected the level of human knowledge of nature in their time.

Once we understand this background, al-Fārābī’s position becomes easier to explain. Throughout his discussions of thinking, he begins from a broadly materialist position. But because he cannot see the internal laws governing the movement of thought itself, he cannot identify the point at which detailed sensory and practical perceptions are transformed into rational concepts—the point where thought moves from the particular to the universal.

Because he cannot explain this difficult transition, he looks for an outside, metaphysical factor to perform it.

This is why al-Fārābī says that potential intelligibles “need something else to move them from potentiality into actuality. The agent that moves them from potentiality into actuality is an entity whose essence is an intellect already in actuality and separate from matter.”[^V4-277] This agent is the Active Intellect itself.

At the same time, al-Fārābī accepts that the intellect human beings possess “by nature at the beginning” is a state existing in matter that is ready to receive the forms of intelligible things. This state, existing in matter, is potentially an intellect—the material intellect—and is also potentially intelligible. It can become actually intelligible, but its own nature is not sufficient to make that transition by itself.[^V4-278]

The appeal to the Active Intellect therefore becomes an appeal to an **outside tool**—and I stress the word *tool*—drawn specifically from the realm of metaphysics to complete the process of thought.

The reason was that ancient philosophy had not yet discovered the higher form of movement involved in thinking itself. The only form of movement it understood clearly was movement through space, or mechanical motion, and that was not enough to explain the process.

Aristotle faced much the same problem. He appealed to intuitive intellect, *Nous*, to explain how we know the first principles of demonstration.

Yet both Aristotle and al-Fārābī reach half of the solution. Both trace this knowledge back to its real source: sensory experience. Both also place the problem in its proper setting by recognizing that knowledge of self-evident truths begins with a movement from particular cases to universal concepts.

What they could not see was the dialectical process taking place within thought itself.

Aristotle, like al-Fārābī and the other philosophers who lived before the great discoveries of modern natural science and technology, could not understand this inner dialectic of thought because he could not yet see it in the movement of nature itself. This same limitation led Aristotle to the idea of the “Unmoved Mover” and to his idealist treatment of “matter and form.”

We can therefore understand al-Fārābī’s position in this way: on the question of the first intelligibles, as with Aristotle, his firm starting point is materialist. The idealist layer placed over this real foundation reflects the inability of the natural sciences of his time to provide a complete and correct solution to the problem.

To support this interpretation, we can turn to another passage by al-Fārābī. It makes his materialist starting point on this very question even clearer than the passages we have considered so far.

While trying to reconcile the theories of knowledge of Plato and Aristotle,[^V4-279] al-Fārābī writes:

> “It is plainly clear that the child has a soul that is potentially knowing, and that the senses are its instruments of perception. Sense perception deals only with particulars, and from particulars universals arise. Universals are, in truth, experiences.
>
> “Some experiences are acquired deliberately. People generally use the term ‘previous experiences’ for universals acquired deliberately. As for universals that arise in a human being without deliberate intention,[^V4-280] either ordinary people have no name for them,[^V4-281] because they do not concern themselves with them,[^V4-282] or scholars have a name for them,[^V4-283] calling them the first forms of knowledge, the principles of demonstration, and similar names.
>
> “Aristotle showed in the *Posterior Analytics* that whoever lacks a particular sense also lacks the corresponding kind of knowledge. Knowledge therefore enters the soul through the senses.
>
> “Because pieces of knowledge first entered the soul one after another without deliberate intention, the person did not remember acquiring them as each part accumulated. This is why most people imagine that this knowledge has always existed in the soul, and that the soul knows through some route other than the senses.
>
> “Once these experiences have accumulated in the soul, the soul becomes rational, because intellect is nothing other than experience. The greater the amount of experience, the more complete the soul becomes in intellect...”[^V4-284]

We should look at this passage as a whole and see how its parts fit together. But we should also pay close attention to several particular statements:

> “From particulars universals arise.”

> “Universals are, in truth, experiences.”

> “Knowledge therefore enters the soul through the senses.”

We should especially notice the force of al-Fārābī’s wording in that last statement: he is saying that knowledge comes through the senses and through no other route.

And finally:

> “Intellect is nothing other than experience.”

This is remarkably clear.

Al-Fārābī is starting from the view that the external world is the objective source from which essence—the universal concept—arises in knowledge.

That is the first point.

The second concerns the “first forms of knowledge” and the “principles of demonstration,” which are the very subject we are discussing here. Al-Fārābī puts our knowledge of these principles on the same footing as all our other knowledge. Both come from the same source: the external world, through experiences of individual things.

For him, there is only one difference between these two kinds of knowledge. Ordinary knowledge is acquired deliberately, while our knowledge of first principles develops without deliberate intention.

That is the only difference.

In this passage, al-Fārābī even removes the role of the Neoplatonic Active Intellect and Aristotle’s intuitive intellect, *Nous*, from the explanation of how we know the first principles of demonstration.[^V4-285]

#### 9 — The Theory of Knowledge in al-Fārābī, Aristotle and Plato

This passage also brings us back to al-Fārābī’s attempt to “reconcile” Plato and Aristotle on knowledge and on the nature of the soul.

What is especially interesting—and very important—is that in trying to bring the two philosophers together, al-Fārābī completely moves beyond Plato and almost moves beyond Aristotle as well.

He takes Plato’s idealist theory of “recollection” and gives it an interpretation based on his own materialist tendency in explaining the foundations of knowledge.

For al-Fārābī, recollection means that a person remembers sensory experiences that had gradually accumulated in the mind from repeated encounters with similar things in the outside world. Those experiences were acquired bit by bit, at different times, and were later forgotten.

He writes:

> “Whenever a person who is trying to know something encounters signs, indications and meanings of what had previously existed in his soul, it is as though he remembers it at that moment. It is like someone who sees a body whose features resemble some of the features of another body he once knew but had forgotten, and remembers it because of the similarity he now perceives.”[^V4-286]

Aristotle explains memory in a similar direction.

From his *On the Soul*, we understand that the soul can preserve an image of an external object it has perceived. When similar sensations are repeated many times, these images accumulate. Repeated perception, together with the memory of earlier perceptions, produces a particular kind of experience (Aristotle: 2, 415b, 24; 2, 417a, lines 3–5; and 2, 417b, 20–25).

But is this what Plato means by the soul’s recollection of knowledge?

Not at all.

In the *Meno* (85 DC), for example, Plato has Socrates say that the knowledge found hidden within one of Meno’s “slaves,” despite the fact that nobody had taught it to him, must have been acquired in one of two ways. Either he acquired it at some earlier time, or it had always existed within him.

If it had always existed within him, then he had always possessed knowledge.

If he acquired it at some earlier time, he cannot have acquired it during his present bodily life, because no other person had ever taught it to him. He must therefore have acquired it at another time, before he became a human being.

Since the soul is immortal, Plato argues, it was able to contemplate the world of true realities before entering the human body. Once it entered the body, those earlier contemplations remained within it. But because the soul now lives in the world of passing and changing things—the sensory world—it forgets them. Through questioning and dialogue, however, it can remember them again, as happens in the dialogue between Socrates and the slave.

In the *Phaedo* (239 DC), Plato likewise presents the process of reaching general concepts—rational knowledge—as nothing more than the soul remembering what it once knew in the world of Ideas, the world of pure and true realities.

Anyone who makes proper use of this recollection can become truly complete. But only the philosopher’s mind can do so, because its memory turns toward the realm in which God reveals his divinity.

The result is clear. For Plato, sensory knowledge does not give us true knowledge, because it deals only with changing and temporary things. True knowledge is knowledge of fixed concepts, and sense perception cannot give us that kind of knowledge.

This is what recollection means in Plato’s theory of knowledge.

As we have seen, it is fundamentally different from what al-Fārābī attributes to Plato.

So how did our philosopher make this mistake?

Was it because he was determined to “unify” Plato and Aristotle in order to preserve the unity of philosophical truth? Or did he receive Plato’s theory in a distorted form through Neoplatonism?

The first explanation seems more likely.

As we said earlier, al-Fārābī’s writings show that he knew Plato’s philosophy from its genuine sources, especially the dialogues, as well as the *Republic*.

It also seems that the widespread practice of interpretive reinterpretation, one of the most striking intellectual and ideological features of al-Fārābī’s time, strongly shaped the way he read many of the theories of both Aristotle and Plato. At times, his interpretations move a long way from what the original texts actually say.

#### 10 — General Conclusion

We can now bring this survey of several parts of al-Fārābī’s philosophy to a close and draw the conclusions that matter for our discussion of existence and essence.

First, it has become clear that in his theory of knowledge al-Fārābī gives primacy to existence.

The external world is the object of knowledge and its first and genuine source. The many passages we have examined show that, for al-Fārābī, this external reality exists objectively and independently of our consciousness.

Second, even the forms of knowledge called the “first principles of demonstration” or the “first intelligibles,” including the most general self-evident truths, ultimately come from this same external reality.

This confirms both the objective existence of the world and its independence from consciousness. It also confirms that existence comes first in relation to essence, or the universal concept.

When we turn from the theory of knowledge to ontology—the question of being itself—the position is somewhat different.

Here, al-Fārābī presents his materialist positions inside an idealist framework. We can see those materialist positions only by looking through that framework.

But if we examine his scattered statements closely, connect them with one another and read them as parts of a single system, a different picture emerges. We must also keep in mind the historical conditions that shaped al-Fārābī’s thinking—socially, politically and intellectually—and set aside the conventional philosophical and theological formulas in which many of his ideas were expressed.

If we do this, we can reach a bold conclusion that goes against the dominant view in much modern scholarship on Arab philosophy. According to that common view, the primacy of existence was unknown in Arab philosophy before al-Shīrāzī, Mullā Ṣadrā (979–1050 AH / 1573–1640 CE).[^V4-287]

Our own conclusion is that, on the evidence of the texts we have examined, al-Fārābī implicitly accepts the primacy of existence over essence—or, at the very least, never explicitly gives primacy to essence.

We have already presented the evidence for this conclusion. Its main points can be summarized as follows.

First, natural things—the material world—have a real existence. Their existence is not merely formal or shadow-like, as it is in Plato.

Second, their existence is objective. It is independent of consciousness and is not created by consciousness.[^V4-288]

Third, our concepts of these things arise from their individual existence:

> “From particulars universals arise. Universals are, in truth, experiences.”

(*The Harmonization*, p. 98)

Fourth, the natural movement of these things follows its own internal laws, “without this being imposed upon them by some other thing foreign to them” (*The Virtuous City*, p. 46).

Fifth, the elements shared by all natural things—the elements from which these things arise in al-Fārābī’s account of the order of existence—are never described in his texts as absolute essences.

Instead, his texts suggest that these elements are a form of indefinite, undetermined matter. This matter takes its place within a moving series of transformations produced through the motion of the heavenly bodies.

This “matter,” in turn, goes back to the motion of the first heaven, which is itself bodily. The existence of that first heaven was one of the necessary conditions for the First Intellect to proceed from the One, or God.

This account of the origin of “matter” supports Hādī al-Sabzawārī’s statement that the leading Peripatetic philosophers regarded the first act of creation as applying to existence rather than essence.[^V4-289]

Taken together, these points allow us to challenge the common view that no Arab philosopher before Mullā Ṣadrā accepted the primacy of existence.

We can now add another piece of evidence, drawn from al-Fārābī’s discussion of “natural science” in his *Enumeration of the Sciences*.[^V4-290]

There, al-Fārābī suggests that our true knowledge of the essences of bodies comes from direct experience of material reality rather than from abstract thought alone.[^V4-291]

For artificial objects, this experience takes the form of analysis and reconstruction. For natural bodies, it involves reconstructing them in a way that resembles their natural composition.

That is the first point.

The second is that the essences of these bodies arise from the union of their qualities with their matter. By a body’s “quality,” al-Fārābī means its shape and form.

In artificial objects, this union is produced through human action and intention.

With natural bodies, human beings can reproduce it by learning how the bodies are naturally composed and then constructing similar bodies themselves.

Al-Fārābī then explains that the invisible “powers” found in natural bodies and in many artificial ones—for example, the intoxicating power of wine—cannot be known by simply seeing them with the senses. Instead, “we understand them rationally, and this is the highest form of comparison with the forms of artificial bodies” (*Enumeration of the Sciences*, p. 115), which we can observe directly through the senses.

In other words, the starting point is still direct practical experience.[^V4-292]

Al-Fārābī illustrates this method with the fermentation of wine and the preparation of compound medicines.

We know the intoxicating “power,” or essence, of wine and the corresponding “power” in a medicine by observing their actual effects. We then reason from those effects and use certain proofs built on this experimental comparison.

This is what al-Fārābī means here by “reasoning and certain proofs.” He means reasoning based on induction from experience, not purely mental deduction based on abstract contemplation.

These explicit statements open a clear path for confirming our earlier conclusion that al-Fārābī gives primacy to existence rather than essence.

At the same time, they give us a vivid example of the two-way relationship between the level reached by the natural sciences in his time and the level reached by materialist tendencies in Arab philosophy.

## Conclusions of Chapter Two

### 1

In this chapter, we examined how Arab-Islamic philosophy, at an early stage of its maturity, approached the problem of existence and essence. We focused on this question because it is one of the central problems of philosophy.

Abū Naṣr al-Fārābī was the clearest representative of this stage in the development of Arab-Islamic philosophy. With him, philosophical concepts began to take on a new form that was more independent of the theological concepts that had come before them.

Al-Fārābī played an important historical role in the development of philosophical theory among Arab and Muslim thinkers. His work rested on the rapid development of scientific activity that had begun in the early Abbasid period.

He was the first to place Plotinus’s theory of emanation within the historical development of Arab philosophy as both a science and an ideology. He also tried to adapt that theory to the needs of his own period and of Arab-Islamic society.

In Plotinus’s philosophy, the theory of emanation had served as a philosophical and ideological expression of the social relations of a slave-owning society.

The theory reached Arab thinkers through a well-known historical mistake: the work known as the *Theology*, which was Plotinian in origin, was wrongly attributed to Aristotle.

Because Aristotle enjoyed enormous authority among Arab philosophers, they accepted the theory on the assumption that it was Aristotelian.

This does not mean that they were unfamiliar with Aristotle’s genuine works or with his actual ideas. They continued to hold to the theory of emanation even though they could see that it conflicted with Aristotle’s philosophical teaching as presented in his authentic works.

They did so because the theory of emanation served an ideological purpose.

It allowed them to oppose the official ideology, which separated God from the world and restricted the connection between the two to the prophet and the revelation given to him.

We therefore reject the claim that al-Fārābī “was unfamiliar with historical criticism, for otherwise he could have dealt more accurately with the contradiction between the views contained in the *Theology* and Aristotle’s views in the other books written by Aristotle” (Dr. Jamīl Ṣalībā, *From Plato to Ibn Sīnā*, p. 57).

The question we should ask instead is: what did “truth” mean to al-Fārābī?

Is it really correct to say that, for him, this truth simply meant that “he believed in the unity of philosophy and affirmed the unity of reason” (same source)?

Al-Fārābī defines philosophy as “knowledge of existing things insofar as they exist.” His insistence that this definition must correspond to what philosophy really is suggests something more basic: he believed in the unity of philosophical truth itself because that truth was objective—the truth of things “insofar as they exist.”

For al-Fārābī, the “unity of philosophy” and the “unity of reason” follow logically from the unity of this truth and from his confidence in the ability of the human mind to make its understanding correspond to the object it is trying to understand: philosophical truth itself, meaning the truth of existing things as they exist.

From this we conclude that, in al-Fārābī’s theory of knowledge, existence comes first.

The existence that becomes the object of knowledge is prior to knowledge, or consciousness.

### 2

Of course, our understanding does not always correspond perfectly to reality. If it did, every truth we grasped would be an absolute truth, and that is clearly not the case.

Why, then, did al-Fārābī make this need for correspondence the basis for treating the “unity of philosophy” or the “unity of reason” as a consequence of the “unity of philosophical truth”?

Here we reach the ideological side of the question.

The idea of one philosophical truth stands in opposition to the “religious truth” on which the existing social order rested—that is, the order of absolute and authoritarian rule.

The common claim among bourgeois scholars that Arab philosophy was essentially an attempt to “reconcile” philosophy and religion is therefore mistaken.

What al-Fārābī in particular tried to reconcile were the ideas of Plato and Aristotle, as an expression of the “unity of philosophical truth.” The purpose of this reconciliation was to oppose religious truth with a single philosophical truth.

Arab philosophy may have appeared outwardly as an attempt to combine religion and philosophy. But in substance, what it did was place religious truth under philosophical truth—under reason.

We have already shown this through concrete examples that reveal what lay beneath the outward form.

### 3

In the natural philosophy of al-Fārābī, we examined several advanced examples of how developments in the natural sciences were reflected in his philosophical thought.

These examples show that when al-Fārābī discusses what Aristotle calls the relationship between matter and form, he gives it a meaning close to what we, in the age of Marxism, call the dialectic of the internal relations through which things move, change and come into being.

Al-Fārābī’s texts show this relationship working internally and independently of any outside factor.

We find in them an early glimpse of matter moving from within itself, of movement driven by internal forces. We also find the beginnings of an awareness of movement through the struggle of opposites, along with a principle resembling the persistence of matter as a kind through the transformation and disappearance of its individual forms.

### 4

In al-Fārābī’s social philosophy, especially in *The Opinions of the People of the Virtuous City*, we find the ideological basis for his materialist position on nature.

Several important features stand out in his picture of the virtuous society, or city.

First, its members possess will and freedom of choice. This stands against determinism.

Second, the position each member holds in society depends on the level reached by that person’s rational and voluntary abilities to take part in directing society.

Third, people’s intellectual abilities and social positions are not fixed by fate once and for all. They are shaped by social activity carried out through choice.

All of this conflicts with the official feudal class interpretation represented by the ideology of the state at the time.

It also sheds light on al-Fārābī’s own class position.

This is supported by his view of the village, or countryside, as serving the city. In this sense, he expresses the ideology of the growing urban groups: merchants, craftspeople and ordinary people organized in guild-like associations.

For the same reason, he judges occupations as honorable or low according to how useful they are to society. Differences in value depend on the kind of work being done, not on the worker’s position within the social hierarchy.

Natural abilities, moreover, are shared by all people. No group or class possesses a special natural privilege over another.

And the first duty of the ruler of society is to secure the happiness of its members.

### 5

What does al-Fārābī mean by happiness?

His ideas about happiness are wrapped in mystical language, and bourgeois scholars have made great use of that language. Yet the ideas themselves are closely connected with the social reality of his own time and society.

One revealing statement attributed to him says:

> “Happiness exists only in this life and in this world. Everything said beyond this is nonsense and the superstitions of old women.”

Al-Fārābī believes that human communities have opportunities to achieve happiness in this “first life.” These opportunities can be realized through theoretical, intellectual, moral and practical virtues.

By practical virtue, he means turning the first three kinds of virtue into material action.

He also believes that even when the human soul reaches perfection, it remains connected with acts of choice that are not separated from bodies, because they are bodily actions.

Happiness therefore remains connected with the virtues of good, as opposed to evil.

### 6

Why, then, does his realism appear wrapped in a mystical tendency?

The answer lies in the unstable social and political conditions of his time.

Those conditions pushed independent thinkers to look for the happiness of oppressed and exploited groups somewhere outside the world of the caliphate, its state and its authoritarian order—in other words, in a utopian world.

They could not see a path toward the happiness of these groups within the world created by the existing system. They therefore expressed the better world they wanted in mystical and utopian forms.

For this reason, although al-Fārābī’s virtuous city is utopian, his picture of it begins from the real and concrete social conditions around him.

The qualities he gives to the ruler of the “virtuous city” can also be read as an indirect protest against the opposite qualities found in the rulers of the caliphate in his own time.

The same is true of his descriptions of cities, or societies, that are not virtuous. These descriptions amount to a realistic criticism of the societies of his day and of the political and social systems he rejected.

In this context, he rejects the doctrine that force should determine social relations. He also rejects social theories that base society on family or tribal descent, submission to a victorious ruler, religious ties, and similar principles.

Likewise, he rejects ideas of justice based on force, seizure or simple self-interest.

He rejects the doctrine that the beings governing nature possess another existence beyond sensible existence. He rejects the Stoic idea that human beings must submit to the authority of nature in order to live within it.

And he rejects the policy of rulers “each of whom governs the city placed under his authority only so that he may satisfy his own desires and inclinations.”

Finally, al-Fārābī says that differences in people’s happiness rest on three grounds: kind, quantity and quality.

By **kind**, he means differences such as those between weaving, making cotton or linen cloth, perfumery, writing, dancing and jurisprudence.

By **quantity**, he means differences in the amount of practical experience a person possesses in a craft.

By **quality**, he means differences in how well a craft is performed.

Taken together, these ideas show that al-Fārābī’s social thought is consistent with his view of the order of the universe, which rejects the idea of “divine providence” in its official ideological form.

### 7

At the same time, we must recognize the contradictions in al-Fārābī’s work.

Although he says that the world is eternal, some of his texts say things that appear to contradict this.

Although he rejects the idea of “divine providence,” other texts accept it.

These contradictions are only apparent.

They are the result of the intellectual and ideological repression of the period, which forced independent thinkers to cover their “unacceptable” ideas with a formal religious language.

Even so, reactionary forces continued to uncover those ideas and persecute the thinkers who held them.

### 8

We examined how al-Fārābī explains our knowledge of rational first principles, which Aristotle calls the first principles of demonstration.

From al-Fārābī’s texts, we concluded that even when he connects knowledge of these self-evident truths with the Active Intellect, this “intellect” is not something spiritual. Nor does knowledge come from it through mystical illumination.

Al-Fārābī presents it instead as an outside agency that he believes is needed to move the human intellect from a state of possibility, or potentiality, into actual realization, and to turn potential intelligibles into actual intelligibles.

The reason ancient philosophers—even materialist ones—felt the need for such an outside factor was their historical inability to understand self-movement in nature or to recognize forms of movement other than mechanical movement through space.

The appeal to the Active Intellect therefore serves as an outside means of completing the process of thought, much as Aristotle appealed to intuitive intellect, *Nous*, to solve the problem of how we know the first principles of demonstration.

Yet both Aristotle and al-Fārābī reach half of the solution. They trace this knowledge back to its real source in sensory experience.

What they cannot see is the dialectical relationship within the movement of thought itself, because they could not yet see the same dialectic in the movement of nature.

Al-Fārābī nevertheless has a passage in which he treats external, material existence as the objective source of essence in knowledge.

In this passage, he places knowledge of rational first principles on the same basis as all our other knowledge: everything comes from the same source, the external world.

Here, he even removes the Active Intellect from the explanation of how we know the first principles of demonstration.

### 9

One particularly interesting and important point appears when al-Fārābī tries to “reconcile the views of Plato and Aristotle” on the theory of knowledge.

In doing so, he moves beyond Plato and almost beyond Aristotle as well.

He takes Plato’s idealist idea of “recollection” and interprets it through his own materialist understanding of the basis of knowledge.

For al-Fārābī, recollection simply means remembering sensory experiences that had previously accumulated in memory.

This is very far from Plato’s own theory.

### 10

Our general conclusion from this study of al-Fārābī’s philosophy is as follows:

1. Al-Fārābī gives primacy to existence, both in the theory of knowledge and in ontology.
2. Natural things have a real and objective existence.
3. Our concepts of these things arise from their particular, sensory existence.
4. Their natural movement follows its own independent internal laws.
5. The basic elements of nature are not absolute essences. They are forms of material existence—indefinite and not yet fully determined.

Our final conclusion is that al-Fārābī’s own statements give us good reason to confirm what we argued earlier: he gives primacy to existence over essence.

This conclusion runs against the widespread view among other scholars that no Arab philosopher accepted the primacy of existence before Mullā Ṣadrā al-Shīrāzī (1573–1640).

At the same time, these passages give us a vivid example of the two-way relationship between the level reached by the natural sciences in al-Fārābī’s age and the level reached by materialist tendencies in Arab philosophy at the same time.

# Chapter Three

## The Problem of Existence and Essence in Ibn Sīnā’s Philosophy

### 1 — Preliminaries to Ibn Sīnā’s Theory

#### 1 — Introduction

As we study the problem of existence and essence in Ibn Sīnā,[^V4-293] we will find that we are dealing not with one problem, but with a whole range of them—more numerous and varied than those we encountered with al-Fārābī.

There are two main reasons for this.

First, Ibn Sīnā himself brought together, in his personality, his intellectual interests, and the whole course of his life, elements that were not only varied but sometimes contradictory. Yet all of them came together within a single, coherent whole.

Second, the problem of existence and essence in Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy draws into itself many of the other questions his philosophy deals with, whether in logic, natural philosophy, or metaphysics. Here too we find the same kind of unity made up of diversity and contradiction that we see in his personality, his scientific knowledge, and his life.

For this reason, it is difficult to identify Ibn Sīnā’s final position on existence and essence if we isolate the problem from the rest of his philosophy and treat it as though it stood on its own. It does not.

We therefore have to look for the roots of the problem in several different parts of the vast philosophical world Ibn Sīnā created, and also in the historical relationships connected with that world or reflected in it in one way or another.

This makes it necessary to begin with a few preliminary points that will help clear the way before we reach the heart of the subject.

#### 2 — The Internal Logic of Philosophy

We do not dispute that social reality provides the basic conditions that drive the development of philosophy. But that does not mean we can ignore another factor: the internal logic of philosophy’s own development.

Every philosopher, through his positions and philosophical outlook, represents a particular kind of response to the society and historical period in which he lives. The external course of philosophical history—the way philosophy changes within particular social relationships—sets the broad direction of its development.

But philosophy also has an internal course of development, and this too helps shape the direction it takes.

What do we mean by this?

Every later philosophy is connected to an earlier one. In a sense, it is born from it. It therefore remains related to what came before. It takes over some of its foundations, rules, and concepts. It begins from questions and problems that earlier thinkers had already worked on. It asks how earlier philosophers understood this or that problem, what solutions they proposed, and then develops new positions by critically examining what they had done.

This is one form of the internal process at work in the history of philosophy.

To a large extent, it helps determine how that history develops. It may slow development down or hold it back, depending on how far a later philosophical direction breaks with the one before it—or fails to break with it.

This was the situation Ibn Sīnā inherited.

His philosophy followed not only that of al-Fārābī, but an entire philosophical generation in which al-Fārābī was the most prominent figure, though he was by no means the only one. That generation included a number of philosopher-scientists, or scientist-philosophers, whose remarkable work brought together natural and mathematical science on the one hand and philosophy on the other.

Their scientific work fed directly into their philosophy. As a result, they made important changes to Greek philosophical theories, both Athenian and Alexandrian.

This was especially true of the theory of emanation. That theory opened new possibilities for Arab philosophy in dealing with some of the most important questions carrying ideological meaning in the Islamic Middle Ages, including the relationship between God and the world.

In earlier chapters, we saw how Arab-Islamic philosophy, in its different forms—rationalist, Sufi, and illuminationist—began from the logic of *tawḥīd*, the doctrine of divine unity.

But this starting point created a sharp contradiction.

Arab philosophical thought had to reflect the social and ideological conflicts taking place within society. In doing so, it was driven to rebel against the traditional official view of the relationship between God and the world. This reflected, at the level of ideas, a rebellion against the ideology of a social order based on absolute theocratic rule.

The logic of *tawḥīd* governed Arab philosophical thought as a whole. Yet any ideological position outside the official ideology appeared to contradict this same principle of *tawḥīd*.

Here we find the great problem that Arab-Islamic philosophy had to confront.

On the one hand, the doctrine of divine transcendence required God to remain separate from the world. On the other hand, thinkers who were breaking with the ideology of the ruling social order needed some way to cross that gap and establish a connection between God and the world.

When the theory of emanation entered Arab-Islamic philosophical thought through the work known as the *Theology*, attributed to Aristotle, Arab philosophers found in it a way of dealing with this contradiction.

But they did not simply take over Plotinus’s theory in its complete, ready-made form.

They used it creatively, taking from it what helped them address a contradiction that stood not only in the way of philosophy’s own development, but also in the way of their ideological struggle.

Their work in the natural sciences, which developed in response to the needs of society, played a decisive role here. It helped them make major changes to the theory of emanation and push it toward the materialist positions they had adopted—for example, their recognition that the external world exists objectively and independently, in contrast to the Plotinian view.

This scientific and philosophical ferment was already well developed by the time Ibn Sīnā entered the history of Arab philosophy.

He therefore had little choice but to work with the intellectual material already before him and to build his philosophy on foundations that had already been prepared.

The general social situation had certainly changed in important ways by Ibn Sīnā’s time. The power of the Abbasid caliphate had declined so badly that it had become little more than a weak shadow with almost no influence over events. Feudal relations had also broken down into a disorderly and fragmented system of military fiefs.[^V4-294]

Even so, the internal logic of philosophy’s development pushed Ibn Sīnā to continue along the same broad path whose foundations had already been laid by a whole group of encyclopedic thinkers, with al-Fārābī standing as the philosophical culmination of their enormous effort.

#### 3 — Abū Bakr al-Rāzī: A Turning Point

To move directly from al-Fārābī to Ibn Sīnā without stopping, as far as possible, at the turning point represented by Abū Bakr al-Rāzī would be to take part in the historical injustice suffered by this remarkable figure from his own time down to ours.

Muḥammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī[^V4-295] (854–932) is a striking example of the scientist-philosopher who built his philosophy on the foundation of his scientific experiments, especially in medicine and chemistry.[^V4-296]

Because of this, his philosophy developed in a materialist direction. We can see its effects in a number of positions that led Abū Bakr al-Rāzī to argue for the eternity of matter, time, and space.

He counted five things as eternal: God, the Universal Soul, matter, time, and space.[^V4-297]

Unfortunately, we do not know this theory directly from al-Rāzī’s own surviving writings. We know it mainly through the troubled reactions it produced among his contemporaries and later critics.

These included al-Bīrūnī (973–1048), the Persian poet and traveler Nāṣir Khusraw (1003–1060) in his book *Provisions for the Travelers*,[^V4-298] ʿAbd Allāh ibn Aḥmad ibn Maḥmūd al-Balkhī al-Kaʿbī, leader of the Baghdad Muʿtazilites (d. 319 AH/931 CE),[^V4-299] the famous physician and scientist Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥasan ibn al-Haytham (c. 965–1039), the jurist and theologian Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī (d. 606 AH/1209 CE),[^V4-300] the Andalusian Ẓāhirī scholar Ibn Ḥazm, who was also a jurist, physician, and philosopher (994–1064),[^V4-301] and many others who would be difficult to list in full.

In most of these discussions, Abū Bakr al-Rāzī is described as an “atheist.” We also find that some of his critics write about him in a highly emotional and agitated tone.

This gives us two reasons for caution.

First, since we cannot consult al-Rāzī’s original texts, we have to be careful about reconstructing his real position from the writings of his opponents.

Second, these reactions suggest that Abū Bakr al-Rāzī spoke openly and boldly about views that challenged the official ideas and dominant intellectual positions of his time.

History has preserved three important documents containing strong attacks on al-Rāzī’s views about the eternity of matter, space, and time, as well as his position on prophetic miracles.

The first was written by Abū Ḥātim al-Rāzī, an Ismāʿīlī missionary who was one of the leading Ismāʿīlī propagandists in Ṭabaristān and Azerbaijan during the fourth Islamic century, or tenth century CE.[^V4-302]

This material appears in Abū Ḥātim’s book *The Signs of Prophecy*.[^V4-303] It is written in the form of debates between himself and Abū Bakr al-Rāzī.

Its value as a historical record is doubtful, however, because the dialogue is presented entirely by one side. Abū Ḥātim controls the exchange in a way that is clearly meant to make his opponent look weak and defeated.

The second document comes from another Ismāʿīlī figure, described as “the chief of the Ismāʿīlī missionaries in Iraq during the reign of the Fatimid caliph al-Ḥākim bi-Amr Allāh.”[^V4-304]

This was ʿImād al-Dīn Aḥmad ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Kirmānī, known as the “Proof of the Iraqis.”[^V4-305]

His work continues and supplements the first. In other words, it adds further arguments against Abū Bakr al-Rāzī’s rejection of prophecy.[^V4-306]

The third Ismāʿīlī document is found in Nāṣir Khusraw’s *Provisions for the Travelers*, which we mentioned earlier.

Khusraw quotes selected passages from Abū Bakr al-Rāzī on pleasure and on the eternity of matter, space, and time, and then replies to them.[^V4-307]

Khusraw himself was also an Ismāʿīlī missionary, active in Khurasan during the reign of the Fatimid caliph al-Mustanṣir bi-llāh.[^V4-308]

His response to Abū Bakr al-Rāzī’s views in this document, however, consists mainly of rejecting and attacking the doctrine rather than proving why it should be rejected.

It seems to us that the Ismāʿīlīs attacked Abū Bakr al-Rāzī’s ideas so strongly mainly because they were defending the concept of the Imamate,[^V4-309] which formed the ideological foundation of the Fatimid state.

There is good reason to think so.

All the Ismāʿīlī documents we have mentioned were produced while the Fatimid state was still in existence and actively trying to extend its control over areas that remained under Abbasid rule.

It is also no accident that Ismāʿīlī missionary activity during this same period was especially active in Persia, Central Asia, and Transoxiana.

Nor is it a coincidence that the two Ismāʿīlī missionaries, Abū Ḥātim al-Rāzī and Ḥamīd al-Kirmānī, focused their attacks on Abū Bakr al-Rāzī’s complete rejection of prophecy and miracles.

Later, the Ismāʿīlī historian ʿImād al-Dīn Idrīs ibn al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAbd Allāh (d. 872 AH)[^V4-310] wrote enthusiastically in his history of the Ismāʿīlī mission, *The Wellsprings of Reports and the Arts of Tradition*, about the miracles of the Fatimid ruler al-Ḥākim bi-Amr Allāh and about the efforts of the Ismāʿīlī missionary Ḥamīd al-Kirmānī to fight Abū Bakr al-Rāzī’s rejection of miracles.[^V4-311]

Still, fairness requires us to acknowledge a major debt to Nāṣir Khusraw.

By summarizing Abū Bakr al-Rāzī’s ideas about matter, space, and time before replying to them, he preserved those ideas for us.

We owe an equal debt to the Orientalist Kraus, who translated and published this material separately in a careful scholarly edition.

Because of this, we are able to examine an important link in the development of Arab philosophy, one in which a new form of philosophical materialism appeared during the Islamic Middle Ages.

From the summaries preserved by Nāṣir Khusraw, we can see that Abū Bakr al-Rāzī’s creative experimental work in medicine, chemistry, and natural science led him to reshape the metaphysics of Plato and Plotinus—especially the idea of emanation—in a new materialist direction.

He did not completely remove its original idealist elements. But he reduced some of the more extreme idealist features that appeared in the work of other Arab philosophers of his own time.

His step in this direction was not limited to saying that matter, space, and time were eternal.

He also treated matter as a category of existence, which he called “absolute matter,” and saw it as the conceptual expression of a material existence that he called “particular matter.”

Absolute matter, in his view, exists eternally in actuality and later takes the form of bodies.

Does this mean that, for al-Rāzī, essence comes before existence?

Nāṣir Khusraw’s summaries[^V4-312] suggest instead that essence and existence are inseparable.

Al-Rāzī says that “absolute matter consists of nothing but indivisible parts, each of which has magnitude.”[^V4-313]

His reason is that if none of those parts had magnitude, then putting them together could never produce something that did have magnitude.

He explains the differences among the four material elements by differences in how densely the parts of matter are combined.

When the parts of matter are packed together most densely, they form the substance of earth.

When they are less dense than earth, they form water.

When they are more dispersed than water, they form air.

And when they are more dispersed than air, they form fire.[^V4-314]

He also believes that the elements can change into one another on this same basis: differences in density.

Water becomes earth when it grows denser. When it becomes less dense, it turns into air.

Air, in turn, becomes water when the material parts within it grow denser, and becomes fire when their density decreases.[^V4-315]

This is how he explains bodies in the earthly world.

The heavenly bodies, in his view, are also made from parts of matter, but their composition differs from that of bodies in our world.

He explains this difference through the movement of the heavens.

The movement of the celestial sphere, he says, is not directed toward the center of the world but toward its outer regions. This is because its body is not as densely packed as the substance of earth, which settles into a confined place, nor is it as dispersed as the substance of fire and air, which escapes from such confined places.[^V4-316]

On the basis of this difference between the natural motion of the celestial sphere and the natural motion of earthly bodies, al-Rāzī explains why the first moves in a circle while the second moves in straight lines.

In other words, he explains the difference on a material rather than a spiritual basis, unlike the tradition that had dominated ancient philosophy before his time.

One of the most important parts of al-Rāzī’s doctrine is the argument he uses to defend the eternity of matter.

His starting point is that nothing can come from nothing, “because reason does not accept such a claim.”[^V4-317]

Therefore, he argues, “natural things must come into being from something, and that thing must be eternal.”[^V4-318]

There is another point of major importance for his method.

Al-Rāzī argues that things—that is, material beings—come into existence through combination, not through creation from nothing.

He reaches this view after setting out a number of logical premises.

Those premises would lead to the conclusion that all things owe their existence to the Maker of the world through creation rather than combination.

But al-Rāzī believes that this logical conclusion conflicts with the conclusion reached through “complete induction”: namely, that “nothing comes into being in the world except through combination from those primary elements whose origin is matter.”[^V4-319]

This means that reality judges the logical conclusion, not the other way around.

That is a materialist methodological position, and it is especially important for our present study.

The absolute matter from which combinations arise, meanwhile, “was not itself combined, but dispersed.”[^V4-320]

In Abū Bakr al-Rāzī’s *Philosophical Life*, we find a reference to a work of his titled *That the Body Has Motion from Itself and That Motion Is Known*.[^V4-321]

Perhaps de Boer had this title in mind when he wrote that al-Rāzī departed from the followers of Aristotle by arguing that “the body contains within itself the principle of motion.”[^V4-322]

Yet Abū Bakr al-Rāzī reaches these positions through idealist ideas drawn from several different sources: Platonic, Plotinian, and Gnostic.

Very little in them can clearly be traced to specifically Islamic origins, apart from his belief in a Maker of the world who possesses “perfect knowledge and wisdom and is never subject to error or neglect.”

For the first time in Arab philosophy, we also find no clear sign here of the traditional commitment to absolute divine transcendence, or *tawḥīd* understood as complete separation between God and the world.

Instead, we find an eternal and necessary relationship connecting our world with God in two ways: through the soul and through reason.

The soul gives life to the bodies of our world, just as light flows from the disk of the sun. This is the Plotinian element.

It does so with God’s participation.

The soul itself is ignorant. It does not know things but simply experiences them.

God knows that the soul is drawn toward matter, that it loves matter, and that it hates leaving bodies behind. The soul becomes so attached to them that it forgets itself and its original world. Here we find the Platonic element.

God helps the soul satisfy this desire by arranging matter into different combinations: the heavens, the elements, and the bodies of animals.

All of these are composed in the most perfect way.

Yet earthly bodies still contain a degree of corruption and evil in their very substance. This cannot be removed because it follows necessarily from natural laws, which must take their course according to the principle of causality.

As for reason, God sends it to the soul so that the soul may remember its original world—again a Platonic idea—and understand that as long as it remains in the material world, it cannot escape suffering.

The soul then longs to return to its first world and free itself from this suffering. Here we find the Gnostic element.
