We therefore see the connection between God and the world working in two opposite directions.

One direction moves toward matter, as God helps the soul fulfill its desire to become attached to it.

The other moves away from matter, toward freedom from that attachment.

There is therefore a downward movement and an upward movement.[^V4-323]

In both directions we find a kind of divine presence connecting God with the world.

We do not find, in this doctrine, a clear idea of a divine unity that stands completely above and apart from the world.

Abū Bakr al-Rāzī’s theory of space can be summarized as follows.

Space is infinite, and it is eternal, because everything infinite is eternal.

Matter is also eternal.

Since matter is made up of indivisible parts, and since each of those parts has a magnitude so small that it cannot itself be divided, and since every eternal thing that has magnitude must exist somewhere, it follows that space must also be eternal.

Al-Rāzī offers another argument for the eternity of space.

Nothing in the world, he says, comes into being except from something else. This proves that creation from nothing is impossible and that God cannot bring something into existence from nothing.

If creation from nothing is impossible, matter must therefore be eternal.

And since matter must exist in space, space too must be eternal.

Abū Bakr al-Rāzī also says that anything occupying a place has its individual parts spread through particular space, while the whole thing exists in absolute space.

By “particular space,” he means the area occupied by a body whose outer surface contains another body.[^V4-324]

He therefore distinguishes between two kinds of space: absolute space and particular space.

He also calls the second kind “relational” space, meaning relative space, because it is measured according to its relationship with the thing occupying it.

In this theory, it appears to be absolute space rather than relative space that is eternal.

At the same time, absolute space remains inseparable from actual existence, much as absolute matter does in Abū Bakr al-Rāzī’s theory.

Nāṣir Khusraw’s account[^V4-325] of those who believed that space was eternal—including Abū Bakr al-Rāzī—also suggests that their view was not far from understanding space as a form in which matter exists, even though they did not state the idea directly.

From this account, we understand that “there is no empty void in the universe,” even though they themselves sometimes call space “the void.”

To explain what they mean, they say that something far from another thing can later become close to it, and something close can later become far away.

But distance itself does not become nearness, and nearness itself does not become distance.

For example, if two people stand ten cubits apart, the distance between them is ten cubits.

They may then move toward each other until no distance remains between them.

But the two places where they originally stood are still ten cubits apart.

If the two people leave those places, air or some other body will occupy them instead.

The distance between the two places therefore neither increases nor decreases from what it was before.[^V4-326]

We can draw two conclusions from this.

First, distances between bodies are objective features of the relationships between them. They are not purely subjective.

This is a dialectical way of understanding the relationship between bodies and space.

Second, there is no empty void in the universe. Everything is filled with matter in motion.

We can see in this a very early form of the idea that space is one of the forms in which matter exists.

Dialectical materialist philosophy later gave this idea a scientific formulation on the basis of modern science, especially the natural sciences.

It was further confirmed by Einstein’s theory of relativity, which established an inseparable relationship between matter and space.

As for time, Nāṣir Khusraw also describes the position taken by this school.

He says that the group of philosophers who believed matter and space to be eternal also held that time is an eternal, extended substance.

They rejected the claim that time is simply the number of a body’s movements.

If time were only the number of bodily movements, they argued, two moving bodies could not move during the same period of time while making different numbers of movements.

Khusraw states explicitly that Muḥammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī believed time to be a flowing substance—that is, something extended and in motion.[^V4-327]

This was also the position of the other philosophers in this group.

From Nāṣir Khusraw, we understand that those who followed the doctrine represented by al-Rāzī defended both the eternity of time and its status as a substance by arguing that when a thing that exists in time disappears, its own time disappears with it, just as the time of a person ends when that person dies.[^V4-328]

What we can take from this is that they saw a necessary connection between the existence of things and the existence of time, treating it as a natural law.

For this reason, they linked the eternity of time and space with the eternity of matter.

In other words, they correctly understood that matter has an objective relationship with both time and space.

#### 4 — One Language for Many Peoples

Ibn Sīnā’s intellectual life also brings us to a new historical reality that must have a place among the preliminary points of this study.

What matters most for us here is that Ibn Sīnā never visited an Arab country.

His vast and wide-ranging education was completed in the region of Bukhara in Central Asia.

In the autobiography that he dictated himself,[^V4-329] he tells us how, while staying at the court of Nūḥ ibn Manṣūr al-Sāmānī, ruler of Bukhara, he was allowed to enter the palace library.

He describes a building with many rooms.

In each room, chests of books were stacked one above another.

One room contained books on Arabic and poetry. Another contained books on jurisprudence. Each field of knowledge had its own room.

Ibn Sīnā tells us that he examined the catalogue of the “books of the ancients”[^V4-330]—which, we believe, means the books translated into Arabic during the early Abbasid period.

He asked for the books he needed, read them, discovered what they had to offer, and learned the standing of each scholar in his own field.

We also know the names of many people who studied under Ibn Sīnā in the different cities where he lived across the Iranian regions of Central Asia and Persia—Isfahan, Rayy, Hamadan, and elsewhere.

We also know the names of scientists and philosophers whose fame continued across both medieval and modern history: al-Bīrūnī, al-Khwārizmī, al-Jūzjānī, and others.

All of them came from regions and peoples that were not Arab in origin.

These details, to which history adds many others, express in a highly concentrated form the historical reality we mentioned at the beginning of this section.

By Ibn Sīnā’s time, the culture of Arab-Islamic society had developed flourishing centers far beyond Baghdad, the original center from which its great expansion had begun.

The most important feature of this new reality was that a great number of peoples, spread across different geographical regions and continents, speaking different languages, belonging to different ethnic groups, and possessing very different historical and cultural backgrounds, managed to find a single language they could share.

This linguistic unity went far beyond ordinary communication in everyday life.

It grew deep and broad enough to create a shared culture among all these peoples—a culture capable of expressing some of the deepest and highest demands of human thought.

It also gave the different classes of this vast society many ways to express their own class ideologies, according to the level that class struggle had reached at each stage in the society’s development.

Arabic deserves credit for becoming the center around which this broad cultural unity formed.

But these different peoples deserve equal credit for enriching Arabic through the achievements of the great scientists and philosophers who came from among them, in both East and West, across Asia, Africa, and Europe.

This was an early historical experiment for which we find no real parallel in ancient or medieval human history, especially in the lasting effect it had on the scientific, philosophical, and literary heritage that formed a living bridge between the ancient and modern periods of human knowledge.

When we now turn to Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy, we cannot help seeing this historical reality reflected in Ibn Sīnā himself.

Indeed, it appears in him in a more concentrated and complex form than in many other scientists and philosophers who belonged to Arab culture without themselves being of Arab descent.

Ibn Sīnā emerged in one of those centers where the culture of Arab-Islamic society had flourished outside its original heartland.

Arabic was the language of the sciences he studied.

It was the language in which he taught his students in every Persian city where he lived.

And it was the language he used for science, philosophy, poetry, and most of his writing.[^V4-331]

In one of these centers, Ibn Sīnā found the great library at the court of the Samanid ruler of Bukhara.

He himself described it with amazement.

There was a room for Arabic—that is, Arabic grammar—and poetry, another for jurisprudence, another for the philosophy of the ancients, and so on, with each field of knowledge given its own place.

In one of these flourishing cultural centers, Ibn Sīnā also found a group of students who learned from him and then added their own work to the common language, enriching it further in science, philosophy, and literature.

He also found contemporary scientists and philosophers such as the great al-Bīrūnī, who left Arabic a body of knowledge that still holds an important place in the history of human thought.

#### 5 — Ibn Sīnā: His Personal Logic

A philosopher, like an artist, has a personal logic of his own.

This does not contradict the principle that philosophers and artists think within their own historical period and social environment rather than independently of them.

In the end, the two things are connected.

Even a philosopher’s or artist’s personal way of thinking does not form outside the conditions of a particular society and time.

This point matters because, although Ibn Sīnā followed both the internal logic of Arab philosophy’s development and its external logic as part of the history of Arab-Islamic society, he also had a personal logic that can sometimes look like an exception to both.

To begin with, Ibn Sīnā grew up in a household with Ismāʿīlī beliefs and sympathies, as he himself tells us.[^V4-332]

Yet he remained intellectually independent of his Ismāʿīlī father and brother.

He listened to them discussing the doctrine, and they tried to persuade him to accept it, but he did not simply adopt their position.

That is the first point.

Second, Ibn Sīnā entered the world of politics in a way that differed from his predecessors.

He even took part in government two or three times.[^V4-333]

For long periods he enjoyed political influence and a luxurious way of life.

Yet here too he remained independent.

He did not adopt the ideas of the rulers around him.

He did not turn his philosophy into an expression of the ruling class’s ideology.

And he did not allow political life to overwhelm his intellectual work.

He continued teaching and writing even while traveling,[^V4-334] and spent his nights debating with his students and writing his books.[^V4-335]

It can almost seem as though his nights and days belonged to two separate lives: one intellectual and the other political.

That is the second point.

Third, his involvement in politics did not compromise him in another sense.

It did not take control of his personality.

He did not sell his ideas or his intellectual dignity to princes and sultans in return for status, office, and comfort, even though he was a man who loved the pleasures of life.[^V4-336]

At one point, for example, he refused the post of vizier under Majd al-Dawla ibn Shams al-Dawla al-Būyī, choosing instead to risk wandering and imprisonment.[^V4-337]

Earlier, he had also refused to join the court of Sultan Maḥmūd of Ghazna when Maḥmūd ordered him and a group of other scholars—including al-Bīrūnī—to enter his service.

This happened at a time when Maḥmūd had reached the height of his victories over the Buyids and Samanids in Persia and Bukhara.[^V4-338]

Ibn Sīnā refused even though he was then living on very modest means from an allowance arranged for him by his friend Abū al-Ḥusayn al-Sahlī, minister to Prince ʿAlī ibn Maʾmūn in Khwarazm.[^V4-339]

And when Maḥmūd of Ghazna invited Ibn Sīnā and the other scholars to his court, the stated reason was “to honor himself through their presence and to benefit from their knowledge and abilities.”[^V4-340]

Joining Maḥmūd would therefore almost certainly have brought Ibn Sīnā wealth and a respected position.

Why, then, did he refuse?

The historical sources do not answer the question.

But perhaps we can explain his refusal in a way that fits not only his personal character but also the ideological content of his philosophy.

Maḥmūd of Ghazna was known as a strong supporter of conservative traditional Sunni doctrine.

He actively promoted it throughout the Islamic world and opposed the intellectual, social, and religious movements that challenged the Abbasid authority associated with that doctrine.

For this reason, Maḥmūd was particularly hostile to the Muʿtazila, the Shīʿa, the Qarmaṭians, and the Ismāʿīlīs.[^V4-341]

We have little doubt that Ibn Sīnā would not have accepted a position at Maḥmūd’s court if it meant being forced to take part in persecuting any of these groups or to support the official Sunni ideology.

Perhaps this and similar episodes in Ibn Sīnā’s life are what led de Boer to say that Ibn Sīnā “never bowed his head to a prince.”[^V4-342]

In our view, that judgment is correct.

There is a fourth point that belongs to Ibn Sīnā’s personal logic: his attitude toward life itself.

He loved life passionately.

He took a generous share of every pleasure it offered, whether physical, intellectual, or social.

He fully enjoyed the pleasures and beauty available to his senses: sex, music, singing, and wine.

He also satisfied his intellectual curiosity through scientific research, both theoretical and experimental, and fulfilled his ambition for political office and high social standing. As a result, his outlook on life was optimistic, and this optimism appears in many of his philosophical views. He believed, for example, that the world is arranged in a beautiful and remarkable order; that good outweighs evil in the world around us; that even the evil found in this world exists for the sake of some good; and that existence is itself good, while nonexistence is a greater evil than any evil that comes with existence.[^V4-343]

This also explains why, in his theory of pleasure, Ibn Sīnā did not look down on physical pleasure in the way ascetics and Sufis did. As he puts it: “Not all pleasure is sensory. Some pleasures are not sensory at all, and sensory pleasures do not come close to them.”[^V4-344]

This statement first recognizes sensory pleasure as something real and acceptable, rather than something shameful or contemptible. Second, it implies that most pleasures—not all of them—are sensory, while the rest are exceptional and therefore relatively rare, even if these rarer pleasures are higher in rank.

But Ibn Sīnā then qualifies even this distinction by defining pleasure in a way that places all kinds of pleasure, sensory and non-sensory, on the same basic level. In that sense, they are equal in value.

His definition is that “pleasure is the perception of what is fitting.” He then explains that “what is fitting is whatever helps complete the essence of a thing and bring its activity to fulfillment. What is fitting to the senses is what perfects the essence of sensation; and what is fitting to anger, desire, imagination, thought, and memory is fitting in each case according to the same principle.”[^V4-344]

So anything that is fitting in this sense—anything that helps complete the nature of a thing—is a pleasure. On this basis, one pleasure is not better than another except according to this same measure.

#### 6 — Ibn Sīnā: His Class Position

There is another preliminary question we need to address, because it is important for this study. Identifying a philosopher’s class position can help us understand the direction of his philosophy.

That does not mean the relationship is simple. The way a person’s class position appears in philosophical work depends on extremely complicated conditions. This is mainly because philosophy itself is a particularly complex form of human consciousness.

There is another complication as well. If a person’s class position does not come directly from his place in material production, it can be fluid rather than fixed. It may shift within the social structure as circumstances change. This is often true of the middle social groups connected with, or descended from, sectors such as services.

Even this rule, however, cannot be applied rigidly to scientists, philosophers, artists, or cultural producers in general. Knowledge can reveal objective truths, and those truths can lead a scientist, philosopher, or artist toward a class position different from the one he originally came from. Through knowledge and intellectual work, he may come to express the outlook of a class he has consciously sided with. Intellectual commitment can then become an emotional commitment as well.

With this in mind, let us return to Ibn Sīnā and ask about his class origins.

From what we know of his life and family, his father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sīnā, worked as a money changer in the town of Kharmaythan before Nūḥ ibn Manṣūr, the Samanid ruler of Bukhara, appointed him to an administrative position there. We also know that his father was not originally from the town, but had come from Balkh.[^V4-345]

This gives us a starting point for identifying Ibn Sīnā’s social background.

He came from a socially mobile group whose position could shift as its ambitions and circumstances changed. His family then moved into administrative and political office.

Al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sīnā grew up in a comfortable household that also had direct ties to two important forces in the political and intellectual life of Arab-Islamic society: the Samanid state and the Ismāʿīlī movement.

The two had something in common. Both stood at some distance from the Abbasid caliphate, and we might even say that both had a negative attitude toward its authority. But they differed in important ways.

The Samanid state had a national character and sought a measure of political independence from the authority of the caliphate, whose rule had an Arab character. Ismāʿīlism, by contrast, contained from its beginnings a universalist tendency that aimed to overcome religious and national divisions. At the same time, it sought to extend the authority of the Fatimid state across the different regions of the Islamic world.

Ibn Sīnā’s family was active, both politically and intellectually, between these two opposing forces.

For the son of this family—the man who would later become known as Ibn Sīnā, the “Shaykh al-Raʾīs”—this background made it possible to devote himself fully to the sciences of his time from a very young age. He first studied with private teachers in the family home, and later continued largely on his own through an extraordinary amount of personal effort.

This reliance on himself may have helped give him the independence of mind to resist Ismāʿīlī influence, even though Ismāʿīlī teachings had been around him from the very beginning of his intellectual life. He heard them discussed both inside his home and in the wider world around him.

Another detail is worth mentioning when we consider Ibn Sīnā’s class position. His first teacher in arithmetic and geometry was an ordinary man from Bukhara who worked as a grocer. His name was Maḥmūd al-Massāḥ.

It seems that Ibn Sīnā went to this man for lessons at the grocer’s own place of work rather than receiving him at the family home.[^V4-346]

By repeatedly visiting this teacher in one of the city markets, Ibn Sīnā must have gained some impressions of the lives of ordinary working people. Those impressions may have stayed with him and formed one small part of the many influences that shaped the social outlook we later find in *The Healing*.[^V4-347]

That outlook suggests that Ibn Sīnā saw the unemployment of working people, and their enforced removal from useful work, as both a social defect and an injustice to those left without work.

In his picture of a well-ordered city, therefore, unemployment is forbidden. No one is allowed to remain idle. There should be no “person without work and without a defined place; rather, every one of them should have some useful role in the city.”[^V4-348]

He is equally strict in insisting that usury must be rooted out of the city—that is, out of society.

Still, we probably come closest to Ibn Sīnā’s actual social position if we place him among the independent professions, above all as a physician.

The most striking fact in his life is that medicine repeatedly changed his circumstances. It was medicine that first drew him into the risks of political life, which brought him hardship and trouble as well as comfort and privilege.

It was through medicine alone that he first gained access to the court of the ruler of Bukhara, Nūḥ ibn Manṣūr. This gave him access to the ruler’s library, from which, as Ibn Sīnā himself tells us, he learned a great deal.[^V4-349]

His father later died, and, as Ibn Sīnā puts it, “circumstances carried me from place to place.”

One of those changes in circumstance was his departure from Bukhara. He left because Sultan Maḥmūd of Ghazna wanted him to join the scholars at his court, and Ibn Sīnā chose to escape instead. After leaving Bukhara and moving through many different regions, he continued to support himself through his work as a physician.

The historian al-Bayhaqī tells us about another escape, this time from ʿAlāʾ al-Dawla ibn Kākawayh, who had become angry with him.

Ibn Sīnā went to Rayy in disguise, “with nothing on him that he could spend on himself. He entered the market in search of a living until he came upon a refined young man who had set up a place at the door of his house”—in other words, what we today would call a doctor’s clinic—“and a great crowd had gathered around him” for treatment.[^V4-350]

A woman then showed Ibn Sīnā a *tafsira*.[^V4-351] He said to her, “This *tafsira* belongs to a Jew.” She admitted that it did, and the story continues from there.[^V4-352]

Before his connection with ʿAlāʾ al-Dawla, Ibn Sīnā had also served two brothers from the Buyid ruling house, Majd al-Dawla and Shams al-Dawla. Once again, medicine was what brought him into contact with them.

It was through medicine that he first became vizier under Shams al-Dawla, and through medicine that he later returned to the same office for a second time under the same ruler.[^V4-353]

His famous medical work *The Canon* gives us further evidence that his relationship with medicine was not simply academic. The book is full of observations, experiments, and medical discoveries that came from practical work and direct experience.

So we are not far from the truth if we define Ibn Sīnā’s basic social position in terms of his profession.

This will matter when we turn to his philosophy.

His philosophy expressed the outlook of a number of different social groups that together formed one side of the main social conflict of his time. Opposing them stood the principal privileged upper group, which represented feudal power in its specifically eastern historical form.

The differences between the middle and lower groups, however, were still not sharply defined.

### B — Ibn Sīnā’s Theory

#### 1 — Ibn Sīnā’s Method

We should begin by establishing how Ibn Sīnā went about searching for “truth.” This will help us understand the method he used to deal with the intellectual and ideological problems that his historical period placed before philosophy, especially the problem of existence and essence.

Now that many of Ibn Sīnā’s major works have been published and we have reliable access to his actual texts, there can be little doubt that his method was based on direct experience of the subject he was studying.

There can also be little doubt that this approach grew above all out of his medical research and experiments.[^V4-354] This helps explain why he often relied on induction: beginning with observed cases and using them to reach broader conclusions, both theoretical and practical.

We should make an important distinction here.

Ibn Sīnā’s experimental method is different from the modern philosophical doctrine usually called empiricism.

Empiricism, in the sense meant here, treats sensory knowledge as the only road to truth. It reduces the role of thought to a simple mechanical process of collecting what the senses provide, without using sensory evidence to reach general rational conclusions through conceptual abstraction.

What it calls a universal concept, in this view, is little more than “the result of counting individual cases.”[^V4-355]

This is the doctrine Lenin attacked in his philosophical work *Materialism and Empirio-Criticism*.[^V4-356]

Ibn Sīnā’s experimental approach moves in a different direction. He uses the results of sensory experience as material for theoretical reasoning and for drawing general conclusions.[^V4-357]

When he discusses experiment itself, he also lays down general rules for it and sets conditions that must be met before its results can safely be generalized.

At the same time, he does not reject deductive reasoning. In medicine, for example, he treats it as one of two ways of discovering the properties of drugs.[^V4-358]

This experimental method, rooted in practical work in the natural sciences and especially in medicine, also appears very clearly in Ibn Sīnā’s logic.

We can see this in his theory of definition.

Aristotle’s “complete definition” identifies a thing through its genus and its distinguishing difference. In other words, it defines the thing through the fixed essence that makes it what it is.

Ibn Sīnā does not always accept this restriction.

Because he gives such importance to experience, he also pays attention to the particular, observable features through which things actually exist. His definitions therefore tend to give more weight to a thing’s real and changing existence, rather than focusing only on its fixed essence.

As he puts it, “a thing may be considered in itself”—meaning in terms of its essence—“or it may be considered according to a condition that is incidental to it or inseparable from it.”[^V4-359]

He also says that “inseparable features are what constitute existence.” If a descriptive definition[^V4-360] is built from features that help constitute a thing’s existence, even though they do not constitute its essence or concept, then causes and effects can enter into that definition as features and consequences of the thing’s existence, even though they are not part of its essence or concept.[^V4-361]

What Ibn Sīnā means is that causal relationships belong to the actual existence of things. A thing can therefore sometimes be defined through the causes of its existence rather than only through the elements that make up its essence, “because its substance is connected with those causes.”[^V4-362]

So Ibn Sīnā does not think a definition must always be based on essence alone. He also considers how the thing actually exists.

He even argues that some causes must already exist before a thing’s essence can be realized at all.

As he puts it: “Causes exist before the essence exists. Many things are defined not in terms of what they are in themselves”—that is, their essences—“but in terms of the accidents, accompanying features, and relations that belong to them.”[^V4-363]

This method may also have helped Ibn Sīnā see that Aristotelian logic is not purely formal. We must also consider the material that thought is dealing with.[^V4-364]

Starting from this understanding of logic, he added new kinds of propositions to the familiar logical forms. He called them “types of existential propositions.”[^V4-365]

These are propositions in which the necessary connection between the subject and what is said about it depends on the connection that actually exists between them in the external world, even when that connection does not belong to the subject’s essence.

Here again, Ibn Sīnā takes account of how things actually exist in the real world, even in a field where logicians had often treated them only as abstract categories in the mind.

This is another reflection of his experimental method in his work on logic, just as the same method clearly appears in his studies of nature.

Using this same approach, he sets out a practical principle while discussing the natural elements and their four qualities:

“Our affirmation that four elements exist does not rest on division alone, but on a division followed by existence. When the intellect arrives at something through division and existence then confirms it, nothing can be clearer than that.”[^V4-366]

In other words, reason is not more evident than reality itself.

We will see later how this same method also shaped Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy.

#### 2 — Theory of Knowledge

We can now move directly from Ibn Sīnā’s experimental method to his theory of knowledge.

The question of knowledge is where this method faces its most demanding test, because this is where we see how far he actually applies it.

The extent to which he follows the method here also helps us judge how consistently he follows it in other philosophical questions connected with knowledge: the relation between the universal and the individual, the relation between existence and essence, and Ibn Sīnā’s philosophical view of the relationship between the human being and God.

The central problem that philosophy always faces in the theory of knowledge can be divided into two parts.

The first is how far human beings are capable of knowing the world.

The second is how knowledge is acquired: what methods, tools, and human or nonhuman sources people rely on in gaining it.

A thinker’s position on the basic philosophical question—the relationship between being and consciousness—also helps determine what he means by the “truth” that knowledge is trying to reach.

To understand Ibn Sīnā’s position on both sides of the problem, we have to draw mainly on the many passages he wrote about the soul. These are scattered through his major works—*The Healing*, *The Salvation*, and *Pointers and Reminders*—as well as through a number of shorter works devoted specifically to the soul.

One thing stands out in these writings. Ibn Sīnā repeatedly gives special attention to the problem of how we know universals and intelligible concepts. The subject seems to have occupied him to an unusual degree.

For that reason, we have followed his psychology closely in order to draw out the main lines of his theory of knowledge.

If we begin with the first side of the problem, several questions immediately arise.

Does Ibn Sīnā believe that human beings can know the essence of all things?

Is there anything in the world that human beings cannot know according to his philosophy?

What does he mean by the “truth” that knowledge seeks?

Does an objective truth exist in the world?

And what is this truth that forms the content of knowledge?

These questions matter not only for understanding Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge itself. Their greater importance is that the answers will open the way to identifying the basic direction of his philosophy—if they do not already determine it.

It is extremely difficult to find clear and final answers to these questions simply by reading Ibn Sīnā’s texts at face value.

Most of those texts are written in metaphysical forms inherited from the philosophical traditions that had already shaped Arab philosophy before him. These traditions combined influences from Platonism, Aristotelianism, and Neoplatonism, though the most obvious influence, as is well known, came through the work called the *Theology*, which ultimately derived from Plotinus’s *Enneads*.

This gives bourgeois scholars of Arab philosophy room to interpret Ibn Sīnā as a purely idealist philosopher, and especially to answer the questions above in ways that fit their own idealist outlook.

Ibn Sīnā himself made such readings possible because, objectively, he remained connected with the philosophical structure he had inherited from his Islamic and Greek predecessors.

Still, this difficulty can be overcome.

First, we need to look at Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy as a whole.

Second, we need to get behind the traditional metaphysical form of his language and reach the real content it contains.

But we must do this without stepping outside the historical conditions that set definite limits on both the content of his thought and the form in which he expressed it.

With this method in mind, we can proceed by starting with the texts themselves and then moving into their deeper content.

Which texts should we choose?

In fact, we have little choice.

The key to the whole problem lies in the passages where Ibn Sīnā discusses the tools of knowledge available to human beings. These texts bring us directly to the second side of his theory of knowledge: the way knowledge is acquired.

This also shows how closely the two sides of the problem are connected, and why separating them is mistaken except when we do so temporarily for explanation and analysis.

##### 1 — The Tools of Knowledge

According to Ibn Sīnā’s psychology, human beings possess two main sets of tools for knowledge: the faculties of the human soul and the intellect.

The faculties of the human soul stand at the top of a single, connected hierarchy of the powers of “soul” found in the three kinds of living beings: plants, animals, and humans.

In this hierarchy, the human soul is not simply the highest level. It also contains all the powers found at the lower levels: the powers of the plant and animal “souls.”

These range from nourishment, growth, and reproduction, through sensation, and finally to rational thought.

All these powers are present in the human being through an organic development within the human body.

The faculties that belong specifically to the human soul—the rational soul—are different. No plant or animal faculty shares them.

They represent the highest level reached by “soul” as such.

Ibn Sīnā defines the human soul as “the first perfection of a natural organic body, insofar as it performs actions through thoughtful choice and reaches conclusions through judgment, and insofar as it grasps universal things.”[^V4-367]

This is our starting point.

We have already seen how its elements rise step by step from the lowest powers of the “soul”—or, as we might say, the powers of life—to the highest.

They develop from powers that are simply powers of life into powers that combine life with perception.

Ibn Sīnā includes two elements within perception.

One is practical. It allows action through “thoughtful choice” and “reasoned judgment.”

The other is theoretical. It allows the mind to grasp “universal things.”

So both sides of perception—the practical and the theoretical—are built on a material base: the organic powers of life within the human body.

From this starting point, Ibn Sīnā begins to classify the human faculties of perception.

First come the faculties of sensation. Then come the faculties of rational understanding.

Sensory perception has two kinds: external sensation and internal sensation.

Rational understanding also has two kinds: practical and theoretical.

External sensation works through the familiar five senses: hearing, sight, touch, smell, and taste.

Their function is to perceive sensory forms directly, together with their material relations as they exist in the outside world.

Internal sensation also has five faculties. Ibn Sīnā calls them “the inward faculties of perception.”[^V4-368]

Their functions differ from one another.

Some perceive the forms of sensory things.

Others perceive meanings found in sensory things without perceiving their outward forms.

Still others analyze and combine forms and meanings, separating them from one another or joining them together.

Ibn Sīnā explains the difference between perceiving a form and perceiving a meaning.

The perception of form involves both the external and internal senses, in sequence.

The external sense first perceives something and then passes its form on to the internal sense.

A sheep, for example, first perceives the form of a wolf—its shape, appearance, and color—and then also perceives it internally.

The perception of meaning is different.

Here the internal sense alone grasps something in the sensory object without the external sense directly perceiving it.

A sheep, for example, perceives the threatening meaning of the wolf, or the fact that the wolf is something to fear and flee from, even though none of the external senses directly perceives “hostility” or “danger” as such.[^V4-369]

Ibn Sīnā arranges the five internal senses according to the role each one plays in the upward movement of perception toward its highest stage: thought, or rational understanding.

The first internal faculty is what Ibn Sīnā calls *fantasia*.[^V4-370] By this he means the common sense.

He describes it as the faculty that receives all the forms impressed on the five external senses and passed on from them to this inner faculty.[^V4-371]

After the common sense comes the imagination, which he also calls the form-retaining faculty.

Its job is to preserve what the common sense has received from the five external senses, so that these images remain even after the sensory objects themselves are no longer present.[^V4-372]

Next comes the imaginative faculty.

Its role is to combine and separate. It can join together the images preserved in the imagination, or separate them from one another, according to choice.[^V4-373]

After this comes the faculty Ibn Sīnā calls estimation.

This faculty perceives meanings that are not themselves sensory, but that exist in particular sensory things. It is, for example, the faculty that judges that the wolf is something the sheep must flee from, or that a child is someone toward whom a human being feels affection.[^V4-374]

The process of internal perception ends with the fifth faculty, the retentive memory.

Its job is to preserve “the nonsensory meanings found in particular external sensory things” once the estimative faculty has perceived them.[^V4-375]

At this point, the process of thought has received the basic material it needs from both external and internal sensory perception—or, in Ibn Sīnā’s terminology, from the outer and inner senses.

The way is now prepared for rational understanding.

This is the specifically human stage, belonging to the human rational soul.

The earlier levels of sensory perception are shared by human beings and other animals. This is why Ibn Sīnā calls them the “faculties of the animal soul.”[^V4-376]

So what happens when rational understanding begins?

The forms of perception we have just described provide its foundation.

Rational understanding remains connected with them and represents their highest development.

According to the texts we are examining, human beings cannot reach rational knowledge outside this ascending sequence that begins with external and internal perception—with the outer and inner senses.

Rational understanding itself has two faculties: one practical and one knowing, or, in other words, the practical intellect and the theoretical intellect.

The practical intellect has an active character.

It is “a principle of movement for the human body” and directs the body toward particular actions that involve deliberation.

Its activity is therefore joined to thought and reflection, and proceeds “according to judgments particular to it and based on convention.”[^V4-377]

With this new element of deliberation—of looking, thinking, and judging—we begin to see the objective difference between human perception and the perception of other animals.

From this point onward, perception takes on its highest form, because choice and will now enter into it.

Ibn Sīnā therefore describes the practical intellect as the faculty that “works out what ought to be done in particular human matters, so that chosen aims may be achieved, using primary, commonly accepted, and experimental premises.”[^V4-378]

He also reinforces the link between this higher form of perception and the sensory forms that came before it.

He says that practical understanding can be considered in three relations: “in relation to the animal appetitive faculty,”[^V4-379] in relation to the animal imaginative or estimative faculty, and in relation to itself.[^V4-380]

In the first relation, practical reason produces states that prepare a person for quick action or reaction, such as shame, laughter, crying, and similar responses.

In the second, it is used to work out practical arrangements for everyday human life and to develop human crafts and skills.

In the third relation, practical intellect becomes connected with theoretical intellect.

Through this connection, practical intellect gains knowledge of opinions widely accepted among people.

These are socially shaped judgments connected with general moral standards: for example, that lying is bad, injustice is bad, and so on.[^V4-381]

There is an important point here for understanding Ibn Sīnā’s moral philosophy.

As we can see, he connects ethics with practical intellect rather than theoretical intellect.

This means that moral standards are drawn from changing practical life rather than from fixed and unchanging rules.

This is a correct insight, because morality is in reality part of society’s superstructure. Its standards change as the underlying social structure changes.

There is a second important point in the relationship between the practical and theoretical intellects.

It suggests that Ibn Sīnā understood, in some form, the dialectical content of this relationship.

He says that the faculty called the practical intellect uses the theoretical intellect to reach a universal judgment and then carries that judgment down to the particular case.[^V4-382]

When we analyze this idea, we find that it points toward what dialectical materialism says in the theory of knowledge about the mutual interaction between living observation, thought, and practice, as Lenin expressed it.[^V4-383]

Scientific theory understands this dialectical process as a single connected whole.

Ibn Sīnā, however, could see this unity only by dividing the intellect into a practical and a theoretical side.

We therefore see this division as a form of a basically correct insight that, because of the historical conditions of his time, he could not yet express in the scientific form later developed by dialectical materialism.

Another feature of Ibn Sīnā’s practical intellect is that it is supposed to control the powers of the body.

It must regulate their actions and prevent them from simply following natural impulses without restraint.

For that reason, the practical intellect must also be influenced and guided by the theoretical intellect, so that it has a theoretical understanding of how to direct the body’s natural powers.

Once again, this shows how far Ibn Sīnā understood the relationship between the practical and the theoretical—or, more precisely, between human social activity and human thought—in the production of knowledge.

##### B — The Theoretical Intellect

We now reach the central point of our discussion: the stage at which human perception reaches its highest form and becomes capable of rational knowledge.

Ibn Sīnā calls this form the theoretical intellect.

How does the theoretical intellect produce rational knowledge?

Before answering that, we should ask another question: what exactly do we mean by rational knowledge?

We often speak of sensory knowledge and rational knowledge.

Does that mean there are two separate kinds of knowledge running in parallel, or even opposed to one another?

Not at all.

To separate them in this way would mean separating thought from sensory experience, and therefore cutting thought off from reality. That leads into idealism and metaphysics.

A little earlier, we referred to the idea of a mutual interaction between living observation, thought, and practice.

Living observation is another way of describing sensory experience.

Thought is the mind’s way of turning that sensory experience into concepts, or universals.

Practice then applies those concepts back to reality—the same reality that first supplied the material of sensory experience.

But the point is not simply to repeat the original experience.

By returning concepts to reality through practice, we develop that reality and, in turn, develop the concepts themselves.

Concepts can develop in several ways.

They may be reformulated.

Their existing form may be confirmed, so that their content moves from relative truth toward absolute truth.

Or they may be brought one step closer to absolute truth.

This is the dialectic of the process of knowledge through its different stages.

Throughout this process, the intellect itself gives sensory experience a conceptual form.

That is the basic function of the intellect.

The intellect is therefore a specifically human capacity that allows us to form general concepts and scientific theories.

From this point of view, Ibn Sīnā was right to call it the theoretical intellect.

He may also have meant by the practical intellect something close to what we call sensory experience.

If so—and we make the point for a third time—Ibn Sīnā had grasped, or at least come close to grasping, the dialectical unity between sensory and rational knowledge.

The question now is this:

How consistently did Ibn Sīnā actually follow this unity in his theory of knowledge?

What we have already seen about external and internal sensory perception gives us good reason to think that he was building an empirical foundation for his theory of knowledge.

This would be an application, or reflection, of the method he used in the natural sciences.

There is further evidence in the way he studies the faculties of the soul as a rising sequence, moving from the lowest to the highest: from the powers of the plant “soul” all the way to the theoretical faculty in the human being, which belongs to the rational soul.

It is difficult to doubt that he saw all these faculties as connected within a real unity.

What remains is to see how far he follows this logic, and where it eventually leads him.

He defines the theoretical faculty—the theoretical intellect—as the faculty “whose nature is to receive universal forms abstracted from matter. If the universal form is already abstract in itself, then it receives it as it is. If it is not, then the theoretical faculty makes it abstract by stripping it of its material connections until none of those connections remain.”[^V4-384]

This definition of the theoretical intellect still fits Ibn Sīnā’s general view of the relationship between sensory and rational knowledge.

The intellect really does form universals by abstracting them from their particular material connections.

But one part of Ibn Sīnā’s definition needs closer attention.

He speaks of universal forms that are “abstract in themselves.”

What exactly does he mean?

He may mean objective truths that are not themselves material.

Such things do in fact exist in our world.

We say that the world exists objectively and independently of consciousness. But this does not mean that everything objective is material.

Everything material is objective, but not everything objective is material.

Speech, for example, can have material effects on the consciousness of other people. When it exists, it has an objective existence, but it is not itself material.

If Ibn Sīnā means something like this by forms that are “abstract in themselves,” then he is right. In that case, his definition of the theoretical intellect remains sound as a whole and consistent with the logic we have traced so far.

We cannot rule out the possibility that this meaning formed part of what he had in mind when he gave this definition. There are passages in his works that support such a reading.[^V4-385]

But there is another possibility.

By forms that are “abstract in themselves,” Ibn Sīnā may mean the metaphysical beings that he usually describes as “separate from matter.”

He expresses this idea elsewhere.[^V4-386]

In that passage, he tries to organize the relationship between the practical and theoretical intellects—the practical and theoretical faculties—on a different basis from the one we have just examined.

He begins from the idea that the soul is a single substance, but that it stands in relation to two directions: one below it and one above it.

For each direction, the soul has a corresponding faculty through which that relationship is organized.

The practical faculty belongs to the lower direction.

The theoretical faculty belongs to the higher one.

He then says:

> “It is as though the soul has two faces: one turned toward the body, and this face must never accept any influence of the kind demanded by the body’s nature; and another turned toward the higher principles, and this face must always remain open to what comes from there and be affected by it.”

This view of the soul shows the difficulty Ibn Sīnā faced in explaining the human powers of perception and their relationship to the body. He could see the material side of this relationship and could not deny it. At the same time, he could also see the abstract character of the higher powers of perception. But he could not identify the real, objective basis of either phenomenon. So he turned to the metaphysical explanation we have just seen.

Still, Ibn Sīnā does acknowledge here that the different “psychic” or perceptive phenomena belong to a “single substance.” This is important. He also recognizes—and this is important too—that there is an objective inner contradiction between the underlying substance and the phenomena through which it appears. To resolve this contradiction, he divides the substance itself into two sides, or two “faces.” He links one to the material phenomenon and the other to the abstract phenomenon, though he explains the latter on a metaphysical basis.

Could it be, then, that when he earlier defined the theoretical faculty and spoke of forms that are “abstract in themselves,” he meant the forms of those “higher principles” which, in his view, exist “above” us in the metaphysical world?

Here too, we cannot rule this out.

But the context of this particular “division” is not really about knowledge. It concerns the ethical side of the practical faculty, or practical intellect. This is why Ibn Sīnā says of the soul’s first face, the face turned “toward the body”:

> “…this face must never accept any influence of the kind demanded by the body’s nature.”

The word “must” makes his purpose clear. He is giving ethical guidance: the soul should not submit to the desires of the body. He is not making a scientific statement about the nature of knowledge.

The same applies to what he says about the other face, the one turned “toward the higher principles”:

> “…this face must always remain open to what comes from there.”

What he means is that it should remain open to principles that carry moral guidance about how a person should act.

So this final detour does not leave us with a definite answer. Up to this point, then, we still have no reason to claim that Ibn Sīnā has departed from the sound empirical basis of his theory of rational knowledge.

We must now return to his account of the stages of intellectual perception, beginning with the stage of complete readiness and ending with the “intellect in actuality.” Through these stages, Ibn Sīnā—the “Chief Master”—maps the steps by which the intellect comes to grasp universals.

And this question—**how the intellect grasps universals**—is the central issue for us.

He sets out the stages in the following ascending order:[^V4-387]

1. **The stage of the “material faculty,” or “material intellect.”** This is the human being’s natural capacity to acquire knowledge, like a child’s capacity to learn to write. It is called “material” by analogy with prime matter, which in itself has a form of some kind and serves as the basis for every form.

2. **The stage of the “possible faculty,” or “intellect by disposition.”** A person reaches this stage when the powers of perception have developed enough to grasp no more than the first intelligible principles. These include self-evident rational judgments, such as knowing that the whole is greater than the part, or that things equal to the same thing are equal to one another. This knowledge prepares the mind for the “secondary intelligibles”—that is, knowledge acquired through learning and practice.

3. **The stage of the “intellect in actuality.”** The first condition for this stage is that the primary intelligible form has been stored in the mind, even though the mind “is not actually looking at it or referring back to it … but can look at it whenever it wishes.” The second condition is that when the mind does look at it, it understands it and knows that it understands it without needing to acquire it again.

4. **The stage of the “acquired intellect.”** At this stage, “the intelligible form is present in the intellect; the intellect is actually looking at it and understanding it, and it understands that it is actually understanding it. With the acquired intellect, the animal genus and the human species within it reach completion. At this point, the human faculty comes to resemble the primary principles of existence.”[^V4-388]

##### C — The Source of Theoretical Knowledge

This outline of the stages of intellectual perception is, in itself, a reasonable picture of how such stages develop. If we set aside the terminology and the explanations Ibn Sīnā later attaches to it, we have no objection to the outline as such.

But it does not solve the basic problems of the theory of knowledge.

The important question is not simply what stages Ibn Sīnā thinks the intellect passes through. We first need to know **where the intellect gets its knowledge during these stages**. Second, we need to know **how the intellect turns this knowledge into general concepts, or universals**.

The final sentence quoted above may give us part of the answer to the first question:

> “With the acquired intellect, the animal genus and the human species within it reach completion. At this point, the human faculty comes to resemble the primary principles of existence.”

As we understand it, this means that the human intellect reaches the stage of actively thinking through a connected and integrated process involving sensory perceptions and the mind’s abstraction of those perceptions.

When Ibn Sīnā speaks of the completion of the “animal genus,” he means the completion of sensory perception. When he speaks of the completion within it of the “human species,” he means that these perceptions—which human beings share with other kinds of animals—rise to a new level. The human intellect turns them into an abstract mental form specific to the human species. This happens when the stages of human intellect reach their completion in the “acquired intellect.”

This state of completion places the human intellect at a very high level. It allows the intellect to “resemble the primary principles of existence”—that is, the immaterial intellects that, in Ibn Sīnā’s system, emanated from the First Being.

But the issue here still remains one of **resemblance** to those intellects in their higher power of knowledge. In other words, this resemblance still belongs to the theory of knowledge. It does not yet move the question into the realm of ontology, or the nature of being itself.

That is what we take the passage to mean.[^V4-389]

It therefore gives us part of an answer to the question: **Where does the intellect get its knowledge as the theoretical faculty develops through these stages?**

The answer seems to be this: the intellect draws the material of knowledge from the perceptions it has already gathered. Some of these remain close to sensory reality, while others are further removed from it. The intellect then transforms them into a form specific to the human species: the intelligible form.

But this passage alone is not enough to answer the question fully.

And it does not answer the second, equally pressing question:

**How does the intellect form concepts?**

Ibn Sīnā gives us a number of chapters in *The Salvation* and several concentrated passages in *Pointers and Reminders* that can help answer both questions.

With some rearrangement of their form, his answers can be summarized as follows.

Perception receives the form of the thing perceived while separating that form from matter. This is the general principle.

But the process differs according to the level of perception and according to the material relationship between the perceived thing and its form. Abstraction therefore varies from one level of perception to another, and according to the material relations of what is being perceived.

Let us begin at the beginning.

The **external senses** receive the form together with its actual matter and with the material features that accompany it—such as quantity, quality, place, and position. They also preserve the relationship between these features and the matter itself.

If that relationship disappears, the act of perception is no longer the work of the external senses. It belongs to another faculty. The external senses, Ibn Sīnā says, “do not strip the form from matter in complete separation from all its accompanying features.”

Next comes **imagination**.

This is a new level of perception and therefore a new level of abstraction. Imagination takes the form from the matter reflected in it, but “the form does not need the matter itself to remain present in imagination. Even if the matter is absent or destroyed, the form remains present in imagination.”

In this sense, imagination can separate the form completely from the actual matter.

But that does not mean it separates the form from the material features that accompanied the matter.

The matter itself is no longer present in imagination, but its image remains there in the same sensible form—with a certain size, quality, and position. Imagination cannot produce a completely independent image from which all such features have disappeared.

So imagination separates the form from the actual matter, but not from the features attached to that matter. To conceive general characteristics independently of the characteristics of an individual thing would go beyond imagination’s proper function.

Next comes the **estimative faculty**.

Here abstraction rises another level.

The estimative faculty does not take the material form itself. It takes the **meaning** expressed through the material thing. Such meanings are not themselves material, even though they may exist in material things. Examples include good and evil, agreement and opposition, and similar meanings.

The estimative faculty therefore takes these meanings from material things. But when it needs to preserve their particular character—to distinguish, for example, one particular instance of “good” from another—it makes use of the images already provided by imagination.

Finally, we reach the **intellect**.

Here abstraction reaches its highest point.

The forms established in this highest faculty of perception may belong to things that are not material at all and can never become material. They may belong to things that are not material in themselves but can sometimes occur in matter. Or they may belong to things that are material by their very nature.

Whatever the case, the intellect grasps all these forms by taking them in complete abstraction from matter.

Take its understanding of the human being, for example.

When the intellect understands a human being, it takes the nature that applies to many human beings and separates it from everything that distinguishes one particular person from another—such as quantity, quality, place, and position.

In other words, the intellect takes the general characteristics shared by all human beings and leaves aside the individual characteristics that distinguish one person from another.

Taken together, these shared characteristics form the universal “human being.” This is the intelligible form.

> “This is how sensory judgment, imaginative judgment, estimative judgment, and rational judgment differ from one another.”[^V4-390]

Ibn Sīnā develops the point further in another chapter,[^V4-391] where he stresses that the intellect uses sensory perceptions to form universals from particulars—that is, from things that exist outside the mind.

He expresses this by saying:

> “The animal faculties”—that is, sensory perception—“help the rational soul”—the intellect—“in several ways. One is that sensation presents particulars to it, and from these particulars four things arise for it.”

These four things can be summarized as follows:

1. **The intellect extracts the universal from particular things.** It does this by separating them from their matter and from the material relations and accidental features connected with that matter. Imagination and the estimative faculty assist in this process.

2. **By observing particular things, the intellect[^V4-392] discovers certain relationships between universals.** These relationships may be positive or negative.

3. **By observing particulars, the intellect forms empirical premises that support inductive reasoning.** This gives it confidence that the relationship between a predicate (*Prédicat*) and a subject (*Objet*) is not accidental. The resulting judgment is therefore “a belief produced by sensation and reasoning.”[^V4-393]

   Ibn Sīnā gives the example of the judgment that “scammony naturally purges yellow bile.”[^V4-394] We reach this conclusion because we have repeatedly observed the effect and then reasoned that if it did not happen by the nature of the substance itself, but only by chance, we would sometimes observe it and sometimes not. It would not happen always, or in most cases.

4. **Through particular cases, the intellect also makes use of widely transmitted reports**—reports repeated by many people—which help it reach assent, or a judgment that it accepts as true.

If we add other passages to this evidence, the picture becomes even clearer.

For example, Ibn Sīnā defines induction as:

> “A judgment about a universal because the same judgment is found in the particulars belonging to that universal: either in all of them, which is complete induction, or in most of them, which is the commonly used form of induction.”[^V4-395]

We now have enough evidence to answer the two central questions we raised about knowledge.

First, the intellect gets knowledge through sensory perceptions and through empirical premises based on sensible things.

Second, the intellect comes to know universals by observing particular things, identifying the characteristics they share, and then separating those shared characteristics from the individual features that distinguish each particular thing from the others.[^V4-396]

Up to this point, Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge begins from a **materialist starting point**.

Later we will also see that he recognizes the objective existence of the external world and the existence of objective truth within that world. This further confirms the materialist basis of his theory of knowledge.

##### D — Knowledge of First Principles

But does Ibn Sīnā follow this line consistently all the way to the end?

To answer that question, we need to examine two issues raised by passages in *The Salvation*, *The States of the Soul*, *Pointers and Reminders*, and other works in which he discusses the soul.

The first is the problem of **rational first principles**, or the “primary intelligibles.”

The second is knowledge acquired through **intuition**.

Some of Ibn Sīnā’s writings on these two subjects suggest that he does not remain completely consistent with the direction we have identified so far in his theory of knowledge.

Idealist scholars, both Arab and Orientalist,[^V4-397] have seized on these passages to present Ibn Sīnā as an idealist, mystic, or illuminationist thinker, while obscuring the openly materialist side of his thought.

The problem of rational first principles, or self-evident truths, was already well known in ancient and medieval philosophy.

The question was simple:

**How do we know these first principles?**

Aristotle called them the first premises of demonstration.

He began from the idea that all scientific knowledge must be demonstrated. But a chain of demonstrations cannot continue forever. At some point it must come to an end, or we fall into an infinite regress.

So how can the chain be stopped?

Aristotle’s answer was to appeal to self-evident first principles. Some principles, he argued, cannot and need not be demonstrated, because they are primary in themselves. They must instead serve as the basis of all demonstration.

But how do we know these principles?

Aristotle answered that we know them through reason alone. Human beings must possess a power of discrimination that allows them to grasp these first principles directly, without proof, just as animals possess the natural discriminatory power of sensation.[^V4-398]

We might call this human power a kind of **rational sense**.

Aristotle therefore failed to discover the bridge between rational knowledge and sensory knowledge. He remained within a contemplative approach.

Modern rationalism did the same. From its great representatives—Descartes, Leibniz, and Spinoza onward—it also failed to find this bridge and instead reinforced the philosophical separation between thought and sensory experience.

So how did Ibn Sīnā deal with the problem?

First, let us read the following passage:

> “First principles are propositions and premises that arise in the human being through the rational faculty, without any cause requiring assent to them except the propositions themselves and the meaning that makes them propositions. This meaning is produced by the thinking faculty, which combines simple meanings by affirmation and negation.
>
> “When these simple meanings arise in the human being with the help of sensation and imagination, or in some other way in the human being, and the combining faculty then joins them together, the mind must immediately assent to them without any further cause. It does so without being aware that this is something it has just acquired. A person may instead imagine that he has always known it, and without the estimative nature itself calling him toward it (…).
>
> “An example is that the whole is greater than the part. This is not acquired through sensation, induction, or anything else. Sensation may, however, provide us with the concepts of the whole, the greater, and the part.”[^V4-399]

This passage contains the main elements of Ibn Sīnā’s theory of how we know first principles, at least as he presents it in works written before *Pointers and Reminders*, which Munk believed to be Ibn Sīnā’s final work.[^V4-400]

In *Pointers and Reminders*, his position is not fundamentally different. But there it appears in a lightly mystical form, which Ibn Sīnā uses as part of his philosophical interpretation of a Qurʾānic passage.[^V4-401]

We will therefore take the passage just quoted from *The Salvation* as the best expression of his real position on our knowledge of self-evident rational truths, and try to understand that position through the text itself.

First principles are, by definition, propositions and premises that the intellect accepts immediately. It does not need a proof that persuades it to accept them, because they themselves serve as their own proof.

But Ibn Sīnā’s position is not as absolute as it may seem at first.

In his philosophy, nothing happens without a cause.

So when he says that the intellect needs no cause in order to accept these first propositions and premises, he is speaking only about the moment **when the intellect uses them**. He means the stage at which they are already available as premises for acquiring secondary intelligibles, or for moving from the known to the unknown through logical reasoning.

At that stage, the primary intelligibles are already present and ready for use. The intellect accepts them on their own authority.

But that is different from asking how they were formed in the first place, and how they acquired this self-evident character.

That process did not happen without a cause.

Ibn Sīnā explains the cause in a way that fits his realistic method.

He says that what turns these simple meanings into propositions is the thinking faculty—the theoretical intellect. This faculty combines the simple meanings it receives. These meanings themselves come from particular things in the external world, with the help of sensation and imagination, or “in some other way in the human being.”

Notice that he says **“in the human being.”**

Once these meanings, which have an objective basis in reality, come together and become established in the human mind, “the combining faculty joins them together.”

Through long repetition and the familiarity produced over time, “the mind must immediately assent to them without any further cause.”

The passage also contains an important point about method. Ibn Sīnā rejects the idea that these primary intelligibles are simply innate in the human being. They are acquired in the way he has just described—and he describes that process with remarkable scientific precision.

If we look closely at Ibn Sīnā’s explanation of self-evident truths, our thoughts immediately turn to Lenin’s explanation of the same problem.

In his *Philosophical Notebooks*, Lenin was commenting on Hegel’s attempt to subordinate human activity to the aim of logical categories. Lenin turned Hegel’s formulation in the opposite direction and wrote:

> “Human practical activity had to lead human consciousness to repeat the various logical forms billions of times before these forms could acquire the significance of axioms.”[^V4-402]

Even though Hegel’s idea appeared in an inverted form before Lenin turned it the right way around, Lenin still said that it contained “very profound and purely materialist content.”

We are justified in saying something similar about Ibn Sīnā’s idea, especially since it does not appear in the same inverted Hegelian form.

By this comparison, we can see that Ibn Sīnā moved far beyond Aristotle in dealing with this problem of knowledge.

He explained first principles on a material basis. Their origins lie in repeated experience, even when the mind does not consciously notice the repetitions as they occur. Over time they become so deeply established in the mind that we imagine they arose without any cause at all.

But there is still a problem hidden somewhat vaguely inside the passage we quoted. It becomes much clearer in other passages, both in *Pointers and Reminders* and in Ibn Sīnā’s earlier works.

This is the problem of **intuition**.

We should recall his words:

> “…when the simple meanings arise with the help of sensation and imagination, **or in some other way in the human being**…”

So there seem to be two ways of knowing first principles.

The first is the realistic path we have traced so far: the simple meanings arise “with the help of sensation and imagination.”

The second is the path by which these meanings arise “in some other way in the human being.”

In this passage, Ibn Sīnā does not tell us what this “other way” is.

Could he mean **intuition**?

In the same chapter of *The Salvation*,[^V4-403] Ibn Sīnā goes on to give another analysis of how universals are grasped through abstraction and through the connected chain of sensory perceptions we have already discussed.

But here we find another expression. At the stage of “assent,” which follows the stage of “conception,” he says that the intellect may be helped by “sense and imagination through experience or intuition, and it may also be helped by induction.”

Intuition therefore has a role here.

But from the context, that role seems to concern the abstraction of universals in general. It is not clear that Ibn Sīnā is talking specifically about our knowledge of first principles.

So we cannot yet identify the “other way in the human being” with intuition.

If we turn to *Pointers and Reminders*, however, the explanation becomes explicit.

There Ibn Sīnā is discussing the stage of the “intellect by disposition,” the stage at which the human soul acquires the primary intelligibles and becomes ready to acquire the secondary ones—the intelligibles that come after the first principles.

How does the mind become ready for this?

Ibn Sīnā says it happens:

> “either through thought, which is experience (…) or through intuition…”

In other words, the primary intelligibles reach the human soul, or intellect, in one of two ways: through reflective thought based on sensory experience, or through intuition.

##### E — The Problem of Intuition

We have now reached the second of the two issues that Ibn Sīnā’s writings force us to examine when testing the materialist character of his theory of knowledge.

That issue is intuition.

Up to this point, our conclusions have strengthened the case for his materialist position. From here onward, however, that position may begin to waver.

Everything depends on what Ibn Sīnā means by **intuition**.

Is it a rational kind of intuition?

Or is it mystical and illuminationist?

Most modern idealist scholars have treated it as mystical and illuminationist. Massignon even connects Ibn Sīnā’s illuminationism with the mysticism of al-Ḥusayn ibn Manṣūr al-Ḥallāj.[^V4-404]

We must test this claim first against Ibn Sīnā’s own texts, and second through our own method of analyzing those texts.

Let us begin with what he says in the chapter on “the ways in which the rational soul acquires the sciences.”[^V4-405]

From this chapter, we can draw the following points.

People differ in their readiness to learn.

These differences appear from the stage of the “intellect by disposition” onward,[^V4-406] after the stage of the “material intellect” has been passed.

Some people may be more capable than others of forming concepts because their natural readiness is stronger at this stage.

Such a person may also be more able to complete the development of the theoretical faculty independently, without outside help—in other words, to reach the stage of the “acquired intellect,” where, in Ibn Sīnā’s words, “the animal genus and the human species within it reach completion.”

It is this higher readiness that Ibn Sīnā calls **intuition**.

In his theory, then, intuition is an exceptional power possessed by some people. It allows them to acquire knowledge by themselves, without being taught by someone else.

In some people this capacity may be stronger still, allowing them to connect with the “Active Intellect” without any need for instruction.

It may even be stronger than that. A person might be able to connect with the Active Intellect while still at the stage of the “material intellect,” before reaching the acquired intellect, almost as though that person could know everything independently.

This, Ibn Sīnā says, is:

> “the highest degree of readiness. This state of the material intellect should be called a **holy intellect**. It belongs to the same general kind as the intellect by disposition, but it stands at a very high level that is not shared by all people.”

Ibn Sīnā also allows for the possibility that knowledge of this very elevated kind may overflow into the imaginative faculty. The imagination then responds by producing sensible images and audible expressions—in other words, concrete examples that give form to the intuitive knowledge flowing into it.

Despite how far this takes him toward the idea of self-generated knowledge arising from pure inward contemplation, Ibn Sīnā then returns to logical reasoning.

He says:

> “What confirms this is the obvious fact that intelligible things that can be acquired are acquired through obtaining the middle term in a syllogism. This middle term[^V4-407] can be obtained in two ways. Sometimes it is obtained through intuition. Intuition is an act of the mind by which it discovers the middle term on its own, and intelligence is the power of intuition. At other times, the middle term is obtained through teaching. But the beginnings of teaching themselves go back to intuition, because things must ultimately end in intuitions discovered by those who possessed them and then passed on to learners.”

That is the end of Ibn Sīnā’s statement.

But his return to logical reasoning does not really change the direction of the argument.

As long as the middle term ultimately appears in the mind through intuition—whether through one’s own intuition or through instruction passed down from “those who possessed the intuitions”—the whole process still ends with intuition.

Nor does it solve the problem to say that intuition comes from intelligence, as Ibn Sīnā does when he says, “intelligence is the power of intuition.”

The result is still that logical reasoning itself becomes dependent on intuition, rather than intuition being explained through reasoning.

The position moves even further away from external reality and objective knowledge when Ibn Sīnā says that it is possible for:

> “a person’s soul to be strengthened by such great purity and such a strong connection with the intellectual principles[^V4-408] that it bursts into intuition—that is, into a readiness to receive inspiration from the Active Intellect in all things. The forms of all things contained in the Active Intellect are then impressed upon it, either all at once or almost all at once.
>
> “They are not impressed upon it through imitation, but in an order that includes the middle terms, because beliefs based on imitation cannot count as rational certainty in matters that are known through their causes.
>
> “This”—that is, this powerful connection with the intellectual principles—“is a kind of prophecy. Indeed, it is the highest power of prophecy. This faculty is most properly called the holy faculty, and it is the highest level of the human faculties.”[^V4-409]

Taken on its own, this passage seems to support the claim that Ibn Sīnā’s theory of intuition moves in a mystical and illuminationist direction.

It ends by making intuition an inspiration received from the Active Intellect, and by using this illuminationist process to explain prophecy.

At this point, his materialist position in the theory of knowledge appears to waver so seriously that its foundations themselves seem threatened.

But if we take this passage alone and turn that last conclusion into a final judgment, we would be falling into an idealist method ourselves.
