We would be building our conclusion from a partial and one-sided view, or from phenomena that remain on the surface, rather than reaching the underlying reality.

We must look for the other parts of Ibn Sīnā’s theory that complete the picture.

Then we must identify the gap inside the theory—the gap through which the idea of intuition entered his theory of knowledge in the first place.

Let us begin with the first task.

Should we draw a firm dividing line between the passage we have just examined on intuition and the chapter that follows it immediately, on “the ordering of the faculties according to command and service”?[^V4-410]

That new chapter begins:

> “Now look, and consider these faculties: how some command others, and how some serve others…”

The opening words, “Now look…,” and even the conjunction linking them to what came before, are not empty details that can simply be ignored.

They express the continuity and progression of Ibn Sīnā’s argument.

This observation is therefore not merely formal, though the wording itself is not our main concern.

What matters is the relationship between this chapter and the previous one. Together, they reveal Ibn Sīnā’s view of the relationship among all the faculties of sensory and rational perception—and indeed among the powers of nature, the powers of life, and the whole chain of human perception up to the “holy intellect,” which is the source of intuition.

Ibn Sīnā says:

> “You find the acquired intellect—and indeed the holy intellect—at the head, with everything serving it, for it is the highest end.
>
> “Then the intellect in actuality is served by the intellect by disposition, while the material intellect, through the readiness within it, serves the intellect by disposition.
>
> “Then the practical intellect serves all of these, because the bodily connection, as will become clear, exists for the sake of perfecting and purifying the theoretical intellect. The practical intellect governs that connection.
>
> “The practical intellect is then served by the estimative faculty. The estimative faculty is served by two powers: one before it and one after it. The power after it preserves what it has received, while the power before it consists of all the animal faculties.
>
> “Then the imaginative faculty (…) the appetitive faculty (…) and the imaginative power serves it (…) As for the imaginative power, it is served by *phantasia*, and *phantasia* is served by the five senses…”

He continues down the chain in this way, moving from the animal faculties to the vegetative faculties, then to the four natural powers—the material elements—and finally to the four qualities: heat, coldness, dryness, and moisture.

These, he says, are “the last levels of the faculties.”

If we now stand back and look at this coherent hierarchy from its base to its summit—from the powers of nature, through the powers of life, all the way to the “holy intellect”—what does this coherence mean?

And what does Ibn Sīnā mean by the “service” that holds the whole structure together?

We can find only one meaning for this “service.”

The lower level gives the higher level the material from which its real, objective existence is formed within this integrated structure.

By the time we reach the “holy intellect,” the contribution of all the lower powers has been gathered together and integrated in the “service” of that intellect.

Or, to put it more precisely, the holy intellect is itself the product of all those powers.

So when it produces knowledge, that knowledge reflects this real, objective existence: the powers of nature, the powers of life, and the powers of the human being, in all their forms of movement and transformation.

That is what we take from the chapter as a whole.

##### F — Ibn Sīnā’s Contradiction and Its Source

The conclusion of this chapter is clearly different from the conclusion of the chapter before it.

Indeed, the two stand in contradiction.

But let us return to our earlier question:

Should we draw a firm dividing line between them?

Our answer is no.

There is no real break between the two chapters.

Does this mean, then, that Ibn Sīnā contradicts himself?

Yes.

He contradicts himself in a way that cannot simply be explained away.

But our task is to discover the source of this contradiction. We must also identify the gap through which “intuition” entered Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge and find the theoretical idea that created that gap.

Let us begin by looking for the source of the contradiction between two sides of Ibn Sīnā’s position.

On one side, he ties rational knowledge firmly and explicitly to the objective external world, as we saw in the last chapter.

On the other, he turns to intuition, and this breaks the connection. Rational knowledge becomes “alienated” from external reality and receives intelligible forms from the “Active Intellect” and the immaterial world of emanation.

We saw this in his chapter on “the ways in which the rational soul acquires the sciences.”

Yet the two chapters are not separate or disconnected.

We would go so far as to say that, in their content, one continues directly from the other. They form a connected sequence.

It is difficult to believe that Ibn Sīnā simply fell into this contradiction without noticing it, or that he failed to understand the conflict between the two positions.

It is equally difficult to explain it by claiming that his philosophy is itself disjointed and lacks any principle holding its different parts together.

In our view, all of these explanations are very unlikely.

We believe the opposite.

Behind this contradiction lies the very strength of the connections within Ibn Sīnā’s thought and throughout the different parts of his theory. It also reflects how determined he was to make those parts fit together into a coherent whole.

That may sound strange.

But we will see why.

There was a problem that long predated Ibn Sīnā in ancient and medieval philosophy. Materialists and idealists alike had struggled with it.

It was the problem of the relationship between the system of sensation and the system of thought.

They did not understand the true nature of that relationship.

Given what people of those periods knew about the world, this was entirely natural.

But many philosophers were satisfied with speculative ideas about the relationship. They did not even recognize clearly that they faced a problem that required serious effort to solve, even within the limits of the knowledge available to them at the time.

This is where Ibn Sīnā stood apart from many thinkers of his age.

He **recognized that there was a problem**.

He struggled with it consciously.

His theoretical and practical method placed the problem before him as a real and difficult puzzle.

Most importantly, he tried to define it. He made a serious effort to solve it in a way that would allow him to remain faithful to his method.

But he could not reach that solution.

His method required a level of scientific and technical development that his age had not yet reached.

This is why we find the tension we have described between two positions.

The first is a materialist position, deeply rooted in Ibn Sīnā’s thinking and pressing strongly upon it, from which he could not simply turn away.

The second is an idealist position imposed on him by the inner logic of the philosophy of his age—a logic that itself rested on the inherited structure of traditional philosophy, stretching back to ancient Greece and to the ways Greek philosophy interacted with Eastern traditions during the Middle Ages.

Ibn Sīnā understood that there was an objective relationship between the process of thinking and the process of sensation—that is, between thought and external reality.

Many of the passages we have already cited in this study show that he also saw quite clearly the basis of the unity between the two processes.

Indeed, the chapter we have just examined, with its hierarchy of the powers of nature, the powers of life, and the powers of human reason, suggests that he saw all of them as parts of a single process, even though that process contains many different elements.

But he also understood that intelligible forms differ from sensory forms.

Sensory forms never become completely separated from the material relationships that connect them to their external objects. They therefore remain connected to the organic, material organs of sensation in the human body.

Intelligible forms are different.

They are completely abstract. None of the material relationships belonging to their external objects remains attached to them.

And here Ibn Sīnā confronts the problem directly.

He describes it in the following way:[^V4-411]

> “That in us in which the intelligible form is impressed is something that is neither a body nor in a body. But that in which the form before it”—that is, the sensory form—“is impressed is either a power in a body or a body itself. And you know that a faculty’s awareness of what it perceives consists in the impression of the thing’s form within it.”

After explaining this in relation to the different sensory faculties and the sensory forms impressed upon them, Ibn Sīnā turns to the rational faculties and to the intelligible forms impressed within them:

> “But the substance in which the intelligibles are impressed is not bodily and is not divisible (…) because intelligibles are not impressed in a body.
>
> “It therefore remains that there is something outside our own substance[^V4-412] in which intelligible forms exist in themselves, since it is a substance that is intellect in actuality.
>
> “When a certain connection occurs between our souls and this substance, the intelligible forms suited to that particular readiness are impressed upon our souls, producing particular judgments.”

This is the problem as Ibn Sīnā himself understood it, exposed it, and defined it with great clarity and precision.

His account of knowledge begins with sensory perceptions and with the external world understood as an objective reality.

He follows the chain of perceptions as they become more abstract, step by step.

But when this chain reaches the point where sensory perceptions are transformed into rational ones, he runs into a barrier inherited from the science and philosophy of the ancient world.

He sees that intelligible forms are completely abstract. In other words, they are not material in any sense.

So where can they exist within the human body?

The body is purely material, while the intelligible forms are purely intellectual.

What is non-material cannot exist inside what is material.

So, on these assumptions, intelligible forms cannot have a place in the body.

But then another question arises.

How do perceptions that are connected with matter become perceptions that have no connection with matter at all?

And what transforms them from one side of this divide into its opposite?

From his empirical knowledge, Ibn Sīnā is certain that there is a connection between intelligible and sensory perception.

But he cannot see the material basis of the relationship between thought and the brain.

Given the historical level of human knowledge in his time, he could not have reached the conclusion made possible in our own age by Marxist science and by scientific and technical progress: that the brain is matter in one of its highest and most highly organized forms, that thought is itself a higher property of this matter, and that matter is therefore the basis of thought.

Ibn Sīnā had already gone halfway toward solving the problem of the relationship between reason and the senses, between thought and matter. As we saw in the section summarized earlier, all the natural powers and all the powers of life serve the human intellect, even at the level he calls the “sacred intellect.” This means that Ibn Sīnā had come to see human consciousness—thought—as a product of matter.

That was half the journey, and it was an extraordinary distance for a philosopher of his time to travel.

But the other half was where the real difficulty began. Ibn Sīnā was fully aware of the problem and devoted great attention to solving it. Yet the only tools available to him were the philosophical ideas inherited from his own age and from the Greek world, both classical Greek and Alexandrian.

He found the other half of his solution in two of these inherited ideas. The first came from Aristotle, and the second from Plotinus.

The first was Aristotle’s view of matter and form. Matter, Aristotle held, exists potentially before it takes on a particular form. Once form is present, matter exists actually. That is the first principle.

A second Aristotelian principle was also important here: something that exists only potentially can become actual only through something that is already actual.

This gave Ibn Sīnā the Aristotelian basis for his first step toward solving the problem. But what was this thing that was “always actual”?

At this point he departed from Aristotle and took his second step along the path of Neoplatonism, especially the philosophy of Plotinus:

> “The potential intellect becomes actual through an intellect that is always actual.”[^V4-413]

In Plotinus’s theory of emanation, the “Active Intellect” is precisely such an intellect: an intellect that is always actual.

In Ibn Sīnā’s terminology, the “habitual intellect” is, as we saw earlier, the stage at which the first intelligible concepts have become available to human understanding. It is still only potential when compared with the “acquired intellect,” or with the “absolute intellect.”

The latter means that the intelligible form is present to the mind, which looks at it and actually understands it, while also knowing that it is understanding it. At that point, Ibn Sīnā says, it becomes an acquired intellect.[^V4-414]

For the intellect to reach this stage—for what exists only potentially to become actual—it needs a cause. And according to Ibn Sīnā, nothing can bring about this change except the intellect that is “always actual”: the Active Intellect.

This creates another gap in Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge. Thought, in moving from potential to actual knowledge—from having the capacity to know something to actually possessing that knowledge—needs an external cause. It needs a movement coming from outside its own internal development.

This was not a problem unique to Ibn Sīnā. It was one of the wider problems of ancient and medieval philosophy, not only in theories of knowledge but in theories of existence itself. Its historical source was the inability to understand that things can contain the source of their own movement and change within themselves.

There were therefore two gaps through which the idea of intuition entered Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge.

The first came from his inability to understand the real relationship between the material brain and thought as one of the brain’s properties or functions. The second came from his inability to understand the self-movement of things.

It was through these two gaps that intuition entered his theory. For Ibn Sīnā, intuition is a form of contact with the Active Intellect. This is what he means when he describes someone whose soul is:

> “strengthened by extreme purity and by such a close connection with the intellectual principles that intuition flares up within it—that is, a readiness to receive inspiration from the Active Intellect in everything...”[^V4-415]

We should also notice a third gap in his argument.

This one also came from the historical limits of scientific knowledge in his time, especially the limited understanding of the relationship between the organs of thought and the organs of sensation.

As we have seen, Ibn Sīnā repeatedly says that intelligible forms do not exist in a body. Elsewhere he states:

> “The substance in which the intelligibles reside is neither a body nor something that exists in a body.”[^V4-416]

This shows that he understood the relationship between intelligible forms and the human power of thought—or between sensory images and the powers of sensation—as a relationship between something that occupies a place and the place that contains it.

Thought therefore becomes a kind of container for ideas. In the same way, the powers of sensation become containers in which sensory perceptions are placed. The relationship is understood almost spatially.

This made the problem even more difficult.

Modern science, however, does not understand the relationship in this way. Thought, in its relationship with matter, is simply a natural function of highly organized and highly developed matter.[^V4-417] The same is true of sensation. Sensation is a natural function of the sensory organs—the five senses.

##### G — Two Conclusions

Our examination of these gaps in Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge leads us, in our view, to two important conclusions.

First, these gaps may themselves provide the main explanation for why Ibn Sīnā remained close to earlier philosophers in accepting a dualism between soul and body.

This happened despite his enormous command of the knowledge available in his time, despite his own contribution to developing that knowledge, and despite the fact that this learning and scientific work led him to positions that were more materialist than those of many of his predecessors.

It also happened despite another important feature of Ibn Sīnā’s thought: his intellectual independence, his strong scientific personality, and his rejection of blind imitation.

One fact strongly supports this conclusion. Most of the arguments on which Ibn Sīnā based his theory of the soul—the view that the soul is a substance separate from the body and has a different nature from it—are built mainly on the problem of perception.

As we explained earlier, this follows directly from the way he understood intellectual knowledge, and especially the relationship between thought and matter.

In the “Third Mode” of *al-Ishārāt*,[^V4-418] where Ibn Sīnā gives the final form of the position he had reached on the soul, perception becomes the starting point for his attempt to prove this idealist dualism.

He returns to the same problem in the “Seventh Mode” of the same book, in the section on metaphysics.[^V4-419] There he argues that the intellectual power is not “imprinted in a body, whether in the heart or the brain.”

From this he concludes:

> “The rational substance within us”—that is, the rational soul—“can understand through itself. And because it is a principle, it cannot be composed of a corruptible power joined to a power of permanence.”

In other words, because the soul can understand intelligible things by itself, Ibn Sīnā treats it as prior to the body. It is therefore a spiritual substance rather than a combination of two powers: a material power that can decay, change, and be transformed, and another power that remains permanent.

In ancient philosophy, this permanence was considered a property of the metaphysical world, which was understood as a fixed world beyond change and transformation.

Yet despite this sharp dualism between soul and body, something else appears when we return to Ibn Sīnā’s writings on the practical intellect, or the active power of the soul, and examine how it deals with the outside world and with the theoretical intellect.

If we also return to our earlier analysis of these texts and the conclusions we drew from them,[^V4-420] we find good reason to say that Ibn Sīnā could not completely ignore the signs of unity between soul and body, even though he could not grasp the real basis of that unity.[^V4-421]

This recalls Engels’s observation that:

> “The systems of the idealists acquired more and more materialist content as they tried to resolve the contradiction between soul and body in the direction of a pantheistic solution.”[^V4-422]

Second, our examination of these gaps in Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge suggests another conclusion.

The idealist side of his theory, which became visible through these gaps, did not arise primarily from a basic philosophical commitment to idealism.

Instead, it seems to have grown mainly out of the scientific limitations of his historical period. The knowledge available at the time was not yet sufficient to explain the real relationship between the general laws of nature, society, and thought, on the one hand, and the abstract logical concepts and categories in which those laws are reflected, on the other.

More precisely, the idealist side of Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge does not, in our view, express the main ideological content of his philosophical outlook.

The gaps in his understanding of knowledge pushed him in an idealist direction and brought him into conflict with himself—that is, with the materialist tendencies that seem to us more deeply rooted in his philosophy as a whole than his idealist positions were.

Those idealist positions were produced partly by the complex internal development of philosophy itself, and partly by the relatively limited scientific understanding of the process of thought available in Ibn Sīnā’s time.

---

##### H — Can the World Be Known?

Strictly speaking, we should have begun our discussion of Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge with this question.

But the special character of his writings on the soul led us to begin with another question: How does knowledge arise, and what tools does the human being use to acquire it?

We now turn to the question: **Can the world be known?**

The answer depends first on another question: What is the world?

Or, to put the issue more directly: What is truth, since truth is both the content and the object of knowledge?

Once we have answered that, we can ask whether human beings are capable of knowing this truth.

This question lies at the heart of any theory of knowledge. Every such theory must take a position on it. Otherwise, it is missing one of the basic elements that make it a theory of knowledge at all.

So what is Ibn Sīnā’s position?

---

There are two main philosophical positions on the content of knowledge—that is, on truth.

**1 — The materialist position**

The materialist position holds that the external world itself is the great, all-embracing truth.

Within this larger truth are countless particular truths, all forming part of the same world. Some have already been discovered. Others remain unknown but can still be discovered, because knowledge has no final limit as long as the world itself has no final limit.

The essential point, however, is that the world as a whole exists objectively and independently of human consciousness. The same is true of the individual truths within it. They too are objective in this sense.

Dialectical materialism therefore treats the objectivity of truth as the defining principle of the materialist position.

Lenin expressed this by saying:

> “To be a materialist means to recognize the objective truth revealed to us by our sense organs.”[^V4-423]

**2 — The idealist position**

The idealist position says that truth is not this external world that lies within the reach of our senses. Truth lies outside this world and outside the limits of the human being—that is, beyond the limits of human knowledge.

There are two different forms of idealism here.

Objective idealism holds that this truth beyond our world has an objective existence of its own.

Subjective idealism, by contrast, denies that truth is objective at all, whether in our world or beyond it. For subjective idealism, there is no truth apart from what the thinking subject itself thinks. Truth is entirely subjective and has no existence outside human consciousness.

These two different positions on truth also lead to different positions on knowledge and on the limits of what human beings can know.

In its theory of knowledge, dialectical materialism argues that once we recognize the external world as an objective reality, we must also recognize that this reality and its laws can be known.

It reaches this conclusion by connecting knowledge with human activity in the external world.

Knowledge is another form of this activity—or, more precisely, the side of human activity that corresponds to practical action.

Human beings came to know the world because they acted on the things within it. They did this because material needs forced them to deal with those things in practice.

The history of knowledge is therefore tied to the history of human activity and practice.

Every advance in humanity’s productive material activity has helped improve the tools of knowledge. In turn, better tools have expanded the content of knowledge itself.

The development of technology—the microscope, the telescope, the electron microscope, and so on—has allowed our senses to discover facts about the world that they could never have discovered by their unaided powers alone.

This is what made it possible for human beings to travel to the Moon and to place scientific instruments on the surface of Venus.

Human beings possess more than sense organs. They also have the ability to grasp, understand, and form concepts. This ability lets them turn the discoveries and experiences gained through practical activity into concepts that express the laws governing the movement of things in the world.

So through the senses, through the development of technology, and through the human ability to understand and form concepts, it becomes possible to know the inner nature of things.

This is the basic conclusion reached and defended by dialectical materialism.

But human knowledge develops step by step along with the history of human activity, and this process has no final endpoint.

Idealist philosophies take a different position.

On the one hand, they build their theories of knowledge on inward reflection. On the other, they explain the origin of knowledge not through material needs and practical activity, but through motives such as curiosity, wonder, or amazement at the phenomena of the universe.

For these reasons, idealist philosophies from ancient times down to the modern period have generally treated human knowledge as limited and unable to reach the inner nature of things.

Kant, for example, tried to prove that human knowledge cannot reach things as they truly are.

He based this conclusion on two ideas within his philosophical system: the *Transcendant*[^V4-424]—which he understood as the thing in itself—and the *Transcendental*[^V4-425]—what arises from the conditions that make knowledge possible before experience.

His starting point was therefore subjective.

This is also why Kant approached logical categories from a subjective position, and why his definitions of them reflected the same approach.

Instead of treating each category as reflecting a law of nature, as dialectical materialism does, Kant said the opposite:

> “I do not want to learn from nature, nor do I derive the laws of nature from nature itself. Rather, I impose laws on nature.”[^V4-426]

This is a particularly clear example of the general idealist tendency to restrict the limits of human knowledge and to separate knowledge from the external world.

The exact form this takes differs from one historical period to another and from one philosopher to another. Philosophical systems differ in their structure, in the elements they contain, and in the intellectual methods they use to address philosophical problems. But the general tendency remains.

---

After this digression—which we believe was necessary—we can return to Ibn Sīnā and ask where he stands on this question.

We can begin with a simple question: Does objective truth exist?

Then, based on his answer, we can ask a second: Is the external world itself an objective reality?

Taken as a complete system, Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy answers that objective truth does exist and that it takes two forms.

The first is metaphysical: the First Existence and the primary existents.

The second is natural: the external world around us.

But these are not two separate realities. They are connected by a single principle: one underlying substance belonging to one world, and therefore one objective reality appearing in these two forms of existence.

Ibn Sīnā writes:[^V4-427]

> “A thing is either an existing thing in external reality, or a form taken from it in the mind, and these do not differ from one region or nation to another. Or it is a spoken expression that refers to the form in the mind, or a written sign that refers to the spoken expression, and these two do differ among nations. Writing refers to speech, speech refers to the form in the mind, and that form refers to existing things.”

According to this passage, the primary form in which things exist is their concrete existence—that is, their external, material existence.

Their existence in the mind, their form as knowledge, or their form in consciousness is a reflection of that external existence. It is, as Ibn Sīnā says, “taken from it in the mind.”

Things therefore exist as realities independently of consciousness. Their existence comes before our awareness of them. Their external existence does not depend on their existence in our minds—that is, it does not depend on whether we know them.

This is objective existence in the fullest sense.

But this passage does more than affirm that things exist objectively. It also contains two further points that deserve attention.

The first is a clear explanation of what the objectivity of truth means.

The same thing exists as the same thing wherever it is found and among whatever people or nation encounters it.

This is the practical meaning of objective truth.

That is what Ibn Sīnā means when he says that an existing thing itself, and the mental form taken from it, do not differ from one region or nation to another.

The second point is that the passage expresses not only the objectivity of truth but also the objectivity of knowledge.

This is an important point in the dialectical-materialist theory of knowledge.

Knowledge gives us objective truth when it reflects the world correctly.

For this reason, people in different countries and among different peoples do not differ in the basic way objective things are reflected in their minds—that is, in their knowledge of those things.

They may differ in the form through which they express that knowledge. Their languages may differ, and their systems of writing may differ. But these differences in form do not change the objective content expressed through those languages.

The particular form used by each language changes nothing about the objective content itself.

The reason, in fact, is that human knowledge is one form taken by objective truth.

We cannot ignore this relationship between truth and human consciousness. In other words, we cannot ignore the human role in discovering truth, knowing it, and interacting with it.

If we removed this relationship altogether, there would be little meaning in speaking about truth and knowledge in the first place.

But if knowledge is itself one form taken by truth, does that not introduce a subjective element into truth?

Yes.

But this is truth’s only subjective side, and it does not change its objective reality.

In philosophical language, truth has the character of universality and necessity. This means that all people can recognize it.

But this does not mean that truth becomes objective because everyone agrees that it is true. If that were the case, it would no longer be objective.

We are not claiming that the passage from Ibn Sīnā contains all of these ideas in a fully developed form.

What we can say is that it contains living seeds of them.

At the most basic level, it clearly recognizes the objective existence of the external world.

Nor is this the only passage in Ibn Sīnā’s writings that points clearly in this materialist direction.

The passages we have already examined in our discussion of his theory of knowledge all show that his starting point in the process of knowledge is the objective existence of the material world.

We need only recall the passages in which he describes the natural powers and the powers of life as being reflected in the organs of sensory perception and then represented together at the level of rational understanding, even at what Ibn Sīnā considers its highest stage: the sacred intellect.[^V4-428]

The passages in which he introduces intuition do not contradict his belief in the objective existence of the external world.

They simply provide two different routes toward knowledge of that same world.

One route begins directly from the world’s concrete material reality.

The other leaves this starting point behind and turns instead to a supernatural source of knowledge.

But in both cases, the objective world remains the essential content of knowledge.

If we go further and move beyond the idealist interpretations usually given to Ibn Sīnā’s metaphysics, we can discover within it a continuous line running through one eternal material world with an objective existence of its own.

We can see this line in Ibn Sīnā’s view that matter and form are eternal and exist necessarily in relation to the “First Cause,” which in turn exists necessarily through itself.

Ibn Sīnā used the principle that “from the One, only one can proceed” in an ingenious and complex way.

It allowed him to deny that the “First Existent” was the single absolute cause of everything, and to give the primary existents a share in the causation of the world.

The multiplicity of existence, in his view, could not come directly from the One alone. It had to arise through all of these primary existents.

This allowed him to give these existents—including form, matter, and eternal motion—a real causal role in the world.

At the same time, these things were eternal within his system and were tied to the eternity of the First itself, since an effect cannot be separated from its cause.[^V4-429]

The result was to give objective existence to one world extending eternally into the past, with its different parts arising from one another through a form of relative dialectical causation.

Within this dialectic, Ibn Sīnā also found room to suggest that the chain itself might be infinite.[^V4-430]

This is only a condensed reference to philosophical questions that Ibn Sīnā discusses at great length in his wider system.

We will return to them later in other parts of this study.

Recognizing objective truth as a principle already opens the possibility that this truth can be known.

From this we can draw a first conclusion: Ibn Sīnā’s theory of existence leads toward the view that human beings can know that existence, and that there is nothing in the truths of the world that the human mind is fundamentally incapable of understanding.

But we should not stop with this first conclusion about the possibility of knowledge in Ibn Sīnā.

Other parts of his philosophy expand it and provide practical examples of it.

Two areas in particular show how his system allows the world to be known.

The first is **scientific experiment**.

Here we mean his work in the natural sciences, medicine, and chemistry.

In these fields, Ibn Sīnā combined observation and practical experiment in a genuinely reciprocal relationship. He did not rely on experiment without theory, and he did not rely on theory without experiment.

In the process, he subjected logical reasoning to experiment and induction. At the same time, he also subjected experiment to logical reasoning.

In these studies, logic stopped being a collection of dead formal patterns and became a set of forms filled with living reality—reality in movement and change.

This helps explain his definition of logic as:

> “The science through which one learns the different ways of moving from things already present in the human mind to things that are newly reached; the conditions of those things; and the different kinds of transition in which the order and form of the movement are sound, as well as those in which they are not.”[^V4-431]

In this definition, logic becomes a dynamic tool for organizing the relationship between the form of knowledge and its content.

The movement of reasoning from the known to the unknown reflects the movement between the image in the mind and the reality outside it.

Ibn Sīnā looked at natural phenomena in order to explain them through the laws of nature.

He treated these laws as expressions of the inner nature of the phenomena themselves.

In other words, he believed that the inner nature of things was within the reach of human knowledge.

We have a concrete example that clearly shows the connection between his scientific research and his theory of knowledge.

Interestingly, this example appears in the metaphysical section of *al-Ishārāt*[^V4-432] and also in the logical section of the same work.[^V4-433]

Ibn Sīnā argues that particular things can be understood rationally. They can enter the process of intellectual knowledge and take the form of universals.

This happens when we study their natural causes and observe that these causes produce their effects in a regular way.

Through induction, the universal becomes visible in the particular, and the particular itself can then take on a universal form in the mind.

His example is a lunar eclipse as a particular event.

Although the eclipse is a single event, the mind can understand it through its relationship with its natural cause, which Ibn Sīnā describes as the Moon coming between the Sun and the Earth.

Once the event is understood through this causal relationship, the particular becomes universal.

The particular statement “the Moon was eclipsed” becomes the universal statement: “Whenever the Moon comes between the Sun and the Earth, an eclipse occurs.”

In logical language, the particular moves from being a “minor term” to functioning as a “middle term.”[^V4-434]

We said that this example has several meanings for our discussion.

First, it shows that Ibn Sīnā treated natural laws as objective laws, including the law of causal relationships between things.

Second, it shows that the things expressed through these laws are themselves objective realities, which become forms of knowledge in the human mind.

Together, these two points lead to a third: the human mind is capable of knowing these realities, their inner laws, and the laws connecting them with one another in an objective way.

But the example reveals something else as well—and this is an unexpectedly interesting point.

Ibn Sīnā already had the beginnings of a realistic solution to the problem of rational understanding.

That solution could have started from real individual things themselves, without the intervention of the Active Intellect and without appealing to a power outside the mind’s own internal movement.

Yet Ibn Sīnā does not seem to have realized that this idea could solve the problem he was facing.

Instead, it faded into the background when he tried to explain God’s knowledge of individual things through his theory that the Necessary Existent knows things in a universal way.[^V4-435]

The idea was then used only in that context.

It is unfortunate that its practical use stopped there, because it had the potential to open the road that had otherwise become blocked for Ibn Sīnā in the problem of rational abstraction.

There is one more important implication.

By allowing the universal to arise through the particular, Ibn Sīnā moved beyond Aristotle’s method, which confines true knowledge to universals and refuses to treat knowledge of particulars as genuine science.

The second area is **metaphysics**.

If we look beyond the idealist and metaphysical form of this part of Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy and focus instead on the real content within it, we can see something important.

In his system of “separate intellects,” and in his account of the chain of causes extending from metaphysical forms down to the material things of our world, there is not only an eternal process of existence. There is also a process of understanding.

Within Ibn Sīnā’s metaphysics, this process of understanding becomes a kind of cosmic bridge for the human mind.

By crossing this bridge, the human intellect can add to its own natural capacities and to its natural tools of knowledge—the outer and “inner” senses—a limitless ability to explore the secrets and endless truths of the universe.

We have already explained why Ibn Sīnā felt the need to build this cosmic bridge for the human mind.

We have also explained the problem that pushed him toward a solution that was metaphysical in form but, in our view, realistic in content.

We say realistic in content without qualification.

A broad view of Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy allows us to say that he clearly understood, in practice, that the human mind has an enormous and potentially unlimited capacity for knowledge.

He also understood that forming abstract concepts from individual things was essential to the creation of knowledge and to the mind’s ability to reach the inner nature of things.

Ibn Sīnā could hardly have doubted that this process really occurred. He knew it from his own experience as a scientist, philosopher, and thinker.

His problem existed only at the theoretical level.

There he encountered the great difficulty of explaining the move from what is sensed to what is abstract.

How does this transition happen?

Where is the “missing link” in the chain of increasingly abstract forms that passes through the different powers of perception?

We have already explained why Ibn Sīnā faced this difficulty and why he could not find the solution within the material workings of the human brain.

Instead, he had to look outside them.

But we should understand exactly what this outside factor does in his theory.

It does no more than help something that already exists potentially become actual.

It is an agent of transformation, not an agent that creates something out of nothing.

Let us read Ibn Sīnā’s own explicit statement that nothing in existence is, in itself, incapable of being understood.

At the same time, he states the problem once again in equally clear terms:

> “...We know that the nature of existence, and the nature of the divisions of existence simply as such—that is, as the nature of existence and the nature of its divisions—are not incapable of being understood. They fail to be understood only when they are in matter or hidden beneath the accidents of matter. In that condition they are sensed or imagined. As was shown earlier, once that existence is stripped of this obstacle, it becomes an intelligible existence and essence.”[^V4-436]

In the same chapter, Ibn Sīnā makes an even more explicit statement:

> “...For we know with certainty that we possess a power by which we understand things...”[^V4-436]

We place special emphasis on every word in this sentence.

We could hardly ask Ibn Sīnā for a clearer statement in support of our interpretation.

If we possess a power by which we understand things, and if matter and its accidents are what prevent this power from working freely, and if Ibn Sīnā could not find within our own organs of perception the “missing link” that removes this obstacle, then he had to search for that missing link outside those organs.

He found it in the Active Intellect.

This allowed him to preserve what he regarded as the immense human capacity to know everything, because he could see the evidence of this capacity too clearly to deny it.

This metaphysical relationship between the human intellect and the Active Intellect also reflects one part of Ibn Sīnā’s wider picture of the unity of the world.

Matter belongs to this eternal order.

It passes from “potential existence” to “actual existence,” while remaining objectively real in both states, as Ibn Sīnā’s metaphysics itself shows us.

---

##### I — Conclusion of the Discussion

We can now draw the following final conclusions from our examination of Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge:

1. Human knowledge is not subjective in the sense of being an inward contemplation with purely subjective content. It is objective because its content is objective reality, which human beings receive through nature, society, and the objective universe as a whole.

2. The tools of knowledge are the human senses, both external and internal, followed by the intellect—that is, the power of abstraction.

3. The human powers of perception cannot, by themselves, complete the process of abstraction through which universals and concepts are formed. They require help from an external factor: the Active Intellect, which is free from material relations.

4. With the help of this external factor, the human powers of knowledge can understand the inner nature of things and comprehend the world.

5. But this outside help—contact with the Active Intellect—is not available to every person. It depends on exceptional intellectual qualities. These are not innate but acquired, either through learning or through gaining the first intelligible principles and the middle term—that is, universal demonstrative propositions—through intuition.

6 — Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge rests on a materialist basis, though it contains idealist gaps that arose from the scientific limits of his particular historical period.

#### 3 — The Question of Prophecy

Ibn Sīnā does not have a separate theory of prophecy and prophetic revelation. His position on the subject grows directly out of his theory of knowledge. We already touched on this when discussing intuition in our study of that theory.[^V4-437]

At the most basic level, his position is essentially the same as that of his predecessor al-Fārābī. Both treat prophecy and revelation as forms of human knowledge. They arise from a very high level of human perception and depend on the same faculties that form the common basis of knowledge in every human being.

The difference between one person and another lies in the strength of these faculties: how receptive they are, and how pure and refined they become. At their highest level, rational perception can rise to what Ibn Sīnā calls the “holy intellect,” which is the level of prophecy.

Both philosophers give the main role in reaching this level to the inner faculty of perception known as the **imaginative faculty**.

This means that Ibn Sīnā’s idea of prophecy, like al-Fārābī’s, differs from the purely Islamic conception.

In Islam, understood as both a religion and an ideology, prophecy is a divine message that the prophet receives from God through revelation. Revelation is the means by which God delivers this message to the prophet. It belongs to prophets alone, and no other human being has access to it. On this view, revelation is the only means of communication between God and humanity.

The prophet can therefore rightly say, in effect: “I alone, and no one else in your age, receive revelation from God. Through it I tell you the truth and reality, without error, falsehood, or forgetfulness in what I say about God.”[^V4-438]

There is also, in this understanding of prophecy, “no room for argument and debate, nor for logic and syllogisms.”[^V4-439]

Prophecy in this ideological form is therefore something quite different from what al-Fārābī and Ibn Sīnā describe. It cannot be reduced to a purely psychological explanation, as al-Fārābī tried to do, or to a psychological and physiological explanation, as Ibn Sīnā did.

Either kind of explanation has two consequences.

First, it removes the idea that prophecy is a special divine office granted only to certain individuals.

Second, it breaks with the religious view that communication with God is limited to the prophet alone, and that even this communication is limited to receiving the divine message and carrying out the duties of prophecy.

In our earlier discussions of similar philosophical positions toward religious questions that stood at the heart of official ideology, we did not treat such positions as purely philosophical and separate from ideology.

In fact, it would be wrong to draw a firm line between the two unless we were also prepared to draw a firm line between philosophy and reality itself. And that would go against the truth of philosophy.

A few lines ago, we said that Ibn Sīnā’s position on prophecy is basically the same as al-Fārābī’s. That is true. But Ibn Sīnā differs from his predecessor in the way he handles the question.

He treats it in much greater detail and approaches it as a scientist and physician. He analyzes the functions of the psychological faculties together with the bodily functions of sensation, the practical intellect, and the theoretical faculty in human beings.

He also uses the general laws of nature and the principles of experimental science to support his conclusions, as far as those laws and principles were capable of expressing objective truth in his time.[^V4-440]

Because that scientific knowledge was still limited, Ibn Sīnā sometimes falls into scientific error in the course of his analysis. This is only natural. What matters is that he continues to apply the same practical method.

From this starting point, he studies prophecy as a human phenomenon and as a form of knowledge.

Finally, we need to distinguish between two different ways Ibn Sīnā treats this question.

The first is the one we have just examined. The second belongs to the field of social philosophy.

In the first, he approaches prophecy as a philosophical problem.

In the second, he approaches it through society’s need for a social system: one that can show people how to establish social justice and cooperate with one another in meeting their material needs.

Here Ibn Sīnā sees a necessary role for the prophet and for religious law among the “common people.”

But the way he discusses the question also shows that he did not develop a social philosophy equal to the level of his philosophy as a whole.

What he offers is mostly broad and conventional language of the kind jurists had already been using before his time. It does not even rise to the level reached by the early theologians.

He also repeats an idea we have already seen among the philosophical Sufis: that religious law is necessary for the “common people,” and that the prophet should be satisfied with their simple and absolute faith.

He should not burden their minds with deeper questions about the nature of God. It is enough for them to know that God is one, true, and without equal.

They do not need to understand ideas such as God being a simple substance that cannot be divided, or that he is neither inside the world nor outside it, and so on.

The “common people” must follow the teachings of religious law in full.

The “elite,” by contrast—meaning philosophers and Sufis—need only train their souls not to give in to bodily desires and keep themselves away from bodily states that stand in the way of happiness.[^V4-441]

Ibn Sīnā therefore has two different ways of establishing prophecy.

One grows out of his own philosophical outlook and method of inquiry.

The other comes from outside both his philosophy and his method.

This difference between what we read about prophecy in *Aḥwāl al-Nafs* and what we read in *al-Najāt* gives us reason to think that the relevant chapter in *al-Najāt* may have been added by later copyists and was not part of the original book as Ibn Sīnā dictated it to his student al-Jūzjānī.

This is supported by what al-Ahwānī says in his introduction to *Aḥwāl al-Nafs*: that this chapter is not found in *al-Najāt*.[^V4-442]

If the chapter really did come from Ibn Sīnā himself,[^V4-443] then it would not reveal a deep contradiction at the heart of his thought. It would be more likely to show a deliberate contradiction—something he may have resorted to as a form of precaution under unstable political conditions that were themselves full of conflict and contradiction.

Nor can we say that the difference between these two chapters is simply an example of a supposed general feature of Arab philosophy: “reconciling religion and philosophy.”

We reject that claim in the first place, because we do not accept that such a general feature exists at all.

But even if we did accept it, the relationship between these two chapters could not be explained in that way. There can be no meaningful question of “reconciliation” when the two texts differ in method, direction, and the whole way they approach the subject.

### C — The Question of Existence

### in Ibn Sīnā’s Philosophy

#### Introduction

To study the question of existence in Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy is, in effect, to take in almost every side of his philosophy, including questions we have already discussed and others we have not yet reached.

This is because existence is one of the central questions of philosophy. It draws in many of the other problems that repeatedly occupy a philosopher’s thought.

Above all, it includes the relation between existence and essence. It also includes the relation between existence and motion, place, and time.

Then there are the traditional categories connected with metaphysical and natural existence: substance and accident, objective and subjective existence, and Ibn Sīnā’s own categories of impossible, possible, and necessary existence.

A philosopher’s position on questions like these helps determine the broader philosophical and ideological direction of his thought.

In Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy, then, the question of existence becomes a meeting point for many important issues. It is here that we can see both the unity of his philosophy and its contradictions.

By studying this question, we are therefore also studying many of the philosophical positions of the “Master Philosopher” and their connection with his method of studying nature.

With that general aim in mind, we will examine the relation between the question of existence and the following specific issues as Ibn Sīnā presents them within his philosophical system.

#### A — Existence and Essence

The question here is simple to state: which comes first in Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy, existence or essence?

As we said earlier, the answer helps us distinguish between two broad philosophical directions.

The materialist direction gives priority to existence.

The idealist direction gives priority to essence.

The second view leads to the denial of the real, objective existence of the thing itself. Essence, understood as an “idea,” becomes the only thing with true and stable existence.

Giving priority to essence therefore leads to both an idealist and a metaphysical position.

Giving priority to existence leads in the opposite direction. The real thing exists objectively and independently, while the “idea”—its essence—is a reflection of that existence.

This is the basis of philosophical materialism.

So where does Ibn Sīnā stand between these two positions?

##### 1 — “Nothing Can Exist Before Existence”

We have enough texts to give us an answer.

They show that Ibn Sīnā chose to give priority to existence. In other words, on the question of existence and essence, he placed himself on the side of the materialist direction.

Objectively, this also tells us something about his position on what we have called the central question of philosophy: the relation between being and consciousness.

Ibn Sīnā writes:[^V4-444]

“It is possible for a thing’s essence to be the cause of one of its attributes, and for one attribute to be the cause of another. But the attribute that is the thing’s existence cannot be caused by its essence, which is not itself existence,[^V4-445] nor by another attribute. For a cause must come before its effect in existence, and nothing can exist before existence itself.”

Ibn Sīnā does more here than make the firm claim that existence comes before essence.

He also turns this into a general rule when he says that “nothing can exist before existence itself.”

In other words, absolutely nothing can come before existence.

He then gives a reason for this judgment.

A thing’s essence may cause one of its attributes, but it cannot cause the attribute of existence itself.

That is, essence cannot be the cause of the thing’s existence.

Why not?

Because if essence caused the thing to exist, essence would have to come before existence. A cause must come before its effect.

But since nothing at all can come before existence, essence cannot be the cause of existence.

So Ibn Sīnā does not simply state the conclusion. He also gives a logical argument for it.

He writes elsewhere:[^V4-446]

“The cause that brings a thing into existence, when that thing has causes that make up its essence, is the cause of some of those causes—such as form—or of all of them, and it is also the cause that brings them together...”

Taken as a general rule, this means that the cause of a thing’s existence is also the cause of the elements that make up its essence.

Its essence therefore depends on the cause of its existence.

And since a cause comes before its effect, the cause of existence comes before essence. The result is that existence comes before essence.

There is, however, another possible reading.

Ibn Sīnā may mean that when a thing is made of matter and form, its matter and form are the causes that make up its essence, while both depend in turn on the cause that brings the thing into existence.

Since the thing exists only when matter and form come together, he may mean that its existence and essence are realized at the same time.

Even if this is what he intends, however, the basic point remains the same: the cause of existence comes before the cause of essence.

And that still means treating real existence as the basis of essence. A thing can have no essence unless the thing itself actually exists.

##### 2 — Reality and the Concept

We can strengthen this conclusion by returning to what we have already seen in Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge.

He treats the reflection of reality in the senses as the basis from which abstract concepts are formed. And these concepts, at their core, are reflections of reality in thought.

These concepts are what we call essences.

Ibn Sīnā describes the process in these words:[^V4-447]

“A thing may be sensed when it is present before us, and then imagined when it is absent (...) It becomes intelligible when, for example, we conceive in Zayd the meaning of ‘human being’ that is also found in others—that is, the universal meaning that applies both to him and to others, in other words, his essence.

“When the thing is sensed, it is covered by features that are foreign to its essence. If these were removed, the essence itself would not be changed. These include its place, position, quality, and particular quantity. If we imagined these replaced by others, the true essence of its humanity would not be affected.

“The senses receive the thing while it is immersed in these features, which belong to it because of the matter from which it was made. The senses cannot separate it from them (...)

“The inner imagination also represents the thing together with these features. It cannot strip them away completely, although it can free the image from the immediate sensory relation through which it was first received (...)

“The intellect, however, is able to strip the essence of these foreign features that make the individual thing what it is as an individual. It preserves the essence itself, so that it is as though the intellect had worked upon what was sensed and turned it into something intelligible.”

This passage would also have been worth citing in our discussion of Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge. We gave similar examples there.

Here, however, we use it as evidence that Ibn Sīnā begins with the sensible thing as something that exists first.

The thing then reaches the senses as a sensory reflection, still carrying the material features that make it this particular individual.

After that, it reaches the intellect as a mental reflection stripped of those individual features, which Ibn Sīnā calls “accidents” or “foreign attachments”[^V4-448] to its essence.

They are “foreign” to the essence because essence takes from the existing and sensible thing only what it shares with other members of the same kind. It leaves aside the features that belong only to that particular individual.

When the intellect strips the sensible thing of these “accidents” and “attachments,” it is, as Ibn Sīnā says, “as though it had worked upon what was sensed and turned it into something intelligible.”

In other words, it turns the sensible thing into an idea, a concept, or an essence.

On this view of the relation between existence and essence, existence is the source of essence and the root from which essence develops.

Or, to put it another way, essence is existence transformed from the particular into the general, from an individual reality into a conceptual one.

Ibn Sīnā’s description of this process allows us to say that his theory of reflection had a real foundation.

He understood the concept—the essence—as a reflection of the features shared by things belonging to the same genus or species.

The concept applies equally to all of them because it leaves aside the special features that belong only to one individual thing.

This foundation in Ibn Sīnā once again confirms that the materialist tendency is his starting point.

Sometimes it appears openly and clearly. At other times it is hidden, or covered by an idealist layer.

But it becomes more and more visible in his theoretical and practical studies of nature.

##### 3 — Signs of Another Direction?

And yet, despite all the evidence we have seen for a materialist direction in Ibn Sīnā’s treatment of existence and essence,[^V4-449] some of his other texts seem to point another way.

At times, he seems to suggest that essences exist inside things.

At other times, he seems to suggest that they exist before things.

For example, he says in *al-Najāt* that if the universal is considered simply as a universal, without making it a condition that it must be predicated of many things—as in the meaning of “humanity”—then it actually exists in things.[^V4-450]

The author of *Sullam Baḥr al-ʿUlūm* also reports[^V4-451] that Ibn Sīnā was among those who held that the universal exists in the external world through the very existence of individual things.

That is one side of the problem.

There is another.

His theory of matter and form might also seem to suggest that essence comes before existence.

He holds that prime matter exists potentially before it receives form. Once joined with form, it exists actually.

Since potential existence is only possibility, while actual existence is realized and real, one might argue that prime matter as pure possibility is essence, while prime matter as realized reality is existence.

From this, one could conclude that essence comes before existence.

So what exactly did Ibn Sīnā mean when he said that a universal, if taken without making universality itself a condition, actually exists in things?

He did not mean that the universal exists only in things and has no existence as a universal outside them.

That is a different question, and it was not what he was discussing.

His concern was simply to “clarify the meaning of the universal.”

He wanted to say that a universal meaning, considered simply as a nature or meaning—“human being,” for example, considered simply as human being—points only to that universal nature or meaning.

Whether it applies to many or few, whether it is universal or particular, and whether it exists potentially or actually—none of these things belongs to the nature of the concept itself.

They are additional features attached to it.

So if we take the concept by itself, without requiring it to carry any of these extra meanings, then calling “humanity” a universal means that it actually exists in individual human beings.

Its existence, in this sense, is real and actual.

But this does not mean that it has no existence as a concept in the mind.

In fact, in the same chapter Ibn Sīnā also affirms this conceptual and rational existence.

He does so when he considers the universal under the condition of universality.

He says that if we take it specifically as something that can be predicated of many things in one way or another, then this universality exists only in the mind, not as something actual in the external world.

In that sense, it exists only as a possibility.

But this potentiality can be understood in two ways.

It can be understood in relation to the “humanity” that actually exists.

Or it can be understood in relation to the intelligible form of the meaning “humanity.”

If we understand potentiality in relation to actual existence, then the universal does not exist in that way. To say that it did would produce a contradiction.

If you say, “So-and-so is a human being,” it would be as though you were saying: “This person, who actually exists, is only potentially a human being.”

Or it would be like saying that a human being who exists only potentially is, at the same time, actually a human being.

That is a contradiction.

But if we understand the possibility of the universal meaning in relation to the intelligible form of “humanity,” then the universal does exist.

It exists as a mental concept, without contradiction.

This is because mental existence itself is also, in a sense, closer to potential existence.[^V4-452]

This summary of Ibn Sīnā’s discussion in “Clarifying the Meaning of the Universal” shows that he did not confine the universal to things in the external world.

It also shows that he accepted the conceptual existence of the universal.

But he did not treat it as nothing more than a name or term, as the nominalists did.[^V4-453]

For Ibn Sīnā, the universal has real content.

As for the claim in *Sullam Baḥr al-ʿUlūm* that Ibn Sīnā believed the universal exists in the external world through the very existence of individuals, this appears in a discussion that divides the universal into three kinds: the logical universal, the rational universal, and the natural universal.[^V4-454]

In that context, the author explains the Peripatetic view that neither the logical universal nor the rational universal exists in the external world.

He then notes that the Peripatetics disagreed about the natural universal.

According to the position of the leading authorities—including the “Master Philosopher,” Ibn Sīnā—the natural universal does exist in the external world through the existence of individual things.

By the **logical universal**, what is meant is the thing as a concept in itself, without regard to this or that particular universal, such as “animal” or “human being.”

The **natural universal** is the particular universal together with its real, natural meaning.

The **rational universal** is what the mind derives from the relation between the logical and natural universal, or from the relation of one to the other.[^V4-454]

Once we place the question back in this context, rather than taking the statement by itself, its meaning becomes clearer.

To say that the natural universal exists in the external world through the existence of individual things does not mean denying the universal as a concept.

It means the opposite.

It recognizes the universal as a concept while linking that concept to real content that actually exists in individual things.

The result is that the general, considered as general, has a relatively independent conceptual existence.

At the same time, it has a real existence tied to the particular things that exist in the external world.

As we understand it, this places Ibn Sīnā on the materialist side of the question, because existence comes before the concept, or essence.

One issue remains.

We still have to consider the interpretation of Ibn Sīnā’s theory of form and matter according to which prime matter exists only potentially until it receives form and becomes actually existing.

From this, it might be argued that essence—prime matter in potential form—comes before existence.

To answer that question, we need to study Ibn Sīnā’s theory of matter and form in much greater depth.

That is what we will do in the next section.

#### B — The Theory of Matter and Form

As is well known, this theory goes back to Aristotle.

But when Ibn Sīnā made it one of the foundations of his own philosophy, he handled it freely and creatively.

He adapted it to the needs of his own age, to the demands created by social development, and to the advances humanity had made by that time in the study of nature.

In other words, Ibn Sīnā reshaped Aristotle’s theory of matter and form in much the same way that he and al-Fārābī reshaped the Neoplatonic theory of emanation.

In both cases, the theory was adapted under the pressure of the same historical conditions.

As we continue, we will see some important points where Ibn Sīnā broke away from Aristotle or moved beyond the “First Teacher” toward a more clearly materialist position within medieval philosophy.

Before examining Ibn Sīnā’s theory of matter and form in detail, we should first deal with a possible misunderstanding we mentioned earlier: the idea that matter in its pure state as prime matter represents essence.

This interpretation does not fit Ibn Sīnā’s theory.

It does not even fit the Aristotelian theory from which it developed.

For both Aristotle and, later, Ibn Sīnā, matter corresponds to the thing itself, while form corresponds to the concept of that thing—that is, its essence.[^V4-455]

So the relation is actually the reverse.

At this point, then, we still have no reason to think that Ibn Sīnā places essence before existence.

We will now see whether the rest of the discussion changes that conclusion.

##### 1 — One Theoretical Foundation

Ibn Sīnā’s position on matter and form has to be studied in two domains: the metaphysical world and the natural world.

To understand the unity of his position, we need to see what place matter and form occupy, and what role they play, in each of these two domains.

We then have to examine the connection between their role in metaphysics and their role in the material world.

By doing this, we can also discover how Ibn Sīnā connects these two worlds.

That, in turn, shows us how he understands the relation between God and the world.

Does he see them as completely separate, as strict monotheism and absolute divine transcendence would seem to require?

Or does he see some form of connection and unity between them?

This is one of the central questions in Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy.

He discusses matter and form in both the metaphysical and natural parts of his system.

It is not difficult to see that the same basic foundation lies behind both.

The reason is that Ibn Sīnā begins, at root, from the external world of sense experience.

His direct involvement in the natural sciences, both theoretically and experimentally, and his close involvement with social and political problems had a deep effect on this starting point.

This helps explain why one of the most important questions in his philosophy—perhaps even the central link running through the whole system—is the movement of the world.

By this we mean the movement of change and transformation, or becoming, in both nature and society, together with the material forms of existence connected with it, such as time and space.

His scientific work in particular opened a much wider horizon for his thought.

It allowed him to see the world as being in constant motion, with movement inseparable from its very existence.[^V4-456]

It also led him to see this movement as a universal law behind the continuing changes and transformations of matter.

His scientific work also led him to discover some of the laws governing movement in things—that is, some laws of nature.

Through them, he began to see more clearly the necessary connection between existence and movement, or more precisely, between matter and movement.

At the same time, in the course of his experiments on nature, he was turning individual events into general logical laws.

These laws of thought reflected the inner laws governing movement in the individual things of nature.

##### 2 — The External World as the Starting Point

This scientific view, with its early dialectical insight, and these theoretical abstractions drawn from things that are always changing and transforming, brought Ibn Sīnā face to face with two major questions.

First: where does the movement of change and transformation that runs throughout the world come from?

Second: how does matter, in human knowledge, become separated from its direct sensory connections and turn into purely rational knowledge?

The natural sciences of Ibn Sīnā’s time had not yet developed far enough to let him answer these two questions entirely from within his own scientific and practical method.

Even so, he was able to glimpse parts of the answer to the second question.

And understanding the relation between movement and existence helps lay the foundation for understanding the relation between thought and reality, and ultimately for solving the problem of knowledge.

Ibn Sīnā inherited a vast philosophical tradition drawn from many different civilizations.

At the center of that tradition stood one great problem above all others: the origin of the world as a whole.

He came to see that the two questions that occupied him—the origin of movement in the world and the source of abstract concepts in knowledge—ultimately led back to this same larger problem.

So he turned directly to it in trying to solve the two major questions that recur throughout much of his philosophical system.

The natural-philosophical part of his work became a theoretical and practical study of the phenomena of nature.

But he was not able to explain the deepest nature of those phenomena entirely from within nature itself, independently of the metaphysical ideas that still dominated the philosophical tradition of his time.

Even so, one feature of Ibn Sīnā’s metaphysics is extremely important.

He refuses to surrender to purely subjective speculation about the world “beyond nature” and then project that speculation back onto the external world.

That had been the approach of Plato and of Neoplatonism in their philosophical systems.

With Ibn Sīnā, we see something close to the reverse.

The external world forces itself into his metaphysical thinking.

As we will see, the results of his study of natural phenomena, and the conclusions he drew from scientific experiment, leave a clear mark on his metaphysical ideas.

The first thing we notice when Ibn Sīnā discusses matter and form is that, on the metaphysical side, he places them within the framework of the Neoplatonic theory of emanation.

This already makes his treatment methodologically different from Aristotle’s, and that difference in method will also produce important differences in content.

But even the framework of emanation does not remain the same as it was in Plotinus or in later Neoplatonism.

Ibn Sīnā gives it a form of his own.

Within this new framework, elements from Plotinus, the Stoics, and Aristotle are brought together.

Among the Aristotelian elements, matter and form occupy a particularly important place.

This combination gives Ibn Sīnā a view of the external world that differs radically from Neoplatonism and also, in part—but in an important way—from Aristotle.

Neoplatonism treats essence as an “idea” and the sensory world as a product of that idea.

This is where its strict idealism appears most clearly.

Aristotle, by contrast, begins from the sensory world itself and recognizes its real, objective existence.

Ibn Sīnā goes further than Aristotle in the direction of philosophical materialism.

Within the process of emanation, he gives matter and form a more active and effective role in bringing about the existence of the material world.

He comes close to recognizing self-movement within things themselves, and to seeing movement as one of the forms in which matter exists.

This marks an extremely important step in the materialist direction of his philosophy.

It is joined by another step in the same direction: his recognition of a dialectical relation between matter and form in a way that works against the dualism Aristotle had imposed on that relation.[^V4-457]

We will see this position in its fuller form through Ibn Sīnā’s own texts.

##### 3 — The Natural Sciences

##### Reflected in Ibn Sīnā’s Metaphysics

We now want to return to a point made a little earlier: Ibn Sīnā’s metaphysics reflects conclusions he reached through both theoretical and practical study of the natural world.

It is easy to see clear reflections of the categories and laws of the material world in the structure of his metaphysical system.

These were categories and laws that he had reached through both scientific experiment and logical reasoning.

When we examine his theories about existence, we find that he builds them from these same categories and laws.

It is true that Ibn Sīnā describes “divine science” as “the science that investigates absolute existence, its attributes and its principles,” and says that this science ultimately reaches the point from which all the other sciences begin, so that “the principles of the other particular sciences are explained within this science.”[^V4-458]

But this does not contradict what we have said.

Here Ibn Sīnā is simply defining the relationship between philosophy[^V4-459] and the particular sciences that study specific kinds of existing things, such as the natural sciences.

Philosophy, as a science, deals with the most general laws and principles.

These are the principles through which the different laws discovered by the particular sciences can be understood in relation to one another.

But this does not mean that the universal principles studied by philosophy exist separately from natural and social phenomena and their specific laws.

Nor does it mean that philosophy creates these principles somewhere outside the world of natural and social phenomena.

In reality, philosophical principles—their content, origins, direction, and contradictions—are the highly developed and complex result of the positions philosophers take toward the external world.

And above all, they reflect their position toward social reality.

So when we say that Ibn Sīnā begins his philosophy of existence from the categories and laws of the material world, we are not claiming that he is unique in this respect.

This is the objective starting point of every philosophy and every philosopher.

What distinguishes Ibn Sīnā is that he does not hide this starting point, ignore it, or bury it under subjective speculation that tries to cut itself off from reality.

Instead, he remains closely tied to the roots from which his “divine science” draws the principles that later become general principles for the particular sciences.

Ibn Sīnā gives us evidence of this connection in two ways.

First, in the way he handles the principles of “divine science” in general.

Second, in the way he treats the theory of matter and form in particular.

The first can be seen in the subjects discussed in the opening section of the metaphysics in both *al-Shifāʾ* and *al-Najāt*, and in the third section of *al-Ishārāt*, which deals with metaphysics.

These subjects include a number of principles that Ibn Sīnā calls the “principles of physics.”[^V4-460]

And they really are questions that express categories and laws drawn from the observation of natural phenomena themselves.

He discusses them in the sections of his works devoted to “divine science” and uses them as the foundation for his metaphysical theories.
