He studies substance and accident, the one and the many, cause and effect, the meaning of the universal, the eternal and the created, sensible qualities, potentiality and actuality, condensation and rarefaction, and the relation between matter and form.

He examines all of these through their existence in nature and then draws general conclusions from them.

Those conclusions become the theoretical basis for his discussions in “divine science.”

The questions of substance and accident; the division of existence into necessary, possible, and impossible; its further division into eternal and created; the distinction within created existence between what is essential and what is temporal; and the question of matter and form together with the theory of causes—all of these are basic pillars of Ibn Sīnā’s metaphysics.

But he places these pillars within the world of metaphysics only after developing them from broader conclusions drawn from his study of nature.[^V4-461]

The case of matter and form is even clearer.

First, the theory is closely connected with every one of the questions we have just mentioned.

Second, it is almost the central nerve of Ibn Sīnā’s entire theory of the cosmos.

It is not enough to say that his general conclusions about matter and form grew out of the study of natural bodies.

We also need to add that the role they play in his metaphysics is an extension and continuation of the material role they play in the natural world.

Through this continuity, they help Ibn Sīnā present the world as one connected whole rather than as two separate worlds, one spiritual and the other material.

The eternal material unity of the world, as it appears in this philosophical system, resolves the contradiction and removes the dualism contained in Aristotle’s idea of the “unmoved mover.”

That idea ends the chain of motion in the eternal stillness of the first mover. This is the contradiction.

It also separates the fixed and unmoving world of the “mover” from the changing and transforming world of what is moved. This is the dualism.

And through both, Aristotle’s idealism appears despite some of the materialist starting points in his philosophy.[^V4-462]

#### C — One Material World

How does this unity of the world appear in Ibn Sīnā’s philosophical system?

To answer that question clearly, we need to separate three interlocking parts of the problem and look at each in turn.

##### First Link

The problem of necessary existence: Ibn Sīnā develops this question in his own independent way. His theory begins by dividing existence, in principle, into three kinds: what must exist, what can exist, and what cannot exist. But when he turns to what actually exists, only the first two remain.

As he puts it:

> “Every existing thing, considered in itself and without reference to anything else, must be one of two things. Either existence belongs to it necessarily in itself, or it does not. If existence belongs to it necessarily, then it is the truth in itself [God], whose existence is necessary through itself, the Self-Subsisting.[^V4-463] If existence does not belong to it necessarily, we cannot call it impossible in itself once we have already assumed that it exists.”

We call something impossible only when we assume that its cause does not exist. If its cause does exist, then its existence becomes necessary. But if we consider the thing simply in itself, apart from any such condition, then it is only possible.

> “Considered in itself, then, it is something whose existence is neither necessary nor impossible. Every existing thing is therefore either necessary in itself or possible in itself.”[^V4-464]

But even this division between necessary and possible existence becomes purely theoretical once Ibn Sīnā divides necessary existence itself into two kinds: what is necessary in itself, and what is necessary through something else. As long as a possible thing actually exists, it becomes necessary through something else.

And when we learn, in addition, that what is necessary through another is “eternally and always existent,” just like what is necessary in itself, then calling it merely “possible” becomes little more than a distinction we make in thought. It serves only to distinguish it, considered in itself, from what is necessary through itself. In actual existence, both are equally necessary.

Ibn Sīnā writes:

> “Every possible existent either has its existence through itself or through some cause. If it exists through itself, then its essence is necessary in existence, not possible in existence. If it exists through a cause, then either its existence becomes necessary when the cause exists, or it remains as it was before the cause existed. The latter is impossible. Its existence must therefore become necessary when its cause exists. Thus everything that is possible in itself is necessary in existence through another.”[^V4-465]

So there is no existing thing whose existence is not necessary.

The distinction between what is necessary in itself and what is necessary through another now becomes a distinction based simply on the fact that the first is regarded as the cause of the second.

And with that, the problem moves to another link.

##### Second Link

The problem of cause and effect: here we can expect an important development in the logic of Ibn Sīnā’s theory. The relationship between the Necessary Existent as cause and the necessary existent as effect will show us where the logic of the entire system is heading.

The first thing we find is that, in actual existence, the relationship rests on a kind of balance between cause and effect, despite the conceptual distinction between what is necessary in itself and what is necessary through another.

Ibn Sīnā says:

> “The principle of all things is an essence whose existence is necessary, and what proceeds from the Necessary Existent must necessarily exist.”[^V4-466]

In other words, the two exist together. One does not come after the other.

Since the “First Principle” necessarily exists and is the cause of everything else that exists, its effect must also exist necessarily. It cannot fail to follow from it, and there is no temporal “before” and “after” between them.

It is true, as Ibn Sīnā says, that “the cause has a prior claim to existence over the effect.” But he also says that cause and effect, “simply as two things, do not necessarily involve the relation of earlier and later, nor even the relation of being ‘together.’ They may instead be correlatives: cause and effect, and in that sense they are together.”[^V4-467]

He continues:

> “One thing can be after another in many ways, such as being after it in time or place. What concerns us here, however, is priority in the claim to existence, even if the two things can exist at the same time. This is the case when one thing exists through another, while the other does not exist through it. The first does not acquire existence unless the other already has existence and existence has come to it (...) This is a kind of posteriority in itself.”[^V4-468]

So the “after” in this case is not an after in time or place. It is purely a relation in meaning: a way of describing the relation between cause and effect.

This brings us to the eternity of existence—that is, the eternity of the world.

If the “First Principle” exists necessarily, there can never have been a time when it did not exist. It is therefore eternal and without beginning.

And if its effect—this existing world—cannot fail to exist whenever the First Principle exists, then the effect must also be eternal and without beginning.

The eternity of the world is therefore built into the heart of Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy.

But it is not there simply as a logical result of the assumption we have just described. Behind it lies a large body of theoretical work that the “Master and Chief” pursued deliberately, persistently, and with great care.

It is very easy for anyone studying Ibn Sīnā to conclude that the eternity of the world stands among the central foundations of his philosophy. We can say this without hesitation.

Some bourgeois scholars—Orientalists, Arab scholars, and Eastern scholars more generally—have allowed themselves to claim that Ibn Sīnā abandoned many of his earlier ideas in his last major work, *Pointers and Reminders*.[^V4-469] But they cannot reasonably claim that he abandoned the eternity of the world there.

The opposite is true. In that very late work, he added new dimensions to the idea that made it even stronger and deeper.

The fact that he kept the theory of the world’s eternity until the end of his philosophical life means that he also kept the theoretical foundation of all the materialist positions contained in his philosophy.

The eternity of the world cannot be separated from the material character of the world.

We do not say this simply because of a theoretical preference. We say it because Ibn Sīnā’s own words lead us there.

His commitment to the eternity of the world led him to explore the relation between cause and effect in much greater depth. From this he developed ideas pointing toward the unity and eternity of motion, matter, and time—and finally toward the unity of the world on this material basis, rather than the Sufi idea of the “unity of being.”

All of these are basic elements of philosophical materialism.

In *Pointers and Reminders* itself, Ibn Sīnā says:[^V4-470]

> “The existence of the effect depends on the cause precisely in the state in which that cause is a cause, whether by nature, will, or something else (...) If nothing external prevents it, and the agent exists in itself but is not a cause merely through its bare existence, then the existence of the effect depends on the presence of the condition just mentioned. When that condition exists—whether it is nature, a firm will, or something else—the effect must exist. Whatever is assumed to exist forever will have what necessarily accompanies it forever; and whatever exists only at a certain time will have what accompanies it only at that time. If something can remain forever in the same condition and has an effect, there is nothing strange in that effect proceeding from it forever. And if we refuse to call this effect a thing acted upon simply because it was not preceded by non-existence, there is no difficulty once the meaning has become clear.”[^V4-471]

This passage marks an important development in Ibn Sīnā’s understanding of the causal relation between the “First Principle” and the world.

He is not merely saying that the world is eternal because there must be a fixed and complete correspondence between the “First Cause” and its effect. The argument goes further, toward the denial of creation itself—that is, the denial that the world was brought into existence from nothing.

Of course, that idea is already contained in the claim that the world is eternal. Something eternal cannot have been preceded by non-existence. If it had been, it would not be eternal in the first place.

But Ibn Sīnā states the point here in a way that carries a further implication.

Look again at his words: “If we refuse to call this effect a thing acted upon simply because it was not preceded by non-existence...”

The effect—that is, the world—is therefore not a “thing made” by the First Cause in the ordinary sense.

If that is so, then the First Cause cannot be called a “maker” in that same sense either.

And if there is neither a maker nor a thing made, then there is no act of making.[^V4-472]

The relation between cause and effect here is therefore not a relation in which one thing acts and another receives the action.

What, then, is the real nature of the causal relation between what is necessary in itself and what is necessary through another?

We find the key in another statement by Ibn Sīnā:

> “Origination is when the existence of one thing comes from another and depends directly on it, without any intermediary of matter, instrument, or time. Whatever is preceded by non-existence cannot do without an intermediary. Origination is therefore higher in rank than formation and temporal bringing-into-being.”[^V4-473]

This passage comes immediately after the previous one. It therefore explains the relationship Ibn Sīnā has just described between cause and effect—that is, between God and the world.

It is a relation of **origination**, not one of **formation** or **temporal bringing-into-being**. And origination stands above the other two.

We therefore have three concepts: origination, formation, and temporal bringing-into-being.

What exactly does each one mean?

Ibn Sīnā defines origination clearly. It is the direct dependence of the effect’s existence on its cause, without being preceded by matter, an instrument, or time.

His commentator Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī[^V4-474] explains the other two terms. Formation, he says, “is when one thing produces a material existence, while temporal bringing-into-being is when one thing produces an existence in time. Each stands opposed to origination in one respect. Origination comes before both, because matter itself cannot arise through formation, and time itself cannot arise through temporal bringing-into-being. Otherwise matter would have to be preceded by another matter, and time by another time. Formation and temporal bringing-into-being therefore come after origination. Origination is closer than either of them to the First Cause, and so it is higher in rank.”[^V4-475]

From Ibn Sīnā’s text and al-Ṭūsī’s explanation, we can draw the following conclusion.

The world came into existence through origination because its existence follows necessarily from the eternal relation between the First Cause and its effect.

It cannot have come about through formation, because that would require matter to have been preceded by some earlier matter. Yet matter proceeds directly from the First Cause and therefore cannot have come from matter before it.

Nor can the world have come about through temporal bringing-into-being, because that would require time to have been preceded by another time. But because time is eternal, there was no earlier time before it.

This is what al-Ṭūsī means when he says that formation and temporal bringing-into-being come after origination. Origination is the “process” that comes before the other two.

The main conclusion we can draw from these two passages by Ibn Sīnā, together with al-Ṭūsī’s explanation of the second, is that the origin of the world lies in an eternal bond of existence between the “First Originator” and what it originates.

This bond is governed by a necessary law. Ibn Sīnā expresses it by saying that “the existence of the effect depends on the cause precisely in the state in which that cause is a cause.”

It is not governed by the relation between a maker and something made, because what is originated cannot properly be called a “thing made.” That description applies only “when something was non-existent and then, because of something else, came to exist after its non-existence.”[^V4-476]

But we have already assumed that what is originated is just as eternal as the “First Cause.”

The unavoidable logical conclusion is therefore that the origin of the world lies in a necessary and eternal unity of existence between the first, originating cause and the matter of the world that proceeds from it.[^V4-477]

We can state the same conclusion in another way.

If the world is just as eternal as its cause, and if both equally share in beginningless and everlasting existence; if this equality or balance means that the originated effect cannot be called a thing “made”—which also means that its originating cause cannot be called a “maker,” and that “origination” cannot be called an act of making—then the difference between the “First Cause” and its effect, the world, becomes purely conceptual.

Everything said so far leads to the conclusion that the two belong together within a single unity of existence, one in which the dividing line between cause and effect finally disappears. The cause becomes the effect, and the effect the cause.

If this conclusion is correct—and the texts already quoted support it, as do other passages we will examine later—then we can agree with the view reached by one contemporary Arab researcher.[^V4-478]

He argues that when Ibn Sīnā describes the Necessary Existent as “separate from matter,” a phrase Ibn Sīnā uses often, he does not mean that it is completely without matter. Instead, this researcher argues, “separation” refers only to a difference in rank among existing things. The Necessary Existent is “separate from matter” in the sense that it is more perfect and higher than matter.

The same researcher is also right to say that even this “difference in rank” almost disappears when we find passages in Ibn Sīnā suggesting a certain likeness or continuity between cause and effect.[^V4-479]

Indeed, as we have just argued, the relation between them appears as a bond within a necessary unity of existence.

If that is so, how can Ibn Sīnā mean by “separate from matter” that the Necessary Existent is completely free of matter?

But while drawing this conclusion from the logic of Ibn Sīnā’s texts, we cannot ignore other passages in which he says very clearly that “separate from matter” does mean complete freedom from matter.

For example, in *The Salvation* he writes:[^V4-480]

> “The Necessary Existent is not a body, nor the matter of a body, nor the form of a body, nor intelligible matter for an intelligible form, nor an intelligible form in intelligible matter. It is not divisible in quantity, in principles, or in definition. It is one in all three of these respects.”

This statement could hardly be clearer. There is no room to explain it away through interpretation.

How, then, can we reconcile it with what we have just seen?

Should we take this passage as the real foundation of Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy and interpret the earlier passages through it? If so, would our conclusion about Ibn Sīnā’s idea of one material world turn out to have been based on illusion or wishful thinking?

Our answer is no.

First, the earlier passages are no less clear in what they imply than this one. The idea we drew from them is not based on illusion or emotion, nor did it result from forcing an interpretation onto them. There is no need for interpretation when the wording and the content point in the same direction without ambiguity.

Second, no scholar who has seriously studied Ibn Sīnā’s thought—from al-Ghazālī and Ibn Rushd down to the present—has been able to deny that Ibn Sīnā believed in the eternity of the world. This is true whether the scholar opposed him or supported him, and whether the scholar was an idealist or a materialist.

The eternity of the world is a foundational principle of Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy.

That is precisely why it became one of the main points of attack for al-Ghazālī and his followers, who for a long time fought relentlessly against what they saw as the materialist tendency in Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy.

Across Ibn Sīnā’s works, the eternity of the world appears again and again, supported through different explanations and arguments. Sometimes he states it directly, as in the examples we have quoted. Elsewhere he reaches it through other questions: coming-to-be in itself and in time, motion and time, and, beneath all of these, the relation between matter and form.

We therefore cannot claim that the passages describing the Necessary Existent as completely separate from matter represent the basic foundation of his philosophy.

If the eternity of the world is the foundation, then that idea loses its meaning if we also insist that the Necessary Existent is absolutely cut off from matter.

Accepting the eternity of the world means accepting the necessary bond between what is necessary in itself and what is necessary through another—that is, between the First Cause and its effect, the material world.

Otherwise Ibn Sīnā would have had to accept two separate eternal realities: the Necessary Existent in itself on the one hand, and the world, or matter, on the other. He would also have had to separate the First Cause from its effect.

His philosophy rejects both conclusions.

On the one hand, Ibn Sīnā strongly insists that there can be only one eternal principle. On the other, he insists on the necessary bond between the First Cause and its effect in the process of “origination.”

He treats this bond as an absolute law that even the Necessary Existent cannot escape.

We can see this necessity in the way he turns the “possible in itself”—the world—into something that is “necessary through another.”

What, then, does it mean to call the possible “necessary”?

This is not an empty matter of terminology. In Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy, necessity is the real content of the idea.

He devoted great effort to proving that something possible in itself, “once it has existence, is necessary through another”; that it is impossible for it not to exist when its cause exists; and that “everything possible in itself is necessary in existence through another.”[^V4-481]

All of this means that the “necessary” existence of what is possible in itself consists in its necessary submission to the law binding cause and effect together. The two cannot be separated, nor can one be completely detached from the other.

Seen from this angle, the logic of Ibn Sīnā’s theory of the eternity of the world leads us to conclude that his phrase “separate from matter” cannot mean completely free of matter.

Yet other texts clearly do use the phrase in exactly that sense.

We are therefore left with only one conclusion: these different passages place Ibn Sīnā in contradiction with himself.

What is the basis of this contradiction?

This question deserves some attention.

We should not treat it as a personal problem unique to Ibn Sīnā among medieval Islamic philosophers. Almost all of them fell into this same difficulty.

Sometimes they did so knowingly and deliberately. At other times they slipped into it without realizing it.

But in neither case should we explain the contradiction by claiming, as idealist historians do, that they were deliberately trying to construct a compromise between opposing positions.

Whether the contradiction was conscious or unconscious, it had a historical basis.

We see that basis in two features of the period.

The first was **intellectual terror**.

The defenders of official ideology used intellectual repression to hold back the growth of materialist tendencies in philosophy and to protect the absolute, despotic power of the ruling group. Religious ideology was the strongest fortress protecting that power, especially as its other, more material supports were rapidly weakening under the pressure of social and political conflicts on every side.

The second was an **internal contradiction in the development of philosophy itself**.

Knowledge of nature responded more quickly and more fully than philosophy did to the changing needs of Arab-Islamic society. But the interaction between scientific knowledge and social needs inevitably affected the development of philosophy as well.

These effects appeared in historically specific forms in which observation of reality and metaphysics remained mixed together.

The new results of scientific development ran into accumulated metaphysical assumptions inherited from many different sources in the history of philosophy, from different periods and different ideologies.

Above all, they ran into the “spiritualist” tendency that Neoplatonism had introduced into the Greek philosophical inheritance as a whole, including its Aristotelian side.[^V4-482]

There is no doubt that Ibn Sīnā, like al-Fārābī before him, worked extremely hard to put the results of his scientific knowledge—especially Ibn Sīnā’s own more advanced knowledge and experiments—to theoretical use in the development of philosophy.

More than al-Fārābī, Ibn Sīnā was able to reshape many traditional philosophical assumptions in line with his experimental outlook and with the demands of the ideological struggles of his time.

Even so, we have to keep two things in mind.

The first is the historical level that scientific knowledge had reached at the time.

The second is the authority carried by Aristotle’s name through the *Theology of Aristotle*, a work that was actually derived from Plotinus.

Both factors made it harder to arrive at fully materialist solutions to philosophical problems.

They created an internal contradiction within the development of philosophy, added to the external contradiction produced by the atmosphere of official intellectual and ideological terror.

It was extremely difficult to overcome both contradictions completely.

Despite all of this, however, Ibn Sīnā did not resolve his contradiction by abandoning one side of it—his materialist starting point—in favor of the idealist side.

He remained faithful to his basic starting points.

This is the critical, historical, and realistic perspective from which we should approach the materialist side of his philosophy. Otherwise, we fall into the trap of the idealist method and of judgments driven by unjustified emotion.

On this basis, we can continue examining the material unity of the world through another closely connected part of Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy.

##### Third Link

##### — Coming-to-Be in Itself and in Time

Here we find another form in which Ibn Sīnā expresses his firm belief in the eternity of the world.

This form also reveals a new dimension of the idea, one that will connect directly with his treatment of matter, motion, and time.

In Ibn Sīnā’s philosophical system, existing things are divided into what is eternal and what comes into being.

But some things that “come into being,” in one sense, turn out in another sense to be eternal.

He writes:

> “A thing is called eternal either in itself or in time. What is eternal in itself is that whose essence has no principle through which it exists. What is eternal in time is that whose time has no beginning. What comes into being is also of two kinds. One is that whose essence has a principle through which it exists. The other is that whose time has a beginning: there was a time when it did not exist, and a before in which it was absent (...) For everything whose existence has a temporal beginning, rather than the beginning of origination, is preceded by time and by matter existing before it.”[^V4-483]

What Ibn Sīnā seems to mean by what is eternal in itself is the Necessary Existent in itself.

What is eternal in time, however, includes every being without a beginning in time. That includes both what is necessary in itself and what is necessary through another, because neither has a first moment of existence.

The thing that “has a principle through which its essence exists” is what Ibn Sīnā treats as coming into being **in itself**. This description applies to what is necessary through another and receives its existence through “origination.”

The thing “whose time has a beginning,” by contrast, is what comes into being **in time**.

This second kind is what truly deserves to be called something that has come into being in the philosophical sense.

Even here, however, it is not preceded by absolute nothingness.

Ibn Sīnā says that the “before” preceding it is itself something that exists:

> “The before that belonged to it is something existing. And that existing thing is no longer present now, so it is something that has passed and once existed. That earlier existing thing is either an essence in itself, which is time”—by which he means time in general—“or an essence belonging to something else, which is its time”—that is, the particular time belonging to that particular thing. “In either case, time is established.”[^V4-484]

That is one side of the problem.

There is also another: “Nothing can come into being unless it is preceded by the existence of something capable of receiving it, and this is matter.”[^V4-485]

And there is a third side.

Anything that needs to come into being before it exists must, in itself, be possible rather than impossible. If it were impossible in itself, it could never exist at all. But we have already assumed that it does exist.

The important question is what Ibn Sīnā means by saying that it is possible.

He writes:

> “Its possibility of existence does not mean that the agent has the power to produce it. The agent cannot produce it unless it is already possible in itself (...) Its being possible in itself is considered in relation to its own essence, while its being within the power of an agent is considered in relation to the one that brings it into existence (...) Whatever can come into existence is preceded by its possibility of existence. That possibility is therefore something that exists. And every existing meaning either exists independently of a subject or exists in a subject (...) The possibility of existence is not an independent substance without a subject. It is therefore a meaning existing in a subject, an attribute of that subject. We call the possibility of existence the power to exist, and we call what carries this power—the thing in which the power of the thing’s existence resides—a subject, *hylē*, matter, and so on. Therefore every thing that comes into being is preceded by matter.”[^V4-486]

What do these passages tell us, once we focus on the parts that matter most to our discussion?

They tell us four things.

First, anything that comes into being in time must be preceded by some time. Time therefore necessarily exists before the thing that comes into being.

Second, it must also be preceded by matter that already exists.

Third, the thing itself, insofar as it is possible, already had a possible mode of existence before its actual existence. It existed potentially in matter before becoming actual.

Fourth, this possibility belongs to the thing itself as something possible. It does not receive that possibility from the power of whatever brings it into existence. Its possibility does not come from outside. If possibility did not already belong to it in itself, its producer would have no power to produce it.

That is what these passages tell us directly.

But behind their direct wording lies a deeper foundation:

1. **Time is eternal.** Every particular stretch of time comes from an eternal time.[^V4-487]

2. **Matter is eternal.** No being comes into existence except from eternal matter.

3. **It follows from the first two points that nothing comes into existence from absolute nothingness.**

4. Ibn Sīnā imagines a thing coming into being as a change from an earlier time, in which the thing did not yet exist in its present form, to a later time in which it does. This change takes place through a subject, *hylē*, or matter.

If we translated this picture into the language of our own time—the language of dialectical materialism—we could describe it as the movement of change and becoming within the material world.

But Ibn Sīnā expresses the idea in the language of his own age and through a scientific outlook that had not yet matured enough to express fully what dialectical materialism would later express through the historically developed categories of Marxist science and through the achievements of modern science and technology.

All of this concerns what comes into being **in time**.

But that is different from what comes into being **in itself**.

What is the difference between them?

In one respect they agree; in another they differ.

Both can be described as existing “after” not existing, and so both involve a “before” and an “after.”

But for something that comes into being in time, the before and after are temporal.

For something that comes into being in itself, the before and after belong only to its relation of dependence. They are conceptual rather than temporal.

At first sight, this difference may seem merely formal.

In reality it is fundamental. It is the same difference that separates what is eternal from what comes and goes: what exists eternally from what exists only temporarily.

The first is what comes into being in itself. The second is what comes into being in time.

And the existence of the second depends on the existence of the first.

As we have already seen, nothing can come into being in time unless it is preceded by time and matter. This “matter” is itself what comes into being in itself.

If we look more closely at this division in Ibn Sīnā—especially his division of coming-to-be into these two kinds—a question of great importance appears.

Why did Ibn Sīnā make two separate divisions?

First, why did he divide the eternal into what is eternal in itself and what is eternal in time?

Second, why did he also divide what comes into being into what comes into being in itself and what comes into being in time?

Why did he not simply keep the traditional philosophical division between the eternal and the originated?

In our view, the answer lies in Ibn Sīnā’s desire to find a way of expressing what we would call an early scientific view of change and becoming.

He sees motion as a law running throughout existence: an absolute law and an objective reality about which, for him, there is no doubt.

For that reason, he sees motion and existence—that is, matter—as inseparable.

There is no existing thing without motion, and no motion without something that moves: without a subject in which motion takes place.

I call this an outlook with a scientific character because I believe that Ibn Sīnā’s philosophical understanding of motion grew out of his scientific work with nature.

He established the inseparability of motion and matter in several different ways, all of which lead him toward the conclusion that motion is as eternal as matter.

One example is his use of a chain of continually renewed causes arranged by their nearness to or distance from the “First Cause.”

He writes:

> “The principles of coming-to-be ultimately depend on the nearness or distance of causes, and this occurs through motion. If one motion is preceded by another motion, and that earlier motion brings the causes to this later motion, then the two are like things touching one another.”[^V4-488]

He then develops the point further.

Motion, for him, means change and renewal, and this is also connected with time.

For one motion to follow another—for one change and renewal to follow another—one “now” must follow another “now.”

He says:

> “There must be one of the successive ‘nows’ of time in which something has been brought near through a change, or brought near after a change. That ‘now’ will then mark the end of the first motion and the beginning of another motion or another event. If it leads to another motion and produces another new motion or new event, then the motion that serves as the immediate cause of this motion touches it directly. What this touching means is clear: there cannot be a period of time between two motions in which there is no motion. For it has already been shown in natural philosophy that time follows motion.”

He concludes by saying that whatever form its coming-to-be takes, what comes into being “depends on motion; it cannot be otherwise.”[^V4-488]

We have already seen how Ibn Sīnā argues for the eternity of time.[^V4-489]

Here he also establishes a necessary connection between time and motion. Notice his statement that “there cannot be a period of time between two motions in which there is no motion.”

And in the same passage he goes on to connect motion and time with the formation of matter, saying that whatever comes into being, however it does so, “depends on motion; it cannot be otherwise.”

In this sense, Ibn Sīnā comes close—or almost reaches in his own mind—a view of the essential relation between matter, motion, and time: that motion and time are forms of matter’s existence.

This objective connection between matter, motion, and time forms the basis of the law of change and transformation as a universal law.

Ibn Sīnā therefore saw this law in some form, and he also saw the foundation on which it rests.

That is why he repeatedly tried to express the idea in the different forms available to him.

His division between what comes into being in itself and what comes into being in time is, in our view, not merely one such form. It is also one of his most scientific and realistic ways of understanding change and transformation as a process that takes place in a lasting subject: its appearances change and transform, while the underlying subject itself remains eternal and does not perish.[^V4-490]

What comes into being in itself is this lasting subject.

What comes into being in time is the changing and transforming appearance of that subject.

As for the claim that the subject itself—what comes into being in itself—is eternal and does not perish, Ibn Sīnā expresses this in his discussion of coming-to-be in itself when he says that what is originated in itself is “originated throughout all time and eternity.”[^V4-491]

##### Fourth Link

##### — The Self-Motion of Matter

We should say at once that by raising the question of the self-motion of matter here, we are **not** claiming that Ibn Sīnā explicitly established this as a doctrine in his philosophy.

Nor are we joining the tendency to “modernize” ancient thought by projecting modern ideas and theories back onto medieval thinkers, as though everything our own age thinks must also have been thought by ancient scientists and philosophers, and even thought in exactly the same way.

This kind of “modernization” is foreign to the historical materialist method of studying the intellectual heritage because it rests fundamentally on an **ahistorical** way of thinking.

But we must also be careful not to fall into ahistorical thinking from the opposite direction.

An exaggerated attempt to avoid anachronism can itself become ahistorical.

If we deny every connection between modern scientific or philosophical theories and older scientific and philosophical hypotheses, we break the historical continuity of human knowledge between its past and its present.

That too is a form of ahistorical thinking.[^V4-492]

So we do not claim that Ibn Sīnā possessed the idea of “self-motion” exactly as it is understood in advanced modern theories.

But neither can we say that the idea was completely absent from ancient science and philosophy.

That claim would be just as mistaken, and the historical materialist method rejects it for the same reason that it rejects the opposite tendency to “modernize” the past.

Our position, then, is that the idea of self-motion has roots reaching far back into the history of human knowledge.

We find some of those roots in Ibn Sīnā, and we can present them in the following order.

First, in one of his *Pointers*, Ibn Sīnā puts forward the idea that “what is higher does not seek something for the sake of what is lower.”

In other words, something higher “does not act for a purpose connected with something beneath it in rank.”

Ibn Sīnā means that God has no purpose directed toward any created being.

We then discover that this statement prepares the way for another claim:

> “Everything that moves continuously through will expects some purpose from its motion, such as displaying its generosity—that is, showing favor toward what it moves for—or becoming worthy of praise.”

But one who stands above all such things—God—“is far too exalted for motion and will.”[^V4-493]

This means that motion does not come from God. God is above motion and will.

Al-Ṭūsī explains Ibn Sīnā’s statement in these words:

> “The point is that the Creator and the perfect intellects, in their origination, do not directly carry out motion.”[^V4-494]

Second, Ibn Sīnā says in another *Pointer*:[^V4-495]

> “If a body is left to itself and to its own nature, and no foreign influence acts on it from outside, it must have a particular place and a particular form. Therefore its own nature contains the principle that requires this.”

We understand this to mean that a body, simply by being what it is and according to its own nature, tends toward a particular place and form.

The word “nature” here is a technical philosophical term.

For philosophers, the nature of a thing is the principle from which its motion arises, whether that motion is conscious or unconscious.[^V4-496]

What, then, does Ibn Sīnā mean when he adds that “no foreign influence acts on it from outside”?

Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī explains that this phrase is directed against the theologians’ claim that bodies occupy their particular places only because a freely choosing agent assigns each body to a particular place.[^V4-497]

In other words, Ibn Sīnā rejects the idea that a body’s presence in a particular place, or its movement toward that place, is caused by a free agent outside the body.

Instead, it comes from the body itself: from its nature and from what belongs to it by its own character.

We have not left the subject of motion here, because a body’s tendency toward a place is spatial motion.

This is motion of matter, and it is also connected with matter’s form.

Form consists of the internal relations within matter itself, including relations of place, time, position, quantity, quality, and others.

In another *Pointer*, Ibn Sīnā states:[^V4-498]

> “If a body finds itself in conditions that are not required by its own nature—that is, conditions not demanded by what it is in itself—then its being in those conditions belongs to the realm of possibility and may be due to some agent. But its own nature allows it to change away from them unless something prevents it. If these conditions concern place or position, then according to its nature it is possible for it to move away from them.”[^V4-499]

In other words, if a body is forced into a state that does not belong to its own nature, it can move out of that state by itself unless some external force prevents it.

Third, Ibn Sīnā speaks about the **primary** and **secondary perfections** of bodies.[^V4-500]

By primary perfections, he means those things that make the body’s very existence possible. If they disappeared, the body itself could no longer exist.

By secondary perfections, he means things that are not conditions of existence itself but conditions of the body’s development and advancement.[^V4-501]

Among these secondary perfections, he includes the actions performed by bodies.

These actions take place through powers present within bodies, powers through which their existence is continually renewed.

From this he concludes:

> “There is nothing among existing bodies that moves or comes to rest by itself or through a form, or performs any other action, without this coming either from another body or from a power flowing from a body. Nothing proceeds from a body unless there is within it one of the powers already mentioned, and from that power come both the action in question and every other action that proceeds from it.”[^V4-502]

He then divides these “powers immersed in bodies” into three kinds.

There are **natural powers**.

There are **psychic powers**, meaning the powers of life: the vegetative soul, animal soul, and human soul.

And there are **celestial powers**, belonging to the celestial soul.

He defines the natural powers as:

> “The principle in itself of their motions in themselves, their states of rest in themselves, and their other perfections that belong to them in themselves. No natural body is without this power.”[^V4-503]

As for the source of these powers, Ibn Sīnā traces them back to only two principles that remain joined to things rather than existing separately from them: matter and form.

He then traces these two principles back to the “separate principle.”

We have already discussed how this “separate principle” should be understood within Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy.

The important point here is that the actions of natural bodies—including their motions—come from the body itself. They do not come “from another body or from a power flowing from a body.” Nothing proceeds from a body unless the body itself contains the power from which that action proceeds.

Fourth, in a chapter on motion,[^V4-504] Ibn Sīnā describes its function within a body.

Motion, he says, is:

> “The act and first perfection of something that exists potentially, insofar as it is potential. A body that is actually in one place and potentially in another, so long as it remains still in the first place, is potentially moving and potentially arriving. Once it moves, it acquires a first actuality and perfection, through which it reaches a second actuality and perfection: arrival.”

This means that when a body stands between two states—one possible or potential, the other actual—it depends on motion both to achieve its first actuality and perfection and to reach its second.

Motion is continually present within it. The body is “potentially moving and potentially arriving.”

This statement tells us more than simply that motion accompanies matter both in its potential and actual forms of existence.

It also suggests that Ibn Sīnā sees motion as an **internal activity** through which matter realizes its “perfections.”

But placing motion in such a close relation with matter creates a conflict with another position Ibn Sīnā takes: his denial that motion is a substance in the way matter is.[^V4-505]

Here he separates the two.

In fact, this position becomes caught in a whole network of contradictions.

The theoretical basis of the problem is that Ibn Sīnā’s concept of substance is static and “metaphysical.”[^V4-506]

He argues:

> “Every substance exists all at once, and its existence also ceases all at once. Motion therefore enters neither into its coming-to-be nor into its passing-away.”

Motion, by contrast, is said of a stable state in a body that is left behind little by little as the body moves toward something. Reaching that thing remains potential rather than actual until the process is complete.

He therefore concludes that motion must always involve leaving one state for another. The state in question must also be capable of decrease and increase, because when a thing leaves it gradually while moving toward something else, some of it still remains.[^V4-507]

Here Ibn Sīnā is trying to distinguish substance from motion.

Motion means gradual change and transformation from one settled state toward another, with the process taking place step by step. Something of the destination remains only potential until it has been fully reached in actuality.

That is his concept of motion.

Substance, by contrast, exists actually. It has already arrived and is settled, without gradual stages. It exists “all at once,” in the present instant.

This is why Ibn Sīnā came to associate motion with **accident** rather than with substance.

But separating substance from motion in this way led him to treat motion as the sole explanation of change and transformation.

In doing so, he neglected the internal relation between the thing undergoing change and the motion through which that change takes place—even though, in other passages, he acknowledges exactly this internal relation.

We have already seen examples of this.

We find another in his discussion of forms whose matter can lose them.

Here the change and renewal of things through becoming appears as a single, complex process. It does not depend on motion alone, separated from the thing that moves.

Instead, one form replaces another within matter in a single process: the new is born from within the old itself, and the old dies as the new is born.

Speaking of forms whose matter can lose them, Ibn Sīnā says that when one such form disappears from matter:

> “Another must replace it, since it has been established that this matter cannot exist without form. There is therefore a new coming-to-be of the thing whose form is the second, and the passing-away of the thing whose form was the first.”[^V4-508]

What we have here is therefore a single process of “coming-to-be and passing-away”: the birth of something new and the death of something old.

Both take place successively in the same subject, matter, as forms replace one another within it.

Coming-to-be and passing-away are therefore intertwined, and both have the same subject.[^V4-509]

Taken by itself, this explanation comes close to a scientific understanding of change and transformation.

It is worth noticing, however, that Ibn Sīnā then describes this very change by saying:

> “It is not coming-to-be and passing-away, but alteration, growth, locomotion, or something similar.”

This final statement is based on the Peripatetic doctrine that coming-to-be and passing-away happen all at once, in an instant, rather than gradually.[^V4-510]

To bring change and transformation within the laws of motion, and to keep them separate from substance, which he believed did not undergo coming-to-be and passing-away, Ibn Sīnā therefore treated this change as alteration, growth, and locomotion.

These are different forms of motion: alteration is motion in quality, growth is motion in quantity, and locomotion is motion in place.[^V4-511]

But notice the contradiction.

In the first statement, he explicitly calls this same change “coming-to-be and passing-away”:

> “There is therefore a new coming-to-be of the thing whose form is the second, and the passing-away of the thing whose form was the first.”

Then, in the later statement, he denies that it is coming-to-be and passing-away.

Whatever we make of this contradiction, Ibn Sīnā’s treatment of “change” brings him remarkably close to the heart of the internal relation between matter and motion within the process of transformation.

Here he does not separate them in the way we might expect from his claim that matter is substance while motion is not.

And even when he does make that separation—even when he denies that motion is substantial—we should understand the separation as purely conceptual.

For Ibn Sīnā later states plainly that although accidents are, by their nature, things whose subject can in principle be separated from them, in actual existence they cannot exist without a subject.[^V4-512]

The result is that the contradiction we have identified in Ibn Sīnā—between treating matter as substance and denying that motion is substance—does not, in actual terms, overturn the point with which we began: that he sees motion as an internal activity through which what he calls the “perfections” of matter are realized.

Fifth, Ibn Sīnā says:

> “A mover requires something that moves. But this requirement does not in itself mean that the thing moved must be something other than the mover.”

That is, it does not require the mover and what moves to be two separate things.

He continues:

> “There is therefore nothing impossible in our conceiving something as moving itself, until a proof is given that this is impossible.”

In fact, he goes further and argues that it is not impossible:

> “If what moves requires something from which its motion comes, this does not require its mover to be something other than itself rather than itself. And if we reverse the relation and say that a mover requires something to proceed from it, this likewise does not require that what moves as a result must be something other than it rather than itself. Since neither relation contains such a condition, there is therefore nothing to prevent the mover and what moves from being one and the same thing—that is, for motion to belong to the thing itself.”[^V4-513]

It is true that Ibn Sīnā makes this argument while trying to prove that the Necessary Existent in itself is intellect, intellecting, and intelligible.

But he states the point as a general principle—as a universal law.

He brings it into this particular discussion as an application of that general principle, not as something restricted only to the case he is discussing.

We can therefore take this statement out of its immediate context and consider it in its broader, more general sense without being accused of breaking Ibn Sīnā’s words into pieces. If that is legitimate—and it is—then we have another clear piece of textual evidence before us, clearer than any we have seen so far. It raises a serious question: when Ibn Sīnā formulated this general principle and applied it to the Necessary Existent in itself, describing it as intellect, thinker, and object of thought, was he also thinking in terms of things moving by themselves—that is, of the world moving by itself?

Sixth, in a separate chapter devoted to proving that every moving thing has a moving cause other than itself, Ibn Sīnā states that this moving cause can take two forms. It can exist within the body itself, in which case the body is said to move by itself. Or it can exist outside the body, in which case the body is moved by something else.

A thing that moves by itself can also be divided into two kinds. Its internal cause may sometimes move it and sometimes not, in which case its movement is voluntary. Or its cause may never cease to move it, in which case it moves by natural disposition. Movement by natural disposition can itself take two forms. The thing may be compelled to move, with its cause moving it without will, and this is called movement by nature. Or it may move through will and intention, and this is called movement by the celestial soul.[^V4-514]

I have quoted the passage in full so that one point will be clear. When Ibn Sīnā uses the repeated structure “either... or...” here, he is not expressing doubt. He is laying out a classification. He is describing what, for him, are real and established possibilities.

So the cause that moves a body can be of two kinds. It may exist within the body itself, in which case the body moves by itself. Or it may exist outside the body, in which case the body is moved by something else. The first kind of cause—the internal one—may produce movement voluntarily, by natural disposition, by nature,[^V4-515] or through will and intention, as in the case of the celestial soul.

What matters to us here is that Ibn Sīnā clearly accepts the principle that a body can move by itself. More than that, he classifies different forms of such movement. Among the real forms he includes natural movement: movement required by the nature of the body simply because it is that kind of body.

This last point is extremely important if we want to understand his position on the relationship between motion and substance in a new way. Ibn Sīnā clearly regards the body as a substance.[^V4-516] Yet he, like the Peripatetic school generally, is well known for denying that substance itself can undergo motion.

But in the passage we have just examined, he says that a body can move by itself. If so, he must also be allowing motion in substance—or, more precisely, what we should call **substantial motion**. If this conclusion is sound, then it breaks with the usual view attributed to the Islamic philosophers, including Ibn Sīnā himself, that substantial motion is impossible, as we noted earlier.[^V4-517]

#### D — Looking Toward New Horizons

So far, we have presented several pieces of textual evidence from Ibn Sīnā that can serve as a basis for examining the limits of his position on matter and motion, and then on the unity of the material world. What new possibilities do these texts open up?

To explore those possibilities, we must not isolate the ideas in these passages from their place within the connected chain of ideas that make up Ibn Sīnā’s philosophical view of the world as a whole.

At the same time, we must not allow these ideas to disappear beneath the many contradictions in his system—contradictions that are often more formal than real. We believe Ibn Sīnā was frequently forced into such contradictions for two reasons. On the one hand, he needed to avoid openly revealing all of his materialist positions. On the other, he was still working within the logic of the philosophical tradition he had inherited and within which he continued to think.

We must also be careful not to become so accustomed to the language and formal habits of ancient and medieval philosophy that we forget the language and method of our own time. Our modern way of thinking is the tool we need if we are to understand this philosophy clearly, explain it, analyze its different parts, and distinguish what is still alive in it from what has become dead for our own age.

With these principles in mind, we can begin to look beyond the literal wording of these texts and beyond the particular historical forms and circumstances in which they were written. What we want to discover is the basic direction of Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy as a whole, without separating its logical, natural, and metaphysical parts from one another.

Any such separation gives us an incomplete picture of his philosophy. At the same time, it creates another opportunity to obscure what we see as its basic direction. We have no doubt that Ibn Sīnā himself was fully aware of this direction as it developed, step by step. He seems to have calculated each move carefully in order to keep the different parts of his system in balance, even though the differences between them often reached the point of outright contradiction.

In our view, he was usually well aware of these contradictions. That is why we so often find him trying to work around them, soften them, or repair their outward appearance. Yet these efforts frequently expose the contradictions instead of hiding them.

We can see this, for example, in his conflicting explanations of the relationship between matter and form within his system of causes and effects, or in his treatment of matter and motion when he applies the philosophical categories to them.

If we understand Ibn Sīnā’s philosophical system as a complete and unified whole, using the method of historical materialism, we are led through a long series of turns, dead ends, and apparent obscurities until we can finally see the path connecting the different parts of the system.

This is precisely what we mean by Ibn Sīnā’s **materialist tendency**. That is the basic direction we are referring to.

One major problem still remained: the doctrine of divine unity, which in Ibn Sīnā’s thought took the form of God’s absolute transcendence from the world, or the world’s relationship to God.

This became the point around which all of Ibn Sīnā’s contradictions gathered. It was the one obstacle that continued to prevent his materialist tendency from developing in a straight line without bends or detours. He had already removed other obstacles, such as creation from nothing and the idea that the world began at a particular moment in time.

Yet he still tried to preserve the traditional philosophical idea of divine transcendence in all the forms given to it by the rationalist Islamic philosophers, while at the same time preserving the idea of the material unity of the world.

This is what made Ibn Sīnā’s position so difficult and complicated.

It is from this point that we can begin to look beyond the literal wording of the texts. Ibn Sīnā had placed himself—whether by choice or necessity—in an extremely difficult position.

He needed to find a way to bring together two opposites: God’s absolute transcendence from the material world, and the unity of the “First Cause” with its own eternal effect, which is that same material world with its motion and time.

In other words, he needed a solution that would somehow make connection and separation into the same thing; the connected and the separate into the same thing; and cause and effect into the same thing: necessary existence. The distinction between what is necessary in itself and what is necessary through another would then remain only a distinction in the mind.

Of course, no such solution can actually exist. Ibn Sīnā himself could hardly have been unaware of that.

Yet his attempt to arrange the first existents and define the necessary relationships between them shows that such a solution nevertheless seemed to him to exist in some form. More exactly, he used a **formal** version of such a solution. It solved the problem in appearance, not in substance.

He turned to the familiar proposition:

**“From one, only one can proceed.”**

From the one “First Principle,” then, there proceeds one first effect.

But what is this single first effect?

Here Ibn Sīnā faces two conflicting problems.

First, the first effect cannot be a material form, “and still less can it be matter.”[^V4-518] In other words, if it cannot be a material form, then it is even more obvious that it cannot be pure matter.

Second, Ibn Sīnā also says:

> “Yet you know that among the existents proceeding from the First there are bodies, since you know that every body is possible in itself and becomes necessary through another.”

Bodies, then, are necessary through something other than themselves. In this respect, their existence cannot be separated from the Necessary Existent in itself, even though bodies are material, or are material forms.

This creates a third problem.

Bodies are also many. But how can multiplicity proceed from the One?

How did Ibn Sīnā try to solve these three conflicting problems?

This is where his attempt to find a formal way out becomes clearer.

He begins with the conflict between two points. On the one hand, bodies—matter—must proceed necessarily from the Necessary Existent, without any interruption or delay. On the other hand, bodies contain multiplicity.

To solve this, Ibn Sīnā says that bodies proceed from the First through an intermediary existence.

But that intermediary cannot itself be completely one and simple. If it were, the original problem would remain exactly where it was. We would still have to ask: how can multiplicity proceed from this completely simple intermediary?

So the intermediary must contain some kind of multiplicity while still remaining one.

Ibn Sīnā finds such an intermediary in the **separate intellects**.[^V4-519]

But another problem immediately appears.

As Ibn Sīnā himself says, “there can be no multiplicity in the separate intellects.”

To deal with this problem, he adopts the approach first developed by al-Fārābī,[^V4-520] though he makes an important change to it. Both al-Fārābī and Ibn Sīnā understood the “separate intellect” as containing a kind of multiplicity even while remaining one in itself.

Ibn Sīnā explains this multiplicity in two ways.

First, the separate intellect is an effect. From this point of view, it is possible in itself but necessary through its First Cause.

Second, it is also an intellect. It therefore thinks both itself and its cause.

This gives it a kind of multiplicity, because it thinks three things:

1. It thinks itself as possible in itself.
2. It thinks itself as necessary through its cause.
3. It thinks its cause.

But can we not then say that this multiplicity is itself caused by the Necessary Existent in itself? After all, the separate intellect is itself an effect of the Necessary Existent.

If so, multiplicity would once again be proceeding from the One, and the whole problem would return to where it began.

#### E — A Turning Point in Ibn Sīnā’s Thought

The question with which we ended the previous section is our starting point here.

Searching Ibn Sīnā’s work for an answer leads us to a turning point in his thought. It reveals the secret behind his attempt to work around the problem through what we have called a merely “formal” solution.

What we discover is that the multiplicity contained within the “First Intellect” is not, according to Ibn Sīnā, caused by the First, the Necessary Existent.

It comes instead from the nature of the possible existent itself, insofar as it is possible in itself.

Ibn Sīnā says:

> “Its multiplicity does not come to it from the First.”[^V4-521]

And elsewhere:

> “It belongs to it because it is possible... Its possibility of existence belongs to it in itself, not because of the First. What it receives from the First is only the necessity of its existence.”[^V4-522]

Its multiplicity, he says, is “a necessary consequence of the necessity of its coming from the First.” In other words, the multiplicity follows from that necessity, but it is not itself caused by it.

It therefore has no cause outside the possible existent itself.

Ibn Sīnā continues:

> “This kind of multiplicity must therefore be the cause that makes possible the existence of multiplicity among what proceeds from the first effects. Without this multiplicity, only one thing could proceed from them, and no body could proceed from that unity. There is no possibility of multiplicity there except in this way...”

Then he adds:

> “Beneath every intellect there is a celestial sphere with its matter and its form.”[^V4-523]

He also says that from what belongs to the First Intellect in itself, under its two aspects—its being possible in itself and necessary through the First—

> “there follows the first multiplicity in its two parts, namely matter and form: matter through the mediation of form, or through participation with form. In the same way, possibility is brought into actuality through the act that corresponds to the form of the celestial sphere.”[^V4-524]

At this point we should add an explanation given by the editor of the edition of *al-Najāt*[^V4-525] from which these passages are taken. The editor says that the explanation comes from a marginal note in the original manuscript of the book.

It reads:

> “Because the possibility of the first effect did not precede its existence. Its possibility was brought into actuality through the very act that was its existence.”

This addition is extremely important because it makes the meaning of the preceding passage clearer and stronger.

If the first effect passed from possibility into actuality through the very act that also gave actuality to the form of the celestial sphere; if form cannot exist apart from matter; and if the possibility of the first effect did not exist before the effect itself, but became actual through its own existence—then one conclusion necessarily follows.

Matter and form both exist eternally, not merely as possibilities, but as actual realities existing from eternity.

That is what we find when we look both at the explicit wording of the text and at where its argument leads.

No “separate intellect,” then, ever existed entirely apart from matter and form, because:

> “Beneath every intellect there is a celestial sphere with its matter and its form.”

Matter and form therefore did not exist only potentially. They always existed actually and eternally, because “the possibility of the first effect did not precede its existence.” What is meant here is its actual existence.

This also means that the “first multiplicity,” which exists actually and consists essentially of matter and form, is caused by itself rather than by the First.

And it is itself:

> “the cause that makes possible the existence of multiplicity among what proceeds from the first effects.”

Without this multiplicity, Ibn Sīnā says, only one thing could proceed, and no body could come from such unity.

Yet bodies plainly exist in objective reality. Their existence is not in doubt.

So this multiplicity is its own cause, and it is also the cause of the effects that follow it.

For now, we will leave this conclusion where it stands. We will return to it after comparing these passages with others we have already discussed and with further texts that we will examine in the sections that follow.

#### F — Defining the Relationship Between Matter and Form

After the conclusion we have just reached, the first thing we need to examine is Ibn Sīnā’s understanding of the relationship between matter and form.

We need to clarify this relationship at this stage because our confidence in the previous conclusion depends on it.

As we noted earlier, Aristotle began from the idea that natural substances are made up of matter and form as part of his attempt to understand what things really are.

We also said earlier[^V4-526] that Ibn Sīnā handled this Aristotelian idea with considerable freedom and originality.

What we meant above all was that Ibn Sīnā was not mainly interested in the inner “reality” of things considered in isolation from the concrete ways they appear in the world. His main interest was in those concrete appearances themselves, because he wanted to use them to explain the movement of the world and the changes and transformations that constantly accompany that movement.

This reflected his deep—and perhaps central—interest in the natural sciences.

By his time, these sciences had become one of the major needs created by the development of Arab-Islamic society. They were no longer simply an intellectual or speculative pursuit, as they had been during some periods of ancient philosophy.

For the same reason, we argued earlier[^V4-527] that the natural-philosophical part of Ibn Sīnā’s system provided a body of theory tied closely to the practical study of natural phenomena. Through those phenomena, he made a serious attempt to arrive at a realistic explanation of the world itself.

The historical state of the natural sciences in his time prevented him from relying completely on this scientific method, but the attempt was still there.

Working from this method, despite its limitations, Ibn Sīnā made an important change to Aristotle’s theory of matter and form.

The change affected both sides of the theory: matter and form.

For Aristotle, matter is pure potentiality. It is completely indeterminate and has no definite character of its own. It is little more than the capacity to receive form, or the underlying subject that carries form.

Matter is therefore purely passive. It plays no active role in the “process” of existence beyond receiving the form through which a thing comes to exist actually.[^V4-528]

Because of this, Aristotle also failed to explain clearly how matter is connected to motion, or how matter and form are connected to one another in the formation of bodies.

Ibn Sīnā greatly reduced this vagueness and near-nothingness in the concept of matter.

He did this in two ways.

First, he treated **extension** as something belonging to the nature of matter itself.

Extension means the three dimensions that can be found in matter: length, width, and depth. This applies to the matter of every body, including celestial bodies.

Ibn Sīnā therefore says:

> “The nature of extension is one in itself. Since it is one in all its different instances, this proves that it needs something in which it exists, and that is prime matter, which belongs to all bodies.”[^V4-529]

Matter, he also says,

> “is never without the dimension in which these measurements—that is, the three dimensions—can be supposed... There is therefore nothing surprising in one and the same matter receiving one size, then a larger or smaller one, and passing from one size to another.”[^V4-530]

Matter therefore no longer remains as vague and undefined as it was in Aristotle.

It now has extension, which gives it a definite feature and provides the basis on which bodily forms can take shape. We will return to bodily form shortly.

The second way Ibn Sīnā redefined matter was even more important.

He took matter out of the realm of pure receptivity and gave it an active role as well. Matter now has some power to affect the process of existence.

This active role follows from the extension that Ibn Sīnā places within the nature of matter.

As he explains it, bodily extension necessarily belongs to prime matter. If extension belongs to matter, then shape also necessarily follows, which means actual existence.

Both of these—extension and shape—come about “through the participation of the underlying subject,” meaning matter.

Ibn Sīnā therefore concludes:

> “Prime matter has an effect on the existence of what form itself requires in order to exist, such as finitude and shape.”[^V4-531]

Elsewhere he makes this active role of matter even clearer by saying that form and prime matter each become individuated through the other.[^V4-532]

Form is therefore no longer, as it was in Aristotle, the only thing responsible for making an individual thing what it actually is.

This new understanding of matter went together with a new understanding of form.

Here again, Ibn Sīnā goes beyond Aristotle.

Aristotle limits the meaning of form to its relation to species. Form represents the species to which a particular thing belongs.[^V4-533]

The relationship between matter and form therefore becomes the relationship between an individual existence and its species. Form corresponds to the universal features shared by things of the same kind, while matter accounts for what is individual in a particular concrete existence.

This is why Aristotle says that Socrates and Callias do not differ in species. What distinguishes them is the particular matter belonging to each of them.[^V4-533]

Aristotle has been criticized here for contradicting his own theory of matter.

If matter is the principle of individual existence, then it must somehow be distinct from form. But if matter considered in itself is only common, indeterminate prime matter—that is, matter existing only potentially—how can it at the same time be the principle of individuality and determination, which belong to actual existence?

Aristotle himself does not grant matter actual existence in that sense.[^V4-534]

Ibn Sīnā approaches the problem differently.

He speaks of two kinds of form: **specific form** and **corporeal form**.

He also calls corporeal form the “bodily form,” and at times even the “material form.”[^V4-535]

Specific form is the universal form that accompanies prime matter before that matter takes on a particular body—that is, before it receives corporeal form.

Prime matter is never entirely without specific form.

These specific forms are many. Each class of bodies has its own specific form, and no single body can contain more than one specific form at the same time, because each form gives the body different properties.

In some bodies, for example, specific form makes separation, joining, and shaping easy, as in moist bodies.

In others it makes these processes difficult, as in dry bodies.

And in still others it prevents them altogether, as in celestial bodies.[^V4-536]

That, then, is specific form.

But what does Ibn Sīnā mean by **corporeal form**, and how does prime matter acquire one?

He begins by saying:

> “A body is a body because it is such that three dimensions can be supposed within it, each standing at right angles to the others... The first supposed dimension is length, the second is width, and the dimension standing on both of them is depth... A body is a body insofar as it is like this, and this meaning in it is corporeal form.”[^V4-537]

Corporeal form, then, is the possession of extension in three dimensions.

It is matter becoming definite and individual through extension.

How, then, does prime matter acquire this corporeal form?

Ibn Sīnā examines this question through the relationship between matter and form and through the role each plays in producing a definite, individual existence.

He begins by assuming that prime matter in general and specific form always exist together.

The question is therefore not why matter and form exist at all, but why prime matter becomes individualized through a particular corporeal form.

His starting point is that prime matter is unquestionably a cause of the finitude of extension and of shape.

What remains to be explained is the cause of corporeal form itself.

After a dense discussion in *al-Ishārāt*, Ibn Sīnā arrives at a conclusion that can be summarized in one sentence:

> “Form becomes individuated through prime matter, and prime matter in turn becomes individuated through form.”[^V4-538]

Before reaching this conclusion, he says that form comes before prime matter, and that prime matter depends on an “original cause” and on “an assisting cause that follows form; when the two come together, the existence of prime matter is completed.”[^V4-539]

By this he means individuated prime matter.

But what exactly are this “original cause” and the “assisting cause that follows form”? And what does the whole statement mean?

The commentators on *al-Ishārāt* say that the “original cause” is a permanent and eternally existing being that is separate from matter and from everything bodily connected with it.

The “assisting cause that follows form,” they say, is:

> “the eternal motion that gives prime matter the finite dispositions needed to receive renewed and successive forms.”[^V4-540]

If we look at all these passages on specific form, corporeal form, and their relationship to matter, without getting lost in the abstract speculations of commentators and interpreters, we can see something important in Ibn Sīnā.

He is making a cautious but determined attempt to investigate something more concrete than the mysterious relationship between two philosophical terms called “matter” and “form.”

He is trying to understand their living, changing content as it appears in the phenomena of the material world.

His move beyond Aristotle’s specific form, and his introduction of the additional concept of corporeal form, was essentially an attempt to get at the basis of those phenomena—the “renewed and successive forms.”

If we translate this into modern language, we can see more clearly what Ibn Sīnā was trying to reach and what starting point guided him.

In scientific terms, he was investigating the relationship between matter and its manifestations—that is, between substance and phenomenon.

The German scholar of philosophy[^V4-541] was therefore right to translate these “attachments” as **natural phenomena**, because Ibn Sīnā used the term for the changing and transforming phenomena that “attach themselves” to bodies.

He was deeply concerned with these phenomena throughout his studies and writings. In *al-Shifāʾ*, he even says that he intended to write a separate book called *al-Lawāḥiq*—*The Attachments*—which would serve almost as a commentary on *al-Shifāʾ*.[^V4-542]

If we look more closely at Ibn Sīnā’s attempt to discover the source of transformations in matter and form, we can see that he repeatedly hesitated to accept highly abstract theories that reduced matter to pure receptivity and denied it any active role.

He repeatedly worked his way through the difficulties created by these theories in order to make them yield an admission that matter itself participates in the process of change.

As we have seen, he eventually reached the conclusion that prime matter and form each become individuated through the other.

He had to overcome many abstract theoretical obstacles before he could reach this conclusion.

But he did not express the idea only in that form.

Elsewhere he states it in ways that are even more direct about the active role of matter.

In another passage from *al-Ishārāt*, for example, he says:

> “Neither prime matter nor form is, in an absolute sense, the cause of the other. Nor is either of them, in an absolute sense, an intermediary. Rather, both require something that is the cause of each of them separately, or of both together.”[^V4-543]

We will return later to this “cause of each of them.”

In *al-Najāt*, Ibn Sīnā also says:

> “Material form, even though it is a cause of matter insofar as it brings matter into actuality and completes it, is itself affected in its existence by matter, which specifies and determines it.”[^V4-544]
