By saying that matter affects the existence of form because matter gives form its particular and definite character, Ibn Sīnā places their relationship on a basis of mutual involvement and interaction.

In other words, he treats it as a dialectical relationship that plays an active role in the movement of material phenomena.

In the same discussion, Ibn Sīnā himself makes this mutual character clear:

> “Each of them must therefore be a cause of the other in some respect, though not in the same respect. Otherwise it would be impossible for material form to have any relationship to matter at all.”[^V4-545]

After all this, however, the whole debate over whether matter causes form or form causes matter ultimately becomes a kind of pure mental exercise.

For Ibn Sīnā accepts without question that matter exists objectively and that its existence has no beginning in time.

Matter is eternal.

That is the basic point on which everything here rests.

#### G — Natural Existence and the “First Principle”

We now return to the “formal” solution Ibn Sīnā was seeking to the problem of existence and its relationship to the “First Principle.”

We saw earlier[^V4-546] how he dealt with the problem of the first effect, which contains several aspects within itself, in order to resolve the contradiction between the principle that “from one, only one can proceed” and the obvious multiplicity of the world.

He devoted a number of chapters in *al-Shifāʾ* and *al-Najāt* to this problem. He also wrote extensively about it in *al-Ishārāt* and in other works of his that we have been able to examine.

Yet the more he tried to escape the contradiction, the deeper he made it.

At the same time, he made the idea of God’s absolute transcendence stronger and more complete.

But this, from another direction, also pushed him toward affirming the unity of the material world.

In effect, he placed the “First Principle” in one of two possible relationships to the world.

* **Either the First Principle is completely separate from the world.** Ibn Sīnā attributes to it a state of total “otherness” or complete separation from the matter of the world.

  In that case, the material world stands on its own. It becomes its own effect and its own cause, moving according to the internal laws of its own motion.

  There would then be only one world: the material world. Anything beyond it would exist only as a conceptual or mental abstraction, pushed almost to the point of nonexistence.

  This is the direction suggested by passages like those we cited when discussing “self-motion” and in the section on the turning point in Ibn Sīnā’s thought.[^V4-547]

* **Or the First Principle has a material connection with the world.** In that case, Ibn Sīnā’s repeated idea of “separation” must have a special meaning different from the Plotinian idea of separation.

  In Plotinus, separation means being completely free of matter and absolutely transcending it.

  In Ibn Sīnā, however, it would instead mean a kind of “intrinsic superiority”[^V4-548] over matter without complete detachment from it.

  The unity of the material world would then mean the unity of the “First Cause” with its effects—the existents—in all their material forms.

  This would be different from the idea of divine indwelling and different from the Sufi doctrine of the “unity of being.” It would come closer to a unity of opposites within nature.

  This kind of connection is suggested by the passages we cited in the section “One Material World.”[^V4-549]

If we now look at the form of existence found in the natural elements and in the three realms that arise from them—minerals, plants, and animals, with human beings included here within the animal realm—we find that Ibn Sīnā places this kind of existence within the same overall relationship to the “First Principle” as the so-called “separate world.”

Ancient and medieval philosophy called this natural realm the **world of generation and corruption**.

The “separate world,” by contrast, is the world of “celestial” beings, or the world of “angels,” as some of Ibn Sīnā’s texts sometimes call it.[^V4-550]

The world of generation and corruption is not outside either of the two possible forms of material unity we have just described.

It belongs to the same overall cosmic order.

There is, of course, a supposed difference between the two worlds: the earthly world is a world of change, while the celestial world is a world of stability.

But one of the distinctive features of Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy is precisely that it tries to build connections between these two “worlds.”

More accurately, it tries to break down the sharp barriers that older idealist philosophical systems, both ancient and medieval, had placed between them.

Ibn Sīnā’s decision to treat extension and shape as belonging to matter, and his addition of corporeal form alongside Aristotle’s specific form, are two of the clearest signs of this attempt to break down those barriers.

The “world of generation and decay” does not differ from the “separate realm” in Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy when it comes to the basis of its existence. It is therefore not cut off from it. The existence of the “elements”—the original elements of nature—is the decisive link in the chain of cosmic existence. However much Ibn Sīnā tried to obscure the material basis of his view of this entire connected chain, he could not hide many of its features, especially when he discussed the existence of the natural elements.

We saw earlier that bodily substance, with its matter and form, exists alongside the first effect, or First Intellect. This is because the first effect already contains multiplicity, and body is one of the forms in which that multiplicity exists. We may remember Ibn Sīnā’s statement that “beneath every intellect there is a celestial sphere with its matter and form,” and that from the First Intellect there follows, “through what belongs to it in itself in its two aspects, the first multiplicity in its two parts: by which I mean matter and form.”

From this whole passage, we concluded that none of the “separate intellects” ever existed completely separate from matter and form—that is, from body. Matter and form are therefore eternal.

If the natural elements are the decisive link in the chain through which the multiplicity contained successively within the “separate intellects” comes into existence, then the Active Intellect is not something that pours a purely spiritual existence onto the world of generation and decay. Nor is it the “Giver of Forms” to our earthly world, the “world beneath the sphere of the Moon,” as idealist scholars try to make Ibn Sīnā say.

For Ibn Sīnā, the “natural elements” are simple bodily substances. They mark the point at which bodily substances that have always existed potentially begin to exist in an actual, realized form.

They are therefore another stage in the existence of body itself, with its prime matter and its specific form. What changes at this stage is that body becomes particular and definite through individual matter and “corporeal form.”

In Ibn Sīnā’s philosophical system, then, the Active Intellect is only the final stage in this eternal potential process. From there, bodily existence enters the first stage of its actual existence.

This is what Ibn Sīnā means when he describes the process as follows:

> “The First—that is, the First Principle—brings an intellectual substance into being, and this is truly brought into being. Through it, another intellectual substance and a celestial body are brought into being. The same happens from that intellectual substance, and the process continues until the celestial bodies are complete and reaches an intellectual substance from which no celestial body follows.”[^V4-551]

At this point the sequence of celestial bodies ends because it is about to change into a new form of body: bodies that undergo changes in quantity, quality, and so on.

Ibn Sīnā clearly expresses this idea of transformation. At the same time, he makes clear that the process leading up to the Active Intellect itself is eternal. He writes:

> “Once the celestial bodies have reached their full number, the existence of the elements must follow. This is because elemental bodies come into being and pass away—that is, they change and transform. Their immediate principles must therefore be things capable of some kind of change and motion. Pure intellect alone cannot be the cause of their existence (...) These elements share one matter but differ in form. The differences in their forms must therefore be helped by differences in the conditions of the celestial spheres, while what their matter has in common must be helped by what the conditions of the celestial spheres have in common...”[^V4-552]

So neither the matter nor the forms of the natural elements are simply an emanation from a “pure intellect,” whether that means the Active Intellect or any of the “separate intellects” that come before it.

Instead, there is matter embodied in the celestial bodies, and this matter passes through different stages of existence. Differences in the conditions of the celestial spheres help produce differences in the forms of the elements, while what the spheres have in common helps explain what the elements share in their matter.

And the celestial spheres here are bodies—which means that they are material.

Ancient and medieval philosophy, especially Neoplatonism, nevertheless drew an important distinction between the realm of the celestial spheres and the earthly world. Ibn Sīnā continued this philosophical tradition.

The difference was supposed to be that celestial bodies were fixed and unchanging, while elemental, or natural, bodies changed and transformed.

On this basis, philosophers assumed that the movement of the celestial sphere was circular. Circular motion returns to its starting point. A round body rotates around its own center rather than moving from one place to another. According to this view, it therefore undergoes neither a change of place nor a qualitative transformation. In other words, this kind of motion does not produce change in the body itself.

Straight-line motion, by contrast, was treated as a feature of natural bodies precisely because natural bodies were thought to change.

This supposed difference between celestial and elemental bodies led to another distinction: celestial bodies were regarded as simple, while elemental bodies could be composite. That, in turn, led philosophers to assign different shapes to the two kinds of body.

If celestial bodies were completely fixed and incapable of change, then they also had to be simple rather than composite. “Simplicity” was defined in a way that matched this idea of celestial stability. A simple body was one that was not made up of bodies with genuinely different natures. A composite body, by contrast, was made up of bodies whose natures genuinely differed.[^V4-553]

Once the celestial spheres were assumed to be simple, they also had to be spherical. Ibn Sīnā writes:

> “...The shape required by what is simple must be circular. Otherwise, its directions would differ within one and the same matter and from one and the same power.”[^V4-554]

In other words, if the sphere were not circular, one simple matter with one nature would somehow produce different configurations. For Ibn Sīnā, that was impossible.

We therefore have a whole series of differences between two kinds of body, all ultimately arising from the assumption that the celestial spheres are fixed and unchanging.

A scholar of ancient philosophy might argue that this chain of distinctions really begins somewhere else: with the assumption that the celestial spheres are simple in the first place. On this reading, their simplicity follows from a metaphysical view of the “separate” beings. Since these beings were the first effects of the simple First Principle, they too had to be simple.

But looking at the issue from this angle alone is itself metaphysical.

A realistic dialectical approach takes us back instead to the conditions of our own world. Behind the traditional view were two factors: one ideological and one related to knowledge. Together they led thinkers, first, to assume the existence of a “separate” world outside our own and, second, to imagine that this external world remained fixed and could not change.

The ideological factor can historically be traced back to Greek philosophy, both pagan and Alexandrian, especially from Plato through Neoplatonism. It belonged to the ideology of the master class in slave society.

The economic and social basis of this ideology changed in Arab-Islamic society. Yet the internal logic of philosophical development allowed its philosophical form to survive into the time of Ibn Sīnā, and even into the time of Ibn Rushd, among Islamic philosophers.

Official Islamic ideology also helped this inherited philosophical logic to survive.

From al-Fārābī onward, Islamic philosophers opened many breaches in the structure of official Islamic ideology. But they were still unable to free themselves from the philosophical and theoretical foundations that formed the general framework of that structure, even after the structure itself had begun to crack.

The second factor was the limited state of scientific knowledge at the time.

People still knew very little about how celestial bodies formed or about the laws governing the cosmic system beyond the Earth. In fact, this lack of knowledge extended to the laws of nature more generally, including the nature of the earthly world itself.

We could even say that philosophical misconceptions about the heavens reflected misconceptions about the natural laws operating here on Earth.

There is another point we should notice.

In the study of the heavens, limited scientific knowledge went together with—or partly resulted from—the lack of technical development. At the same time, each reinforced the other. Science lacked the practical tools needed to discover the laws governing celestial phenomena. Thinkers therefore had little to rely on except abstract assumptions.

When Arab scientists began responding to the needs created by economic and social development and expanding their scientific knowledge of nature, they developed some early technical instruments for observing the sky. They also began applying mathematical theories to the measurement of distance and time.

But these instruments could have only a limited effect on scientific knowledge because their own design and capabilities were still limited.

None of this means that Ibn Sīnā simply followed the inherited philosophical tradition in everything he said about the heavens.

He rejected many traditional assumptions that had been treated as basic truths about the world. Before him, al-Fārābī, Abū Bakr al-Rāzī, and Arab natural scientists had already helped open the way. Ibn Sīnā’s own theoretical and practical work in physics gave him further tools for understanding the world in a new light.

While medieval Europe was still dominated in physics by the idea that the world had been created from nothing, Ibn Sīnā—and al-Fārābī before him—had already moved beyond this theory, as we explained earlier.

Perhaps the most important problem in Ibn Sīnā’s physics is the problem of motion in nature.

He dealt with this problem in the eleventh century, when mechanics had not yet developed as a science.

Here we should say that when Ibn Sīnā accepted the traditional view that celestial motion was circular, he did not do so simply because he was following an inherited philosophical tradition. His position was also supported by the conclusions he reached through his own study of motion in nature.

His research led him to understand natural motion as circular in character[^V4-555]—in the sense that it returns to its starting point.

This comes closer to a dialectical understanding of motion in nature than either a purely straight-line view or a mechanical understanding of motion. The basis for Ibn Sīnā’s view was his conclusion that the world is completely filled with matter. In other words, there is no empty space in it. A “vacuum,” for him, is impossible in nature.

This idea in fact contains the view that space is a form in which matter exists and that matter cannot exist outside space.

Ibn Sīnā offered several arguments against the existence of empty space.

For example, he imagined bodies in different positions: touching, approaching one another, or moving apart. Between these bodies, he supposed, there are distances of different sizes.

He then argued that if these distances exist between bodies, they have measurable dimensions. They therefore have quantitative existence. Even if this quantitative existence is not itself a body, it is certainly not nothing. It is something that actually exists.

Soon afterward, however, he states the matter more firmly:

> “...Since it has been shown that continuous extension cannot exist without matter, and since it has been shown that bodily dimensions do not overlap precisely because they are dimensions, there can be no vacuum consisting of pure extension. When bodies move, what lies between them is displaced, and no separate, original extension remains behind. Therefore there is no empty space.”[^V4-556]

Ibn Sīnā’s rejection of empty space in the material world goes beyond the atomism of Democritus, which held that matter consists of atoms and empty space.

Isaac Newton (1642–1727) later adopted this idea of Democritus. Like Democritus, Newton regarded the atom as a material substance existing in empty space. Newton also held that atoms travel from the Sun to the Earth through empty space.

Ibn Sīnā’s hypothesis would therefore have gone beyond the position of a thinker who lived almost seven centuries after him.

Modern physics later confirmed Ibn Sīnā’s view that there is no absolute vacuum. The Einstein school demonstrated this, according to this view, by showing that if empty space existed in the material world, rays of light would always travel in straight lines. Instead, they travel along curved paths because they encounter matter.

This scientific finding confirmed the Marxist proposition that the material world contains no empty void and that everything around us is filled with matter possessing different possibilities.

Here, then, we find another materialist insight in Ibn Sīnā. At the same time, it shows the remarkable originality of his study of nature.

After this digression, we can return to our main point.

Ibn Sīnā’s acceptance of circular movement in the heavens did not come only from inherited philosophical tradition. It also came from a materialist position supported by his own independent theoretical and practical research.

We should also notice that he distinguishes between circular motion in the heavens and circular motion in earthly nature.

Circular motion is the only form of celestial motion. On Earth, however, circular motion is only one of several forms of natural motion.

The movement of qualities—which Ibn Sīnā regards as necessary features or “attributes” of natural bodies—is straight rather than circular.[^V4-557]

These qualitative movements include the movements of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness.

According to Ibn Sīnā, these four qualities involve both rest and motion in bodies.

There is rest when the forms of bodies are left to follow their own natures and nothing from outside prevents them from doing so. In that state, the bodies display heat or cold, moisture or dryness.

Motion appears when something happens to a body that takes it away from its natural condition. At that point, inclination and movement arise.

Ibn Sīnā writes:

> “It is not surprising that one and the same form should produce rest in a place, movement toward that place, an active effect through one quality, and a passive readiness through another. When we say that something is naturally cold, we mean that it has within itself a power that cools when nothing prevents it (...) These powers first produce these conditions in their own bodies. Through them, they then act on other bodies, just as they first produce motion in their own body and then, through that motion, move something else by pushing it.”[^V4-558]

After this, Ibn Sīnā concludes that a body moving in a circle “can never naturally come to rest, because continuous motion does not cease...”[^V4-558]

This last statement deserves attention, especially when we place it beside his statement that the four qualities involve both motion and rest.

Does Ibn Sīnā mean that the motion of elemental nature—which is straight-line motion—is made up of a unity of rest and motion, or of interruption and continuity, in something like the way dialectical materialism understands motion?

If this idea really was present in his thinking, it would represent a major development in his understanding of natural motion.[^V4-559]

But if he truly understood motion in this dialectical way, he still excluded circular motion from the same principle, even though the principle should apply to every form of motion.

The reason for this exception is clear. It rests on his mistaken distinction between one kind of motion that produces no change, such as celestial motion, and another kind that is the source of change and transformation, such as the motion of earthly natural bodies.

#### H — No Separation Between Celestial and Earthly Bodies

It may seem that Ibn Sīnā’s treatment of the heavens as fixed, simple, and limited to a single form of motion—circular motion—stands in direct conflict with the idea of one material world that we are trying to identify in his philosophy.

After all, he describes earthly bodies very differently. They change, they are composite, and they can move in more than one way, both circularly and in straight lines.

Does this not separate heaven from Earth and turn them into two worlds with fundamentally different properties?

If so, what becomes of the single material world we claim to find in Ibn Sīnā?

This objection would be valid if the essential relationship between celestial and earthly bodies rested on these differences.

But it does not.

The essence of their relationship lies not in where they differ, but in what they share.

It is true that Ibn Sīnā places the differences within a much deeper framework, and in doing so seems to widen the gap between the two “worlds.”

He says that celestial bodies, their motion, and their time are “brought into being” (*mubdaʿa*)—that is, eternal—whereas earthly bodies, their motion, and their time “come into being and pass away,” meaning that they arise in time and are not eternal.

That is true. But even this deeper distinction does not change the essential relationship between the two kinds of body.

That essential relationship lies in the basis of their existence.

All bodies, celestial and earthly alike, share the same material foundation. This is the real link between the two forms of bodily existence.

In both celestial and earthly bodies there are “prime matter” and “specific form,” inseparably joined so that neither exists stripped of the other.

In earthly bodies, however, this matter and specific form become definite and individualized through “particular matter” and “corporeal form.”

As long as celestial and earthly bodies share the same material basis of existence, that common basis is the essence of the unity between all bodily existence.

The differences Ibn Sīnā repeatedly emphasizes are therefore no more than differences between the various appearances of one underlying reality.

Even the deep separation we just described ultimately turns out to contain a deep connection. It becomes another distinction within an underlying unity.

When Ibn Sīnā explains what he means by natural things that come into being, he writes:

> “...The principles that accompany natural things that come into being are therefore three: form, matter, and privation.”[^V4-560]

What, then, does he mean by “privation”?

He answers:

> “As for privation, it is neither something that exists absolutely nor something absolutely nonexistent. It is the absence of what exists potentially. And not every kind of privation is a principle of something that comes into being. Only the privation connected with the potential for that thing to come into being is such a principle. Thus the privation found in wool is certainly not a principle of the coming into being of a sword, whereas the privation found in iron is. A sword cannot come into being from wool, but it can come into being from iron. When this privation is present in it, it is matter; when the form is present in it, it is the underlying subject. It is therefore matter for the absent form that exists potentially, and the subject of the form that exists actually...”[^V4-560]

It is clear from this passage that when Ibn Sīnā describes natural things in our world as things that “come into being,” his analysis ultimately makes this a description of the natural phenomena belonging to these earthly things—as people understood them in his time.

The underlying basis of their existence remains something that extends eternally as prime matter and specific form.

The idea of “privation,” which Ibn Sīnā treats as one of the three principles accompanying natural things that come into being, also turns out in this passage to have an existential rather than an absolutely negative meaning.

It means relative nonexistence.

More precisely, it describes one form in which matter exists potentially before it becomes individualized. In Ibn Sīnā’s terms, it describes the state of “common prime matter and its specific form,” which, in his view, exist eternally.

So even in the case of things that “come into being” in time, Ibn Sīnā does not accept absolute nothingness.

The difference between something whose existence is eternal and something that arises in time therefore comes down, in the end, only to one existing prior to the other by nature.

But why go through all these steps to establish Ibn Sīnā’s idea of the unity of the world as a material world when he himself settles the question directly, in words that leave no real room for interpretation?

He devoted an entire chapter to the subject, and its title itself gives the decisive answer:

> **“Chapter on the fact that the world is one and cannot be multiple.”**[^V4-561]

His discussion covers the whole world of bodies simply as bodies. It therefore includes simple and composite bodies alike, celestial bodies and earthly bodies.

He states the argument in this way:

> “...Bodies that have similar forms and powers have one natural place and one natural direction. From this it follows that there cannot be two Earths at two centers of two worlds, nor two fires at two surrounding horizons of two worlds. By nature, Earth exists only in one world, and the same is true of fire and all other bodies. Since the first places belong to simple bodies, and since the places of simple bodies, once they reach their limit, mark the limit of the places of all bodies, and since simple bodies, when they follow their own natures and natural forms, are spherical—for the natural form of what is simple is spherical—the whole must form one sphere (...) From this it becomes clear that there cannot be another world besides this world. The world is one. We ourselves are not at its outer horizon, because we occupy the region of bodies whose nature is to move in straight lines. The horizon of the world must therefore lie where there is a body whose nature is not to move in a straight line, but which instead serves as the reference by which the directions of straight-line motion are determined...”[^V4-562]

There is therefore one world: the world of all bodies.

And what can a world made up of bodies be if not material?

This one world must therefore be material.

Within it there is one Earth, at one center, and one horizon surrounding this unified world.

The first places in this world belong to the simple bodies because these come first in existence. And since simple bodies require a spherical shape, the entire world—including the Earth, fire, and all other bodies—lies within a single sphere.

We, as inhabitants of Earth, do not live at this surrounding horizon because the bodily region in which we live is characterized by straight-line rather than circular motion.

The single horizon of this single world must therefore belong to bodies that move in circles: simple bodies with a circular shape, namely the celestial bodies.

These celestial bodies provide the measure by which the directions of straight-line movement are determined. In other words, the positions of the celestial spheres—the stars and planets—serve as the reference points by which we determine direction on Earth.

This explicit statement by Ibn Sīnā does more than undermine the idea that reality is divided into two worlds, one spiritual and one material.

It also undermines the old scientific and philosophical idea that the Earth is the center of the world.

But there is also a negative side.

Ibn Sīnā removes centrality from the Earth only to transfer it to the celestial sphere. Yet the unity of the material world means that no single part of it, and no single planet, can be its center.

#### I — The Idea of the Natural Generation of Bodies

Did Ibn Sīnā really think about the natural generation—or spontaneous generation—of the material world?

And if we speak about such an idea in a philosopher who lived at that particular stage in the history of philosophy—or, more accurately, at that stage in the development of humanity and of human knowledge as a whole—are we abandoning the historical materialist method?

Would this amount to an ahistorical “modernization,” an idealist attempt to read later ideas back into the past?

Let us answer the second question first.

The historical character of thought, like the historical character of human beings and nature, lies at the heart of the historical materialist method.

We have already discussed this when examining the idea of “self-motion” in Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy.[^V4-563] But one more example can make the point clearer.

Lenin’s comments on the ideas of Heraclitus[^V4-564] (530–470 BCE) about the creation of the cosmos and about knowledge are especially revealing.

When Lenin assessed the main propositions of Heraclitus, he began from the fact that they expressed certain basic materialist ideas extremely well and could serve as a foundation for dialectics.

If that can be said of one of the earliest periods of Greek philosophy and of one of the oldest slave societies, there is nothing unrealistic or historically impossible about materialist ideas such as the spontaneous generation of the world appearing in the time and society of Ibn Sīnā.

His society was more developed both socially and intellectually, especially in its knowledge of nature.

In fact, some form of the idea of spontaneous generation had already appeared among the earliest Greek materialist philosophers, including Heraclitus, Thales, who lived between the seventh and sixth centuries BCE, and Anaximander (611–547 BCE).[^V4-565]

The idea did, in fact, find its way into Ibn Sīnā’s thought.

When we read the passages in his works that point in this direction, it seems that he drew significantly on the ideas of those ancient materialists while also adding the accumulated experience of later history and his own direct work in the natural sciences.

His thinking therefore appears less bound to the mythical elements found in the ideas of the early Greek materialists and, by the standards of his time, closer to a scientific way of understanding the world.

When Ibn Sīnā discusses “the bodies from which composite beings are formed,” he speaks of “powers” within these bodies through which “some act upon others and some are acted upon by others.”

He says that these “powers by which simple bodies are distinguished”[^V4-566]—the simple bodies from which composite bodies are formed—belong to the sensible qualities.

All sensible qualities, he argues, can ultimately be reduced to heat, cold, moisture, and dryness.

He then reduces the source of change in bodies to heat and cold alone.

His reasoning is that the “power” that produces change in a body can do so in one of two ways.

It can loosen and break the body down—that is, expand it—which happens through the power of heat.

Or it can contract and condense the body, which happens through the power of cold.[^V4-567]

Alongside these two active “powers,” heat and cold, there are two passive powers that are also necessary for composite bodies to arise from simple bodies: the “power to take shape” and the “power to be divided.”

A simple body can enter into a composite body—we might also say that a composite body can arise from it—because the simple body is capable of being acted upon in both these ways.

But the way it receives these effects can differ.

If a body easily accepts separation and combination, shaping and displacement, then its quality is moisture.

If it resists these changes, its quality is dryness.

From this, Ibn Sīnā concludes that the simple bodies from which composite bodies are formed differ and are distinguished through these four powers. None of them can lack one of the two active powers and one of the two passive powers, because these bodies must be capable of separating and combining. Otherwise, their parts could never join together to form composite bodies.[^V4-568]

What concerns us most in this passage is the real nature of these “powers.”

Are they invisible spiritual powers? Are they “psychic” powers, in Ibn Sīnā’s own terminology? Or are they material powers?

How we answer this determines the philosophical direction of the passage.

The rest of the discussion suggests that Ibn Sīnā is moving toward a material meaning for these “powers,” which he says allow bodies to act on one another and to be acted upon by one another.

In the same context he writes:

> “...When we say that bodies are naturally cold, we mean that they possess a power that cools by itself when nothing prevents it. Since we have no established names for these powers, we derive their names from what they do, just as we speak of a ‘rational power’ for the power that belongs specifically to human beings. The powers we have mentioned first produce these conditions in their own bodies. Through them, they then act on other bodies. In the same way, they first produce motion in their own body and then, through that motion, move another thing by pushing it...”[^V4-569]

Several points follow from this.

First, Ibn Sīnā says that these powers did not already have established names.

That suggests that he is not referring to supernatural powers, which already had traditional names in the spiritual terminology available in Arabic at the time. If that were what he meant, those established terms would have been available to him, and he would have had no need to create names based on the powers’ actions.

Second, deriving the names of these powers from their actions points directly toward their material character, because the actions themselves are material.

These are therefore material powers that actively transform bodies from simple to composite forms—that is, from the basic elements of nature into bodies with more complex qualities and forms.

The passage also clearly suggests that their activity comes from within.

These powers exist in the bodies themselves and act through their own nature, not because an outside agent sets them in motion.

Third—and this is the most important point—the activity of these internal powers is necessarily and objectively connected with motion.

At the same time as these powers produce the qualities and forms of bodies, they also, according to Ibn Sīnā’s text, produce motion within those bodies. Through that motion, they then produce further motion in other bodies.

Ibn Sīnā’s recognition that motion necessarily accompanies every active process of matter supports what we argued earlier: that he saw motion as one aspect, or perhaps one mode, of matter’s existence—or at least something very close to this idea, if we wish to remain cautious and avoid making the conclusion too absolute.

Finally, there is a fourth point in the passage, no less important than the third.

It concerns the way bodies interact with one another and the way this interaction means that forms of matter can transform into other forms.

Ibn Sīnā gives the “powers” within bodies the property through which bodies “act upon one another and are acted upon by one another.”

At its core, this “property” describes a process of mutual transformation between bodies—that is, between forms of matter.

It belongs to the law by which one form of matter changes into another, and this is one of the basic properties of matter.

Was this what Ibn Sīnā meant when he gave the powers that transform bodies from simple to composite forms the ability to act upon one another and be acted upon in return, as the passage clearly says?

This idea does not appear in the passage accidentally, casually, or without reflection.

It seems much more likely that Ibn Sīnā studied and observed the phenomenon carefully.

He returns to it repeatedly in his works and stresses it in several places. He explains it more fully in *The Remarks and Admonitions* and *The Healing*.

In *The Remarks and Admonitions*, he discusses it more than once and connects it with concrete examples. He treats the transformation of elements and the transformation of sensible qualities within composite bodies into one another as an established fact, and he offers arguments for it.[^V4-570]

He even defines the phenomenon more precisely in a way that suggests that transformation changes the appearances of things while the underlying substance preserves its essential character.[^V4-571]

In several passages of *The Healing*, Ibn Sīnā examines matter changing into different forms in a way that also reveals a clear idea of the spontaneous generation of things in the material world. He does this while analyzing a number of processes of transformation and generation.

In one such passage, for example, he writes that:

> “...A thing may, through putrefaction, become prepared to receive another form, and from it another thing may be generated: a plant or an animal...”[^V4-572]

He also describes the natural foundations of transformation and generation.

He traces them back to the action of heat and cold and treats maturation as one of the basic processes through which these continuous changes in nature occur.

He distinguishes several kinds of maturation.

There is the maturation of the thing itself, the maturation of food, and the maturation of waste products.

His classification depends on what produces the maturation.

In the first case—the maturation of the thing itself—the active cause exists within the substance of the thing undergoing maturation, as when fruit ripens.

In the second case, the active cause does not exist in the substance being transformed but in the substance into which it is being transformed. Food is an example. What causes its maturation is found in the digestive system rather than in the food itself.[^V4-573]

He then defines the material basis of the process in these words:

> “...The material that undergoes maturation is a moist body. It must not be so dry and hard that it cannot preserve its moisture, as in the case of wood. The active cause is its natural heat. Its form is the change of its moisture into a condition suited to nature’s purpose. Its end is the completion of the growth of individual beings.”[^V4-574]

In this part of Ibn Sīnā’s discussion, we find another point of great importance for the idea of spontaneous generation.

He believes that this process does not occur only in nature. Human beings can also produce it artificially.

He writes that “what is produced through craft may also be called maturation.”[^V4-575]

Even in this artificial case, however, maturation still means heat transforming moisture into a state that suits the intended result: “and the same applies to artificial maturation.”[^V4-576]

In the botanical section of *The Healing*, Ibn Sīnā classifies plants according to their forms, the properties of the soil, and differences of climate. He then asks what causes the different “properties” of plants.

He begins by tracing these causes to a “psychic cause”—the vegetative soul.

But he immediately adds a qualification:

> “It is nevertheless possible that many of these conditions arise from the necessity of matter and the motion of nature, rather than for the sake of an end.”[^V4-577]

He then explains the general process through which matter changes in all composite bodies—mineral, plant, and animal.

He says that the basic elements of all these composite bodies are “the four elements.” These mix together and act upon one another until they reach either a balance or a state in which one predominates over the others.

Once this state is established, it forms the true mixture or *mizāj*. And once this mixture exists within the composite body, it prepares that body to receive the powers and qualities appropriate to it.[^V4-578]

These passages—and many similar passages scattered throughout Ibn Sīnā’s works—show that this idea was not a passing thought.

It was the result of a rich body of study through which he had examined and understood the phenomenon both theoretically and experimentally.

#### J — General Conclusion

Earlier, we stopped at a conclusion drawn from Ibn Sīnā’s statement[^V4-579] that:

> “The possibility of the first effect did not precede its existence. Possibility passed into actuality through the actuality that is its existence.”

From this statement, we drew three conclusions.

First, the “separate intellects” were never in fact separate from matter.

Second, matter was never merely potential. It always existed actually.

Third, the “first multiplicity,”[^V4-580] in this actual state of existence, is its own effect rather than the effect of a cause outside itself.

This follows from a passage we quoted earlier.[^V4-581]

And this, in turn, means that the world is the effect of itself.

At the time, we set this conclusion aside until we had compared a number of other passages.

We have now completed that comparison.

We could have gone further. There is no shortage of texts and evidence supporting the conclusion we reached. But adding more quotations would not make the idea any clearer.

The time has therefore come to return to that conclusion and place it within the broader conclusion with which we will end this chapter.

If we take this conclusion as we have drawn it from Ibn Sīnā’s main texts, and do not separate it from the six points[^V4-582] in which we found material for studying the idea of “self-motion” in his thought, a broader picture begins to emerge. We must also connect it with the evidence we have just examined concerning the “natural generation” of bodies, or spontaneous generation, and with the mutual interaction between bodies as they change into one another.

In other words, we should not treat these ideas, drawn from a large number of texts, as separate from one another. If we look at them together, as parts of one connected whole, and relate them to the other materialist positions we have identified throughout this study, the way becomes clear toward a general conclusion about Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy as a whole.

After examining his philosophical positions in their entirety, we no longer need to hesitate in saying that his basic starting point was to view the world through a materialist philosophical lens. The social basis of this starting point lay in the special character of the historical period in which Ibn Sīnā—the “Chief Master,” as he was known—lived and worked.

That period, with all its particular features, found a philosophical expression within the great historical civilization of medieval Arab-Islamic society. It did so in both of its main dimensions: ideology and knowledge.

The period was marked by two developments that may at first seem contradictory.

First, the absolute and despotic social order was beginning to crack under the pressure of repeated social and political upheavals. These conflicts had a class content, even when they sometimes appeared in forms that almost concealed their real basis.

This breakdown in the social and political order also had a noticeable effect on the ideology that supported it. Cracks began to appear in many of its basic assumptions and foundations.

Philosophy, as an advanced form of human thought, could not remain untouched by this process. In some respects, philosophy itself is a form of ideology. Arab-Islamic philosophy therefore had to reflect the breakdown taking place around it. It did so by expressing, in one form or another, the outlook of new social forces that opposed the ideology of the existing social and political order, which was fundamentally a feudal-bureaucratic system.

What united these new forces was that they all stood outside the small upper group that directly controlled the main structures of the system.

But socially, they occupied very different class positions.

They included:

1. A merchant class that was growing strongly and energetically within the particular form of feudal relations found in Arab-Islamic society.

2. Broad sections of the urban population: craftsmen, skilled workers, small earners, employees, and the destitute poor. Among them were agricultural workers and small peasants who had moved from the countryside into the cities, along with large numbers of unemployed, homeless, and struggling poor people.

The second major development was the growth of the natural sciences. This growth responded to the needs of social development and, at the same time, actively interacted with those needs.

On one side, this interaction helped create new social needs as society developed. On the other, those needs pushed the natural sciences toward further research, experiment, and discovery. The sciences moved steadily toward a deeper understanding of nature and, from there, toward a new understanding of the world based on its material character.

At the time, there was still no clear dividing line between science and philosophy. Philosophy was still regarded as the “science of sciences.” The natural sciences and mathematics were themselves parts of philosophy.

It was therefore natural that progress in these sciences toward a material understanding of nature and the world should affect philosophy as well.

This also helped philosophy, in its ideological opposition to the existing order, draw support from the logic of this relatively advanced knowledge of nature. Philosophy could then extract the most general principles from this knowledge. Taken together, these principles did not fit comfortably with the supernatural foundations of the official ideology.

This was the historical setting in which Ibn Sīnā developed his philosophy.

It was natural that this setting should leave its mark on his thinking and on his philosophical system as a whole. It was equally natural that he should be influenced by everything from the wider philosophical heritage that had become available in Arabic, especially Greek philosophy, and above all the teachings of Aristotle and Neoplatonism.

But Ibn Sīnā was not simply a passive receiver.

He had an independent intellectual personality strong enough to take from his own age and from ancient philosophy whatever could help develop his own thought and release his creative powers in research, experiment, and reasoning.

Yet he could not step outside his age. No thinker can ever do that completely.

Ibn Sīnā subjected the philosophical knowledge he inherited from earlier civilizations to the logic of his own historical period. He also added to that inheritance a new body of observations, ideas, and practical experiments.

In doing so, he strengthened the movement within Arab-Islamic society—socially, scientifically, and ideologically—toward materialist ways of understanding the world.

From his own texts, we can summarize the materialist positions he strengthened as follows:

1. Matter, motion, and time are eternal.

2. The material world exists objectively and independently of human consciousness.

3. Human consciousness is capable of knowing the world and understanding its realities and phenomena.

4. Existence has primacy, not essence.

5. There is one material world. Everything in it is filled with matter, and there is no empty void.

6. Nature is constantly moving and changing. Things turn into other things and arise from one another. Through this continuous process of change and generation, nature develops from the simple to the complex and from lower forms to higher ones.

These are extremely important materialist positions. But they do not make Ibn Sīnā a completely materialist philosopher.

Although they touch on some of the basic foundations of philosophical materialism, several idealist assumptions remain in his work. We can see them in the following areas.

1

Like the ancient materialists, Ibn Sīnā placed the original matter from which the natural elements are formed outside the existing world of nature.

A sound materialist philosophy rejects this. It recognizes no matter other than the matter that objectively exists in the universe. To search for some kind of matter outside existing bodies is really to search for an abstraction—something that exists only as a concept.

The scientific definition of matter is the objective world in all its many forms.

By this definition, matter is the atom. It is the living cell. It is natural bodies and living organisms. It is the process of material production. It is our bodies and ourselves.

Finally, it is everything that provides the basis for the existence of thought and consciousness.

In short, matter is objective reality existing independently of any consciousness.

2

Ibn Sīnā also kept the ancient philosophical idea that the soul and the body are two separate things.

For him, the soul is not material. It is a pure essence.[^V4-583] It does not die when the body dies, and its existence is not tied to the body in any way. Instead, its existence is connected with other principles that do not undergo change.[^V4-584]

This is therefore an idealist position on the soul, and it differs fundamentally from the materialist philosophical position.

In ancient philosophy, the soul could mean the powers of nutrition, growth, and sensation. But these are simply the natural powers of life in living bodies. Life consists of a particular chemical material, and modern science has discovered this material and identified its chemical structure with precision.

If by the soul we mean thought—the “rational soul”—then philosophical materialism sees thought as a function of the brain, and the brain is material.

Thought, in other words, is a function of matter organized at a very high level of complexity: the brain.

3

It is true that Ibn Sīnā recognizes motion as the main property of matter. Some of his texts almost suggest that motion is an expression of matter’s very existence.

It is also true that he understood the role of motion in changes of quantity, quality, and place.

All of this clearly begins from a materialist position.

But another problem appears here.

Like the ancient materialists in general, Ibn Sīnā reduces all forms of motion to one basic form: mechanical movement from one place to another.

He even reduces changes in quality and quantity to movement in space, both in the basic natural elements and in compound bodies.

This limited understanding of motion led him, as it had led the ancient materialists, to say in some texts that motion must be caused by an impulse from outside the moving thing.

This is why some of his texts say that every moving thing has a cause that moves it and that this cause is something other than the moving thing itself.[^V4-585]

In other words, the cause of motion comes from outside.

Here Ibn Sīnā falls into idealism. He also falls into the contradiction we discussed earlier when examining the problem of self-motion.[^V4-586]

At that point, we tried to explain the contradiction on the basis of our conviction that Ibn Sīnā did not simply fall into such contradictions unconsciously.

But that interpretation does not make the contradiction disappear.

One reason for it—apart from the personal factor we discussed earlier—is his view that motion basically has only one form: mechanical movement in space.

This limited view also affected the way he understood the relationship between motion and rest.

In one text, Ibn Sīnā seems to suggest that rest is part of motion—or, in the language of dialectical materialism, a stage within motion.[^V4-587]

But elsewhere he adopts another view that was also common among the ancient materialists. There he treats motion and rest as equal in their relationship to matter, as though they were two equally important properties of it.

Ibn Sīnā says that rest in something capable of motion stands opposed to motion in the way that absence stands opposed to possession:

> “Absolute rest is opposed to absolute motion, and a particular rest is opposed to a particular motion.”

He then explains that the rest he means here is:

> “rest in the place in which motion can occur. Motion in that same place is separation from that same place, and every separation from a place takes place through movement away from it, not movement toward it.”

Rest in a particular place, he says, is therefore opposed to movement away from that place, not to movement toward it.[^V4-588]

In this explanation, motion and rest appear to have an equal status in relation to place and to moving matter.

But the correct materialist position recognizes motion alone as a fundamental property of matter. It does not treat rest as another independent property of the same importance. Rest is no more than a stage within motion.[^V4-589]

Yet immediately after the passage just quoted, Ibn Sīnā adds an important qualification:

> “...indeed, this rest may perhaps be the completion of motion.”

This is extremely important.

It shows that Ibn Sīnā was reaching toward a more accurate understanding of the relationship between rest and motion, and of motion itself.

But this insight seems to have remained trapped within the traditional, one-sided, mechanical understanding of motion. Ibn Sīnā therefore remained caught between two very different ideas.

4

It was natural that the social form of material movement should be absent from Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy, since, as we have seen, he reduced the movement of matter to its most basic form: movement from one place to another.

What is more surprising is that his philosophical system contains no clear and developed philosophy of society.

This is striking because Ibn Sīnā had unusually rich direct experience of social and political life. He worked close to centers of political power. He encountered his society from many different sides. He experienced many of its contradictions firsthand and lived close to the sources of its internal social and political upheavals.

As a whole, the ideological content of his philosophy can be seen as expressing the outlook of the growing middle social groups of Arab-Islamic society at the time.

The military-feudal order then in power did not satisfy these groups’ political ambition to gain a real share of influence within the state.

It is true that Ibn Sīnā wrote some political passages in the tenth section of the *Metaphysics* in *The Book of Healing*. But these passages do not reflect the broad and deep experience he gained at the heart of social and political life as fully as we might expect from someone who had lived through so much of it.

This is especially surprising because social and political life in his time had reached an intense level of conflict and contradiction.

Ibn Sīnā himself suffered badly from these conflicts more than once. He was persecuted by the military class, which was one of the most destructive forces in society at the time. The soldiers of the Buyid ruler Shams al-Dawla forcibly removed him from his post as vizier and nearly killed him because he had tried to introduce reforms that would have taken away some of their material privileges.

There is another surprising side to this.

Despite Ibn Sīnā’s great interest in the philosophy of the “First Teacher,” Aristotle, he paid much less attention to Aristotle’s political philosophy than to the rest of his thought.

He did little to develop that part of Aristotle’s philosophy or adapt it to the conditions of his own society and time.

Instead, the political sections of Ibn Sīnā’s work give us only very broad outlines. We do not see clearly in them the features of a particular historical period, a particular society, or particular social relations.

Yet his own experience should have pushed him toward much greater detail and precision.

Indeed, he had more direct experience to draw on than his predecessor al-Fārābī, the author of *The Opinions of the People of the Virtuous City*.

It seems to us that the reason lies in the relentless pressure of political events on Ibn Sīnā’s life and in the instability created by his movement between rival regional rulers.

This may have pushed him to hide his real political views and limit himself to general statements that did not commit him to a particular position—one that might have exposed him to dangers even worse than those he already faced.

Even so, the general political ideas he did write show an idealist tendency that does not fit easily with the basic materialist starting points found in his philosophy of nature, metaphysics, and logic. Nor does it fit with his own direct experience of social and political life.

This idealist tendency appears in the way he explains class inequality in private property.

He attributes it partly to divine destiny and partly to another supposedly natural or predetermined factor: differences between people in intelligence and ability.

In this second explanation, he makes the character of social relations depend on ideas and mental qualities, when in reality the relationship runs the other way.

A realistic explanation begins from the fact that the character of people’s ideas and opinions is shaped by the character of their social relations.

This idealist view of society also led Ibn Sīnā to argue that the social character of human activity comes from these predetermined and individual differences between people.

Yet a democratic tendency also found its way into his short political discussions at the end of the *Metaphysics* in *The Book of Healing*.

This tendency can be understood as a reaction against the despotic political order and the widespread militarism of the period.

It appears despite Ibn Sīnā’s effort to remain general and avoid specific political positions.

We can see it most clearly in his outline of the duties of the ruler, or head of state.

His picture contains a kind of protest against concentrating all political and administrative responsibilities in the hands of a single ruler.

Instead, he proposes distributing these responsibilities across a series of ranks filled by people with the necessary experience and ability.

These ranks would form a chain connecting the highest and lowest levels of society, giving every social group a part to play in maintaining the overall organization of social life.

His general remarks also suggest that Ibn Sīnā was deeply troubled by the spread of usury and unemployment, and by the enormous damage they caused to everyday life and to the widening divisions between social classes.

This is the one part of his political thought in which we find a relatively concrete reflection of actual social conditions, although even here the discussion remains mostly general.

---

The general conclusion, then, is that Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy has both a materialist side and an idealist side.

But the materialist side reaches into the basic foundations of philosophical materialism itself. It deserves much deeper and more sustained study so that its historical significance—both intellectual and ideological—can be brought more fully to light.

## Conclusions of Chapter Three

### 1

From the beginning, we found that Ibn Sīnā’s treatment of existence and essence was connected with a much wider range of questions than we had found in al-Fārābī.

This was because Ibn Sīnā’s personality, knowledge, and practical experience were more varied and richer.

We therefore had to study these different questions in all their diversity.

His final philosophical position cannot be understood from any one issue in isolation. It becomes clear only when his philosophy is viewed as an interconnected whole.

Before beginning that study, we first had to establish several points.

1. According to the inner logic of philosophical development, Ibn Sīnā had to deal not only with al-Fārābī’s philosophy but with the accumulated history of many generations of philosophy from around the world.

At the same time, he also had to respond to what we might call the external logic of philosophy: its connection with changing social relations and with the political upheavals of his own time.

The objective relationship between these two forms of development naturally slowed the pace at which Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy could move forward.

The Islamic principle of divine unity, *tawḥīd*, stood at the point where these two pressures—the internal and external development of Arab philosophy—met.

Ibn Sīnā therefore also had to begin from this point when using earlier philosophical theories, especially the Neoplatonic theory of emanation.

The principle of *tawḥīd* governed Arab philosophical thought as a whole whenever it tried to explain the relationship between God and the world.

Yet any ideological position in philosophy that moved outside the ideology of the state was, in practice, in tension with this same principle of *tawḥīd*.

This was the great problem facing Arab philosophy.

Arab philosophers used the theory of emanation in an effort to solve this problem, which was obstructing both their ideological struggle and the independent development of philosophy itself.

At the same time, the practice of the natural sciences, driven by the needs of social development, helped them modify the theory of emanation and find partial solutions to the problem created by *tawḥīd*.

These solutions were naturally incomplete and relative. But they drew on Arab achievements in the natural sciences and gave philosophers room to push the idealist theory of emanation in a more materialist direction.

All of this was already part of the historical situation when Ibn Sīnā entered the history of philosophy. He had no choice but to work within it.

2. Between al-Fārābī and Ibn Sīnā there was an important historical turning point in philosophy: the thought of Abū Bakr Muḥammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī (854–932 CE).

Al-Rāzī represents a model of the experimental scientist because he built his philosophy on practical experiment, especially in medicine and chemistry.

We therefore considered it necessary to study this major turning point.

Our study of Abū Bakr al-Rāzī led us to identify a number of distinctly materialist philosophical positions that remain striking even to a modern researcher.

Indeed, the unusual character of these views in his own time may have been one reason his contemporaries described him as an “atheist.”

Al-Rāzī was extremely bold both in defining his materialist positions and in openly declaring views that opposed the dominant official ideas of his time.

Even the Ismāʿīlīs fought against his ideas because his position on the Imamate differed from theirs. The Imamate was the ideological foundation of the Fatimid state.

Our study of Abū Bakr al-Rāzī led to the following conclusions about his philosophy:

* Matter, place, and time are eternal.

* Matter is an ontological category that he called “absolute matter.” For him, it refers to a material reality that he called “particular matter.” “Absolute matter” exists actually, not merely potentially.

* Existence and essence are inseparable.

* Natural things arise through the gathering and concentration of parts of matter. The natural elements differ because the degree of this concentration differs.

* Nothing can come from nothing.

* Things come into existence through combination, not through creation from nothing.

* Among his works was a book entitled *The Body Has Motion from Itself, and Motion Has a Cause*.

* He denied the miracles of the prophets.

* Yet Abū Bakr al-Rāzī reached these openly materialist positions through idealist ideas that originally came from Platonism, Neoplatonism, and dualism.

3. Ibn Sīnā’s circumstances, his philosophy and writings, and his intellectual, social, and political activity all took place in a part of Arab-Islamic society far from the original Arab lands.

This reflected a historical reality that was rare in the history of relations between peoples in earlier ages.

It was not unique to Ibn Sīnā as an individual.

It was also the reality of the peoples to whom he and his fellow scientists and philosophers belonged. They were educated in Arabic, spoke Arabic, wrote their science and philosophy in Arabic, and taught their students in Arabic as well.

This created a remarkable form of cultural unity among peoples spread across different geographical regions and continents, with different populations and very different historical and cultural backgrounds.

Despite all these differences, they were able to share a common language of culture.

That language expressed their material and spiritual needs as well as their class ideologies, and all of these peoples took part in enriching the culture they shared.

This was also a rare historical experience because its results survived in an intellectual and literary heritage that became a living link between the medieval and modern periods of human knowledge.

Ibn Sīnā himself never lived a single day in an Arab country.

Yet he learned and taught in Arabic. He wrote his philosophy, his poetry, and the story of his life in Arabic.

He became a powerful example of this historical experience—one that still stands as real evidence that different peoples can create a common language between them.

4. Ibn Sīnā also had his own personal logic, alongside the internal and external logic of philosophy more generally.

One sign of this independence was that he was not shaped by the Ismāʿīlī beliefs of his family environment.

When he entered political life, he was not drawn into the ideas of the rulers he served.

Nor did political concerns overwhelm his philosophical work or change his ideological direction.

He did not submit his thinking to loyalty toward any ruler or to the ruler’s wishes.

He remained independent in his opinions, thought, and will.

Another part of this personal outlook was his optimistic attitude toward life.

He freed himself from traditions that condemned the enjoyment of many of life’s pleasures, even though his own life was full of hardship.

In doing so, he also gave philosophical recognition to the value of physical pleasure instead of restricting pleasure to the spiritual and mystical meaning that was common among philosophers of his time.

5. Ibn Sīnā’s class position can also be identified through his profession as a physician.

On this basis, he belonged to the middle social groups.

But even with this more specific social classification, we can still say that his philosophy expressed the ideology of the different rising groups developing within feudal society.

The divisions between these groups were not yet sharply defined.

Taken together, however, they formed one side of the main social conflict of the period.

### 2

After establishing these points as an introduction to Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy, we began to study his own theory.

We first defined his intellectual method.

We found that it was based on induction, practical experiment, and the use of logical deduction whose starting points were drawn from real individual cases.

We tried to show that Ibn Sīnā’s experimental method should not be confused with modern empiricism.

His method uses the results of sensory experience as material for rational conclusions and for the application of logical concepts.

### 3

We then studied Ibn Sīnā’s position on the theory of knowledge and asked how this position was related to his experimental method.

We examined the subject from two sides: how knowledge is acquired, and whether human consciousness is capable of knowing the world.

Ibn Sīnā discussed both questions as part of his study of the soul.

We began with the faculties through which knowledge is acquired.

His texts showed us that these consist of the powers of the human soul and of reason.

The powers of the soul include all the powers of life: the vegetative, animal, and human faculties.

The human faculties stand at the top of this hierarchy.

It is an ascending and developmental order, beginning with the simplest powers of life, moving to more complex ones, and finally reaching the powers of human perception.

Ibn Sīnā divides these powers of perception into sensory perception and rational perception.

He also gives anatomical descriptions of the parts involved in sensory perception and their connection with the objects of the senses.

Between the outward senses and rational thought, he places a series of internal sensory faculties that help carry out the process of thinking.

These internal senses are the common sense, imagination or image-forming faculty, the compositive imagination, estimation, and memory.

Ibn Sīnā gives each of them a particular role in the process by which sensory experience is gradually stripped of its immediate material connections and turned into internal perceptions, until this process of abstraction reaches the intellect.

Rational perception is therefore produced from both outward and inward sensory perception.

Ibn Sīnā divides reason itself into two forms: theoretical reason and practical reason.

With theoretical reason, human beings begin to differ from the rest of the animal world in the way they perceive things.

At this level, consciousness reaches its higher form, in which choice and will enter the process.

This is also where the ability to form judgments begins.

Practical reason makes use of theoretical reason in developing crafts and skills and in organizing both private and public—that is, social—human life.

Here we can see a dialectical relationship between theory and practice: each affects the other.

This recalls Lenin’s idea of the mutual relationship between living observation, thought, and practice.

One of the functions of practical reason is that it must keep control over the powers of the body—that is, over people’s practical behavior and instincts.

How, then, does theoretical reason produce rational knowledge?

First, Ibn Sīnā does not place a barrier between sensory knowledge and rational knowledge.

Sensory knowledge is a stage in the development of rational knowledge.

In keeping with his experimental method, he therefore establishes a close connection between the two.

He defines the theoretical faculty as the power that receives universal forms abstracted from matter—that is, forms after they have been separated from their direct sensory relations.

At this point, however, his texts become uncertain when he tries to explain what these universal abstract forms are in themselves.

We examined the different possible meanings of this uncertainty and concluded that it came from his inability to explain the powers of human perception in their real relationship to the body.

Ibn Sīnā then describes the stages through which the theoretical intellect comes to grasp universals:

1. The stage of natural readiness.

2. The stage of “possible capacity,” or the “intellect by disposition,” when understanding begins to develop.

3. The stage of the “actual intellect,” when knowledge has been acquired and stored in the mind but is not currently being used.

4. The stage of the “acquired intellect,” when stored knowledge is actively being used.

As for the source of theoretical knowledge, Ibn Sīnā’s texts show that it comes from an interconnected whole made up of sensory perceptions and rational abstractions.

One important question remains: how do we come to know self-evident rational truths, the first principles from which demonstration begins?

By comparing Ibn Sīnā’s texts and judging them against one another, we concluded that he went beyond Aristotle by giving a materialist explanation of this kind of knowledge.

On this reading, basic truths become “self-evident” because they arise from real information that has been repeated and accumulated again and again throughout everyday human life.

Their original source is eventually forgotten, while the ideas themselves become so familiar to the mind that we imagine they were there from birth.

But they are not innate. They are acquired.

Ibn Sīnā also allows another route to knowledge of first principles: intuition.

Here we find an idealist position.

We examined this question at length so that we could compare his texts carefully and reach a convincing conclusion.

What interested us was the meaning of intuition in Ibn Sīnā.

Was it rational intuition, or was it mystical and illuminationist?

Bourgeois researchers, and Orientalists in particular—Massignon, for example—interpret it in a mystical and illuminationist direction.

We examined the question in light of Ibn Sīnā’s own texts and through our historical materialist method.

When we compared the texts, we found a contradiction that cannot simply be explained away.

In some passages, Ibn Sīnā ties rational knowledge firmly to its real external object.

Here his position is materialist, with no trace of mystical or illuminationist thinking.

In other passages, however, he turns to intuition.

There, the connection between rational knowledge and external reality is broken, and intelligible forms are received instead from the “Active Intellect” and the abstract world of emanation.

We therefore had to ask where this contradiction came from.

In our view, Ibn Sīnā was far too serious a thinker simply to be unaware of such a contradiction.

There must have been a problem behind it that he could not solve.

It was the same problem that had troubled earlier philosophers, both materialist and idealist: their inability to understand the real material relationship between the system of sensation and the system of thought.

What distinguished Ibn Sīnā from his predecessors was that he sensed where the problem lay and tried to define and address it.

But he could not solve it because the level of scientific knowledge at the time was still limited, especially by the lack of the necessary technical development.

He therefore moved uneasily between two positions, one materialist and the other idealist.

Yet his basic theoretical position still leaned in a materialist direction.

He understood that there was an objective relationship between abstract thought and direct sensation—in other words, between thought and reality.

At the same time, he recognized that intelligible forms were not the same as sensory forms.

How could he reconcile their connection with this difference in kind?

That was his problem.

Ibn Sīnā himself describes the problem in a passage that takes him halfway toward a solution.

He clearly recognized the connection between what we understand through reason and what we perceive through the senses, but he could not discover the true nature of that connection.

In the end, he therefore had to look for an external impulse that would turn what existed only potentially into something actual.

From this discussion, we reached two further conclusions.

First, these gaps in his theory of knowledge—the gaps through which intuition entered—were also the reason he followed his predecessors in accepting a dualism between soul and body.

Second, the idealist side of his theory of knowledge did not come from an essentially idealist philosophical outlook.

It came instead from the scientific limits of his time, which prevented him from understanding the real relationship between the general laws of nature and the abstract logical concepts and categories in which those laws are reflected.

### 4

#### Can human consciousness know everything about the world?

This is the second side of Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge.

Before asking how he answers this question, we first tried to establish his answer to another necessary question:

Does objective truth exist in the world?

We found that Ibn Sīnā’s answer is yes.

He recognizes an objective truth that appears in two forms of existence: a metaphysical form and a natural form.

But his texts indicate that the primary form of a thing’s existence is concrete existence—that is, its external material existence.

Mental existence is a reflection of external existence.

The truths of the world therefore exist objectively and independently of consciousness.

Indeed, Ibn Sīnā sometimes gives the idea of truth a scientific direction and recognizes the objectivity of knowledge, although only in an early and undeveloped form.

Recognizing the objective truth of the world is, in principle, the key to accepting that this truth can be known.

And Ibn Sīnā does in fact accept that it can be known.

We can see this in both his studies of nature and his work on “what lies beyond nature,” or metaphysics.

Within his system of “separate intellects,” there is a kind of cosmic bridge that the human intellect can cross in order to complete its capacity for knowledge.

Ibn Sīnā says that nothing in existence is incapable of being understood by reason.

Our study of his theory of knowledge therefore led to the following final conclusions:

1. The content of human knowledge is objective truth.

2. The tools of this knowledge are the outward and inward senses, together with reason and abstraction.

3. Abstraction does not operate independently of the other powers of human perception.

4. With the help of an external factor—the Active Intellect—these powers are capable of knowing the essences of things and understanding the world.

5. But this external help is not available to everyone. It depends on exceptional intellectual abilities that are acquired rather than innate.

6. Ibn Sīnā’s theory of knowledge has a materialist basis, but idealist gaps remain within it.

### 5

#### What is Ibn Sīnā’s position on prophecy and divine revelation?

Like al-Fārābī, Ibn Sīnā treats prophecy as a form of human knowledge based on the same faculties that underlie knowledge in every other human being.

There is only one difference.

In the prophet, the ability to receive knowledge is clearer and more highly developed than it is in other people.

This explanation of prophecy undermines its Islamic meaning as a doctrine and an ideology at its foundation.

### 6

The question of existence is the central axis around which many other questions turn.

It is also where we can see the underlying unity of Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy.

Within this larger question, we examined the following issues.

A — The relationship between existence and essence

Which of the two comes first?

After studying, analyzing, and comparing Ibn Sīnā’s texts, we concluded that he gives primacy to existence rather than essence.

This differs from the views of a number of earlier scholars of Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy.

B — The theory of form and matter
