Ibn Sīnā dealt with this question on two levels: the metaphysical and the natural.

Yet beneath both levels we can identify the same basic theoretical foundation.

Our discussion of the texts showed that Ibn Sīnā begins, fundamentally, from the external material world—our world.

His approach was shaped by the combination of theory and practice in his own life and by his direct involvement with social and political problems.

One of his main concerns was to discover the source of change and transformation in the world.

This led him back to the problem of the world’s origin.

Here he used Aristotle’s two categories of matter and form.

But he placed them within the Neoplatonic theory of emanation, modifying both Aristotle’s idea and that of Plotinus.

His wide knowledge of nature also shaped the way he imagined the metaphysical world.

He arranged matter and form within it in a way that shows a continuous sequence running from the celestial world down to the natural elements and compound bodies.

We observed that Ibn Sīnā’s general ideas about matter and form were drawn from the natural laws he had discovered through his studies and experiments with things in nature.

It is therefore easy to see that the role these general ideas play in his metaphysics is really a continuation of the role they already play in his understanding of nature itself.

Because of this, they helped present the world within his philosophical system as one continuous material whole.

C — One material world

We examined the texts supporting the unity of the material world through four connected questions.

1. **The Necessary Existent as presented in Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy.** Here we found his view that matter and form are eternal, that the world is eternal, and that motion and time are eternal.

2. **Cause and effect.** Here we found a basic continuity between the “First Cause” and its effects—that is, the world.

3. **Coming into existence in itself and in time.** Here Ibn Sīnā insists that every existing thing comes from something that already existed before it. Nothing ever comes from absolute nothingness. Every idea of “nothing” turns out to mean only a relative absence connected with some form of existence.

4. **Self-motion.** Here we found a body of textual material suggesting that Ibn Sīnā was moving toward the idea that change and transformation arise from within things themselves. We also examined the contradictions in these texts and discussed their meaning.

D — Looking toward new possibilities

At this point, we tried to trace Ibn Sīnā’s understanding of the Islamic principle of *tawḥīd* and the contradiction between this principle and the material unity of the world.

We also examined his attempt to solve that contradiction.

E — A turning point in Ibn Sīnā’s thought

Here we examined the problem expressed in the formula:

> “From the One, only one can proceed.”

How did Ibn Sīnā solve it?

We found that his solution marks an important turning point.

It leads toward the conclusion that the plurality of existing things does not proceed from the one God. Instead, plurality arises from within itself.

F — Defining the relationship between matter and form

Our final conclusion here was that Ibn Sīnā made an important change to Aristotle’s theory.

He made matter and form act on one another.

In doing so, he weakened the idealist content of Aristotle’s theory, which makes form the absolute cause of matter and leaves matter merely receptive and passive.

Taken to its logical conclusion, Aristotle’s position makes the concept the cause of material existence.

G — The relationship between natural existence and the “First Principle”

After studying the previous questions in light of Ibn Sīnā’s texts, we found that those texts fall into two broad groups.

The first implies that God is completely separate from the material world. On this reading, the material world exists independently as a unified whole, while the “First Principle” becomes a purely abstract idea with no real basis of existence.

The second group of texts implies that the First Principle is absorbed into the material world itself.

In either case, the result is one material world.

While examining this subject, we also studied Ibn Sīnā’s ideas about place, his rejection of empty space in the world, circular and straight motion, and the scientific and materialist value of his views on these questions.

H — No separation between celestial and earthly bodies

To support our earlier conclusions, we examined passages that might appear to separate celestial bodies from earthly ones.

We found that Ibn Sīnā does not ultimately maintain such a separation.

His philosophy therefore provides further support for the idea of one material world.

I — The natural generation of bodies

Ibn Sīnā’s texts contain signs that he may have held some version of this idea.

But it appears only in an early and undeveloped form, so it must be treated with caution.

### 7

We ended our study of Ibn Sīnā by trying to draw a general conclusion about the tendencies that define the overall direction of his philosophy.

This conclusion brings together both his materialist and his idealist positions and traces each of them back to its intellectual, ideological, and social foundations.

Our final judgment was that Ibn Sīnā cannot be regarded as a completely materialist philosopher, because the idealist elements in his system are not merely marginal.

But we can say that the general direction of his thought is materialist, and that its central starting points are materialist.

## Conclusion

### 1

Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy marked the culmination of the stages through which Arab philosophical thought had developed from the eighth century to the first third of the eleventh—a period of roughly three centuries. This was a short span compared with the much longer histories of the Eastern and Western, especially Greek, philosophical traditions that came before it.

Yet despite this relatively short period, the conditions of Arab-Islamic society allowed Arab philosophy to play two important roles in the history of world philosophy.

First, it brought Greek philosophy back to life after it had become scattered, fragmented, and confined for several generations to a number of monasteries and Nestorian, Jacobite, and Persian schools in the East, such as those of Edessa, Nisibis, Harran, and Jundishapur.

Second, Arab philosophy left its mark on part of medieval European philosophy through what it had achieved in the study of nature. Historically, serious discussion of nature in Europe, and attempts to understand what nature itself was, began only after Europeans became familiar with the philosophy of Ibn Sīnā. As his name spread among medieval European philosophers, they began discussing such questions as the division of natural things into living and non-living forms, movement in nature, and other problems of this kind.

### 2

This double historical role of Arab philosophy raises two main questions for any scientific understanding of the history of philosophy.

The first is how Greek philosophy was brought back to life during the age of Arab philosophy.

The second is how Arab philosophy, in turn, influenced medieval European philosophy.

On the first question, we need to stress something we have tried to show throughout this book. When Greek philosophy, in both its Athenian and Alexandrian forms, came to life again in the philosophical work of Arab thinkers, it did so in a social, intellectual, and ideological setting that was fundamentally different from the one in which Greek philosophy had originally appeared and developed.

The main reason was the difference between the social relations of the ancient Greek and Roman worlds and those of medieval Arab-Islamic society.

Ancient Greek and Roman society was based on slavery. Society was divided mainly between two classes: slave-owning masters and slaves.

Arab-Islamic society, by contrast, was based mainly on feudal relations of production. Alongside these were relatively developed forms of trade and banking, which had become an integral part of the society’s economic and social structure.

This fundamental difference necessarily changed the basis on which classes were formed. It also greatly widened the range of people who could take part in cultural life.

In slave-owning Greek society, slaves and some groups among the so-called “free” population were completely excluded from cultural activity and had no right to take part in it. Cultural life was largely restricted to the upper layers of the free population.

In medieval Arab-Islamic society, the situation was very different.

The kind of practical social activity people perform is one of the factors that shapes cultural activity, both in the forms it takes and in the kinds of people who take part in it.

It was therefore natural that Arab-Islamic society should differ from ancient Greek society not only in the groups of people producing culture, but also in the culture they produced. This difference necessarily appeared in the ideological sphere as well. Intellectual activity, like social and political activity, always has a class character, and philosophy is one of the highest forms of intellectual activity.

The history of knowledge in ancient Greece itself confirms this close and objective connection between practical social life and intellectual activity.

Why, for example, was Greek philosophy so closely connected with the study of nature?

The reason is that the earliest Greek philosophers were also engaged in practical life. Some were sailors, merchants, or politicians. Their theoretical ideas were therefore closely connected with, and shaped by, their practical activity.

That is why philosophical knowledge did not first appear in ancient Greece as philosophy in a narrow sense. It began as knowledge about nature. Only later was that knowledge organized into philosophical theories and hypotheses.

Given the deep difference between the ancient Greek-Roman world on the one hand and medieval Arab-Islamic society on the other, it would have been completely unnatural and unrealistic for Arab philosophy to take over Greek philosophy with exactly the same meanings and content it had possessed in a slave-owning society.

The old forms of production had disappeared. New forms had taken their place, together with new historical forms of knowledge and new ways of understanding the world.

We should also remember that Greek philosophy was not the only source of Arab-Islamic philosophy.

Its sources were many and varied.

One was Islam itself—as religion, thought, law, and ideology. Islam formed the theoretical climate in which Arab-Islamic philosophy grew and was shaped.

Other sources included the ancient and contemporary philosophical traditions of the East: Persian and Indian thought, as well as the older traditions of Mesopotamia, Egypt, and Syria.

These sources were historically and socially connected with Arab-Islamic philosophy even more deeply than Greek philosophy was.

This makes it even clearer that Greek philosophy could not simply enter Arab-Islamic thought unchanged. Its philosophical meanings and content had to be altered, and so did its ideological content.

The ideology of a pagan slave-owning society could not simply remain the same when it entered an Islamic society based on feudalism and trade—a society that was far more varied and complex in its economic, social, political, and cultural structure, especially in its class composition. Cultural activity itself also rested on a much broader social base, with new classes and groups taking part in it.

This was one of the most important questions we tried to study carefully in this book.

Our purpose was not simply to argue against bourgeois and Orientalist views of Arab philosophy and of the Arab intellectual heritage in general.

More importantly, we wanted to show the deep connection between that heritage and the historical development of Arab-Islamic society over roughly four centuries of the Middle Ages.

We also wanted to uncover the materialist tendencies and positions that appeared within Arab-Islamic philosophy through an internal struggle with idealist tendencies and positions. These included the idealism found even in Greek philosophy up to Aristotle, as well as the much clearer idealism of Plato and Neoplatonism.

We discussed this question at length in the introduction to the first volume.

The second question is the influence of Arab-Islamic philosophy on medieval European philosophy. This needs some further explanation here, because it helps show the active historical role Arab philosophy played in the development of world philosophical thought.

We should begin with a historical fact that helped make this influence possible.

During the Middle Ages, an Arab state arose within which a number of different states later developed. Despite their differences, they remained culturally connected, at least, with the Islamic caliphate in the East, whose centers shifted between Syria, Iraq, and Egypt.

These states spread across countries with different peoples, histories, civilizations, and cultures. They also extended across different geographical regions in all three continents: Asia, Africa, and Europe.

This was the external historical factor we referred to earlier. It created the conditions for many different cultures to become absorbed into Arab culture.

The same situation later helped this many-sided Arab culture come into contact with European culture.

That exchange could have been much broader and deeper if Arab culture had been able to continue developing creatively for a longer historical period. It could also have gone further if Arab thinkers had studied the other emerging European cultures of the Middle Ages as closely as they studied one European culture in particular: ancient Greek culture.

But that is not what happened.

Instead, medieval European culture took the initiative in studying Arab culture. The relationship between the two therefore became largely one-sided. For that reason, the influence during the Middle Ages was also largely one-sided.

Before looking at concrete examples of Arab philosophy’s influence on medieval Europe, we need to understand the social differences reflected in the two philosophical traditions.

When we speak of “medieval philosophy,” we are speaking, first, of the philosophy of European feudal society and, second, of the philosophy of Arab-Islamic feudal society.

Both had feudal relations of production as their social base. In that sense, they belonged to the same broad type of social system.

But European feudalism was different from feudalism in the East.

This was a historical difference that the founders of Marxism, Marx and Engels, themselves took into account when they used their well-known term, the “Asiatic mode of production.”

The difference was clearly reflected in philosophy.

One important historical sign of it can be seen in what happened after the collapse of the centralized Roman Empire.

Europe broke up into principalities. Wars then broke out among them as larger feudal powers tried to gather smaller principalities around themselves.

Out of this process came the gradual formation of the European nations, a process that continued throughout the Middle Ages and was not completed until the nineteenth century.

During this long period, the Vatican was the only major center of unity in Europe.

It therefore developed into a state that exercised authority over Europe’s feudal powers while still retaining its Christian religious character.

Within the Arab-Islamic Empire, the situation was different.

The caliphate ruled as both religious and civil authority. There were not two separate powers. The caliphal state represented the feudal social order as a whole.

Later, when relatively independent principalities and small states began to appear inside the caliphate, the centralized feudal order began to break apart.

At the same time, the feudal structure became increasingly military in character. This deepened its weakness, held back rural production, and exhausted the productive forces of the countryside.

As a result, the main forms of production increasingly overlapped, and the boundaries between them became unclear. It became difficult to say whether the economy was mainly rural, craft-based, commercial, or all of these at once.

### 3

These two different historical situations were reflected in philosophy in different ways.

In Europe, philosophy took an almost entirely theological direction and revolved around one central question: God.

During the Middle Ages, philosophical thought was largely confined to questions about the relationship between God, Christ, and Mary, together with the question of how God created the world.

The doctrine that God created the world from nothing became dominant, along with the doctrine of the Trinity and the idea of original sin.

The Vatican drew part of its authority from these three doctrines.

In the Islamic East, the situation was different.

In content, philosophical thought turned toward the human being and nature. In form, it still expressed itself in terms of God.

From the very beginning of philosophical thought in Arab-Islamic history, the human being was one of its central concerns.

The Qadarites raised the issue first, followed by the Muʿtazilites. They emphasized human freedom of choice and human responsibility for action.

Against them stood the Jabrites, whose doctrine of predestination gave ideological expression to a ruling power that presented its authority as the expression of divine will and therefore as a fate people had no choice but to accept.

This question of the human being remained one of the basic themes of Arab-Islamic philosophy all the way to Ibn Rushd, where it reached its highest development.

The question of nature also became central because the development of society itself created a need to study it.

During the early period of the centralized Abbasid caliphate, wide possibilities opened up for the growth of the economy, science, and philosophical thought.

At the same time, early ideological struggles under a centralized system of absolute and authoritarian rule often took place in the language of Islamic theology.

But there is another important feature here.

Most of the thinkers who built the early movement of philosophical thought came from the middle social groups, especially from people connected with trades and professions. We can see this even in names such as al-Naẓẓām, al-ʿAllāf, al-Khayyāṭ, al-ʿAṭṭār, and al-Ghazālī.

Many of these thinkers were also of non-Arab origin, and under Umayyad rule the political authorities often looked down on people from such backgrounds.

All of this helped give philosophical thought, from an early stage, an ideological direction that stood in opposition to the ideology of the existing state.

These were among the factors that helped produce materialist tendencies within Arab philosophy, in forms shaped by their own particular historical conditions.

### 4

It is not by accident that historians of medieval European philosophy mark the end of its first stage and the beginning of its second around the time Europeans first became familiar with Arab philosophy.

This shows how important that historical encounter was for the development of European philosophy.

It helped produce a major shift in direction.

European philosophy moved from what is usually called its **patristic period**, the age of the Church Fathers, which reached its end with Saint Augustine, into what historians have called the **period of Arab and Jewish philosophical influence**.

At that time, economic development in the Arab world was far ahead of Europe.

There was hardly any comparison.

Much of Europe was still dominated by the remains of a largely self-sufficient local economy within feudal principalities.

In the Arab world, by contrast, a market and money economy was developing and flourishing. It was opening foreign markets even in distant regions such as Russia and had begun reaching as far as Scandinavia.

During this period, Europe produced few philosophers in the strict sense of the word until the appearance of Thomas Aquinas, who lived from 1225 to 1274. By then, the great period of Arab philosophy had already come to an end.

Aquinas’s philosophy showed a much stronger interest in nature, and here he was influenced by Arab philosophy, whose influence was spreading among European thinkers.

The names of Arab scientists such as Jābir ibn Ḥayyān, al-Ḥasan ibn al-Haytham, al-Khwārizmī, and Ibn Sīnā were becoming increasingly well known in these circles.

European thinkers began to feel a need to study their achievements in astronomy, chemistry, medicine, anatomy, and mathematics, even though popular hostility toward Arabs and Jews was widespread in Europe at the time.

### 5

As we have already argued, the rise of materialist tendencies in Arab philosophy was closely connected with economic development in the Arab world.

That economic development created the original drive toward scientific and technical achievements in the natural sciences and mathematics, which were still considered part of philosophy.

From this point of view, the entry of Arab philosophy into European intellectual history, and the influence it had on European philosophy, also helped open European philosophy toward the study of nature.

This was one of the first paths through which European philosophy began moving toward materialist tendencies.

Early signs of this change appeared in debates over the creation of the world.

Before Europe came into contact with Arab philosophy, the dominant doctrine held that the world had been created from nothing.

Later, the views of Arab philosophers such as al-Fārābī, Ibn Sīnā, Ibn Rushd, and others began to spread among European philosophers. These views rejected the idea of creation from nothing.

From the time Europeans became familiar with Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy, they continued drawing on his ideas about nature and the creation of the world until roughly the eighteenth century.

We have already mentioned his influence in encouraging European philosophers to discuss such questions as the division of nature into living and non-living forms, natural motion, and other problems concerning the material world outside the mind.

### 6

Medieval European **scholastic** philosophy developed teachings about different forms of essence and often explained them in extremely simple and superficial ways.

For example, scholastic thinkers might say that a clock shows the time because it contains an invisible inner power that makes it do so.

They might explain the fact that a flea can jump a distance greater than its own size by appealing to its essential form: the flea’s essence, they argued, contains the power that allows it to jump so far.

Ibn Sīnā’s physics was far more advanced than explanations of this kind.

He tried to understand natural phenomena through the phenomena themselves—that is, through their own laws.

Both he and Ibn Rushd, for example, studied the nature of heat, cold, moisture, dryness, and vapor, as well as the workings of hearing, sight, and other natural processes.

This way of approaching nature as a subject for scientific study reached Europe before the European Renaissance.

Through Arab philosophy, especially the work of Ibn Sīnā and Ibn Rushd, the idea of **“double truth”**[^V4-590] also entered medieval European philosophy: the idea that there is a philosophical truth and a religious truth.

In reality, however, the Arab philosophers did not truly believe there were two separate truths. According to our interpretation, this apparent division was a mask that allowed them to conceal their belief that philosophical truth was ultimately one.

The spread of the teachings of Ibn Sīnā and Ibn Rushd in medieval Europe also gave logic a much more important place.

This influence appeared clearly in European scholastic debates over the relationship between the universal and the individual.

Ibn Sīnā described this relationship in three different ways, all of which later appeared in scholastic philosophy: the universal exists before things, within things, and after things.

Before its encounter with Arab philosophy, the medieval European Church recognized only the Bible as a source for knowledge of God.

After that encounter, it also came to recognize Aristotle as another path toward such knowledge.

We have already mentioned one historical example of this influence.

Thomas Aquinas studied Ibn Sīnā and Ibn Rushd for many years. Through them, he also became familiar with Aristotle by reading Arabic translations of Aristotelian philosophy, since Aquinas knew Arabic.

Arab philosophy continued to influence thinkers of the European Renaissance.

Ideas found in Ibn Sīnā and Ibn Rushd—such as the eternity of matter, its circular motion, and the transformation of forms while matter itself remains—later appeared as accepted ideas in thinkers such as Giordano Bruno, who lived from 1548 to 1600.

They also appeared in Pietro Pomponazzi, who lived from 1464 to 1524 and was executed on charges of promoting atheism because he was regarded as an Averroist.

Another Italian philosopher, Bernardino Telesio, who lived from 1508 to 1588, adopted Ibn Rushd’s theory that heat is what causes non-living matter to pass into life.

Finally, we need only look at the philosophical schools that arose in Europe either in support of Averroist philosophy or in opposition to it.

Their very existence is clear evidence of the important historical role Arab philosophy played in the development of materialist tendencies—not only in the East, but in the West as well.

Materialist philosophy continued to develop from there until the eventual appearance of dialectical materialism.

### 7

Arab philosophy entered ideological struggle from the very beginning of its formation.

It first appeared through the ideas of the group known as the Qadarites. They rejected the idea that human beings were helpless before fate, while also rejecting the rule of an absolute and authoritarian political system that justified itself in the name of divine destiny.

The Muʿtazilites later continued along the path opened by the Qadarites.

As philosophical thought developed into more complete systems in the work of al-Kindī, al-Fārābī, Abū Bakr al-Rāzī, Ibn Sīnā, and eventually Ibn Rushd, the ideological struggle also rose to a higher level.

This happened alongside the growth of class conflict, which became sharper as social divisions increased along both class and ethnic lines in a society whose economy was developing at different rates under different historical conditions.

Arab philosophy later continued this struggle even after it had left its original historical environment in the Arab-Islamic lands and entered medieval Europe.

There, it helped the growing progressive forces within medieval, and later modern, European philosophy move toward materialist philosophical positions.

These positions became one of the main fronts of ideological struggle in Europe between Christian theology and science.

In our own time, Arab philosophy has once again entered this field of struggle, though now in a new and modern form.

The conflict today is between the forces of the reactionary Arab bourgeoisie, allied with and dependent on world imperialism, and the national and progressive forces fighting for national liberation and social progress.

Culture in all its forms—literature, art, philosophy, and the other human sciences—has become one of the main fields in which this struggle takes place.

As a result, cultural class struggle has become increasingly important within the wider struggle for national and social liberation, and the role played by revolutionary intellectuals has become broader and more influential.

It is in this same context that a new question has emerged: how should we deal with our national cultural heritage from an ideological point of view?

The heritage itself has become an intellectual weapon used by each side in a struggle being fought on many fronts, both intellectual and social.

Historically, general interest in the Arab cultural heritage goes back to the early period of what is usually called the **Arab Renaissance**, or *Nahḍa*.

This period began around the middle of the nineteenth century.

At that time, most Arab countries were under the rule of the Ottoman Turkish Empire. Some, such as Egypt, were under British military occupation. Others, such as Algeria, were being subjected by French colonialism and occupation to attempts to erase their national identity.

Under these conditions, interest in Arab cultural heritage became one means of resisting foreign rulers and occupiers.

By teaching the Arab public about the historical achievements of earlier generations, intellectuals hoped to awaken a sense of national identity and a desire for liberation.

A generation of intellectuals appeared in Egypt, Lebanon, Syria, and Iraq.

Among them were Muslim religious thinkers such as Muḥammad ʿAbduh in Egypt, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Kawākibī in Syria, and Maḥmūd Shukrī al-Ālūsī in Iraq.

There were also Christian and Muslim Lebanese thinkers such as Buṭrus al-Bustānī, Nāṣīf al-Yāzijī, and Aḥmad Fāris al-Shidyāq.

Later generations of Arab intellectuals continued to show growing interest in the national cultural heritage, though in new forms.

They used it to strengthen national feeling, encourage resistance to colonial rule, and help build an Arab public opinion that demanded independence for the Arab countries still under the Ottoman caliphate.

After the First World War, these countries separated from Turkey.

Some then came under French mandate rule, including Syria and Lebanon.

Palestine came under British mandate rule.

Iraq gained only a formal kind of political independence.

Egypt remained under British occupation.

The countries of North Africa—Tunisia, Algeria, and Morocco—remained under French control, while Libya remained under Italian occupation.

Under these conditions, interest in the cultural heritage kept its role as part of the struggle for liberation.

During these periods of resistance, interest in the heritage increasingly took the form of publishing old works and writing studies about them.

In that sense, the approach became more systematic and rational.

Arab intellectuals studying in Western European universities also began using bourgeois methods of research to study their national cultural heritage.

But throughout all the earlier stages we have described, the relationship with the heritage was still shaped by a basic assumption: the past was surrounded by a kind of sacred halo simply because it was the past.

It was praised for its own sake.

Any critical re-examination of one part of the heritage or another could be condemned as a national or religious form of heresy.

Certain areas of the heritage were treated as forbidden territory, with a clear red line placed in front of scientific research.

After the Second World War, the method of studying the heritage began to change.

National liberation movements had started to win important victories.

Popular movements were spreading and taking a larger role in struggles for liberation.

Arab forces of liberation and progress were also coming into closer contact with revolutionary movements elsewhere in the world, especially after the democratic alliance decisively defeated the Nazi and fascist Axis powers at the end of the war.

Under these conditions, and especially from the 1940s onward, attitudes toward the national cultural heritage increasingly became part of a much sharper and deeper ideological struggle.

Positions toward the heritage now divided along these lines.

Bourgeois and right-wing thought turned toward the idealist and supernatural sides of the heritage.

Progressive thought, by contrast, turned to the heritage in order to uncover its real historical foundations and to see all its different sides, both positive and negative.

This book itself was written as a serious contribution to building an understanding of our heritage that can strengthen the fighting spirit of our people in their struggle for national and social liberation across the Arab world.

It was also written as an intellectual weapon against a reactionary effort that has been taking place for a long time in the field of Arab intellectual heritage.

The aim of that effort has been to hide the heritage’s real historical character and, by doing so, to keep the present tied to the backward thought of our distant past.

### 8

The research in this book stops with Ibn Sīnā, whom we have treated as marking the end of the mature stage of Arab-Islamic philosophy in the East.

About a century after Ibn Sīnā, Arab-Islamic philosophy resumed its great struggle in the Maghreb against the reactionary intellectual repression whose banner al-Ghazālī had carried for several generations.

That struggle found its strongest expression in the philosophy of Ibn Rushd and in the progressive Averroist current that grew around it.

The author of this book will continue his study of this final stage of Arab-Islamic philosophy once the work required for the present part of the book has been completed.

* Husayn Muruwwa was born in 1910, according to his official papers, though he himself believed that he was probably born in 1908. He was born in the village of Hadatha, in the Bint Jbeil district of southern Lebanon. He received his early education in Bint Jbeil and Nabatieh.

* In 1924, he travelled to Iraq to study religious sciences, Arabic language, and Arabic literature at the religious college in Najaf. He completed his studies there in 1938.

* In 1948, he took an active part—as a political participant, journalist, and writer—in Iraq’s national popular uprising known as the Wathba, which brought down the colonial British Portsmouth Treaty with the Iraqi monarchy. After the conservative reaction that followed, Nuri al-Said—described here as “Britain’s man in Iraq”—returned to power. In May 1949, he ordered Husayn Muruwwa to be expelled from Iraq. Muruwwa returned to Lebanon in June of that year.

* Back in Lebanon, he began writing his well-known daily column, “With the Caravan,” for the newspaper *al-Hayat*. He also helped found the left-wing magazine *al-Thaqafa al-Wataniyya* and worked on its editorial staff from 1952 until it ceased publication as a literary and intellectual magazine in 1959. From the 1950s onward, he also contributed to the magazine *al-Tariq*, and from the late 1960s until the day of his assassination he oversaw its editorial work. He also wrote for the newspapers *al-Nidaʾ* and *al-Akhbar*.

* His books, in order of publication, include: *With the Caravan* (articles), 1952; *Literary Questions* (studies in literary criticism), 1956; *The Iraqi Revolution* (a study in modern history), 1985; *Critical Studies in the Light of the Critical Method* (literary criticism); *Materialist Tendencies in Arab-Islamic Philosophy* (a foundational work in the Marxist study of the Arab philosophical heritage, in two volumes), 1978; *Studies in Islam* (with Maḥmūd Amīn al-ʿĀlim, Muḥammad Dakrūb, and Samīr Saʿd), 1978; *On Heritage and Islamic Law* (studies), 1983; *New Titles for Old Faces* (studies on heritage and the thought of the Arab Renaissance), 1984; *Our Heritage: How Should We Understand It?* (studies on Arab heritage), 1986; and *Studies in Thought and Literature* (literary criticism, published after his assassination), 1993. He also left a large number of studies, research papers, and articles across many fields of knowledge. These were being collected and organized for publication in separate volumes.

* Husayn Muruwwa was assassinated in his home on 17 February 1987. He was 79 years old.

# Footnotes

[^V4-1]: Historians disagree about the dates of al-Kindī’s birth and death. Among modern scholars, some (Muṣṭafā ʿAbd al-Rāziq and Abū Rīda) say that he died in 252 AH/866 CE; others (Henry Corbin and Nallino) put his death in 260 AH/873 CE; and Massignon gives the date as 246 AH/860 CE. See Seyyed Hossein Nasr, *Three Muslim Sages*, p. 165.

[^V4-2]: Al-Kindī was known in Latin as Alkindus. One of the earliest Orientalists to translate his works into Latin was Gerard of Cremona (1114–1187). See Korkis ʿAwwād, *Yaʿqūb ibn Isḥāq al-Kindī: His Life and Works*, Baghdad, 1962, p. 13.

[^V4-3]: See Richard Walzer, *Islamic Philosophy and Its Place in Human Thought*, Dār al-ʿIlm lil-Malāyīn, Beirut, 1958, translated by Muḥammad Tawfīq Ḥusayn, p. 27.

[^V4-4]: Al-Qifṭī, *Accounts of the Sages*, p. 240.

[^V4-5]: Ibn al-Nadīm, *The Fihrist*, p. 357.

[^V4-6]: About forty years ago, virtually all we knew of al-Kindī came from reports preserved by historians and from incomplete excerpts of works whose full texts were unknown to us. This changed when the German Orientalist Ritter discovered a number of al-Kindī’s treatises in the Hagia Sophia Library in Istanbul and published them with the Orientalist Plessner in the Czechoslovak Orientalist journal in 1932. From then on, al-Kindī’s works began to appear in scholarly editions. Dr. Aḥmad Fuʾād al-Ahwānī in Cairo published *Al-Kindī’s Book to al-Muʿtaṣim on First Philosophy* in 1948. Muḥammad ʿAbd al-Hādī Abū Rīda, also in Cairo, published *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises* in two volumes containing twenty-five treatises between 1950 and 1953. Dr. Fayṣal Badawī in Iraq published *Al-Kindī’s Treatise on the Making of Swords* in 1962. Yūsuf Zakariyyā and Maḥmūd Aḥmad al-Ḥifnī worked with several Orientalists on scholarly editions of al-Kindī’s writings on music. Yūsuf Yaʿqūb Maskūnī in Baghdad published *Al-Kindī’s Treatise on the Production of Weather*. Orientalists including Ritter and Karl Garbers published al-Kindī’s work on the chemistry of perfume and sublimation, while Neheimer and others also took part in this work. Yet everything by al-Kindī that has so far been identified and published still represents only a very small part of the total body of work described by Ibn al-Nadīm, al-Qifṭī, Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa, and Qāḍī Ṣāʿid al-Andalusī. Ibn al-Nadīm lists 241 books and treatises by al-Kindī (*The Fihrist*, Leipzig, p. 255); Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa lists 281 (*Classes of Physicians*, Cairo, p. 29); and al-Qifṭī lists 228 (*History of the Sages*, Leipzig, p. 368). Qāḍī Ṣāʿid says that al-Kindī had more than fifty famous works in most branches of knowledge, ranging from long books to short treatises (*Classes of Nations*, Beirut, p. 52). What is actually available to readers today, however, amounts to no more than 42 printed works, 30 works still surviving in manuscript in libraries around the world, and 34 works translated into Latin, English, French, Italian, German, and Persian. Some of these translations survive even though their Arabic originals are lost. See Father Richard Joseph McCarthy, S.J., *Works Attributed to the Philosopher of the Arabs*, Baghdad, 1962.

[^V4-7]: Carl Brockelmann, *History of the Islamic Peoples*, Arabic edition, Beirut, vol. 2, p. 39.

[^V4-8]: A subject-based catalogue of al-Kindī’s writings compiled by Father Richard Joseph McCarthy, S.J. (previously cited source, pp. 115–118) suggests the following totals: 14 works on arithmetic; 21 on geometry and surveying; 8 on dimensions; 12 on spheres; 4 on optics; 15 on music; about 45 on astronomy and meteorological phenomena; about 43 on astrology; 5 on geography; about 36 on medicine; 117 on philosophy, including general treatises, logic, natural philosophy, metaphysics, psychology, ethics, and politics; 20 on *kalām* and disputation; and 15 on chemistry, including perfume-making, swords, gems and stones, and mirrors. This is apart from about 31 miscellaneous works.

[^V4-9]: Roger Bacon, for example, ranked al-Kindī and al-Ḥasan ibn al-Haytham in the first rank alongside Ptolemy (Qadrī Ṭūqān, *Science among the Arabs*, p. 104). Al-Bīrūnī says that in the science of gems and stones he knew no work other than al-Kindī’s book on gems and related substances, and praises al-Kindī’s originality in every field he touched, calling him a leader among modern scholars and a model for researchers (al-Bīrūnī, *The Book of Precious Stones*, Hyderabad ed., 1355 AH, pp. 31–32).

[^V4-10]: Henry Corbin denies that al-Kindī translated ancient texts (*History of Islamic Philosophy*, p. 236), but Arab historians say that he did. Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa (*Classes of Physicians*, vol. 1, p. 207) reports that Abū Maʿshar wrote in his *Memoirs for Shādhān*: “The four master translators are Ḥunayn ibn Isḥāq, Yaʿqūb ibn Isḥāq al-Kindī, Thābit ibn Qurra, and ʿUmar ibn al-Farrukhān al-Ṭabarī.” Sulaymān ibn Ḥassān, the Andalusian physician known as Ibn Juljul (fourth century AH), says that al-Kindī “translated many works of philosophy, clarified what was difficult in them, and summarized what was obscure and hard to understand” (*Classes of Physicians and Sages*, ed. Fuʾād Sayyid, Cairo, 1955, pp. 73–74). Al-Qifṭī also quotes this statement from Ibn Juljul (*Accounts of the Sages*, p. 241). See ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī, *The Role of the Arabs in the Formation of European Thought*. Muṣṭafā ʿAbd al-Rāziq (*The Philosopher of the Arabs and the Second Teacher*, Cairo, 1945, p. 21) confirms that al-Kindī learned Greek. Massignon likewise says of al-Kindī: “He deserves credit for revising a number of Arabic translations of Greek philosophical works” (Massignon, *Collection of Unpublished Texts from the History of Sufism in Islam*, Paris, 1929, in French).

[^V4-11]: Ẓahīr al-Dīn al-Bayhaqī says in *History of the Sages* (Damascus ed., 1946, p. 41) that al-Muʿtaṣim “attached al-Kindī to his court; he was tutor to al-Muʿtaṣim’s son, and he wrote treatises to Aḥmad ibn al-Muʿtaṣim.” Ibn Nubāta al-Miṣrī writes (*Sarḥ al-ʿUyūn fī Sharḥ Risālat Ibn Zaydūn*, Būlāq ed., 1278 AH, p. 123): “The reign of al-Muʿtaṣim was adorned by him”—that is, by al-Kindī—“and by his writings.”

[^V4-12]: In introducing al-Kindī, Ibn Nubāta (previously cited source) says that he was “known in his time as the Philosopher of Islam.” Qāḍī Ṣāʿid (*Classes of Nations*, p. 52) says that no one in Islam except Yaʿqūb had become so famous among the public for the philosophical sciences that people simply called him “the philosopher.” Notice Ibn Nubāta’s words “in his time,” and Qāḍī Ṣāʿid’s phrase “in Islam.” Both point to the title having been used in al-Kindī’s own age. The other sources indicate the same thing.

[^V4-13]: See, for example, De Boer, *History of Philosophy in Islam*, p. 19, and Rom Landau, who says in discussing al-Kindī’s philosophy that he was “famous as the Philosopher of the Arabs because of his unmistakably Arab ancestry” (*Islam and the Arabs*, p. 217). Compare the previous note, where Ibn Nubāta calls al-Kindī “the Philosopher of Islam,” and where Ṣāʿid says “in Islam,” not “among the Arabs.” This clearly shows that the title “Philosopher of the Arabs” does not refer to ancestry. See Majid Fakhry, *Al-Kindī and Socrates*, p. 23.

[^V4-14]: Al-Kindī, *Al-Kindī’s Book to al-Muʿtaṣim on First Philosophy*, ed. Aḥmad Fuʾād al-Ahwānī, Dār Iḥyāʾ al-Kutub al-ʿArabiyya, Cairo, 1948, p. 77.

[^V4-15]: For al-Kindī, “rational knowledge” belongs to the human being’s “rational existence.” He divides human existence into sensory existence and rational existence, and he regards rational existence as the true form of existence. Sensory existence, by contrast, “is not stable, because what it encounters passes away, flows, and changes.” In al-Kindī’s system, rational existence is connected with universals, by which he means genera and species (previously cited source, pp. 84–85). The Platonic influence is obvious in his distinction between rational existence as “true” and sensory existence as “unstable.”

[^V4-16]: Muṣṭafā ʿAbd al-Rāziq regards this division of knowledge in al-Kindī as one of the clearest signs of his philosophical importance, calling it “a mark of genius and the basis of his lasting name in history.” He gives this reason: al-Kindī “was the first to divide philosophy into three sciences: theology, mathematics, and natural science (...). This way of understanding philosophy and dividing it according to subject matter therefore gave Islamic philosophy its direction from the very beginning” (M. ʿAbd al-Rāziq, *The Philosopher of the Arabs and the Second Teacher*, Cairo, 1945, pp. 45–46). See al-Kindī’s *Treatise on the Intellect* and *Treatise on the Nature of Sleep and Dreams*, in *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, ed. Abū Rīda, Cairo, 1950, vol. 1.

[^V4-17]: *Al-Kindī’s Book to al-Muʿtaṣim on First Philosophy*, al-Ahwānī ed., p. 82.

[^V4-18]: Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa (*Classes of Physicians*, vol. 1, p. 212) mentions a treatise by al-Kindī on establishing the truth of the messengers, as does Ibn al-Nadīm (p. 259). Al-Qifṭī (*Accounts of the Sages*, p. 241) lists a work “On Proving Prophecy by the Method of the Logicians.”

[^V4-19]: *Al-Kindī’s Book to al-Muʿtaṣim...* (al-Ahwānī), p. 78.

[^V4-20]: Previously cited source.

[^V4-21]: Same source. This passage from al-Kindī is taken from Aristotle (993b 20).

[^V4-22]: In al-Kindī’s philosophical terminology, the “first cause” means God. See al-Kindī’s *Treatise Explaining the Efficient Cause*, in *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, edited and published by Muḥammad ʿAbd al-Hādī Abū Rīda, Cairo, 1950, vol. 1, p. 219. The term suggests a connection with Aristotle’s “first cause.” Al-Kindī also uses other philosophical terms for God, including “the First Truth,” “the true, first, complete agent,” “the Absolute,” and “the Eternal.” See *Al-Kindī’s Treatise on First Philosophy* and *Al-Kindī’s Treatise on the True, First, Complete Agent*, in the same source. The phrase “the true, first, complete agent” suggests the influence of the Neoplatonic theory of emanation. It points to a series of intermediaries in creation, each of which al-Kindī treats only figuratively as an agent—secondary and incomplete. God alone is the true, first, complete agent.

[^V4-23]: We can see this in his discussion of God through the concepts on which he built his complete theory of intellect and definitions, until he arrives at the concept of the “first cause” and the science devoted to it, which he calls “first philosophy” in keeping with the Aristotelian method. See the opening of *Al-Kindī’s Treatise on First Philosophy*, previously cited source.

[^V4-24]: By these he means genuinely established principles of knowledge that have come to function for us as self-evident first principles. By “the later ones,” he means the latest forms of knowledge available in his own time.

[^V4-25]: Al-Kindī, *Treatise on First Philosophy*, in the previously cited source, vol. 1, p. 101.

[^V4-26]: Aristotle, 993b 10–15.

[^V4-27]: Al-Kindī, *Treatise on First Philosophy*, previously cited source.

[^V4-28]: Previously cited source, pp. 102–103. In the same passage al-Kindī also says: “It is therefore fitting for us (...) to follow in this book our usual practice in all our subjects: to present what the ancients said about the matter, fully but by the shortest and easiest route for those who follow this path, and to complete what they did not discuss fully, in a way suited to the language and practice of our own age, as far as we are able” (same source). This final phrase caught the attention of Richard Walzer, Professor of Islamic Philosophy at Oxford. In his article on Islamic philosophy in the *Encyclopaedia Britannica* (1950s edition), he commented: “This means that al-Kindī is not simply expressing Greek thought in Arabic; he is also claiming a measure of intellectual originality for himself” (Arabic translation by Muḥammad Tawfīq Ḥusayn, Dār al-ʿIlm lil-Malāyīn, Beirut, 1958, p. 38).

[^V4-29]: Al-Kindī means “the science of philosophy,” based on his earlier definition of philosophy as “knowledge of things in their true realities.”

[^V4-30]: Previously cited source, p. 104.

[^V4-31]: See what was said in section (1) on the problem of knowledge (p. 18).

[^V4-32]: We mean in particular the twenty-five treatises edited, introduced, and published by Muḥammad ʿAbd al-Hādī Abū Rīda in Cairo between 1950 and 1953. These include al-Kindī’s *Treatise on First Philosophy*, which Dr. Aḥmad Fuʾād al-Ahwānī had already edited and published separately in Cairo in 1948. Father Richard Joseph McCarthy, S.J., counted more than 500 differences between Abū Rīda’s text and al-Ahwānī’s text (*Works Attributed to the Philosopher of the Arabs*, p. 67), which shows how difficult the manuscript is to read.

[^V4-33]: These works are: Ibn al-Nadīm’s *The Fihrist* (385 AH/978 CE); *Accounts of the Sages* by Abū al-Ḥasan ʿAlī ibn Yūsuf Jamāl al-Dīn al-Qifṭī (646 AH/1248 CE); *Classes of Physicians* by Muwaffaq al-Dīn Abū al-ʿAbbās Aḥmad ibn al-Qāsim, known as Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa (668 AH/1270 CE); and *Classes of Nations* by Qāḍī Ṣāʿid al-Andalusī (462 AH/1070 CE). See Father Richard Joseph McCarthy, S.J., *Works Attributed to the Philosopher of the Arabs*, Baghdad, 1962, pp. 81–90, 91–111, 115–118.

[^V4-34]: Al-Qifṭī gives the title in this form. Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa adds the words “in which there is no injustice.” Ibn al-Nadīm gives it as: *Book on the Fact That All the Acts of the Creator—Exalted Be His Name—Are Just and Contain No Injustice*.

[^V4-35]: Ibn al-Nadīm, al-Qifṭī, and Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa all give the title in this form.

[^V4-36]: Ibn al-Nadīm and Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa give the title in this form. Al-Qifṭī gives it as *Book on Divine Unities*.

[^V4-37]: Ibn al-Nadīm, al-Qifṭī, and Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa all agree on this form of the title.

[^V4-38]: Qāḍī Ṣāʿid mentions it in *Classes of Nations*, Beirut ed., p. 2. He says that al-Kindī “followed in this book Plato’s view that the world came into being outside time, and defended this view with sound arguments, some sophistical and some rhetorical.”

[^V4-39]: This is how Ibn al-Nadīm and Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa give the title. Al-Qifṭī lists it as *The Indivisible Part*.

[^V4-40]: See note 2, p. 20 of this second volume.

[^V4-41]: The title appears in Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa. It refers to a discussion of people who condemn others as unbelievers and misguided. This is a question of *kalām*.

[^V4-42]: Only Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa mentions it. The question it deals with is also one of *kalām*.

[^V4-43]: One example, for now, is the basic assumption or proposition that “the universe was created from nothing.” Al-Kindī retained this proposition even though it conflicts with Aristotle’s theory of eternal matter, with the Neoplatonic theory of emanation, and also with Plato’s theory of Forms, as will become clear later.

[^V4-44]: In Aristotle this goes back to his well-known view that wisdom is the highest form of knowledge. For him, wisdom is the search for the causes and first principles of things (Aristotle, *Metaphysics*, 1, 981b 27). Wisdom holds this rank because it leads to knowledge of the highest principles—that is, the eternal principles. It is therefore knowledge suited above all to God rather than to other beings (Aristotle, 983a 9).

[^V4-45]: See *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, edited, introduced, and published by Muḥammad ʿAbd al-Hādī Abū Rīda, Cairo, in two volumes: vol. 1 printed in 1950 and vol. 2 in 1953. See also *Al-Kindī’s Book to al-Muʿtaṣim on First Philosophy*, edited and introduced by Dr. Aḥmad Fuʾād al-Ahwānī, Cairo, 1948. Al-Ahwānī believes that al-Kindī wrote this book partly as a summary of sections of Aristotle’s *Metaphysics* and partly as an explanation of other sections, while also noting “the central point of disagreement between Aristotle and al-Kindī: the oneness of God” (p. 68, 1st ed.).

[^V4-46]: Aristotle, *Metaphysics*, Book Lambda, translated into Arabic by Abū al-ʿAlā ʿAfīfī from the standard Oxford English text. See *Journal of the Faculty of Arts*, Cairo University, May 1937, p. 117.

[^V4-47]: See *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, ed. Abū Rīda, pp. 182, 214.

[^V4-48]: In the previously cited source, p. 182, al-Kindī says: “The first true act is the bringing of beings into existence from non-being. It is clear that this act belongs uniquely to God Most High, who is the end of every end. For bringing beings into existence from non-being belongs to no one else.” Here “bringing into existence” means causing something to exist; “being” means existence; “beings” means existents; and “from non-being” means from nothingness. The basic idea, then, is that bringing every kind of existence out of nothing is the first true act, and that this act belongs to God alone, because bringing existents into being from nothing is an act that no one but God can perform.

[^V4-49]: See Yūsuf Karam, *History of Greek Philosophy*, Cairo, 1940, 1st ed., p. 235.

[^V4-50]: Aristotle, 12, 1069a 34.

[^V4-51]: In Aristotle’s philosophy, “coming-to-be” or “generation” means that a thing comes to exist after not existing. In other words, it means “creation” in the religious sense.

[^V4-52]: On the eternity of motion and time in Aristotle, see *Physics*, 7, 242a 16.

[^V4-53]: Al-Kindī follows Aristotle in dividing causes into four, but he calls the material cause “the element,” the formal cause “the form,” the efficient cause “the agent,” and the final cause “the completing cause” or “cause of completion.” See *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, ed. Abū Rīda, vol. 1, p. 101.

[^V4-54]: See Aristotle, Book Lambda, Arabic translation cited above, pp. 127–128.

[^V4-55]: In the pages that follow in this chapter, we will try to clarify this point in al-Kindī’s philosophy.

[^V4-56]: “Ideas” here does not have its modern meaning. The word is being used in an ontological sense, referring to existence. In this context, Plato’s “Ideas” do not mean a process by which knowledge is acquired, as the word might first suggest. They mean “special substances that exist independently of any act of knowing.” In this sense, Plato’s philosophy can rightly be classified as objective idealism.

[^V4-57]: *Eidos* is a term Plato used for the “Forms.” Other ancient philosophers used it in the sense of a model or pattern, as did some modern bourgeois philosophers such as Husserl.

[^V4-58]: We find an almost complete expression of these Platonic ideas in the dialogues where Plato explains his theory of beauty, especially *Hippias Major* (291d, 291e). In *Timaeus* (27d [= 27]) we also find an explicit distinction between what always exists—the “being” that does not undergo becoming—and what exists but is constantly becoming, meaning that its existence is changing and unstable.

[^V4-59]: The question of the oneness of God as “creator” receives a great deal of attention in al-Kindī’s philosophical treatises. See, for example, his discussion of unity and multiplicity in his first treatise (ed. Abū Rīda, vol. 1, pp. 132–141, 145), and his *Treatise on the Oneness of God and the Finitude of the Body of the World* (same source, vol. 1, p. 199).

[^V4-60]: In Arabic philosophical literature, Neoplatonism is called “the doctrine of the Alexandrians” because the school arose in Alexandria from the late first century BCE onward. Its influence continued into the period when Arabic philosophy began to develop.

[^V4-61]: Plotinus (205–270 CE) was the second of the two principal figures of Neoplatonism. The first was Philo of Alexandria (25 BCE–40 CE), but it was Plotinus in the third century CE who brought the school to its fullest and most complete philosophical form. The only primary source for Plotinus’s philosophy is the *Enneads*, in which Porphyry of Tyre collected, organized, and classified the writings of his teacher Plotinus into six groups of nine treatises each—hence the title *Enneads*, or “Nines.” By the time Greek philosophy was translated into Arabic, parts of the final three *Enneads* had already been translated into Syriac under the title *The Theology of Aristotle*. The Arabs translated them into Arabic under the same title. This produced the famous historical confusion in which these texts were taken to be works of Aristotle, leading Arab philosophers to mix some of Aristotle’s ideas with those of Neoplatonism. We now have an Arabic translation of several chapters of the *Enneads*, published by Dr. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī in *Plotinus among the Arabs*. In 1970, an Arabic translation of the Fourth *Ennead*, *On the Soul*, was published by Fuʾād Zakariyyā and revised by Muḥammad Salīm Sālim (Ministry of Culture, Egypt).

[^V4-62]: In Neoplatonism, the terms “emanation,” “outflow,” and “procession” are different words for the same idea and have the same meaning.

[^V4-63]: See *Enneads*: 16, IV, 4; 16, 3; 16, 11; 15, 3, IV. The reference is preserved here in the form given in the source.

[^V4-64]: See this passage in the Arabic translation of Plotinus’s Fourth *Ennead* as published by Dr. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī in *The Autumn of Greek Thought*, p. 174.

[^V4-65]: See note 1, p. 44.

[^V4-66]: Plotinus says in the Fourth *Ennead* (V.5.9) that “everything that exists exists in God. The first essence is present in everything, and everything has a share in it—that is, everything is in it.” The Arabic text of this passage is translated by Dr. Fuʾād Zakariyyā. See *Plotinus’s Fourth Ennead: On the Soul*, study and translation by Dr. Fuʾād Zakariyyā, Cairo, Egyptian General Organization for Authorship and Publishing, 1970, p. 44.

[^V4-67]: Al-Kindī gives one definition of nature in his treatise on first philosophy (*Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, ed. Abū Rīda, vol. 1, p. 111), where he says that nature is a primary cause of everything that moves and of everything at rest after motion. He gives another definition in his *Treatise on the Definitions and Descriptions of Things* (same source, p. 165): nature is “a principle of motion and rest after motion, and it is the first of the soul’s powers.” A further definition appears in his *Treatise on the Nature of the Celestial Sphere* (same source, vol. 2, p. 40), which also includes a definition of natural science: “The science of natural things is the science of things that move, because all of nature is that which God made a cause and a reason for the cause of all things that move and all things at rest after motion (...). Nature, as we have defined it, is the cause of motion and of rest after motion.”

[^V4-68]: See the previous note.

[^V4-69]: See al-Kindī’s *Treatise on First Philosophy* (same source, vol. 1, p. 161).

[^V4-70]: See Dr. Philip Hitti, *A Project for a New View of Arab Thought in the Middle Ages*, Damascus, 1971, p. 267; and Dr. Muḥammad ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Marḥabā, *From Greek Philosophy to Islamic Philosophy*, Beirut, 1970, pp. 236–237.

[^V4-71]: See al-Kindī’s *Treatise on the Number of Aristotle’s Books* (same source, vol. 1, p. 375).

[^V4-72]: See pp. 30–31 of this volume.

[^V4-73]: Al-Kindī, *Treatise Explaining the Finitude of the Body of the World* (*Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, pp. 188–189).

[^V4-74]: Same treatise. See also al-Kindī’s *Treatise on the Nature of What Cannot Be Infinite and What May Be Said to Be Infinite* (same source, pp. 194–198).

[^V4-75]: Al-Kindī, *Treatise on First Philosophy* (same source, pp. 120–121).

[^V4-76]: By “body” here he means a physical body—that is, quantity, mass, and ultimately matter.

[^V4-77]: Al-Kindī, *Treatise on First Philosophy* (same source, p. 117). Al-Kindī also gives another proof that matter, motion, and time necessarily exist together; see the same treatise in the same source.

[^V4-78]: Same treatise, same source, pp. 120–121. Here al-Kindī records another disagreement with Aristotle. For Aristotle, time and motion are eternal, like matter. The source gives the reference as *Physics*, 1, 250b 11–13, 1222 w.b.; the final part of that citation is unclear in the source.

[^V4-79]: Al-Kindī, *Treatise on the Definitions and Descriptions of Things* (same source, p. 165). See also his *Treatise on the Nature of the Celestial Sphere* (same source, vol. 2, p. 40), and *Treatise on the True, First, Complete Agent* (same source, vol. 1, pp. 182–183).

[^V4-80]: By “the body of the whole,” al-Kindī means the first body with which creation began, from which all other substances then came into existence in sequence.

[^V4-81]: See al-Kindī’s *Treatise on First Philosophy* (same source, vol. 1, pp. 118–119). In al-Kindī’s terminology, *tahawwī* means the realization of a thing’s identity—that is, its coming into existence. *Ays* means existence, while *lays* here means non-existence. In al-Jurjānī’s *Definitions*, “identity” is explained as an expression for the absolute reality that contains all realities as a seed contains the tree, arising from the absolute unseen. Al-Kindī’s phrase therefore means that the thing came into existence from non-existence.

[^V4-82]: The phrase “the counting of the duration of what changes” means that motion measures the time of a changing thing. The Arabic word used here is an active participle from the verb “to count,” meaning to calculate or enumerate.

[^V4-83]: A thing’s *inniyya* means its reality or true nature. The word is formed from *inna*, the particle used at the beginning of a declarative sentence to affirm the reality of something. *Inniyya* is an abstract noun formed in the same way as “humanity” from “human” or “existentiality” from “existence.”

[^V4-84]: Same previously cited source, p. 120.

[^V4-85]: This title appears in this form as item 11 among al-Kindī’s works in Ibn al-Nadīm’s *Fihrist*, item 10 in al-Qifṭī’s catalogue in *Accounts of the Sages*, and item 15 in Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa’s *Classes of Physicians*. The words “in which there is no injustice” are omitted from the other two catalogues.

[^V4-86]: The title of this work appears in the same form in all three catalogues just mentioned. It is item 154 in Ibn al-Nadīm’s *Fihrist* and item 147 in both al-Qifṭī’s and Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa’s catalogues. See Father Richard Joseph McCarthy, S.J., *Works Attributed to the Philosopher of the Arabs*, Baghdad, 1962, pp. 82, 87, 92, 97, 101–107.

[^V4-87]: We mean the sources already cited: Ibn al-Nadīm’s *Fihrist*, al-Qifṭī’s *Accounts of the Sages*, and Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa’s *Classes of Physicians*, together with Qāḍī Ṣāʿid al-Andalusī’s *Classes of Nations*.

[^V4-88]: Al-Kindī died in 252 AH/867 CE, and Ibn al-Nadīm died in 385 AH/995 CE. The *Fihrist* is said to have been written in 377 AH/978 CE. The gap between al-Kindī’s death and the writing of the *Fihrist* was therefore no more than about a century and a quarter.

[^V4-93]: Same source, p. 245.

[^V4-94]: Same source, p. 246.

[^V4-101]: For al-Naẓẓām, the spirit is the soul. See al-Shahrastānī, *Religions and Sects*, Kilānī ed., Cairo, 1961, p. 55. He says that al-Naẓẓām “agreed with the philosophers when they said that the true human being is this self and the spirit, while the body is its instrument and its mold.” See also al-Shahrastānī, *Religions and History*, Clément Huart ed., Paris, 1901, p. 121.

[^V4-102]: Al-Shahrastānī, *Religions and Sects*, vol. 1, p. 55; al-Ashʿarī, *Maqālāt*, pp. 219, 331, 333.
