[^V4-109]: These treatises are: 1. *On the Soul*, abridged from the works of Aristotle, Plato, and the other philosophers (*Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, pp. 272–280). 2. *A Short and Concise Discourse by al-Kindī on the Soul* (same source, p. 281). 3. *Treatise on the Soul as a Simple, Imperishable Substance That Acts upon Bodies* (Ibn al-Nadīm, al-Qifṭī, Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa, and Father McCarthy, S.J., p. 30, no. 164). 4. *Treatise to Yūḥannā ibn Māsawayh on the Soul and Its Actions* (same sources; McCarthy, p. 50, no. 301). 5. *Treatise on the Existence of Substances That Are Not Bodies*. This treatise asks whether the soul is a substance or an accident, and then argues that it is a substance, not an accident (*Al-Kindī’s Treatises*, vol. 1, pp. 265–269). 6. *Treatise on the Nature of Sleep and Dreams* (same source, pp. 293–311). Two other treatises are listed on the same subject. The first is entitled *Treatise on the Cause of Sleep and Dreams and What the Soul Symbolizes through Them*; Ibn al-Nadīm mentions it, al-Qifṭī gives the word “is commanded” instead of “symbolizes,” and it is also listed by Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa and Father McCarthy (p. 31, no. 168). The second was translated into Latin under the title *On Visions and Dreams* by Gerard of Cremona. These two may in fact be the same treatise published in *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*; see McCarthy, p. 79, no. 78. 7. *Treatise on the Intellect* (Abū Rīda, vol. 1, pp. 353–358). Two other treatises on the same subject are also listed. One is entitled *His Treatise on the Account of the Intellect* (Ibn al-Nadīm, al-Qifṭī, Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa). In four manuscripts of this treatise, the word “account” appears where another reading has “part”; see Father McCarthy, p. 12, no. 22. 8. *His Treatise on What the Soul Remembers While It Is in the World of Intellect before It Exists in the Sensory World* (same sources; Father McCarthy, p. 31, no. 167).

[^V4-110]: Al-Ahwānī ed., pp. 133–134.

[^V4-111]: *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 165. Here al-Kindī defines both the intellect and the soul.

[^V4-112]: *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 272.

[^V4-113]: All the phrases placed in quotation marks here come from the same treatise, previously cited source, pp. 272, 273, 274, 275. The words printed in bold are those that carry an illuminationist tone within a Neoplatonic framework.

[^V4-114]: This is his treatise *On the Existence of Substances That Are Not Bodies*, same source, pp. 265–269.

[^V4-115]: Same source, p. 265. The word “essence” means what a thing is, or its true nature.

[^V4-116]: Same source, p. 265.

[^V4-117]: Same source, p. 165.

[^V4-118]: See Aristotle, 7, 1041b 6.

[^V4-119]: Aristotle defines the soul as “the first actuality of a natural, organized body capable of life” (2, 412b 5). In Aristotle, form plays the role of actuality—or completion—in relation to matter, which remains only potentially existent until form brings its existence into actuality (Aristotle, 12, 1069b 34). Richard Walzer supports our view that when al-Kindī speaks of the soul as “form,” he means the term in Aristotle’s sense. Walzer says that al-Kindī “refers to Aristotle’s definition of the soul as the form of the body or the completion of the body” (Walzer, *Islamic Philosophy and Its Place in Human Thought*, trans. Tawfīq Ḥusayn, p. 41).

[^V4-120]: See Plato, *Timaeus*, p. 45 ff.

[^V4-121]: This conclusion is confirmed by another passage from al-Kindī himself, in his treatise on “the prostration of the outermost body” (*Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 247), where he says: “Form is not separate from its element; the two are united....”

[^V4-122]: Such as his *Treatise on First Philosophy*. See *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 97; and al-Ahwānī, *Al-Kindī’s Book to al-Muʿtaṣim on First Philosophy*.

[^V4-123]: See *Al-Kindī’s Treatise to Aḥmad ibn al-Muʿtaṣim Explaining the Prostration of the Outermost Body...* (*Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 244). We consider it likely that al-Kindī’s *Treatise on the Cause of Generation and Corruption* was also addressed to Aḥmad ibn al-Muʿtaṣim. See Aḥmad Fuʾād al-Ahwānī, *Al-Kindī, Philosopher of the Arabs*, Cairo, no date, p. 77.

[^V4-124]: We think that the treatises addressed to unnamed individuals were probably written to al-Kindī’s students, for two reasons. First, the openings use the tone of a teacher addressing someone who has asked him for knowledge. At the start of the *Treatise on the Intellect*, for example, al-Kindī says: “I understood what you asked me to set down concerning my account of the intellect” (*Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 353). Second, al-Kindī uses the same tone in two treatises that explicitly name men known to have been his students. One is Aḥmad ibn Muḥammad al-Khurāsānī; see al-Kindī’s *Treatise Explaining the Finitude of the Body of the World*, *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 185; and Father McCarthy, p. 63, no. 111. Ibn al-Nadīm (*The Fihrist*, p. 261) says that this man was better known as Aḥmad ibn al-Ṭayyib, that he studied under al-Kindī and learned from him, that he later taught the caliph al-Muʿtaḍid (279–289 AH/892–901 CE), and that he was killed in 286 AH/899 CE; see Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa, *Classes of Physicians*, vol. 1, p. 214. The second student whom al-Kindī names is ʿAlī ibn al-Jahm; see *Al-Kindī’s Treatise to ʿAlī ibn al-Jahm on the Oneness of God and the Finitude of the Body of the World*, *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 201; and Father McCarthy, p. 48, no. 291. This ʿAlī ibn al-Jahm is the well-known Abbasid poet who praised the caliph al-Mutawakkil, was exiled by him in 232 AH, and died in 249 AH. The sources name other students of al-Kindī as well, including Ḥasanawayh, Nafṭawayh, Salmawayh, Abū Isḥāq Ibrāhīm Quwayrī, and Ibn Karnīb. See Ibn al-Nadīm, *The Fihrist*, p. 412; and al-Ahwānī, *Al-Kindī, Philosopher of the Arabs*, p. 77.

[^V4-125]: Al-Kindī, *Treatise on the Intellect* (*Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 353). At the opening of this treatise, al-Kindī writes: “... I understood what you asked me to set down concerning the intellect: a brief account of my view, following the opinion of the most highly regarded of the ancient Greeks, foremost among them Aristotle and his teacher, the sage Plato, since Plato’s position on this matter amounted to the position of his pupil Aristotle.” As this passage clearly shows, al-Kindī states that Plato and Aristotle agree in their theory of the intellect. On that basis, he then goes on to present Aristotle’s theory rather than Plato’s, despite the difference between the two theories.

[^V4-126]: Aristotle, *On the Soul*, Book III, chapter 5, 430a 10–19, according to the old Academy edition (Berlin).

[^V4-127]: See the valuable study by Muḥammad ʿAbd al-Hādī Abū Rīda that serves as an introduction to al-Kindī’s *Treatise on the Intellect* (*Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 313). See especially p. 351, where Abū Rīda rightly shows that al-Kindī learned Aristotle’s theory of the intellect directly from Aristotle’s own text, not through the interpretations of Alexander of Aphrodisias, as the French philosopher Gilson and others claim. See also ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī’s introduction to the old Arabic translation of Aristotle’s *On the Soul* by Isḥāq ibn Ḥunayn (Cairo, 1954, p. 6). Badawī gives three reasons why we cannot confidently say that al-Kindī relied on Alexander of Aphrodisias’s treatise on the soul when learning Aristotle’s theory of the intellect. He also gives a historical explanation of how al-Kindī gained access to Aristotle’s text on questions of the soul.

[^V4-128]: See Muḥammad ʿAlī Abū Rayyān, *The Foundations of Illuminationist Philosophy in Shihāb al-Dīn al-Suhrawardī*, Cairo, 1959, 1st ed., p. 282.

[^V4-129]: This treatise is known in Latin as *De intellectu*.

[^V4-130]: Seyyed Hossein Nasr, *Three Muslim Sages*, Beirut, Dār al-Nahār, 1971, p. 24.

[^V4-131]: Ibrāhīm Madkūr, *On Islamic Philosophy*, Cairo, Dār al-Maʿārif, 1968, p. 128.

[^V4-132]: See note 1, p. 66.

[^V4-133]: The section of this treatise dealing with universals, species, and the rational soul can be found in *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 155, or in al-Ahwānī’s edition of *Al-Kindī’s Book to al-Muʿtaṣim*, p. 134. The passage reads: “The soul becomes actually intelligent when the species unite with it; before they unite with it, it is intelligent only potentially. Anything that exists potentially is brought into actuality by something else that is itself actual in the respect in which it brings the other from potentiality to actuality. What brings the soul, which is potentially intelligent, into actual intelligence is the union with it of the species and genera of things—that is, their universals, and these are the universals of their individual realities [this is the reading in al-Ahwānī’s edition; Abū Rīda’s edition reads: ‘Are universals their individual realities?’]. Through their union with the soul, the soul becomes intelligent—that is, it possesses an intellect, namely the universals of things. The universals of things, when present in the soul and brought from potentiality into actuality, are the soul’s acquired intellect, which it possessed only potentially, through the actual intellect that brought the soul from potentiality into actuality. Since universals are many, intellect is many....”

[^V4-134]: The three kinds of intellect that can be drawn from Aristotle’s discussion in *On the Soul* are: 1. an intellect whose nature is to make all things actual—the active intellect; 2. an intellect that becomes all things—the potential intellect; and 3. the habitual intellect, the human intellect when it is actual. Aristotle illustrates this last state with the example of a learned person who has acquired knowledge and can use it immediately whenever he wishes. This kind of intellect therefore stands between potentiality and actuality: it is actual because the person truly possesses the knowledge, but potential because at a given moment he may not be actively using it. See Aristotle, 3, 430a 10–19; 430a 16; 429b 1–5. Alexander of Aphrodisias, one of the leading commentators on Aristotle between the second and third centuries CE, also divided intellect into three kinds: 1. the material intellect (*intellectus materialis*), which is intellect in potentiality; 2. an intellect that understands—that is, an actual intellect possessing a settled capacity for understanding (*intellectus qui intelligit et habet habitum ut intelligat*), so that it can exercise this ability whenever it wishes; and 3. the active intellect (*intelligentia agens*), an external agent that gives intelligibility to the human intellect and to intelligible forms. Its relation to the potential intellect in the soul is like the relation of light to the eye. In Alexander’s view, this active intellect is necessary for the union of the material intellect with the intelligible form, bringing the intellect from potentiality into actuality. A section of Alexander of Aphrodisias’s *On the Soul* was translated into Latin as a separate treatise entitled *De intellectu et intellecto* (“On the Intellect and the Intelligible”). See Muḥammad ʿAbd al-Hādī Abū Rīda, introduction to al-Kindī’s *Treatise on the Intellect*, in *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, pp. 320–321.

[^V4-135]: Aristotle also recognizes these two states. See *On the Soul*, 2, 417a 22 ff.

[^V4-136]: Aristotle, *On the Soul*, 430a 10–19. For our reading of Aristotle here, we have used Muḥammad ʿAbd al-Hādī Abū Rīda’s direct Arabic translation from the Greek in his introduction to al-Kindī’s *Treatise on the Intellect* (previously cited source, p. 333), comparing it with the old Arabic translation of Aristotle’s *On the Soul* by Isḥāq ibn Ḥunayn (d. 298 AH/910 CE), edited and published by ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī, Cairo, 1954. Aristotle’s discussion of the “active intellect” appears there on p. 74.

[^V4-137]: Previously cited source.

[^V4-138]: Aristotle, *On the Soul*, 2, 417b 21–22.

[^V4-139]: Aristotle devotes chapters 4 and 5 of Book XIII of the *Metaphysics* to discussing Plato’s theory of Forms. If we look especially at the discussion in chapter 5, we can see that Aristotle rejects separating the universal, which can be grasped by the intellect, from the sensory world. He also rejects a metaphysical separation—in the sense opposed to a dialectical one—between essence and appearance. And he rejects treating the universal as independent of the particular, as though the universal could exist outside particular things. From these aspects of Aristotle’s criticism of Plato’s theory of Forms, Lenin drew the conclusion expressed in his *Philosophical Notebooks*: Aristotle’s criticism is directed not only against Plato, but against idealism in general.

[^V4-140]: Lenin says that there can be no doubt about “the reality of the external world [in Aristotle]” (*Philosophical Notebooks*, French ed., 1955, p. 287).

[^V4-141]: Aristotle may appear to be a sensualist when he stresses this point. But he distances himself from sensualism in *On the Soul* (2, 417b 22), where he makes clear that although knowledge begins with the senses, it is not formed from sensation and experience alone. He says that science is knowledge of the universal, not the particular, while actual sense perception deals only with individual things—the particulars—and “science concerns the universal.” Here we have used the Arabic text of *On the Soul*, translated by Isḥāq ibn Ḥunayn, Badawī ed., p. 43. In the *Philosophical Notebooks*, Lenin likewise rejected the charge of “sensualism” against Aristotle, calling him a thoughtful sensualist in contrast to crude sensualists.

[^V4-142]: In *On the Soul*, Aristotle stresses that the senses do not produce sensation unless they are affected by an object.

[^V4-143]: Following Greek philosophical terminology, al-Kindī also calls the “image-forming faculty” by the Greek word *phantasia*. Majid Fakhry translates it as “imagination” (Majid Fakhry, *Aristotle, the First Teacher*, Beirut, Catholic Press, 1955, p. 66), while Abū Rīda translates it as “the imaginative faculty” (*Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 293). In Aristotle, imagination—or *phantasia*—occupies an intermediate stage between sensation and thought in the process of abstraction, or thinking (Aristotle, *On the Soul*, 3, 431a 16).

[^V4-144]: Lenin says: “From living perception to abstract thought, and from this to practice—this is the dialectical path to knowledge of truth, to knowledge of objective reality.”

[^V4-145]: *Philosophical Notebooks*, pp. 146–147. The Arabic translation of this passage appears in *Dialectical Materialism* by a group of Soviet scholars, trans. Badr al-Dīn al-Sibāʿī and two colleagues, Damascus, Dār al-Jamāhīr, p. 342.

[^V4-146]: Al-Kindī, *Book on First Philosophy* (*Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 11).

[^V4-147]: Al-Kindī, *Treatise on the Nature of the Celestial Sphere* (previously cited source, vol. 2, p. 40).

[^V4-148]: Al-Kindī, *Treatise on the True, First, Complete Agent* (same source, vol. 1, pp. 182–283).

[^V4-149]: See al-Kindī’s *Treatise Explaining the Proximate Efficient Cause of Generation and Corruption* (*Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, pp. 214–237). In this treatise he builds his logical arguments on the assumption that what a proximate cause does is a real, actual, tangible event, not something merely figurative. He says, for example, that motion produces heat in the elements and in things composed of them, and that the elements are affected by motion or by contact. Here motion is an efficient cause of heat. Motion is a real material fact, and heat is a real material fact. The first causes the second, and the cause itself is therefore real and material. What sense, then, does it make to call it merely a figurative cause?

[^V4-150]: Al-Kindī expressed his firm scientific belief in the law of causal relations in especially clear and forceful terms when he wrote: “There is nothing in nature that is pointless or without purpose.” See his *Treatise Explaining the Prostration of the Outermost Body*, *Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 253.

[^V4-151]: See note 152.

[^V4-152]: In al-Kindī’s picture of the cosmos, the world of “higher individuals” extends from the lowest point of the moon to the furthest limit of the celestial body. See his treatise on the proximate efficient cause, *Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 219.

[^V4-153]: The Arabic terms used here are plurals of *mizāj*, meaning the constitutions, states, and conditions of the body.

[^V4-154]: Same source, p. 224.

[^V4-155]: “Embryos” is the plural of embryo, and “wombs” is the plural of womb—the place where the embryo develops inside the female body.

[^V4-156]: Same treatise, same source, vol. 1, p. 224.

[^V4-157]: This phrase appears at the end of his *Treatise on First Philosophy* (*Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 162).

[^V4-158]: From the introduction to the same treatise in which we read the previous passage, which, as we understand it, expresses a materialist position (*Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, p. 214). The phrase “the ruler from non-being” means “the one who brings everything into existence from nothing.”

[^V4-159]: See al-Kindī’s treatise on the soul, same source, vol. 1, p. 270. In this treatise al-Kindī describes the soul as simple, says that its substance comes from the substance of God and that it is a light from His light, and says that once it leaves the body all things are revealed to it and it becomes like God, and so on. This way of understanding and describing the soul is close to Plotinus in both terminology and content. Al-Kindī also uses the language and Neoplatonic content of emanation when discussing the relation of the world to God. He writes: “The emanation of unity from the true First One is the coming-into-being of every sensible thing...” (*Treatise on First Philosophy*, same source, p. 164).

[^V4-160]: See, for example, al-Kindī’s *Treatise on First Philosophy* (same source, vol. 1, p. 116), and his *Treatise on the Nature of What Cannot Be Infinite and What May Be Said to Be Infinite* (same source, vol. 1, pp. 194–198).

Edited and introduced by ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī, Cairo, 1964.

[^V4-162]: Aristotle, *Physics* (6, 206a 14).

[^V4-163]: Al-Kindī, *Treatise on the Nature of What Cannot Be Infinite*, previously cited source, same pages.

[^V4-164]: “The body of the whole” means the world in the broadest sense.

[^V4-165]: This is the reading in Abū Rīda’s edition of al-Kindī’s *Treatise on First Philosophy*. In al-Ahwānī’s edition, the word “therefore” appears instead of “if.”

[^V4-166]: Al-Kindī, *Treatise on First Philosophy*, Abū Rīda ed., p. 16.

[^V4-167]: That is, time cannot be everlasting. The eternity of time, motion, and matter is, of course, one of the basic principles of Aristotle’s teaching.

[^V4-170]: Yaḥyā ibn ʿAdī (d. 364 AH/974 CE) was one of the best-known medieval Arab commentators on Aristotle. Ibn al-Nadīm says in *The Fihrist* (Egypt ed., p. 369) that Yaḥyā studied under Abū Naṣr al-Fārābī. Al-Qifṭī (*Accounts of the Sages*, Lippert ed., pp. 362–363) lists two works by him: *Commentary on a Section of Book Eight of Aristotle’s Physics* and *Treatise Showing That Nothing Existing Is Infinite in Number or Magnitude*.

[^V4-171]: Abū ʿAlī Ibn al-Samḥ (d. 418 AH/1027 CE) is mentioned by al-Qifṭī (*Accounts of the Sages*, pp. 411–412), who describes him as “highly accomplished in the art of logic” and says that he wrote “fine commentaries on Aristotle’s books, which became well known and whose influence could be seen among students.”

[^V4-172]: The Arabic word *ṣufr* here means copper.

[^V4-173]: That is, potentiality does not disappear, because not everything that exists potentially is brought into actuality.

[^V4-187]: Historians do not know al-Fārābī’s exact date of birth. Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa, however, considered 259 AH the most likely date (*Classes of Physicians*, vol. 2, p. 134). His reasoning was that Ibn Khallikān gives al-Fārābī’s death as 329 AH (*Deaths of Eminent Men*, vol. 2, p. 100 ff., Būlāq ed., 1899), and al-Fārābī is said to have lived for about eighty years. His birth would therefore fall in 259 AH. See also al-Qifṭī, *Accounts of the Sages*, p. 182 ff.

[^V4-188]: See ʿUthmān Amīn, introduction to al-Fārābī’s *Enumeration of the Sciences*, Cairo, 1968, 3rd ed., p. 38.

[^V4-189]: Ibrāhīm Madkūr, *On Islamic Philosophy*, 2nd ed., p. 35.

[^V4-190]: ʿUthmān Amīn, previously cited source, note 1, quoting O’Leary, *Arabic Thought*, etc., London, 1939, p. 55.

[^V4-191]: Calling al-Fārābī an Arab philosopher does not conflict with the fact that he was of Turkic—or, according to some historians, Persian—origin. As ʿUthmān Amīn rightly says, al-Fārābī “was an Arab philosopher in the totality of his culture and writings” (ʿUthmān Amīn, introduction to al-Fārābī’s *Enumeration of the Sciences*, 3rd ed., p. 38).

[^V4-192]: Al-Fārābī defines philosophy as “knowledge of existing things insofar as they exist” (*The Harmonization of the Opinions of the Two Sages*, Beirut, 2nd ed., ed. Albert Naṣrī Nādir, 1968, p. 80; and *The Pleasant Fruit: Selected Treatises of al-Fārābī*, Dieterici ed., Leiden, 1890, pp. 1–2). Al-Fārābī regards philosophy as the foremost of all sciences because it makes existing things intelligible through certain demonstrations (*Attainment of Happiness*, Hyderabad ed., 1345 AH, p. 36).

[^V4-193]: The ideological character of philosophy—of every philosophy—is an objective fact. It does not depend on whether the philosopher is aware of it, nor on whether we recognize it in one philosopher or another. Like every other form of human consciousness, philosophy has no history separate from the history of real people living under particular economic and social conditions. In every society and every age, philosophy therefore presents a system of ideas about the world in which the character of the prevailing social relations and the ideological struggle within those relations are indirectly reflected. Through these ideas, philosophy also defines each philosopher’s position within that struggle.

[^V4-194]: Medieval Arab philosophers knew the work called *Theology* as *The Theology of Aristotle* or *Aristotle’s Book of Divinity*. The mistaken attribution to Aristotle may have arisen because the work, whose author was then unknown, reached them together with explanations by Porphyry of Tyre, whom they knew as a commentator on Aristotle. They therefore assumed that the work itself was by Aristotle. ʿAbd al-Masīḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Nāʿima al-Ḥimṣī (d. 220 AH/835 CE) translated it into Arabic, and al-Kindī revised the translation. Dieterici first published it in Berlin in 1882, and it was translated into Latin in 1519. In reality, the work is drawn from the Fourth and Sixth *Enneads* of Plotinus. See ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī’s edition of the Fourth *Ennead*, *On the Soul*, Cairo, 1970, pp. 156–157.

[^V4-195]: The Arabic historical sources that record the translation movement under the early and later Abbasids all testify to this. Examples include Ibn al-Nadīm’s *Fihrist* (pp. 263, 348, 411), al-Qifṭī’s *Accounts of the Scholars* (pp. 118, 122, 130, 266, and elsewhere), and Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa’s *Classes of Physicians* (vol. 1, p. 235; vol. 2, p. 335, and elsewhere). Modern historians and researchers—Arab, Eastern, and Orientalist alike—have likewise reached the conclusion, through their studies and textual research, that this historical fact is sound: the Arab philosophers knew Aristotle’s philosophy.

[^V4-196]: Al-Kindī’s *Treatise on the Number of Aristotle’s Books and What Is Needed to Attain Philosophy* (*Al-Kindī’s Philosophical Treatises*, vol. 1, pp. 363–384, Abū Rīda ed.). It is striking that al-Kindī does not mention the *Book of Divinity* (*Theology*) attributed to Aristotle in this treatise, even though he is known to have revised ʿAbd al-Masīḥ ibn Nāʿima al-Ḥimṣī’s translation of that work. See ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī, *Plotinus among the Arabs*, Cairo, 1955, p. 3.

[^V4-197]: Albert Naṣrī Nādir, introduction to *The Harmonization of the Opinions of the Two Sages*, Beirut, 1970, 2nd ed., p. 76.

[^V4-198]: Jamīl Ṣalībā, *From Plato to Ibn Sīnā*, Damascus, 1951, 4th ed., p. 57.

[^V4-199]: Jamīl Ṣalībā, previously cited source.

[^V4-200]: Al-Fārābī, *The Harmonization of the Opinions of the Two Sages*, previously cited edition, p. 80.

[^V4-201]: In *The Opinions of the People of the Virtuous City* (Beirut ed., ed. Albert Naṣrī Nādir, p. 46), al-Fārābī says of the beings beneath the sphere of the moon—that is, material beings—that “some are natural, some are voluntary, and some are composed of both the natural and the voluntary. The natural prepares the way for the voluntary and exists before it in time; the voluntary cannot exist unless the natural exists first.” This is a very clear statement that being comes before consciousness—in other words, that material existence is objective.

[^V4-202]: Al-Fārābī, *The Opinions of the People of the Virtuous City*, same edition, pp. 38–39.

[^V4-203]: Al-Fārābī contradicts himself on this point. His entire philosophical system is built on emanation, which necessarily rules out creation from nothing and implies the eternity of the world; this is also what the meaning of his philosophical texts themselves requires. Yet in *The Harmonization of the Opinions of the Two Sages* (same edition, pp. 100–104), he says that God brought the world into existence from nothing (pp. 101, 103, 104), and that the world came into being. He even calls the belief that Aristotle held the world to be eternal “an ugly and objectionable supposition” (p. 100). How should we understand this contradiction, and which of al-Fārābī’s two positions should we accept? In our view, the contradiction is apparent rather than real. It was a means of self-protection—a method Arab-Islamic philosophers of the period allowed themselves to use to an unreasonable degree. Their real views are those embodied in their philosophical theories, not the statements they make outside those theories. Otherwise their entire philosophical systems would become meaningless.

[^V4-204]: Previously cited source, pp. 93–95.

[^V4-205]: Same source, p. 83.

[^V4-206]: Ibid., p. 74. It should be noted that al-Fārābī understands the relation between matter and form in existing things in the Aristotelian way. When a particular form belongs to a particular matter, the form is not always absolutely form, nor is the matter always absolutely matter. A form can be form in relation to what lies below it and matter in relation to what lies above it. In the same way, matter can be matter in relation to what lies above it and form in relation to what lies below it. In the present case, al-Fārābī says that the imaginative faculty is matter in relation to what stands above it, namely the rational faculty. The rational faculty, in turn, is form in relation to the imaginative faculty because it stands above it. The rational faculty remains form, since no faculty of the soul stands above it. This is what he means when he says: “It is the form of every form that comes before it.” Elsewhere he says that the rational faculty governs the imaginative faculty and the chief sensory faculty—that is, the other faculties of the soul (p. 72). In another passage (p. 70), he says that the rational faculty is what allows us to grasp intelligible things, distinguish ugly from beautiful, and acquire crafts and sciences.

[^V4-207]: Ibid., p. 84.

[^V4-208]: Ibid., p. 85.

[^V4-209]: Ibid., p. 88.

[^V4-210]: Same edition, pp. 103–104.

[^V4-211]: By “intelligibles,” al-Fārābī means “things whose nature is to be represented in the rational faculty. Some intelligibles are, in their own substances, actual intellects and actual intelligibles: these are things free of matter. Others are not in their own substances actually intelligible, such as stones and plants, and in general everything that is a body or is in a material body” (ibid., p. 82).

[^V4-212]: In the theory of emanation, the active intellect is the last of the ten intellects and the point at which emanation ends. Ontologically, it governs the world of earthly bodies. From the standpoint of knowledge, the active intellect “is a being whose substance is an actual intellect and is separate from matter (...) [and] gives the material intellect, which is intellect only potentially, something like the light that the sun gives to sight.” In other words, it is “what potential intelligibles need in order to move from potentiality to actuality” (same source, p. 83).

[^V4-213]: Al-Fārābī divides the rational faculty into theoretical and practical parts. “The practical was made to serve the theoretical, while the theoretical serves nothing else, but exists so that happiness may be reached through it. When happiness is known by the theoretical faculty, set up as a goal, desired by the appetitive faculty, and the deliberative faculty [thought] works out what should be done (...), and those actions are then carried out through the main powers of the appetitive faculty, all human actions become good and beautiful” (same source, pp. 86–87).

[^V4-214]: Same source, pp. 103–104.

[^V4-215]: Many Qurʾānic verses say this. Among them are: “Your Lord surely knows what their hearts conceal and what they reveal” (Qurʾān 27:74); “Not even the weight of an atom in the heavens or the earth escapes Him” (34:3); “Not a leaf falls without His knowing it” (6:59); and “There is no private conversation among three people without Him being their fourth” (58:7), and so on.

[^V4-216]: We should point out that al-Fārābī and the other Arab philosophers who represent the rationalist current, like the Muslim theologians and Sufis, all begin from the principle of God’s oneness understood in terms of absolute transcendence. This is the fixed starting point they all share. Their differences lie in how they deal with the contradiction that arises between absolute transcendence and the ideological positions that force them to seek some connection between God and the world. In our view, this effort expressed their opposition to the theoretical foundation of the official ideology: the absolute separation of God from the world, which in reality reflected the principle of absolute separation between one human being and another—that is, between ruler and ruled. As we saw earlier, the Sufis establish a direct link between the world and God through unveiling and direct vision. The rationalist philosophers, by contrast, establish this link through intermediaries. The theory of emanation gave them the basis for these intermediaries—the separate intellects.

[^V4-217]: *The Opinions of the People of the Virtuous City*, previously cited edition, pp. 30–31.

[^V4-218]: Ibid., p. 31.

[^V4-219]: Shaykh Ṣubḥī al-Ṣāliḥ, *Islamic Institutions: Their Origins and Development*, Beirut, 1965, p. 184.

[^V4-220]: Shaykh Muḥammad Jawād Mughniyya, *Landmarks of Islamic Philosophy*, 2nd ed., 1973, p. 69.

[^V4-221]: See *The Opinions of the People of the Virtuous City*, same edition, p. 14.

[^V4-222]: See note 1 on p. 129.

[^V4-223]: *The Virtuous City*, p. 46.

[^V4-224]: He means the common nature shared by the celestial bodies—“heavenly bodies” in his terminology.

[^V4-225]: He means the first matter common to the whole natural world. Al-Fārābī’s insistence on a “common nature” shared by the celestial bodies, on a common first matter, and on an existential relation between the two points toward a view of the world as a unity. His texts on this subject suggest that he tends to ground this unity materially. In none of these passages does he refer to “essence.” Instead, he repeatedly speaks of a common “matter” passing through the celestial world in a “process” that produces the “matter” of the natural world. This matter is itself common and moves on its own within natural things through another “process” that raises them gradually from lower to higher forms “until each species reaches the highest perfection of its substance.”

[^V4-226]: Although the Aristotelian account of the relation between “form and matter” is idealist because it separates matter’s form from matter itself, its content in al-Fārābī comes closer to materialism. He describes the relation in a way that makes it appear as an internal process within the movement of matter itself.

[^V4-227]: One might ask: how can this relation work independently of every external factor when al-Fārābī ultimately traces the existence of natural things back to the celestial bodies? Our answer has two parts. First, the text we are discussing explicitly says that natural motion in existing things operates according to its own internal laws and is genuinely independent of any external factor. Second, all of al-Fārābī’s texts on the celestial bodies and the emergence of natural beings from them indicate that matter exists throughout the celestial world, that a moving “process” takes place within this celestial world—which is itself bodily—and that this process passes through stages on its way toward natural existence in our earthly world. In the chain of emanation, the first intellect necessarily brings with it the existence of the first heaven, which is material. Each following intellect in turn entails the existence of another heaven, or celestial body, down to the sphere of the moon. We also find that, in al-Fārābī’s system, the celestial bodies “are of the same kind as hylomorphic [material-natural] beings, in that they have subjects resembling the matters prepared to receive forms, and they have things like forms through which they become substances, with those things existing in those subjects” (*The Virtuous City*, pp. 52–53). There is therefore a material link between the celestial world and the natural world. If we also take into account the eternal character of the whole system of emanation, and al-Fārābī’s statement that the celestial bodies share with the ten intellects the fact that from the very beginning they were given the best state in which they could exist as substances, along with their quantities, shapes, and visible qualities (p. 54), we can conclude that matter is eternal. From eternity it has been moving within a continuous process of its own, passing from one stage to another until the natural world comes into existence, where the process continues in new forms. In other words, al-Fārābī does not speak of matter existing apart from nature, nor does he speak here of an essence. He speaks of real matter.

[^V4-228]: We have rendered al-Fārābī’s passage with some freedom while preserving its meaning completely. See *The Virtuous City*, p. 63.

[^V4-229]: To make al-Fārābī’s view clearer in his own terms, it is useful to quote him: “Since one and the same matter is shared by two opposites, and each opposite exists through it, while the matter itself is no more suited to one opposite than to the other, and cannot be made to belong to both at the same time, it must necessarily be given sometimes this opposite and sometimes that one, with the two taking turns (...). A single thing therefore cannot remain forever numerically one; instead, its permanence through time is made a permanence of species. For a species to remain one, individuals of that species must exist for a time, then perish and be replaced by other individuals of the same species. This continues in the same way without end” (*The Virtuous City*, pp. 63–64).

[^V4-230]: *The Virtuous City*, pp. 97–98.

[^V4-231]: Ibid., p. 98.

[^V4-232]: In classical usage, the term “crafts” (*ṣanāʾiʿ*) includes many kinds of occupation and many forms of social activity, even writing. When older authors speak of “people of the crafts,” they mean roughly what we would now call social groups or occupational categories.

[^V4-233]: The difference between the determinism of predestination and the determinism of class position is that the first cannot be changed. The second can change through the action of another class opposed to the ruling class, or when an individual changes his social position.

[^V4-234]: See *The Virtuous City*, pp. 96–97.

[^V4-235]: See ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz al-Dūrī, *Introduction to Arab Economic History*, Beirut, 1969, pp. 65–66, 71–72, and elsewhere.

[^V4-236]: *The Virtuous City*, p. 99.

[^V4-237]: Ibid., p. 100.

[^V4-238]: Ibid., same page.

[^V4-239]: Ibid.

[^V4-240]: Ibid., p. 101. We should recall what we noted earlier: in al-Fārābī’s terminology, the realization of “voluntary dispositions and acquired capacities” in a person is connected with that person’s chosen social activity. Indeed, as we saw, he treats them as equivalent to “crafts and things like them.” In ordinary language and in the terminology of al-Fārābī’s age, a “craft” means knowledge acquired through practicing an activity, such as sewing or weaving, as well as knowledge concerned with how an activity is carried out, such as logic (*al-Munjid*, entry “ṣ-n-ʿ”).

[^V4-241]: Ibid., p. 103. Al-Fārābī calls these natural qualifications “the receptive natural disposition that prepares [a human being] to become an actual intellect.”

[^V4-242]: Ibid., p. 102.

[^V4-243]: Ibid., p. 104.

[^V4-244]: De Boer, *History of Philosophy in Islam*, pp. 172, 175.

[^V4-245]: Ibrāhīm Madkūr, *On Islamic Philosophy*, pp. 36, 39. Henry Corbin agrees with Madkūr’s general reading of al-Fārābī and adds that “it is a mistake to measure what al-Fārābī says about the virtuous city by the modern meaning of the word politics. There is nothing there resembling what we today call a political program” (H. Corbin, *History of Islamic Philosophy*, Arabic ed., p. 245). He also says: “Whatever the virtuous city may be, it was not in itself al-Fārābī’s final aim. It was only a means of guiding people toward the path of absolute happiness” (same source, p. 251).

[^V4-246]: Madkūr, same previously cited source, p. 36.

[^V4-247]: Ibn Ṭufayl al-Andalusī, *Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān*, introduction by ʿAbd al-Hādī Ḥakīm, Dār al-Fārābī, Beirut, 1954, p. 8.

[^V4-248]: See al-Fārābī, *Attainment of Happiness*, Hyderabad, 1346 AH, p. 2.

[^V4-249]: See previously cited source, p. 23.

[^V4-250]: See same source, pp. 24–25.

[^V4-251]: Ibid., p. 31.

[^V4-252]: See *Directing Attention to the Path of Happiness*, Hyderabad, 1346 AH, p. 2.

[^V4-253]: Previously cited source, p. 8.

[^V4-254]: *The Virtuous City*, p. 85.

[^V4-255]: Previously cited source, pp. 85–86.

[^V4-256]: See *The Virtuous City*, p. 110, where al-Fārābī divides the “ignorant city” into several kinds of city, or society. One is what he calls the “city of domination,” whose people “aim to overpower others and refuse to be overpowered themselves, and whose entire effort is directed only toward the pleasure they gain from victory.” On p. 126 he describes the supposedly “natural” basis of this doctrine. The description plainly reflects political and class conflict in al-Fārābī’s own society rather than something taken from outside his historical setting. On p. 141 we again find something like a doctrine of force among one group in the communal ignorant city. Al-Fārābī describes them as “corrupt in soul because they regard overpowering others as the good.”

[^V4-257]: Previously cited source, pp. 129–131.

[^V4-258]: Ibid., pp. 32–34.

[^V4-259]: Ibid., pp. 142–144.

[^V4-260]: Ibid., pp. 110–111. This passage clearly relates to the rulers of Arab-Islamic landed domains and provinces that enjoyed relative independence from the center of the caliphate in the fourth century AH, al-Fārābī’s own period.

[^V4-261]: Ibid., p. 112.

[^V4-262]: Ibid., p. 116.

[^V4-263]: *The Harmonization of the Opinions of the Two Sages*, Nādir 2nd ed., Beirut, 1968, p. 103.

[^V4-264]: Al-Fārābī, *Selected Questions*, Cairo, 1325 AH, in the collection *Treatises of al-Fārābī*, p. 6.

[^V4-265]: See notes 1 and 2 on p. 132 of this chapter.

[^V4-266]: In note 16 on p. 125 of this chapter, we said that al-Fārābī’s contradictions on these sensitive questions—like those of other Arab philosophers—were, in our view, a means of self-defense against the intellectual and physical terror exercised in his age by the enemies of philosophy, the defenders of the official ideology. But it was a method used to an excessive degree and is difficult to justify.

[^V4-267]: Ibn Ṭufayl, *Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān*, introduction by ʿAbd al-Hādī Ḥakīm, Dār al-Fārābī ed., p. 8; and pp. 57–58 of the Dār al-Maʿārif edition, Egypt, 1966, edited and annotated by Aḥmad Amīn. Later Arab scholars and Orientalists knew this statement by Ibn Sīnā only through Ibn Ṭufayl until Ibn Sīnā’s own text finally became available. It appears at the beginning of the logic section of *The Healing*, where Ibn Sīnā says: “I have another book besides these two [meaning *The Healing* and *The Appendices*] in which I presented philosophy as it naturally is and as demanded by a frank judgment that does not defer to our fellow practitioners, nor fear breaking with them in the way one must in other works. This is my book on Eastern Philosophy. As for this book [meaning *The Healing*], it is more expansive and more accommodating toward our fellow Peripatetics. Whoever wants the truth without hesitation should seek that other book. Whoever wants the truth presented in a way that gives some consideration to our colleagues, with fuller explanation and hints which, if properly understood, make the other book unnecessary, should use this book [that is, *The Healing*].” See ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī, *The Greek Heritage in Islamic Civilization*, 3rd ed., p. 277, quoting a copy in the Cairo University Library, nos. 26050–26053, itself taken from a British Museum manuscript, fol. 3b, lines 3–6.

[^V4-268]: A work by Ibn Sīnā entitled *The Logic of the Easterners* was printed in Cairo in 1328 AH/1910 by M. Ṣ. al-Kurdī. The book *Eastern Philosophy*, which Ibn Sīnā mentions in the introduction to *The Healing*—see the previous note—has never been found. It is likely that Ibn Sīnā wrote only its logic section, and that the work that reached us and was printed as *The Logic of the Easterners* is the part of *Eastern Philosophy* that he actually completed. One piece of evidence for this is Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa’s *Sources of Information*, vol. 2, p. 19, A. Müller ed., which lists among Ibn Sīnā’s works: “The Book of Eastern Wisdom: not found complete.” See Badawī, previously cited source, p. 249.

[^V4-269]: See the previous two notes. In this introduction Ibn Sīnā also says: “We have now been moved to collect a discussion of the questions over which investigators have disagreed. In doing so, we will pay no attention to partisan loyalty, desire, habit, or familiarity. Nor will we care if we openly depart from what students of Greek books have become accustomed to through heedlessness and lack of understanding, or from what they have heard from us in books we wrote for ordinary students of philosophy who are infatuated with the Peripatetics and imagine that God has guided no one but them and extended His mercy to no one else.”

[^V4-270]: *Pointers and Reminders*, Cairo, 1947, edited and annotated by Sulaymān Dunyā.

[^V4-271]: Al-Ghazālī “justified” this method by saying that “since people differ in their capacities and understanding, the same intellectual food is not suitable for everyone (...). If wisdom is fed to people suited only to preaching, it harms them just as feeding poultry meat harms a nursing infant. And if disputation [meaning non-scientific argument] is used with people of wisdom [philosophy], they recoil from it just as a strong man recoils from being suckled on human milk” (al-Ghazālī, *The Just Balance*, p. 16).

[^V4-272]: Aristotelian demonstration has a scientific character. Aristotle, however, does not explain the scientific or certain character of a demonstration by its conformity to formal syllogistic rules. He grounds it instead in whether the causal structure contained in the demonstration corresponds to reality. He therefore holds that each kind of being requires its own form of demonstration, suited to that kind of being, because demonstration always has an ontological side. Each type of existence, in his view, opens a particular path by which the researcher can discover what that type of existence contains.

[^V4-273]: Aristotle, *Posterior Analytics* (1.3, 72b 20).

[^V4-274]: Aristotle, previously cited source (1.10, 76a 37; 1.11, 77a 26). To say that axioms are common to all sciences means that each science uses them to establish its own particular subject matter while the axioms themselves remain general. Aristotle gives the example of the axiom that if equal quantities are subtracted from equal quantities, the remainders are equal. The axiom is general in itself, but arithmetic applies it to numbers, geometry to magnitudes, and so on (same source, 76a 41).

[^V4-275]: Same source, 100b 4–5.

[^V4-276]: See *The Virtuous City*, pp. 82–83; *The Harmonization of the Opinions of the Two Sages*, pp. 98–99; and *Enumeration of the Sciences*, pp. 67–68 and pp. 69, 75, 78 on logic, p. 14 ff. on natural science, and p. 120 ff. on divine science.

[^V4-277]: *The Virtuous City*, p. 83.

[^V4-278]: Ibid., p. 82.

[^V4-279]: *The Harmonization of the Opinions of the Two Sages*, pp. 98–99.

[^V4-280]: By the universals that arise without deliberate intention, al-Fārābī means the “first intelligibles” themselves—the first principles of demonstration.

[^V4-281]: He means the majority of philosophers.

[^V4-282]: That is, they do not concern themselves with giving it a name.

[^V4-283]: He means logicians.

[^V4-284]: It is worth noting that al-Fārābī’s explanation of how we come to know the “first principles of demonstration,” including the principles known as “axioms,” comes close to Lenin’s explanation: “Human practical activity had to lead human consciousness to repeat the various logical forms billions of times before these forms could acquire the significance of axioms” (*Notebooks on Dialectics*, trans. Marqus, Beirut, 1971, p. 213).

[^V4-285]: On the basis of this conclusion, al-Fārābī is ahead of Descartes on the question of knowledge, especially our knowledge of first rational principles, or axioms. Descartes traces our knowledge of axioms back to reason itself. For him, knowledge begins with intuition: the mind recognizes axioms that, he says, are already present in the human soul. They do not arise from repeated experience of the external world. God places them in the human mind at birth, and reason later grasps them by itself without sensory knowledge.

[^V4-286]: *The Harmonization of the Opinions of the Two Sages*, p. 99.

[^V4-287]: See, for example, Henry Corbin, *History of Islamic Philosophy*, pp. 245–246; and Hādī al-ʿAlawī, *The Theory of Substantial Motion in al-Shīrāzī*, p. 57. Al-ʿAlawī quotes Hādī al-Sabzawārī (*Manẓūma*, Iran ed., p. 249), who says that the leading Peripatetics accepted that existence is what is produced: “that is, the effect of the cause is existence, not essence.”

[^V4-288]: We should again recall Lenin’s statement that the one property of matter whose acceptance defines philosophical materialism is that matter is objective reality—that it exists outside our consciousness (*Materialism and Empirio-Criticism*, Arabic ed., Damascus, 1971, p. 260).

[^V4-289]: It should be remembered that by “Peripatetics,” al-Sabzawārī means the Arab-Islamic philosophers, including al-Fārābī. We do not, however, agree with the common practice of calling the Arab philosophers “Peripatetics” without qualification. They were not all equally close to Aristotle’s philosophy. Even those most closely connected with it, such as Ibn Rushd, did not agree with Aristotle in all or even most of their views. Al-Fārābī departs from Peripatetic philosophy more than any of them. And how can they simply be called “Peripatetics” when their connection with Neoplatonism is so obvious?

[^V4-290]: *Enumeration of the Sciences*, ʿUthmān Amīn 3rd ed., 1968, pp. 111–115.

[^V4-291]: The way of knowing that al-Fārābī describes here also appears in statements such as “universals [essences] are obtained from particulars” and “universals are, in truth, experiences.” This approach can be seen as an example of Lenin’s statement that the “essence of things” or their “substance” expresses nothing more than human knowledge of things—a knowledge that continuously deepens—and of his statement that essences are relative rather than fixed properties of things (Lenin, *Materialism and Empirio-Criticism*, Arabic ed., trans. Munīr Mashāyiḵ, Damascus, 1971, p. 263).

[^V4-292]: Al-Fārābī’s understanding of the relation between sensory and rational knowledge, as we have explained it, can be seen as an early form of understanding the dialectical relation between essence and appearance. As we see it, he was able, even if spontaneously, to approach the dialectical-materialist proposition that the senses reflect external appearances and particulars in sensory images, while the essence of phenomena cannot be grasped by the senses alone. The internal unity between a phenomenon and its essence is what makes the transition from sensory to rational knowledge possible.

[^V4-293]: His name was al-Ḥusayn, his *kunya* was Abū ʿAlī, and in Arabic philosophical literature he is known by the title “the Supreme Master” (*al-Shaykh al-Raʾīs*). He was born in 370 AH/980 CE in the village of Afshana near Bukhara. He spent his early childhood in the town of Kharmaythan, also in the Bukhara region, where his father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sīnā, served as administrator on behalf of the Samanid ruler Nūḥ ibn Manṣūr. In Bukhara itself, Ibn Sīnā studied with private tutors in his father’s home and later continued learning through his own efforts. He tells us this in the autobiography that he partly dictated to his student Abū ʿUbayd al-Jūzjānī, who completed the account after his teacher’s death. The biography is reproduced by al-Qifṭī in *Informing the Scholars of the Lives of the Sages*, p. 269, and by Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa in *Classes of Physicians*, vol. 2, p. 2, where Ibn Abī Uṣaybiʿa gives Ibn Sīnā’s birth year as 375 AH. Ibn Sīnā died in Hamadan, Iran, in 428 AH/1036 CE.

[^V4-294]: In Ibn Sīnā’s time, the Arab caliphate had reached an advanced stage of division and fragmentation. During the fifty-eight years of his life, the “Supreme Master” lived while three Islamic caliphates existed at the same time: the Abbasid caliphate in the East (132–656 AH/750–1258 CE), the Fatimid caliphate in Egypt and North Africa (297–567 AH/909–1171 CE), and the Umayyad caliphate in al-Andalus (300–407 AH/912–1016 CE). Alongside these caliphates were several Islamic states linked only formally to the Abbasid caliphate: the Samanid state, with its capital at Bukhara (261–389 AH/874–998 CE); the Buyid state in Fars, Rayy, Hamadan, Isfahan, and Iraq (320–447 AH/932–1055 CE); and the Ghaznavid state, with its capital at Ghazna in Afghanistan (351–582 AH/962–1186 CE). There were also the small *ṭāʾifa* states in the Maghreb and breakaway principalities within these larger states, such as the principality of ʿAlāʾ al-Dīn ibn Kākawayh, a former Buyid military commander who seized Hamadan and Isfahan in 414 AH/1023 CE. Ibn Sīnā also lived through the final period of the Hamdanid emirate of Aleppo (333–394 AH/944–1002 CE).

[^V4-295]: Latin translators of his works knew him as Rhases. See ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī, *The Spirit of Arab Civilization*, p. 115.

[^V4-296]: Professor N. Bammate says of Abū Bakr al-Rāzī that in chemistry he gives an abundance of technical recipes and was undoubtedly the greatest Muslim theoretician in the field. He also excelled in the analysis of particular diseases, as in his classic work on measles and smallpox, which continued to be printed in the West until the middle of the eighteenth century. See *The Philosophy and Cultures of East and West*, University of Hawaii, Honolulu, 1962, pp. 172–187.

[^V4-297]: See al-Bīrūnī, Abū al-Rayḥān, *Critical Study of What India Says*, Leipzig, 1925, p. 163; and *Philosophical Treatises of Abū Bakr Muḥammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī*, Paul Kraus ed., Cairo, 1939, p. 195 ff.

In this book Nāṣir-i Khusraw included selections from Abū Bakr al-Rāzī’s treatise on pleasure, selections from his book on matter, and comments on al-Rāzī’s views of space and time. See Kraus, *Philosophical Treatises of al-Rāzī*, pp. 148, 172, 252 ff.

He says that Abū Ḥātim entered the land of Daylam and won over a group of Daylamites, among them Asfār ibn Shīrawayh.

[^V4-303]: The Orientalist Kraus published the texts of this document in *Philosophical Treatises of Abū Bakr Muḥammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī*, pp. 295–313. It should be noted that Abū Ḥātim does not explicitly name Abū Bakr al-Rāzī in this document. He refers to him only as “the heretic.” Another Ismāʿīlī, Ḥamīd al-Kirmānī, reveals in a work called *The Golden Sayings* that the “heretic” in Abū Ḥātim’s debates was al-Rāzī. See Kraus, previously cited source, p. 292.

[^V4-304]: Previously cited source, pp. 7–9.

[^V4-305]: Kraus gives al-Kirmānī’s death as 411 AH/1020 CE.

[^V4-306]: Kraus published this document in the same source, pp. 313–316.

[^V4-309]: In Ismāʿīlī doctrine, the concept of the Imamate is linked to the concept of prophecy.

[^V4-310]: Kraus gives this date in the same source, p. 8, quoting Ivanow, the editor of this work by Idrīs, p. 62. It appears to be a Hijri date.

[^V4-311]: Same source, p. 8.

[^V4-314]: Ibid., p. 221.

[^V4-322]: De Boer, *History of Philosophy in Islam*, p. 117.

[^V4-323]: In reconstructing these ideas of Abū Bakr al-Rāzī, we have relied on what Kraus quotes (previously cited source, pp. 203–206) from Fakhr al-Dīn Muḥammad ibn ʿUmar al-Rāzī’s *Summary of the Ideas of Earlier and Later Scholars, Philosophers, and Theologians* (d. 606 AH), Egyptian Ḥaydariyya ed., 1323 AH, pp. 85–86. Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī attributes these views to the people of Ḥarrān—that is, the Ḥarrānian Sabians—and does not name Abū Bakr al-Rāzī. But Najm al-Dīn ʿAlī ibn ʿUmar al-Qazwīnī al-Kātibī (d. 685 or 693 AH), who wrote a commentary on Fakhr al-Dīn’s *Summary* (see Kraus, p. 202), says in explaining this passage that the Ḥarrānian doctrine described here was the position chosen by Muḥammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī. Al-Bīrūnī says something similar in *Critical Study of What India Says*, p. 163, and attributes it to Abū Bakr al-Rāzī (see Kraus, same source, p. 195). A similar account also appears in Abū ʿAlī Aḥmad ibn Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥasan al-Marzūqī al-Iṣfahānī (d. 421 AH), *Times and Places*, Hyderabad ed., 1332 AH, vol. 1, pp. 143–152. He attributes it to “the fourth group, whose leader is Muḥammad ibn Zakariyyā the physician,” one of the groups holding that “there is more than one eternal principle.” See also Kraus, same source, pp. 196–197. Kraus considers it likely that the doctrine of the five eternal principles was invented by Abū Bakr al-Rāzī himself, and that al-Rāzī enhanced the standing of his own philosophy by attributing it to the Ḥarrānians. Kraus bases this on the fact that no source before al-Rāzī attributes the doctrine of the five eternal principles to the Ḥarrānians (same source, p. 192).

[^V4-324]: Summary from Nāṣir-i Khusraw, *Provisions for Travelers*, Kāwiyānī ed., 1341 AH, pp. 96–108. See Kraus, same source, pp. 252–259.

[^V4-325]: See Kraus, same source, pp. 254–255.

[^V4-326]: Same source, same page.

[^V4-327]: Same source, pp. 266–267.

[^V4-328]: See same source, p. 270. Other later Islamic philosophers already mentioned—note 31 of this chapter—also report Abū Bakr al-Rāzī’s doctrine of the eternity of space and time in roughly the same form as Nāṣir-i Khusraw. They include al-Marzūqī, Ibn Ḥazm, al-Kātibī, and Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī. See Kraus, same source, pp. 241–242. Abū Bakr al-Rāzī himself says in *The Philosophical Life* that he wrote a treatise “On Time, Space, Duration, Eternity, and the Void.” Like so many of his other works, this treatise has not reached us. Everything we say about his philosophical doctrine therefore comes through authors who present it briefly and in fragments. We do not know how much their abbreviated and piecemeal reports may have altered its real substance.

[^V4-329]: See al-Qifṭī, *Accounts of the Sages*, p. 271.

[^V4-330]: In medieval Arabic philosophical literature and histories of Arabic philosophy, “the ancients” means the ancient Greek philosophers. See al-Shahrastānī, *Religions and Sects*, vol. 2, p. 94.

[^V4-331]: Ibn Sīnā is not known to have written in any language other than Arabic except for a treatise on the principles of the sciences dedicated to ʿAlāʾ al-Dawla ibn Kākawayh, who seized Hamadan and Isfahan in 414 AH under the Buyids and for whom Ibn Sīnā later served as vizier, and a second short treatise on “pulses.” Both were written in Persian. He also wrote a *Treatise on the Philosophy of Death* for his brother, of which a Persian copy exists in the British Museum. See Carra de Vaux, *Avicenna*, trans. ʿĀdil Zuʿaytir, p. 149. As for his poetry, no biographer or cataloguer of his works says that he composed anything in Persian beyond a few poems and quatrains totaling no more than sixty-five verses. All the rest of his poetry is in Arabic. See Aḥmad Fuʾād al-Ahwānī, *Ibn Sīnā*, Cairo, no date, p. 38.

[^V4-332]: While dictating the story of his early life to his student al-Jūzjānī, Ibn Sīnā said: “My father was one of those who answered the call of the Egyptians [the Fatimids] and was counted among the Ismāʿīlīs, as was my brother. The two of them would sometimes discuss these matters with each other while I listened and understood what they were saying, and they began to invite me to it as well....” See al-Qifṭī, *Accounts of the Sages*, p. 269.

[^V4-333]: Ibn Sīnā served twice as vizier to the Buyid ruler Shams al-Dawla in Hamadan. Between the two periods, the troops rebelled against him, surrounded his house, arrested him, and demanded that the ruler have him killed because they feared he would punish them for their misconduct. Ibn Sīnā went into hiding for forty days. Shams al-Dawla then called him back to treat him medically and restored him to the vizierate. See previously cited source. It is also said that ʿAlāʾ al-Dīn ibn Kākawayh, ruler of Isfahan, made him his vizier. See al-Ahwānī, *Ibn Sīnā*, p. 10.

[^V4-334]: He is said to have written *The Salvation* while traveling with ʿAlāʾ al-Dawla to Sābūrkhwāst. See Carra de Vaux, *Avicenna*, p. 140.

[^V4-335]: Al-Jūzjānī says in his biography of Ibn Sīnā: “Teaching took place at night because during the day he had no free time from serving the ruler.” Al-Qifṭī, p. 271.

[^V4-336]: Al-Jūzjānī says: “During this period”—that is, during Ibn Sīnā’s service as vizier to Shams al-Dawla—“students of learning gathered every night in the house of the Supreme Master. After the reading was finished, singers of different kinds would arrive and the drinking session would begin....” Al-Qifṭī, previously cited source.

[^V4-337]: Al-Jūzjānī also says: “After Shams al-Dawla died, his son was recognized as ruler, but the Shaykh refused to become his vizier and secretly asked ʿAlāʾ al-Dawla to take him into his service. When the ruler learned of this, he imprisoned him in the fortress of Fardajān, where he remained for four months.” Same source. Henry Corbin says that Ibn Sīnā wrote his *Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān* during this period of imprisonment in the fortress (p. 257).

[^V4-338]: Al-Qifṭī, previously cited source.

[^V4-339]: Al-Qifṭī, previously cited source.

[^V4-340]: Al-Ahwānī, p. 9.

[^V4-341]: See al-Ahwānī, same page.

[^V4-342]: De Boer, p. 247, Arabic ed.

[^V4-343]: See *The Salvation*, pp. 284–285.

[^V4-344]: Ibn Sīnā, *The Adḥawiyya Treatise on the Afterlife*, Cairo, 1949, ed. Sulaymān Dunyā, p. 112.

[^V4-345]: Carra de Vaux, *Avicenna*, p. 133.

[^V4-346]: Ibn Sīnā says in his autobiography: “My father then began sending me to a man who sold vegetables and knew Indian arithmetic, so that I could learn from him.” See al-Qifṭī, p. 269.

[^V4-347]: Ibn Sīnā’s social views appear in the final chapters of the tenth treatise in the metaphysics section of *The Healing*.

[^V4-348]: See Muḥammad Yūsuf Mūsā, *The Social and Political Aspect of Ibn Sīnā’s Philosophy*, Publications of the French Institute of Oriental Archaeology in Cairo, 1952, p. 17.

[^V4-349]: Ibn Sīnā says in his autobiography: “The ruler of Bukhara at that time was Nūḥ ibn Manṣūr. He fell ill with a disease that baffled the physicians. By then my name had become known among them because of the intensity of my study. They mentioned me to him and asked that I be brought in. I came and joined them in treating him, and I entered his service. One day I asked him for permission to enter their library...” and so on. Al-Qifṭī, previously cited source.

[^V4-350]: The words in parentheses were added by us for clarity.

[^V4-351]: *Tafsira* means the flask containing a patient’s urine that was shown to the physician.

[^V4-352]: Ẓahīr al-Dīn al-Bayhaqī, *History of the Sages of Islam*, ed. Muḥammad Kurd ʿAlī, Damascus, 1946, p. 71.

[^V4-353]: In his biography of Ibn Sīnā, Abū ʿUbayd al-Jūzjānī says that Ibn Sīnā’s connection with “the Lady,” the mother of Majd al-Dawla, began because her son suffered from melancholy, and Ibn Sīnā treated him. Shams al-Dawla later called on Ibn Sīnā to treat him for colic, described here as an ulcerative disease of the stomach. Ibn Sīnā’s return to the vizierate under Shams al-Dawla was also connected with this illness. It returned after Ibn Sīnā had gone into hiding following the soldiers’ rebellion against him, forcing Shams al-Dawla to summon him for treatment. Shams al-Dawla apologized to him and then restored him to the vizierate. See al-Qifṭī, previously cited source.

[^V4-354]: We have decisive evidence for this in Ibn Sīnā’s own account of his life: “I attended to the sick, and doors of treatment based on experience opened to me beyond description.” His student al-Jūzjānī continues: “The Shaykh accumulated many experiences from the treatments he personally carried out, and intended to record them in *The Canon*. He had written them down on separate sheets, but they were lost before *The Canon* was completed.” Al-Jūzjānī then gives us a concrete example of Ibn Sīnā’s experimental method: “One day he developed a headache. He ordered a large quantity of ice to be brought, crushed, wrapped in a cloth, and placed over his head. This was done until the area was strengthened and could no longer receive the offending matter, and he recovered. In another case, a woman with tuberculosis in Khwarazm was ordered to take no medicine except sugared *julanjubīn* [a paste made from roses and honey] until, over time, she had consumed one hundred *mann*”—an old measure here estimated at about 25 kilograms—“and the woman recovered.” See al-Qifṭī, previously cited source.

[^V4-355]: Majid Fakhry, *Aristotle*, p. 35.

[^V4-356]: Among the best-known representatives of this doctrine in modern philosophy are Francis Bacon (1561–1626) and John Locke (1632–1704) among the materialists, and George Berkeley (1685–1753) and David Hume (1711–1776) among the idealists. In *Dialectics of Nature*, Engels says that Bacon’s empiricism became the starting point of modern metaphysics, which separates phenomena from one another without attending to the connections between them. Locke, for his part, recognized only the essences of individual things and rejected the idea of a general essence.

[^V4-357]: There is abundant evidence for this in Ibn Sīnā, and it shows that sensory knowledge must rely on reasoning, while reasoning must also rely on sensory knowledge. In *The Salvation* (p. 65), for example, he says that the senses grasp particulars, while rational thought reaches abstract universals. In the chapter on sensory data (*The Salvation*, p. 61), he says that matters established by experience—meaning sensory experience—are propositions to which the senses lead us with the help of reasoning. In defining induction, he says that it is “a judgment about a universal because that judgment is found in the particulars belonging to that universal, either in all of them, which is complete induction, or in most of them, which is the commonly used form of induction” (*The Salvation*, p. 58). See also *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 3, pp. 17–19, where Ibn Sīnā answers those who claim that only what can be sensed truly exists.

[^V4-358]: See *The Canon*, vol. 1, p. 115.

[^V4-359]: In Ibn Sīnā’s logical terminology, “accident,” “inseparable attribute,” and “concomitant” refer to conditions connected with the actual existence of things—that is, with the existence of the individual or particular thing.

[^V4-360]: In logical terminology, a “description” (*rasm*) defines a thing through the properties of its actual existence—its accidents, inseparable attributes, and concomitants.

[^V4-363]: See previously cited source, same page.

[^V4-364]: See *The Salvation*, pp. 3–4. See also ʿAlī Sāmī al-Nashshār, *Formal Logic*, Cairo, 1963, 2nd ed., pp. 20–21. It is useful here to state the position of dialectical materialism on logic as Lenin summarized it: “Logic is not the doctrine of the external forms of thought, but the doctrine of the laws governing the development of all material, natural, and spiritual things—that is, the development of the whole concrete content of the world and of our knowledge of it. In other words, it is the sum of the conclusions drawn from the history of human knowledge of the world.” Lenin, *Collected Works*, vol. 29, p. 84, Russian ed.

[^V4-365]: See *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 1, logic section, pp. 96–107.

[^V4-366]: *The Healing*, natural sciences section, *Generation and Corruption*, Cairo, Dār al-Kātib al-ʿArabī, ed. Maḥmūd Qāsim, p. 176.

[^V4-368]: *Treatise on the States of the Soul*, Cairo, 1952, ed. Aḥmad Fuʾād al-Ahwānī, 1st ed., p. 60.

[^V4-369]: See same source; *The Salvation*, p. 162; and *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 2, natural philosophy section, p. 351.

[^V4-370]: Ibn Sīnā uses this name in *States of the Soul*, p. 61, and in *The Salvation*, p. 163. In *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 2, p. 353, he writes it as *bifṭāsiyā*. The word is Greek in origin: *phantasia*. Aristotle used it in the sense of imagination and says that the word is derived from “light” (*phaos*). See Aristotle, *On the Soul*, Arabic ed., trans. al-Ahwānī, 2nd ed., Cairo, 1962, p. 107. Here Aristotle and Ibn Sīnā differ both in terminology and in substance. Aristotle uses *phantasia* for the imaginative faculty and treats it as the second stage in the process of thinking, with common sense as the first. Ibn Sīnā, by contrast, applies *phantasia* to common sense itself, even though he explains the word as meaning imagination. They also differ in how they explain common sense. Ibn Sīnā says that it is the faculty that receives all the forms impressed upon the senses. Aristotle says that common sense perceives motion, rest, shape, magnitude, number, and unity—things that no single sense perceives on its own. See same source, pp. 93–101.

[^V4-371]: *The Salvation*, p. 163, and *States of the Soul*, p. 61. In *Pointers and Reminders* (vol. 2, p. 353), Ibn Sīnā says that the organ of common sense is “the spirit poured into the beginnings of the sensory nerves.” In *The Salvation* and *States of the Soul* on the pages just cited, however, he says that this faculty is “located in the first cavity of the brain.”

[^V4-372]: *The Salvation*, p. 163; *States of the Soul*, p. 62.

[^V4-373]: The two previously cited sources.

[^V4-374]: Ibid., same pages.

[^V4-375]: Ibid. We should also note the bodily organs in the brain that Ibn Sīnā assigns to each of these powers of internal sensory perception. In *Pointers and Reminders* (vol. 2, pp. 353–355), he calls these bodily organs “instruments,” and sometimes refers to them as the “seat” of perception. In *The Salvation*, he simply gives the place “assigned” in the brain to each faculty. Common sense is “a faculty located in the first cavity of the brain”; the image-preserving faculty is “located at the back of the front cavity of the brain”; the imaginative faculty is “located in the middle cavity of the brain”; the estimative faculty is “located at the rear of the middle cavity of the brain”; and the retaining or memory faculty is “located in the rear cavity of the brain.” These locations naturally reflect the level of scientific knowledge of brain anatomy in the medieval Arab period. Philosophically, however, they also show an advanced tendency to connect the process of thought with the brain’s physical organs, which here points toward a materialist outlook. Modern science identifies the brain’s gray matter, especially the cerebral cortex, as one of the main physical organs involved in cognition.

[^V4-376]: The same two sources. It is notable that *The Salvation* and *States of the Soul* use the same wording here. Aḥmad Fuʾād al-Ahwānī, editor of *States of the Soul*, considers it likely that this treatise was drawn from passages in *The Salvation*, apart from a few chapters. See his introduction to *States of the Soul*, pp. 6–11.

[^V4-377]: The word *iṣṭilāḥiyya* appears in *States of the Soul* (p. 63), while *The Salvation* has *iṣlāḥiyya* (p. 164). The meaning of the latter is clear. The former may refer to views derived from practical experience and common convention.

[^V4-378]: *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 2, p. 361.

[^V4-379]: Ibn Sīnā also describes the appetitive faculty as a power that moves the body through desire toward one of two goals: either satisfying an appetite or repelling something harmful to the self and overcoming it. See *The Salvation*, p. 159.

[^V4-380]: *The Salvation*, p. 164.

[^V4-381]: Same source.

[^V4-382]: *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 2, p. 361.

[^V4-384]: *The Salvation*, p. 165.

[^V4-385]: One example is Ibn Sīnā’s statement: “You should know that people are often led by imagination to think that whatever exists must be something perceived by an outer or inner sense, such as something estimated or imagined [examples of inner sensation]; that if the senses cannot reach a thing in its own substance, it is impossible even to suppose that it exists; and that whatever does not occupy a place or position in itself, as a body does, or through what it inheres in, as the states of a body do, can have no share in existence.” He then gives a real example of something that exists but is not itself sensed: “human being” as a concept. There is no doubt, he says, that this concept applies in the same shared meaning to Zayd and ʿAmr—that is, to perceptible individuals—and that this shared meaning exists even though it is not itself perceptible. “Therefore the human being, insofar as it is one in truth—or rather insofar as it has its basic [specific] reality in which multiplicity makes no difference—is not sensible, but purely intelligible. The same is true of every universal.” *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 3, pp. 17–19.

[^V4-386]: Ibn Sīnā, *States of the Soul*, al-Ahwānī ed., p. 64.

[^V4-387]: For the outline and names of these stages, see *The Salvation*, pp. 165–166; *States of the Soul*, pp. 65–67; and *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 2, pp. 369–376.

[^V4-388]: *The Salvation*, p. 166; *States of the Soul*, p. 67.

[^V4-389]: This conclusion is supported by a chapter in *The Salvation* entitled “On the Assistance Given by the Animal Faculties to the Rational Soul,” pp. 182–183. We will discuss this chapter later.

[^V4-390]: This entire summary is taken from *The Salvation*, pp. 168–171. The same basic material appears in *States of the Soul*, ed. al-Ahwānī, pp. 70–73. In *Pointers and Reminders* (vol. 2, pp. 387–392), Ibn Sīnā presents it in another, more compressed form, as an introduction to the claim that the intelligible form in a human being is neither a body nor located in a body. We will discuss this idea later.

[^V4-391]: See *The Salvation*, “On the Assistance Given by the Animal Faculties to the Rational Soul,” pp. 182–183.

[^V4-392]: Ibn Sīnā uses the word “soul” here—the human rational soul—but in this passage he means the intellect.

[^V4-393]: This statement by Ibn Sīnā is further evidence that his experimental method is far removed from the implications of modern sensualist empiricism. Here he explicitly links sensory experience with logical reasoning in order to reach rational judgments. See pp. 202–203 and note 3 on p. 203.

[^V4-394]: Scammony is a medicinal plant used as a drug. In ancient medicine, “yellow bile” is one of the four bodily humors: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile.

[^V4-395]: *The Salvation*, p. 58.

[^V4-396]: We should quote the following passage because it clearly shows Ibn Sīnā’s method in his theory of universals. He says: “Universals, and the judgments and concepts that concern them, are not grasped by the senses or imagination, nor are they themselves objects of intuition or experience. But the senses and imagination assist the intellect. As for forming concepts: the senses present mixed things to the imagination, the imagination presents them to the intellect, and the intellect then separates, distinguishes, and divides them. It takes each meaning on its own and arranges what is more particular and more general, what belongs to the essence and what is accidental. In this way the first meanings needed for concepts become present in the intellect, and it then combines them into definitions. As for judgments, the senses and imagination may assist [the intellect] through experience or intuition, and they may assist it through induction.... They may also assist indirectly: first by helping to supply the concepts, after which the concepts are combined through affirmation and negation. The intellect then sees what must be accepted as self-evident, and it sees the syllogism by which other judgments must be acquired.” *The Salvation*, pp. 65–66. This passage gives a detailed account of how the intellect moves from sensory experience to the universal. It confirms that, in Ibn Sīnā’s theory of universals, the intellect depends on sensory experience. Intuition is mentioned here only incidentally. We will return to this later.

[^V4-397]: See al-Ahwānī’s introduction to *States of the Soul*, pp. 31–32; Ibrāhīm Madkūr, *On Islamic Philosophy*, pp. 45–48; and Sulaymān Dunyā, introduction to *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 1, pp. 16–20. Among Orientalists, see the views of A. Turloc, E. B. Pusey, De Slane, Munk, and others in Carlo Alfonso Nallino’s article “The Attempt of the Muslims to Create an Eastern Philosophy,” published in ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Badawī’s *The Greek Heritage in Arab Civilization*, pp. 253, 254, 256. See also Louis Massignon’s article on Ibn Sīnā in *Cairo* magazine, Cairo, June 1951, and so on.

[^V4-398]: Aristotle, 2, b, p. 35.

[^V4-399]: *The Salvation*, “Chapter on First Principles,” pp. 64–65.

[^V4-400]: Modern scholars disagree about the intellectual relationship between *Pointers and Reminders* and Ibn Sīnā’s earlier works, especially *The Healing* and *The Salvation*. Some argue that when Ibn Sīnā wrote *Pointers and Reminders*, as well as his symbolic stories—*The Treatise of the Bird*, *Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān*, *Salāmān and Absāl*, and *The Treatise on Love*—he moved away from the Peripatetic ideas that stand out in *The Healing* and finally turned toward a Sufi form of illuminationist wisdom. This view is represented, for example, by L. Gardet in his article on the religious idea in Ibn Sīnā, published in *Revue du Caire*, June 1951, and by Sulaymān Dunyā in the introduction to his Cairo 1947 edition of *Pointers and Reminders*. Other scholars deny that *The Healing* and Ibn Sīnā’s other early works ceased to express his real views. This is the position of scholars such as Carra de Vaux, *Avicenna*, Arabic ed., p. 150, and the French Orientalist A. M. Goichon in *The Philosophy of Ibn Sīnā*, Arabic ed., Beirut, 1950, p. 18. We accept this second view. We have found nothing in *Pointers and Reminders* that Ibn Sīnā had not already said in his other works. The book differs mainly in two ways. The first is formal: the way it is composed, the material is arranged, and the discussion is divided into sections. The second concerns expression: the style used to present the ideas and the beauty of the language. The writing is more concentrated and compressed, and in the later chapters Ibn Sīnā sometimes uses symbols and sometimes interprets the outward wording of Qurʾānic passages. These stylistic features may be what idealist scholars used to support the claim that Ibn Sīnā changed the positions expressed in his earlier works, where some materialist tendencies are visible, and ended by adopting an illuminationist-Sufi outlook. Some of these scholars also appeal to remarks in the introduction to a work published under Ibn Sīnā’s name as *The Logic of the Easterners* (Cairo, 1910), where he appears to disagree with the Peripatetics. They also use Ibn Ṭufayl’s statement in the introduction to *Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān* that Ibn Sīnā said at the beginning of *The Healing* that the truth as he understood it was different from what that book contained. See Ibn Ṭufayl, *Ḥayy ibn Yaqẓān*, Dār al-Maʿārif ed., Beirut, 1954, p. 8. In reality, even in *The Healing*, Ibn Sīnā does not agree completely with Aristotle or with Peripatetic philosophy in general. He differs from them on many points and alters many positions. As for the special section of *Pointers and Reminders* on the “Stations of the Knowers,” first, it is a mixture of ideas scattered through his other books; and second, it is a descriptive discussion, not a reflection of Ibn Sīnā’s own way of life, as we know from his historically well-established biography.

[^V4-401]: The Qurʾānic passage in question is: “God is the light of the heavens and the earth. The likeness of His light is as a niche containing a lamp; the lamp is in a glass; the glass is like a shining star, lit from a blessed olive tree, neither eastern nor western, whose oil would almost glow even if no fire touched it—light upon light...” (Qurʾān 24:35). Ibn Sīnā applies this verse to the levels of intellect in his own theory. He interprets the “niche” as the material intellect; the “olive tree” as the thinking faculty—that is, rational reflection assisted by sensory perceptions; the “oil” as intuition, through which knowledge is gained without discursive thought; the “glass” as the habitual intellect; the phrase “whose oil would almost glow even if no fire touched it” as the highest degree of intuition, which he calls the holy faculty; and “light upon light” as the stage of perfection reached when the intellect becomes an acquired intellect. He interprets the “lamp” as the rational faculty once it becomes an actual intellect: the human soul can then “bring before itself, whenever it wishes, an intelligible already acquired and completed as though it were directly present, without needing to acquire it again” (*Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 2, pp. 369–376). Al-Ghazālī, however, reverses the relation and treats the Qurʾānic verse as an interpretation of these levels of theoretical intellect. See al-Ghazālī, *The Niche of Lights*, p. 58.

[^V4-402]: Lenin, *Notebooks on Dialectics*, Arabic ed., Beirut, 1971, p. 213. All the emphasis here is ours. Lenin himself had emphasized the phrases “so that they could” and “axioms.”
