[^V4-407]: In logic, the middle term is the universal proposition relied on in a syllogism to move from what is known to what is unknown—that is, from the minor premise to the required conclusion. This universal proposition, which functions as the middle term, may come either from first rational principles, or axioms, or from theoretical propositions derived from the particulars of external reality. In both cases, even for Ibn Sīnā himself, it is a reflection of external reality, as we concluded earlier from his theory of how first principles are known.

[^V4-408]: By “rational principles” here he means the “separate intellects” in the metaphysical world of emanation, including the active intellect.

[^V4-409]: We have taken care to present Ibn Sīnā’s position on intuition in this chapter of *The Salvation* accurately. His position in *Pointers and Reminders* is not fundamentally different. The differences are limited to presentation and some explanatory details. See *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 2, pp. 378–386. This supports our earlier view that *Pointers and Reminders* did not introduce any fundamental change into the philosophy of the “Supreme Master.”

[^V4-414]: *The Salvation*, previous page.

[^V4-417]: The question of how consciousness arises in the brain remained unclear to scientists until the famous Soviet scientist Pavlov explained it in the way we described earlier. Before Marx, this question remained a puzzle even for materialist philosophers. Like the idealists, some materialists would say either that consciousness is an essence completely independent of matter, so that body and consciousness work separately but in parallel by some kind of miracle, or they would give a crude explanation and say that the brain secretes thought just as the mouth secretes saliva—that thought simply flows from it automatically. Lenin criticized this crude view, arguing that explaining thought as matter is just as false as explaining matter as thought, because vulgar materialism quickly slips into idealism.

[^V4-418]: *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 2, p. 305 ff.

[^V4-419]: Previously cited source, vol. 3, pp. 196–197.

[^V4-420]: See section “A — The Instruments of Knowledge” in this chapter.

[^V4-421]: Ibn Sīnā wrote a special chapter in *The Salvation* (p. 189) entitled “On the Unity of the Soul,” where he argues that the soul is a single entity in which all the faculties of the soul come together. From this he reaches an important conclusion: he acknowledges that the existence of the soul and the existence of the body are one. Yet he finally maintains that the soul does not arise from a body, “but from a substance whose substantiality is separate” (p. 192). This confirms what we have said: he saw the appearance of unity without seeing the underlying reality of that unity.

[^V4-425]: Kant uses the word *transcendental* for all knowledge that lies outside the framework of experience. —Publisher.

[^V4-434]: In this example, the “Supreme Master” points out that the particular meant in this process is not a “temporal particular”—that is, something defined by having occurred now, at a particular earlier time, or at a particular later time.

[^V4-435]: *The Salvation*, p. 247.

[^V4-436]: *The Salvation*, pp. 243–244. One might object that this statement, the one before it, and the earlier examples we cited of the intellect forming universals from particulars—as in the case of an eclipse—occur in Ibn Sīnā’s discussion of divine matters, or metaphysics, and therefore do not apply to the material world. Our answer is twofold. First, Ibn Sīnā uses ideas of this kind in that context because he treats them as general principles already accepted in the particular sciences, such as natural science and mathematics. He then uses them as grounds for his views in “divine science.” Second, we do not separate a philosopher’s ideas in metaphysics from his ideas in other fields. All of them come from a single source: the real world reflected in his thought in one form or another. His metaphysical ideas are simply one form of that reflection. They are particular echoes of the way he relates to the real world and of his particular position in society.

[^V4-437]: See section “E” of this chapter.

The final part of the statement—“without error, falsehood, or forgetfulness...” —alludes to the Imāmī doctrine that a prophet must be infallible, meaning that nothing blameworthy can come from him, whether deliberately or by mistake. Other Islamic schools hold different views about the principle and limits of prophetic infallibility.

At the beginning of this chapter, Ibn Sīnā introduces the subject by explaining why he must rely on principles and laws established in other sciences: “In learning the sciences, we cannot avoid taking for granted premises that are demonstrated in other sciences. The principles of the sciences, especially the particular [specialized] sciences, are learned either from other particular sciences or from the universal science called first philosophy. The principles of a science therefore cannot be demonstrated by that same science [the science being investigated].” He then mentions some of the general principles borrowed from outside the science in which he is discussing prophecy, including: “Every effect must necessarily follow from its cause in order to exist,” and so on.

[^V4-441]: *The Salvation*, “Chapter on Proving Prophecy and on How the Prophet Calls People to God and the Afterlife,” pp. 303–308. A very short summary of this chapter appears in *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 3, pp. 226–227.

We think the reading found in those three manuscripts, without “from,” is more likely to be Ibn Sīnā’s original wording, because it means that essence is different from existence and therefore cannot be its cause.

[^V4-448]: Ibn Sīnā often describes the individuating features of a particular thing as “accidents” or “concomitants” that are foreign to its essence—meaning its substance and true nature. We should not take this to mean that he imagines the substance of a thing as a spiritual substance to which matter is attached only accidentally or by chance. That idea is far from his thought. Although he calls these material individuating features “accidents” or “concomitants,” he still regards them as necessarily bound up with the existence of things and impossible to separate from them. We will clarify this point further when we study the theory of form and matter in Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy.

[^V4-449]: See pp. 260–262.

[^V4-450]: *The Salvation*, pp. 220–221.

[^V4-451]: Muḥibb al-Dīn ʿAbd al-Shakūr transmits this from Ibn Sīnā in *Sullam Baḥr al-ʿUlūm*, p. 107. See ʿAlī Sāmī al-Nashshār, *Methods of Research among Muslim Thinkers*, p. 41.

[^V4-452]: See *The Salvation*, p. 221.

[^V4-453]: This is the doctrine known in medieval European Scholastic philosophy as “nominalism.” In Marxist literature, the struggle between idealism and materialism in medieval European philosophy is described as taking the form of a struggle between “realism” and “nominalism.” Here we may recall Marx’s statement that nominalism was, in general, the first expression of materialism in that philosophy. See Engels’s introduction to the English edition of *Socialism: Utopian and Scientific*.

[^V4-454]: See al-Nashshār, *Methods of Research among Muslim Thinkers*, p. 41.

[^V4-455]: Aristotle sees the relation of matter to form as similar to the relation of the individual to the universal that includes it and other individuals. Form, in other words, is the concept or essence in relation to matter. Callias and Socrates, Aristotle’s example, are the same in species but differ from one another in their matter (Aristotle, *Metaphysics*, 7, 1034a 5 ff.). Ibn Sīnā, by contrast, treats matter as the underlying “subject” in relation to form, and this subject is a substance. “Subject” and “substance” are not the same as essence. See *The Salvation*, p. 200; and al-Shahrastānī, *Religions and Sects*, vol. 2, p. 74. We will explain this point in detail in the discussion that follows.

[^V4-456]: In defining the subject of natural science, Ibn Sīnā says that it is “existing bodies insofar as they undergo change and insofar as they are described by different kinds of motion and rest” (*The Salvation*, p. 98).

[^V4-457]: Aristotle’s dualism of matter and form appears in two ways. First, he treats matter as merely receptive and passive, while form alone is active and acts upon matter. He does not recognize the dialectical unity of mutual influence between them in the way Ibn Sīnā does. See Aristotle, *Metaphysics*, 7, 1029a 19. Second, matter itself has a double character in Aristotle’s view. Matter is potentiality in relation to form, while form is the actual realization of that potentiality. Matter is therefore pure possibility on one side, and an actual reality realized through form on the other. In this sense it becomes relative: a thing is matter—that is, existence—from one point of view, and form—that is, concept—from another. Matter in itself is therefore not treated as something definite in relation to form. See Aristotle, same source, 7, 1041a–b 5–6. We will see in the course of the discussion how Ibn Sīnā overcame this problem partly, if not completely.

[^V4-458]: *The Salvation*, p. 198.

[^V4-459]: In ancient and then medieval philosophy, the term “divine science” meant philosophy of existence in its broadest sense—universal being as a whole. It was the main branch of ancient philosophy, which is why Aristotle called it “first philosophy.” Philosophy at that time included all the sciences and was even called “the science of sciences.” Sciences that dealt with particular kinds of existence, such as natural science, mathematics, and social science, were called particular sciences. For this reason, when Ibn Sīnā speaks of “divine science” in this older sense, we treat him as speaking about philosophy, and we treat the principles of that science as the principles of philosophy itself.

[^V4-460]: See *The Salvation*, pp. 201 and 207, final section.

[^V4-461]: Alongside the many examples already mentioned and others that will appear later, we can give one concrete example here. It occurs in *The Salvation* (pp. 236–237) in the proof of the Necessary Existent, within the “divine” section of Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy. In arguing that “there must be something that necessarily exists,” Ibn Sīnā uses a proof by division in the Platonic manner. When his divisions of possible existence reach the possibility of something coming into being and then ceasing to exist immediately, with no interval of time—in other words, coming to be and passing away at the same instant—he says this is impossible “because instants do not follow one another [as discrete units], and for numerically distinct individuals to come into existence one after another without the continuity found in something like motion would require instants [times] to follow one another, and natural science has already shown this to be impossible....” Ibn Sīnā is therefore using a law of natural science as part of a proof in “divine science.” This is a concrete example of how he relies on theoretical generalizations drawn from the study of nature in arguments concerning the metaphysical world.

[^V4-462]: Ibn Sīnā does not picture the “first mover” as motionless in the way Aristotle does. He speaks clearly and explicitly on this point: “A mover implies something moved. But this implication does not mean that the moved thing must be something other than the mover, or that it must be the mover itself. (...) We therefore did not rule out the possibility of conceiving something that moves itself until a proof establishes that this is impossible. The mere concepts of mover and moved do not themselves rule it out. A moved thing requires something from which its motion comes, without requiring that this be either another thing or itself; and a mover requires something moved by it, without requiring that this be either another thing or itself....” *The Salvation*, p. 244.

[^V4-463]: *Al-Qayyūm* means the eternal being who exists by Himself.

[^V4-472]: Elsewhere in *Pointers and Reminders* (vol. 3, pp. 88–89), Ibn Sīnā also explains that terms such as “acted,” “made,” and “brought into existence” are used when something was first non-existent and then comes into existence after non-existence because of some cause. We then call it an effect, or something produced. An eternal existent, therefore, is not called an effect because, as he says in the passage quoted above, “non-existence did not precede it.” This confirms that Ibn Sīnā deliberately rejects the relation of acting, agency, and being acted upon between the first cause and its effect, the world, because this effect is as eternal as its cause.

[^V4-474]: This is Abū Jaʿfar Muḥammad ibn Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥasan, known by the title Khwāja Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī, after the city of Ṭūs in Khurasan (597–673 AH/1200–1274 CE). He was one of the leading Islamic philosophers of the seventh century AH/thirteenth century CE and one of the outstanding scholars of natural science, astronomy, and mathematics in his age. His works include a commentary on Ibn Sīnā’s *Pointers and Reminders*. See Shaykh ʿAbd Allāh Niʿma, *The Shiʿi Philosophers*, Beirut, no date, p. 472 ff.

[^V4-477]: After Ibn Sīnā’s explicit statements that what is brought into existence “is not preceded by non-existence,” it is surprising to find a major scholar of philosophy such as Dr. Ibrāhīm Madkūr commenting on Ibn Sīnā’s view that the heavenly world is “brought into existence” by saying that “bringing into existence means creation from nothing.” The idea of creation from nothing is fundamentally foreign to Ibn Sīnā’s philosophy. See Ibrāhīm Madkūr, introduction to *The Healing*, natural philosophy section, *The Heavens and the World...*, Dār al-Kātib al-ʿArabī ed., Cairo, 1969, p. n.

[^V4-482]: It is well known that Aristotle’s philosophy reached the Islamic philosophers in both its original form and a distorted form: the spurious *Theology of Aristotle*, which was a Neoplatonic reshaping of Aristotelian philosophy.

[^V4-487]: Another piece of evidence for the eternity of time in Ibn Sīnā is his statement: “Time is not brought into existence through a temporal coming-to-be. Rather, it comes into existence through origination, in which its originator does not precede it in time or duration, but only in essence (...). Time is therefore originated—that is, its Creator [originator] precedes it only....” The passage goes on to say that motion, like time, also does not come into being through a temporal beginning. It too is originated in this special sense, or comes into being through essential rather than temporal origination. This is another way of expressing the eternity of time and motion. See *The Salvation*, pp. 117, 301. Carra de Vaux misunderstood this point in Ibn Sīnā when he judged it “naive from the intellectual point of view” (Carra de Vaux, *Avicenna*, trans. ʿĀdil Zuʿaytir, p. 185). He may not have taken account of Ibn Sīnā’s doctrine of “origination,” on which the idea of “originated” time rests. Ibn Sīnā’s purpose in this doctrine is precisely to affirm the eternity of what is originated in this sense, whether matter, motion, or time.

[^V4-488]: *The Salvation*, p. 253. Notice Ibn Sīnā’s phrase, “it has become clear to us in natural science....” This is further evidence for our claim that his philosophical structure rests on what he had learned about the laws of nature.

[^V4-489]: See pp. 638–639 of this volume.

[^V4-490]: Ibn Sīnā states this proposition in another form that may be clearer and more direct, though the meaning is the same. He says: “Renewal is possible only with a change of state. A change of state is possible only where there is a capacity for change—that is, a subject. This continuity therefore belongs to motion and to something moving—that is, to change and something changing....” *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 3, p. 100. This statement also shows that change is motion, and that the changing thing is the subject of motion—the thing that moves, namely matter. Ibn Sīnā then explains the unity of motion and time within the subject, matter. He says that this continuity connected with motion and the moving thing can be measured, meaning that the quantity of change can be known. This is time: the measure of motion not in terms of distance, but in terms of before and after. Same source.

[^V4-491]: *The Salvation*, p. 223.

[^V4-492]: Engels says in this connection that theoretical thought in every age—including our own—is a historical product and takes on very different forms and contents. It was on this basis that he valued so highly the view of nature as a whole found among the great founders of Greek philosophy. On the same basis, he made his well-known statement about the historical character of modern ideas, in a sense very different from what is meant by those who try to “modernize” the heritage: the various forms of Greek philosophy already contain, in embryonic and developing form, most of the later kinds of worldview. See *Dialectics of Nature*, Arabic ed., pp. 45, 61, 66.

[^V4-501]: Ibn Sīnā explains the difference between first and second perfections as follows: “The perfections of natural bodies are either first perfections, whose disappearance causes the thing they perfect to cease to be what it is, or second perfections, whose disappearance does not destroy the thing itself but only makes its condition less complete” (same source).

[^V4-503]: Same source.

[^V4-504]: *The Salvation*, pp. 105–108.

[^V4-505]: Ibn Sīnā regards matter as a substance. Among his statements to this effect is: “Accidents come after matter by nature (...), while matter and form come before accidents by nature and causally” (*The Salvation*, p. 99). By contrast, he denies that motion is a substance. For example, he says: “Every motion is something that can increase and decrease, while no substance is like that. Therefore no motion is a substance” (*The Salvation*, p. 105). The basis of this denial is clear: substance is treated as static, while motion consists precisely in change and instability, since it can increase and decrease. Substance, on this view, is fixed and does not admit increase, decrease, transformation, or opposites in itself.

[^V4-506]: By “metaphysical” here we mean the opposite of “dialectical,” not the opposite of “physical” or natural.

[^V4-507]: *The Salvation*, p. 105.

[^V4-508]: Same source, p. 101.

[^V4-509]: Ibn Sīnā says in *Pointers and Reminders* (vol. 2, pp. 65–66): “As for forms that leave matter and are replaced by others, they cannot be called absolute causes of the one continuous existence that preserves its identity.” This indicates that succession through replacement—that is, change and transformation—takes place within one continuing subject. The subject of changing motion here is therefore substance, or matter.

[^V4-510]: In this connection Ibn Sīnā says that “the coming-to-be and passing-away of substances are not motion; they occur all at once (...). Motion exists in time between pure potentiality and pure actuality, and is not one of those things that reaches a stable and complete state of actuality” (*The Salvation*, p. 105). On this basis he distinguishes generation from alteration in *The Healing*, natural philosophy section, *Generation and Corruption*, Cairo ed., 1969, p. 224.

[^V4-511]: Although Ibn Sīnā is generally known to question whether motion occurs in the categories of quality and quantity, here he says that it does occur in both, just as it occurs in the categories of place and position. He limits motion in quality to qualities that can become weaker or more intense, such as whiteness and blackness. As for position, he says that in his own view it does admit motion: the rotation of a round body around itself. We find no motion in the category of time, because matter has time only through motion itself. See *The Salvation*, pp. 101–107.

[^V4-512]: See same source, p. 209. This statement by Ibn Sīnā supports Hādī al-ʿAlawī’s conclusion in *The Theory of Substantial Motion in al-Shīrāzī*, Baghdad, 1971, p. 18: some of what ancient philosophers, including the Arabs, placed under the category of substance reflects their awareness of the objective relation between different aspects of phenomena. Dividing existence into substances and accidents points to an attempt to distinguish what is basic and stable from what is accidental, or to distinguish the content that gives a phenomenon its particular nature from what is external or merely formal, however necessary it may be.

[^V4-513]: *The Salvation*, p. 244.

[^V4-514]: Same source, pp. 108–109.

[^V4-515]: In ancient terminology, the difference between *ṭabʿ* and *ṭabīʿa* is this: *ṭabīʿa*, “nature,” is a principle of motion in the thing itself without awareness, while *ṭabʿ* is a principle in the more general sense, whether or not awareness is involved. See Sulaymān Dunyā’s notes on *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 2, p. 161.

[^V4-516]: See *The Salvation*, pp. 200, 208; and *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 2, p. 296.

[^V4-517]: See p. 647 ff.

[^V4-518]: *The Salvation*, p. 276. All the phrases quoted after this point are from Ibn Sīnā and continue the same passage in the same source.

[^V4-519]: According to the Neoplatonic theory of emanation, the “separate intellects” are the first intellects that emanate in sequence from the First Being, God, from the first intellect through the tenth, the active intellect. They are simple substances. In the original Neoplatonic theory, they are called “separate” because “simple” here means not joined to matter—in other words, separate from it. With Ibn Sīnā, however, this “separation” takes on a new meaning. We will return to this point.

[^V4-520]: Al-Fārābī, *The Opinions of the People of the Virtuous City*, ed. Nādir, Beirut, 1959, p. 44. Al-Fārābī makes the multiplicity within the separate intellect twofold, while Ibn Sīnā makes it threefold. Al-Fārābī’s twofold scheme works as follows: from the First, God, there proceeds a first intellect. Insofar as this intellect thinks its principle, God, a second intellect proceeds from it; insofar as it thinks itself, the “first heaven” proceeds from it. The same pattern continues through the remaining intellects. From each intellect in sequence, in addition to the next intellect, there also proceeds a star or a celestial sphere. These spheres arise in the following order: the sphere of the fixed stars, Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, the Sun, Venus, Mercury, and the Moon.

[^V4-549]: See p. 275 ff. We should note here that this kind of relation between cause and effect comes close to the Stoic idea of the relation between the active principle and the passive principle. The Stoics held that the passive principle is matter as materiality stripped of every quality, while the active, divine principle is reason present within matter, which produces all things by giving them their forms. They also held that a thing is truly real only if it possesses both an active power and a passive power—a capacity to move or a capacity to be moved. See ʿUthmān Amīn, *Stoic Philosophy*, Cairo, 1959, pp. 151–152.

[^V4-555]: Ibn Sīnā expresses his view of circular motion as the basis of the different forms of motion in several ways. In the *Nayrūziyya Treatise*, he divides the natural sciences into the ethereal and the elemental. He says that the first is characterized by circular motion, while the second is characterized by a readiness for different forms of motion. See the *Nayrūziyya Treatise*, in Ibn Sīnā, *Treatises on Wisdom and Natural Philosophy*, Istanbul, 1298 AH, p. 93. In *The Salvation* (p. 138), he says that simple bodies are by nature capable of losing their particular position and place. Anything with this capacity must necessarily contain a principle of circular motion. This statement applies to all bodies. Simple bodies differ only in that the loss of position and place exists in them potentially by nature, while in other bodies it exists actually. Every body therefore necessarily contains a principle of some circular motion. Among compound bodies, some move in circles, while others move in straight lines; most compound bodies belong to the second group.

[^V4-556]: *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 2, pp. 118–119. Emphasis in this passage is ours.

[^V4-557]: Ibn Sīnā also argues that earthly natural bodies contain within themselves an inclination toward straight-line motion. See *Pointers and Reminders*, vol. 2, pp. 216–219. More generally, Ibn Sīnā recognized several forms of motion, much as Francis Bacon (1561–1626), with whom the age of modern philosophy begins, would do about six centuries later. Bacon spoke of the motion of fire, air, water, expansion, and contraction. Similar forms appear in Ibn Sīnā as well. It is clear, however, that Ibn Sīnā ultimately reduces all forms of motion to motion in place.

[^V4-558]: *The Salvation*, p. 142.

[^V4-559]: Ibn Sīnā gives another account of the idea we mean here. He imagines that behind every motion there is an inclination or moving power, and that each segment of motion is driven by a new inclination or a new power. At the end of each segment, the first inclination comes to an end once it has carried the motion to its limit, so the next segment requires another inclination. Between the two successive inclinations—or the two successive segments of motion—there is rest. Every straight motion is therefore followed by rest, as is every motion across a finite distance. Two motions do not continue directly into one another (*The Salvation*, pp. 139–140). This brings Ibn Sīnā close to the idea that motion in matter contains a unity of discontinuity and continuity, as we noted earlier. In dialectical materialism, this means the unity of motion and rest: rest is only one stage within motion.

[^V4-560]: *The Salvation*, p. 101. Emphasis added by the present author.

[^V4-561]: Previously cited source, pp. 136–137.

[^V4-562]: Emphasis in this passage is ours.

[^V4-563]: See p. 293 ff. of this volume.

[^V4-564]: He is sometimes called Heraclitus of Ephesus because he was born in the city of Ephesus. He is one of the best-known early Greek materialist philosophers. Modern materialist historians of philosophy associate the rise of dialectics, as a relatively complete method for analyzing the things that exist, with Heraclitus. He held that the universe was not created, that it has existed from eternity and will exist forever as a living, undying fire. He was also the first to speak of the struggle and unity of opposites. Lenin comments on some of Heraclitus’s propositions in the *Philosophical Notebooks*, in the section on dialectics. See the Arabic edition, p. 263.

[^V4-565]: Thales believed that existing things arise through the power of the gods. But he understood these “gods” as forces within things themselves, not outside them. Anaximander, by contrast, explained the emergence of things through the interaction of hot and cold, which he treated as qualities inseparable from the indefinite and undetermined matter he called the *apeiron*. This matter is the origin of existing things, or the substance of the world. For Anaximander, however, the *apeiron* is not an abstract category like the category of matter in Marxist philosophy. It is a special kind of sensible matter that cannot be concretely specified.

[^V4-566]: By “simple bodies” here he means the four elements from which compound bodies are formed.

[^V4-567]: Something close to the basic idea of compression and condensation already appears in Anaximenes (588–524 BCE), who regarded air as the primary basis of everything that exists—not as an abstract category, but as a concrete material substance. Everything in the world, in his view, comes from the condensation and rarefaction of air.

[^V4-568]: Here we find another ancient basis for Ibn Sīnā’s idea, this time in Anaximander, who explained the emergence of things from the *apeiron* through two factors: combination and separation. Ibn Sīnā can therefore be said to have formulated his idea in a way that combines Anaximenes’ hypothesis of rarefaction and condensation with Anaximander’s hypothesis of combination and separation. Both hypotheses have a materialist tendency.

[^V4-569]: This entire passage is from *The Salvation*, pp. 141–142.

[^V4-580]: By the “first multiplicity,” we mean the multiplicity contained in the existence of the “first intellect,” as Ibn Sīnā understood the way multiplicity proceeds from the First One. In his view, this multiplicity consists of matter and form, represented by the celestial sphere as form and its body as matter.

[^V4-581]: The passage referred to on p. 305 says that the multiplicity contained in what is possible in itself—the being that is necessary through another, that is, the first effect—does not come to it from the “First.” It belongs to it because it is possible. Its possibility of existence belongs to it in itself, not because of the First.

[^V4-589]: This is the position of dialectical materialism, which begins from three basic categories: matter, motion, and human society. It defines motion as the unity of motion and rest, or the unity of space and time, or, put another way, the unity of discontinuity and continuity. Since motion is a property inseparable from matter, it is not produced by some external push. Motion is also change in general and development in particular. Matter therefore does not merely move; it also changes and develops. Through motion, qualitative changes and transformations take place. These forms of motion rise from the simplest to the most complex. Mechanical movement of bodies through space is only the most basic and primitive form. Above it come thermal motion, then the various forms of chemical motion, then the motion of organic life, then the processes of mental life, and finally the highest form: social motion. Mechanical motion remains present within all these other forms of motion.

[^V4-590]: Ibn Sīnā first became known in Europe as a physician and later as a philosopher. Europe learned the idea of “double truth” mainly through Ibn Rushd and his *Decisive Treatise*. But Europe’s backwardness and the dominance of the Church led to serious distortions of the ideas of both Ibn Sīnā and Ibn Rushd. —Publisher.
